WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD DUFF v. PENNSYLVANIA RAILROAD CO. 

Workmen's compensation-Agreement for total disability-Termina tion-Retired railroad employe-Receipt of pension under 

Railroad Retirement Act. 

An annuity received by claimant under the Federal Railroad Retirement Act of 1937 is from a public fund contributed to by his employer within the meaning of The Workmen's Compensation Act of 

June 3, 1937, and accordingly where claimant received compensation at the maximum rate pursuant to an agreement for a period of 500 weeks following which defendant filed a petition for review, requesting termination of compensation on the ground that claimant had retired from defendant's service and was receiving a pension under the Federal act in excess of the amount of compensation otherwise payable to him, compensation will be terminated. Appeal by defendant from order of Referee Carmalt, District No. 8, compensation agreement no. 8,158,842. Reversed. November 16, 1949.

Leonard M. Boehm, for claimant. 

Robert R. Artz, assistant solicitor, for defendant. 

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

The facts in this case are not in dispute. Claimant, J. J. 

Duff, was injured on December 21, 1938, during the course of his employment by defendant, Pennsylvania Railroad Company. Pursuant to a compensation agreement, he was to receive compensation at the maximum rate for a period of 500 weeks and thereafter, "compensation shall be payable at the rate of $30.00 per month until the date of the claim-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

ant's death, unless this agreement is previously terminated by final receipt or supplemental agreement approved by the 

Bureau of Workmen's Compensation or by order of the 

Workmen's Compensation Board". Claimant has received compensation from the date of the accident, December 21, 1938, to and including July 20, 1948. According to our computation the 500-week period expired on August 1, 1948.

On August 13, 1948, defendant filed a petition to review, requesting termination of compensation liability on the grounds that claimant retired from defendant's service on 

November 18, 1939 "and is now receiving a pension under the Railroad Retirement Act of 1937, which sum is in excess of the compensation payments of $30 per month (payable for permanent total disability after 500 weeks) prescribed in the compensation agreement, dated January 5, 1939, and entered into in compliance with The Workmen's 

Compensation Act of 1937 (P. L. 1552, sec. 1, 77 PS §511), which act provides in part 'that such monthly payments shall cease if the person receiving compensation accepts other public funds contributed to by the employer which equal or exceed the amount of compensation payments' ". Hearing was held before Referee Carmalt on March 8, 1949, and the referee entered an order dismissing the petition on April 7, 1949. The parties were notified on April 25, 1949, and this appeal was taken on May 14, 1949, which was heard by the board on June 29, 1949.

The issue in this case is aptly stated by defendant to be "Within the meaning of the Compensation Act of 1937, is defendant required to continue compensation payments of $30 per month in view of the railroad retirement pension annuities now being received by claimant in excess of this amount?"

The Act of June 3, 1937, P. L. 1552, sec. 1, 77 PS §511, provides: "Should total disability become permanent, then, in addition to the compensation provided for five hundred weeks, the sum of thirty dollars per month shall be payable during such permanent total disability prior to death: Provided, however, That such monthly payments shall cease if the

1950] 

 person receiving compensation accepts other public funds contributed to by the employer which equal or exceed the amount of compensation payments. . . ."

It would appear, therefore, that the monthly payments of compensation provided for should cease if the person receiving the compensation should accept (a) other public funds (b) contributed to by the employer, (c) which payments equal or exceed the $30 monthly compensation provided.

It, therefore, becomes necessary to determine whether or not the annuities received by claimant, under the Railroad Retirement Act, are public funds within the meaning of the Compensation Act of 1937. 

The term "public funds" has received various interpretations. A study of the text books and cases dealing with this subject discloses that the accepted definition of "public funds" is funds belonging to any municipality or government raised by the operation of law arising from taxation, duties and the like. In 50 C. J. 854, the term is defined as follows: 

"The term "public funds" means funds belonging to the state or any county or any subdivision of the state; more specifically, taxes, customs, money, etc., raised by the operation of some general law, and appropriated by the government to the discharge of its obligations, or for some public or governmental purpose... 

A more comprehensive definition is set forth in 42 Am.

Jur. 718, §2: 

"Public funds are moneys belonging to the United States or a corporate agency of the Federal Government, a state or subdivision thereof or a municipal corporation. They represent moneys raised by operation of law for the support of the government or for the discharge of its obligations. In other words, they constitute 'revenue' which in turn is defined as 'the income of the government arising from taxation, duties and the like" ".

The aforesaid definition of public funds has been cited with approval in Lower Colorado River Authority v. Chemical Bank & Trust Co., 185 S. W. (2d) 461; Wood Brothers

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Construction Co. v. Bagley, State Treasurer, 6 N. W. (2d) 397. 

In Bedford v. White, 106 Pa. (2d) 469, where an action was brought by the two justices of the Supreme Court, for pensions alleged to be due them as retired justices, the court held: "All of the beneficiaries of these laws are paid benefits from revenues arising in whole or in part from the exercise of the state's power to tax. Even in those cases where partial payment is made by withholding a part of the salary derived from tax produced revenue, it has been held that such amounts merely to a reduction in salary and that the funds so withheld to be applied to pensions are public funds.” 

In Gall v. City of Wheeling, 192 S. E. 116, 112 A. L. R. 

477, it was held that pension funds for the benefit of the members of the fire and police departments of a municipal corporation created from tax levies and assessments are public funds of the municipal corporation. See also 

Lawerence v. American Surety Co., 249 N. W. 3, 88 A. L. R. 

535. In Spreer v. School Directors of Blairsville, 50 Pa. 156, the question raised was the right of a borough to borrow money to procure volunteers to the Army by means of the payment of a bounty. The court, in holding that money could be borrowed, stated that the payment of a bounty is not a private transaction in which the individual involved benefits but was a benefit to the public. That it was not voluntary to those who pay it nor was it a confiscation of one man's property to another's use but was a contribution of the public treasurer for the general good and was a direct appropriation for a public purpose. The court held in effect, that the moneys so borrowed were public funds and subject to appropriation by the proper authority.

The Railroad Retirement Act of 1937, 45 U. S. C. §228a provides for the payment of annuities to employes of railroads designated in the act, the eligibility requirements being set forth in section 228 (b) and the amount of the payments being computed in accordance with the provisions of section 228 (c).

The Carriers Taxing Act of 1937, 45 U. S. C. §261, which

1950] 

 is a companion act to the Railroad Retirement Act, provides in section 262 that employes who are to be the beneficiaries under the Railroad Retirement Act shall pay an income tax on a percentage basis as therein provided. Section 263 of the act provides that the employer shall pay an excise tax with respect to the individuals in his employment as therein provided. Section 267 of the act provides that the taxes imposed by the act shall be collected by the Bureau of Internal Revenue and shall be paid into the Treasury of the United States as revenue collections. It is to be noted that payment is made to the Collector of Internal Revenue, in the same manner as other taxes collected by the Bureau of Internal Revenue and that there is no provision made for the segregation of the funds collected. It is also to be noted that section 228 (c) of the 

Railroad Retirement Act provides that there should be created an account in the Treasury of the United States to be known as railroad retirement account and provides for an appropriation to said account of an amount sufficient for the payment of all annuity benefits and death benefits. In other words, the moneys are collected and paid into the Treasury of the United States in the same manner as other funds and taxes are collected, and thereafter are paid out in specific amounts by means of an annual appropriation. 

We have, therefore, concluded that since the moneys are paid by the employe as income tax and by the employer as an excise tax and is collected by the Bureau of Internal Revenue in the same manner as for the collection of other taxes and is paid into the Treasury of the United States without being earmarked for any special purpose but is commingled with other moneys paid into the Treasury of the United States and considering also that any payments made from the fund created under the Railroad Retirement Act is by virtue of appropriation of Congress that the funds are public funds within the meaning of The Compensation 

Act of 1937.

Since section 263 of the Carriers Taxing Act of 1937 requires the payment of an excise tax by the employer with respect to the individuals in its employment, it is obvious

[36 Dep. Rep. 

that this condition of the Compensation Act of 1937 is met. 

It is admitted in this case that the payments to claimant in the form of an annuity under the provisions of the Railroad Retirement Act of 1937 exceeds the sum of $30 a month and this provision of the Compensation Act of 1937 is likewise met.

It is apparent, therefore, that the proviso of The Workmen's Compensation Act of 1937, which provides that payments shall cease if the person receiving compensation accepts other public funds contributed to by the employer which equal or exceed the amount of compensation payments have been clearly established in this case and that, by reason thereof, claimant is not entitled to further payments of compensation.

We are likewise of the opinion that there is no merit to the contention of claimant that the "public funds" referred to in The Compensation Act of 1937 refers to public funds solely of the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania. It is our opinion that if the legislature so intended it would have made a specific provision to that effect and in the absence of such provision, it must be construed as public funds not only of the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania but of the Gov ernment of the United States. The Statutory Construction 

Act of May 28, 1937, P. L. 1019, provides: "The object of all interpretation and construction of laws is to ascertain and effectuate the intention of the Legislature. Every law should be construed, if possible, to give effect to all its provisions. When the words of the law are clear and free from all ambiguity the letter of it is not to be disregarded under the pretext of pursuing its spirits." 

We are of the opinion that the act is clear and free from ambiguity and to insert a provision in the act that the term "public funds" applies only to funds of the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania would be to effectuate an interpretation clearly not intended by the legislature and would be, in effect, usurpation of legislative authority.

We, therefore, set aside the referee's third finding of fact, conclusions of law and order and, in lieu thereof, substitute the following

1950] 

  

3(a) We find as a fact that claimant herein retired from the service with defendant company on November 18, 1939, and has received since that time, in addition to workmen's compensation payments, a railroad annuity in excess of $30 a month pursuant to the provisions of the Railroad Retirement Act of 1937. (b) We further find as a fact that by virtue of the provisions of the Carriers Taxing Act of 1935, defendant employer, pursuant to section 263 of the said act, has paid an excise tax with respect to the individuals in its employment which tax has been collected by the Bureau of Internal Revenue and has been paid into the Treasury of the United 

States as internal revenue collection.

That since the annuity received by claimant pursuant to the Railroad Retirement Act of 1937 is from public funds within the meaning of The Workmen's Compensation Act of 1937, contributed to by the employer, which payments exceed the amount of monthly compensation payments of $30 the review petition filed on behalf of defendant should be granted and further payments of compensation terminated.

Order 

Review petition no. 93,804 filed on behalf of defendant,

Pennsylvania Railroad Company, is herewith granted and further payments of compensation to claimant, J. J. Duff, are terminated, as of August 1, 1948, the expiration of the 

500-week period.

The remaining findings of fact of the referee are herewith affirmed. 

The appeal of defendant is sustained.

[Dep. Rep. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD GANSOR v. DELMONT FUEL CO. 

Workmen's compensation-Specific loss—Amputation of toes of foot— 

Scoliosis of spine resulting from foot injury.

A referee's order granting defendant's petition for modification of an agreement to compensate claimant for the industrial loss of use of his left foot will be affirmed, where the injury to claimant resulted when a caterpillar ran over his foot causing amputation of all toes of the foot; in such case any disability caused by tilting of the pelvis and curvature of the spine as the result of the injury to the foot was not separate and distinct from disability resulting from the specific loss suffered by claimant, and therefore was covered by the compensation payable under section 306 (c) of the act. 

Appeal by claimant from order of Referee Foust, District No. 9, granting petition for modification under agreement no. 4,868,652. Affirmed. November 30, 1949. 

Coal Operators Casualty Co., insurance carrier. 

Joseph M. Loughran, for claimant. 

R. W. Smith, Jr., and R. H. Cunningham, for defendant.

MURPHY, Chairman: 

This is an appeal by claimant from an order of the referee granting defendant's petition for modification of a compensation agreement.

On December 20, 1946, the parties entered into a compensation agreement, providing for the payment of compensation at the rate of $20 a week, commencing on July 26, 1946, and describing the accident of July 18, 1946 and the resulting injuries as follows: 

"Caterpillar ran over foot, causing amputation of all toes on left foot.'

On June 17, 1948, defendant filed its petition for modification setting out that claimant's disability had resolved itself

1950] 

 into the industrial loss of use of the left foot. Claimant's answer, filed on July 9, 1948, denied the averments of defendant's petition and averred that though he had returned to light employment, he suffers disability resulting from curvature of the back and asked that, after payment of compensation for the specific loss had been made, an order of suspension be entered. After hearings, the referee found that any disability of the back from which claimant suffers is due to the loss of the toes of his left foot and is not a condition separate and apart therefrom and, in accordance with such finding granted the prayer of defendant's petition. 

This appeal followed.

After carefully reviewing the facts of the instant case, we are of the opinion that the action of the referee must be affirmed. The testimony of the medical witnesses, both for claimant and defendant, is to the effect that claimant presently suffers from some tilting of the pelvis and lateral scoliosis of the spine which both physicians agree is a direct result of the loss of the toes of claimant's left foot. Under such conditions, it cannot be held that the tilting of the pelvis or the scoliosis are separate and distinct from the disability resulting from the specific loss suffered by claimant. Compensation payable under section 306 (c) of The

Workmen's Compensation Act covers all disability as a result of specific loss, and includes pain, annoyance, inconvenience, inability to work and everything incident to the permanent injury. It provides for all disability resulting from the permanent injuries and the compensation paid thereunder is fixed without regard to personal capacity to work, or loss of earning power. When it is claimed that some other part of the body is affected, in addition to showing that it is so affected as a direct result of the permanent injury, the disability must be separate and distinct from that which normally follows an injury under section 306 (c) of the act. That the condition complained of by claimant is separate and distinct has not been shown: O'Donnell v.

South Fayette Township School District, 105 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 488.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

We, therefore, affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order. 

Claimant's appeal is dismissed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD HERCHENBERG v. THE GREAT ATLANTIC & PACIFIC TEA CO. 

Workmen's compensation—claim petition—Failure to file within a year of accident.

A referee's order dismissing claimant's petition for compensation on the ground that claim was not filed within one year from the date of her accidental injury will be affirmed, where the only evidence in support of claimant's contention that defendant's conduct induced her to postpone the filing of her petition beyond the period of the statute of limitations was her testimony that she reported her injury to defendant's assistant manager on the day of the accident and again several months later when she was going to a hospital for treatment; that on several occasions thereafter within a year from the date of her injury she inquired of defendant's manager and also of a district representative what they were going to do for her and received a reply that they would do the best they could; defendant's inaction did not constitute conduct which estopped it from pleading the bar of the statute of limitations. 

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Phillips, District No. 3, claim petitions no. 113,413 and no. 114,258, accident no. 5,237,160. Affirmed. November 16, 1949. 

Wm. P. Wilson, for claimant. 

Hugh J. McMenamin, for defendant.

MURPHY, Chairman: 

On May 29, 1947, claimant was injured while in the course of her employment with defendant. She was moving a box

1950] 

 containing canned foods when it accidently struck her right breast. Claimant reported the occurrence to her superior and continued to work to August 10, 1947, when she was taken to a hospital. An operation for the removal of the right breast was performed. Claimant returned to work on 

September 28, 1947, and worked until April 17, 1948, when she quit because her right arm bothered her. The claim petition was filed on July 22, 1948, and a second claim petition was filed on October 14, 1948. Both petitions were dismissed by the referee because they were not filed within one year of the date of the accident. Claimant has taken this appeal. 

Defendant offered no evidence.

Claimant contends that defendant's conduct induced her to postpone the filing of her claim petition and that defendant is therefore estopped from pleading the bar of the statute of limitations: Meyers v. Lehigh Valley Transporta tion Co. et al., 138 Pa. Superior Ct. 569; Machanitz v. P. & 

W. V. Ry. Co., 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 359. These cases hold that if defendant so conducts himself as to mislead or deceive claimant, whether innocently or with a fraudulent purpose and claimant is induced to delay the filing of the claim petition until the period of limitation has run, defendant cannot be permitted to plead the delay as a defense and thus benefit from a neglect for which defendant is responsible. The question that confronts us is whether claimant's evidence is sufficient to constitute an estoppel. 

In the case of Behanna v. Meyers, 163 Pa. Superior Ct. 

200, claimant had discussed his claim with his employer and was told that although the employer was too busy, a report would be filed. About six months after the accident claimant was interviewed by the insurance adjuster and eight months after the accident was examined by a physician for the insurance carrier. Claimant was then confined for 

10 weeks in a hospital for the injury he sustained and 13 months after the accident was advised that his claim was barred. The appellate court held (p. 204) that the statute of limitations was tolled and "the claimant was lulled into

[36 Dep. Rep. 

a sense of security which led him to postpone filing of his claim petition."

Claimant here testified that she reported the occurrence to the assistant manager on the day of the accident and again on the morning of August 10, 1947, when she was going to the hospital. Claimant next spoke to the manager of the store when she left the hospital and asked him what the company was going to do for her. He asked what happened and she gave him a description of the accident. 

Claimant admitted that she had no knowledge of the fact that she was required to file a written claim within a year but explained that after she returned to work she spoke to the "bigger bosses" and they always spoke nicely to her.

She asked what they were going to do for her and they said they would do the best they could and she just let it go at that. She spoke first to the manager and he said he didn't know what they would do for her. When she spoke to the district representative he said he would see. These conversations took place in September, November and December of 1947 and when nothing happened claimant thought she would write to the company president. Claimant finally admitted that she waited so long because she did not know it was necessary to file the claim. 

Claimant's evidence fails to disclose any conduct on defendant's part which would relieve claimant's failure to file her claim petition within one year from the date of the accident. Defendant had apparently shown indifference as to claimant's injury but defendant's inaction does not constitute conduct which induced claimant to postpone the filing of the claim. She admitted that she did not know that she was required to file a claim within one year and we may not extend the period in an effort to aid a meritorious claimant.

Accordingly, we affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order. Claimant's appeal is dismissed.

1950] 

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD DUNSKY v. PHILADELPHIA TRANSPORTATION CO. 

Workmen's compensation-Injury on defendant's premises-Cashier in subway station-Fall on the steps leading from station. 

An award to claimant based on the referee's finding that her disability occurred on defendant's premises while in the course of her employment will be affirmed, where it appeared that claimant, a cashier employed by defendant transportation company, was injured as she slipped on the steps leading from a subway station where she was employed, which she was leaving on the way to lunch; the entrance to the station was an essential part of defendant's operating premises, although the public also used the crossing where the accident occurred. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Alessandroni, District No. 1, claim petition no. 115,232, accident no. 

5,347,345. Affirmed. December 28, 1949. 

Philip J. Franzese, for claimant. 

George H. Detweiler, for defendant.

MURPHY, Chairman: 

Claimant, a cashier employed by defendant, was injured on December 22, 1948, when she slipped on steps leading from the Fairmount Avenue subway station. She was relieved by another cashier and was leaving the station to get her lunch when injured. Claimant suffered a fracture of the right tibia and has been totally disabled from the date of the accident to February 6, 1949, and from April 7, 1949, to the date of the hearing. The referee awarded compensation, finding as a fact that claimant's disability occurred on defendant's premises while in the course of her employment. Defendant has taken this appeal.

Defendant operates the Broad Street Subway in the City of Philadelphia. Claimant's actual work was performed in

[36 Dep. Rep. 

the cashier's booth. The entrances or exits to the Fairmount 

Avenue subway station were maintained by defendant. We are of the opinion that claimant was injured not only on the property of defendant but on that part of the premises which was used by defendant in connection with the transportation of passengers, the business in which the claimant was employed. The entrance was an essential part of the defendant's "operating premises": DiCicco v. Downs Carpet Co., Inc., 137 Pa. Superior Ct. 483, 487; Young v. Hamilton Watch Co., 158 Pa. Superior Ct. 448, 550. The entrance to the subway "does not cease to be the 'premises' of the employer in this case although the public also uses this crossing where the accident occurred": Ganassi v. Pittsburgh Coal Co., 162 Pa. Superior Ct. 289, 292.

Accordingly, we affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and award. 

The defendant's appeal is dismissed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD NALEPA v. STATE WORKMEN'S INSURANCE FUND Workmen's compensation-Employe-painter-Wife as alleged owner of business—Design to conceal business because of other employment. 

A claim for compensation based on the testimony of claimant and his wife that the latter was the owner of a painting and decorating business and that claimant worked for her as a painter and foreman, in the course of which employment he received an injury resulting in disability, will be denied where it appears that claimant was an employe of the Post Office and as such was not allowed to own any business and that he really used his wife as a straw-owner to protect his position in the Post Office, it being admitted that he was an

1950] 

 experienced painter, that he actually selected painting equipment and ordered materials, and that his wife, the alleged owner, was a housekeeper and had little or nothing to do with the painting business. 

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Smith, District No. 10, claim petition no. 114,716, accident no. 

5,298,003. Affirmed as modified. December 14, 1949.

State Workmen's Insurance Fund, insurance carrier. 

J. R. Heyison, for claimant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for defendant.

KNOLL, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by claimant from an order of the referee dismissing his claim petition. 

The petition, filed November 29, 1948, alleged that claimant suffered a right inguinal rupture on September 13, 1948, while he was engaged in his employment as a painter. Defendant, in answer, denied that claimant was an employe within the meaning of The Workmen's Compensation Act. 

The referee's order of disallowance was based on findings of fact that claimant's alleged injury was not the result of an accident.

The only witnesses called in this case were claimant and his wife, Genevieve Nalepa, the alleged employer. Claimant testified that his wife was engaged in the painting and decorating business on or about July 3, 1948, and that he worked for her as a painter and foreman at the rate of $1 per hour; that his wife bought and paid for the equipment necessary for painting, and that she deducted social security and income tax payments from his wages. According to his testimony, defendant made two contracts to paint homes, collected the money for these jobs, hired three painters to do the work and fired one painter whose work was not satisfactory to her. Claimant, who worked nights in the Post 

Office, said that he worked whenever he was not too tired;

[36 Dep. Rep. 

that he thought that his wife could fire him at any time, and that, as an employe of the Post Office, he was not allowed to own any business. Relevant to the alleged injury, claimant testified that he felt a sharp pain in the lower abdomen region on the morning of September 13, 1948, while he was raising a ladder against a building when he gave the ladder a heave in moving it away from an overhead telephone wire; that he noticed a lump on his side soon after and consulted his physician the same day, that he underwent a hernia operation on October 2, 1948, and was disabled until November 23, 1948. 

Genevieve Nalepa, mother of children aged 12 and 10, testified that she went into the painting business in July 1948, because she needed the extra money since she was expecting another child; that her husband picked out the necessary painting equipment for her; that she told him to order paints, etc., that he was present when the prices were set for the two paint contracts, and that she was contacted for the paint jobs because the people knew that "my husband knows how to paint". The records of the business, workmen's insurance policy, withholding statements and social security payments listed Genevieve Nalepa as the employer.

Although uncontradicted, we do not believe the testimony of claimant and his wife with respect to his claim that he was an employe and not the actual owner of the painting business. It appears to us that claimant used his wife as a "straw" owner to protect his position in the Post Office since he was not permitted to engage in business. It is clearly evident that he was experienced as a painter; that this fact was known to others and resulted in the two paint contracts; that he actually selected the painting equipment and ordered the materials; and that the alleged owner, a housekeeper and mother of two small children, had little or nothing to do about the painting. Under all these circumstances, it is improbable that the relationship of master and servant existed between claimant and his wife as required by the provisions of The Workmen's Compensation Act.

1950] 

Further, claimant had failed to prove that his alleged hernia resulted from an accident. He offered no proof of unusual exertion or strain beyond stating that he had to heave the ladder away from some telephone wires while he was raising the ladder in his usual manner. 

Accordingly, the referee's findings of fact and conclusions of law are set aside and we substitute, in lieu thereof, the following

1. On September 13, 1948, claimant was engaged in a house painting business and was using his wife's name, Genevieve Nalepa, to conceal his business activities since he was not permitted to engage in business because he was an employe of the Post Office Department. 

2. On September 13, 1948, claimant, while attempting to raise a ladder and to move it away from some telephone wires, felt a sharp pain in his right abdominal region. He ceased working and examined himself and found a lump in his right inguinal region. He consulted Dr. W. R. Jobson, Oil City, Pa., the same day, and was advised that he had sustained a right inguinal hernia. He entered Oil City

Hospital on October 1, 1948, where he was operated upon for repair of the hernia by Dr. Ronald L. Redfield, Oil City, Pa., and from where he was discharged on October 15, 1948. 

On November 15, 1948, he was able to resume his regular employment at the Post Office. 

3. Notice of the alleged accident was had by the alleged employer within the statutory period. 

4. Claimant was totally disabled from October 2, 1948, to November 15, 1948. 

5. Claimant's disability resulted from a right inguinal hernia, which hernia was not the result of an accident such as is defined in The Workmen's Compensation Act. 

1. On September 13, 1948, claimant was not an employe within the provisions of The Workmen's Compensation Act and amendments thereto.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

2. Claimant did not suffer an accident on September 13, 1948, as defined in The Workmen's Compensation Act and is, therefore, not entitled to compensation payments. Disallowance

In accordance with the findings of fact, and the conclusions of law herein stated, compensation is disallowed claim ant Stanley S. Nalepa. 

Claimant's appeal is dismissed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD CIAMPA v. CITY OF JEANNETTE Workmen's compensation—Employe—Performance of gratuitous serv ice by outsider-Request by regular employe for assistance. 

An award of compensation will be reversed on the ground that claimant was not an employe of defendant at the time of his injury resulting in his disability, where it appears that defendant's regular employes were engaged in the laying down of a sewer and that upon coming to work in the morning of a certain date they found a compressor parked in such a way as to block the progress of their work, whereupon they asked the assistance of claimant and another person who were performing work for another property owner nearby, that claimant in response to the request did help in the moving of the compressor and was injured while doing so; under the circumstances there was no emergency making it necessary for defendant's regular employe to obtain help from claimant so as to make defendant responsible for his injury. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Foust, District No. 9, claim petition no. 116,013, accident no. 5,376,411. 

Reversed. November 14, 1949. 

New Amsterdam Casualty Company, insurance carrier.

1950] 

Joseph Sheridan, for claimant. 

W. W. Matson, for defendant.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by defendant, City of Jeannette, from an award entered by Referee Foust July 14, 1949, in favor of claimant, Joseph Ciampa, Jr. The case was instituted by a claim petition filed March 16, 1949, and all the testimony was taken at one hearing held by the referee June 10, 1949.

The evidence shows that defendant was laying down a sewer in Broad Street. For the preliminary work a ditch digger and an air compressor were being used. Alfred 

Castilla and Joseph Cesarini were employed by defendant to operate these two pieces of machinery. The compressor was usually taken to the city garage at night but because of bad weather had been left at the site of the job over the night of December 16 to 17, 1948. At 7 o'clock in the morning of the latter date, Castilla and Cesarini appeared for work. 

They found the compressor parked in such a way as to block the progress of the ditch digger. They waited a half hour or more for the city fuel truck to show up and when it did not do so decided to seek help in getting the compressor moved. They asked assistance of claimant and Gilbert 

Constantine who were working 75 to 100 yards away, digging a connecting sewer ditch across the lawn of a private property owner on contract. Claimant and his cocontractor agreed to help and while the four men were moving the compressor, one of its wheels slipped into a ditch swinging the tongue in such a way as to throw claimant up against the ditch digger. He sustained a fracture of the pelvis and injuries to his kidneys and bladder. By reason of these injuries he was in the Westmoreland Hospital from the date of the accident until January 24, 1949. He was able to return to work May 16, 1949.

The above is the factual situation to be gleaned from the testimony of the four workmen we have named. The issue

[36 Dep. Rep. 

on appeal is the legal sufficiency of the referee's conclusions of law and award as the same are based on his seventh finding of fact which reads as follows: "There is no testimony in the record to show why the refueling truck did not make its usual appearance on the job at starting time on this particular morning; and it is fair to assume that Cistilla did have in mind calling the garage for assistance, but found no one up and around in the immediate neighborhood, since he testified that it was 'too early to disturb anyone'. There is no question that the 

City of Jeannette stood to benefit by Castilla's action in requesting help, and that a situation existed of an irregular nature that commanded and justified immediate action." 

Defendant's contention is that the undisputed facts do not support the proposition that the relationship of employer and employe was established between claimant and the city. 

Castilla and Cesarini took their orders from the city engineers and ostensibly had no authority to do any hiring. 

The help claimant gave cannot be eliminated on the ground that it was casual and not in the regular course of employment under section 104 of the Act of June 2, 1915, P. L. 736, as amended, for both of these elements must appear (Blake v. Wilson, 268 Pa. 469) and it is clear that though claimant's service was casual it was nevertheless within the course of defendant's business. It is pertinent to note, however, that another element prescribed by the same section as essential to establish the master and servant relationship is lacking in the case before us. An employe is among other things one who performs services for a valuable consideration. Claimant in this case merely tendered a gratuitous favor.

Aside from the above, this case is ruled by the line of decisions that where the other elements raising the relationship in question do not appear, it can be impressed upon the employer only where an emergency makes it necessary for a regularly employed workman to seek help from an outsider (Byrne v. Pittsburgh Brewing Co., 259 Pa. 337; Reis v. Mosebach, 337 Pa. 412; Grauf v. Reing, 95 Pa. Superior

1950] 

Ct. 8; Jacamino v. Harrison Motor Freight Co., 135 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 356). Such an emergency must be a real one.

It must be of a more serious nature than the prospect of losing a little more time in getting work started. It cannot, therefore, be said that under the circumstances of the present case, claimant stood in any better position than an invitee or a volunteer. The referee was in error in deciding that there was an employment. 

The first, second, third, fourth, fifth and sixth findings of fact of Referee Foust dated July 14, 1949, are affirmed.

The seventh finding of fact, conclusions of law, award and bill of medical expense of Referee Foust of the same date are vacated and set aside and the following substituted therefor: 

1.

The parties to this proceeding were not bound by the provisions of article III of The Workmen's Compensation 

Act. 

2. At the time claimant met with his accidental injury he did not bear the relationship of an employe to defendant and is, therefore, not entitled to compensation. Order The claim petition filed in this case March 16, 1949, is hereby dismissed. 

The appeal is sustained.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD WILLIAMS v. PITTSBURGH COAL CO. 

Workmen's compensation - Accidental injury - Hernia - Notice - 

Medical services-Failure of defendant to tender. 

1. An award for disability resulting from hernia due to accidental injury will be affirmed, where in the course of his employment as a

22 

 [36 Dep. Rep. 

machine helper, claimant was assisting in pushing a dump car when the pole on the car pressed in against his abdomen causing a stinging sensation and where claimant having noted a small lump in the right inguinal region the same evening, reported to his foreman the next morning what had happened but did not mention the lump; under the circumstances the notice requirement of section 306 (h) of the Act of June 21, 1939, as amended by the Act of May 18, 1945, was met. 

2. Where defendant receives notice of the accident from which it could reasonably be concluded that medical services are necessary and fails to tender such services, as a consequence of which claimant employs his family physician, defendant will be liable for services so rendered within the limitations of that Act. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Schwing, District No. 7, claim petition no. 116,442, accident no. 

5,392,308. Affirmed. December 28, 1949. 

J. W. Stephenson, for claimant. 

Uhl & Uhl, for defendant.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal from a hernia award entered by Referee 

Schwing June 24, 1949, in favor of claimant, Robert Williams. The claim petition in the case was filed April 30, 1949, and the referee held his hearing June 14, 1949.

According to the testimony, claimant worked as a machine helper for defendant, Pittsburgh Coal Company. 

On February 21, 1949, in the course of his duties, he was helping some fellow workmen push a repaired dump car from the blacksmith shop to the tipple at defendant's Jenners mine. As they neared the top of an intervening slate fill, he slipped back on the loose dirt and the pole on the car pressed in against his abdomen. He felt an immediate stinging sensation at the site of the injury and became nauseated. His discomfort passed away, however, and he finished his day's work. The same evening he discovered a small lump in the right inguinal region and the next

1950] 

 morning reported to his foreman, C. J. Fuetter, what had happened but did not mention the hernial lump. Without delay Fuetter supervised the filling out and filing of an accident report because of the possibility of internal injuries and, called as a witness, he has corroborated claimant's account as to the giving of notice. 

Notwithstanding the hernial condition claimant continued to work until laid off March 15, 1949. He then consulted 

Dr. F. E. Sass, a physician of his own choice, who advised an operation. Claimant conveyed this news to Dwight 

Barron, a clerk in defendant's office, on March 28, 1949, and then was admitted to the Memorial Hospital in Johnstown where he underwent a herniotomy two days later. 

Dr. Sass took part in the operation and has rendered an opinion to the effect that claimant's hernia was due to the alleged accident.

On appeal defendant has pressed many points which are not pertinent. This is due to an obvious misunderstanding of the applicable law. The arguments presented to us bring up all of the jurisdictional requirements of section 306 (h) of the Act of June 21, 1939, P. L. 520, but the amending section of the Act of May 18, 1945, P. L. 671, was in effect when claimant sustained his accidental injury. The only requirement of the latter provision of the law is that notice be given within 48 hours. To us this means notice of accidental injury and does not require an employe to be able to diagnose his case. Even under the earlier amendment of April 13, 1927, P. L. 186, sec. 306 (g), which was as strict in its terms regarding the manifestations of hernia as the amendment of 1939 (supra) it was held that notice within 48 hours of accidental injury and pains in the stomach was sufficient: Hopp v. Taub et al., 117 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 55, 177 Atl. 55. In the case before us the report given to Fuetter by claimant should have had the effect of a red flag. It called for immediate examination whether or not claimant expressed the thought that his discomfort would wear away.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Another phase of this matter is defendant's liability for care of the claimant by the family physician. What we have said above applies to this also. If defendant had acted as the emergency inherent in the notice suggested its medical service would have had claimant in its care. 

However, since neither the notice of accident nor the notice of the necessity of an operation were acted upon without delay, it cannot be said that claimant was precluded from medical benefits because he turned to his own doctor. 

The findings of fact, conclusions of law, award and order for payment of medical and surgical expense of 

Referee Schwing dated June 24, 1949, are affirmed. 

The appeal is dismissed.

1950] 

 SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA KRATSAS v. GUEST ET AL. 

Workmen's compensation-Recovery by employe against third party tortfeasor-Employer's subrogation claim-Section 

319 as amended by Act of May 18, 1945Employe's counsel fees.

Where an employe injured in the course of his employment recovers against a third party tortfeasor, his employer's subrogation claim on account of compensation paid is not subject to reduction by a proportionate share of the employe's counsel fees but the expenses of the action must first be deducted from the verdict, after which the employer is entitled to receive from the balance the amount of compensation paid.

Appeal from the order of Court of Common Pleas of 

Allegheny County, July term, 1947, no. 450. Affirmed. 

January 12, 1950. Superior Court of Pennsylvania, April term, 1949, no. 122. 

Robert A. Jarvis and Beck, McGinnis & Jarvis, for appellant. 

John R. Dierst, Jr., O. W. T. Peterson and Griggs, Moreland, Blair & Douglass, for appellee.

DITHRICH, J.: 

Appellant brought suit in trespass to recover damages for personal injuries resulting from an automobile accident. 

He obtained a verdict and, after motions for a new trial and judgment n. o. v. were filed, settled for $3500. Since the accident occurred while appellant was in the course of his employment, his employer's insurance carrier entered into a compensation agreement with him and paid him $679, taking his final termination receipt for the amount. His attorney was entitled to 40 per cent of $3500 under a contingent fee contract. The employer's insurance carrier con-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

tends that its right of subrogation entitles it to $679 of the balance. Appellant takes the position that a proportionate share of the attorney's fee should first be charged against the $679, and obtained a rule to show cause against the two insurance carriers involved. The court below discharged the rule, holding that the employer was entitled to receive the full amount of compensation payments advanced.

The sole question at issue therefore is: Should the employer's subrogation claim be reduced by a proportionate share of the employe's counsel fees where recovery has been had against a third party tortfeasor?

The precise question was first before the courts in Ellis v. Atlantic Refining Co., 309 Pa. 287, 163 Atl. 531, on appeal from a decision of the Court of Common Pleas of Allegheny County. The Supreme Court affirmed in a per curiam opinion, the gist of which was that "the expenses of the action must be first deducted from the verdict, after which the employer is entitled to receive, from the balance, the amount of compensation already paid, and be relieved from future payments, when as here, the fund is sufficient to pay all these amounts and leave a substantial sum to plaintiff." (Italics supplied.)

To get around that decision concerning which, in the words of President Judge Keller of this court in Conrad v. Arco-Mayflower Transit Co., 152 Pa. Superior Ct. 477, 

480, 33 A. (2d) 91, there was "no doubt of . . . [its] correctness. . . under section 319 of the Workmen's Compensation Act as it then stood," the section was amended in 1937 by inserting immediately after the words "the employer shall be subrogated to the right of the employe or the dependents against such third person," the words "less reasonable attorney's fees and other proper disbursements, With further reference to the Ellis case, Judge Keller continued, pages 481, 482: "There was a feeling in many quarters that the net result was not wholly equitable, and that those who benefited from the recovery in similar circumstances should share the bur-

1950] 

 dens of producing the fund in some proportion to their respective benefits. ... "We think the purpose of the amendment was to make the employer, where the verdict exceeded the amount payable under the compensation agreement, chargeable, as to moneys paid back to it, with a proportionate amount of the reasonable attorney's fees and proper expenses or disbursements incurred in producing the fund, instead of imposing them entirely on the injured employee and his dependents. We can see no other purpose for its enactment, for under the decisions in Wilson v. Pittsburgh B. & I. Co., supra [85 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 537], and Ellis v. Atlantic Refining Co., supra, a reasonable fee for the attorney who produced the fund and the amount of his necessary and proper expenses were to be paid out of the fund before there was any distribution of the balance between employer and employee; and the law was so well settled as to need no amendment or further clarification, if it was to remain as it had been." (Italics supplied.)

Section 319 was further amended by the Act of May 18, 1945, P. L. 671, 77 PS §671, as follows: 

"Section 319. [Where a third person is liable to the employe or the dependents for the injury or death, the employer shall be subrogated to the right of the employe or the dependents against such third person, less reasonable attorney's fees and other proper disbursements.] Where the compensable injury is caused in whole or in part by the act or omission of a third party, the employer shall be subrogated to the right of the employe, his personal representative, his estate or his dependents, against such third party for the balance of any sum recovered in litigation, or paid in compromise settlement, after subtraction of reasonable attorney's fees and other proper disbursements, • • 

When that amendment was first before this court in Funk v. Buckley & Co., Inc., 158 Pa. Superior Ct. 586, 45 A. (2d) 

918, we said, at page 592, speaking through Reno, J.: "Under a title announcing that the purpose of the act was 'changing employer's rights of subrogation,' the legislature

[36 Dep. Rep. 

enacted into law appellants' theory of this case [as stated in the court below] by amending §319, supra, so that it now reads: 'Where the compensable injury is caused in whole or in part by the act or omission of a third party, the employer shall be subrogated to the right of the employe, his personal representative, his estate or his dependents (Italics supplied.) • .. 999. 

But that was not the only change in the "employer's rights of subrogation." If the substitution of the words "after subtraction of reasonable attorney's fees, etc." was all there was to that portion of the amendment, we would agree with appellant that the section had not been materially changed in that respect. But the insertion of the words "for the balance of any sum" before the words "after subtraction of reasonable attorney's fees" permits of no other conclusion than that it was the legislative intent to reëstablish the law as it was at the time of the decision in Ellis v. Atlantic Refining Co., supra, to wit, that "the expenses of the action must first be deducted from the verdict, after which the employer is entitled to receive from the balance, the amount of compensation already paid, . . ." (Italics supplied.) 

Order affirmed.

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA GILL v. UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION BOARD OF REVIEW 

Unemployment compensation—Appeal by claimant—Time for taking.

An order of the Unemployment Compensation Board of Review affirming a referee's decision dismissing claimant's petition for appeal on the ground that it was filed too late will be reversed, where it appears that the bureau denied her claim by a decision dated February 16, and mailed the same day to claimant in California, that on February 21, five days later, claimant sent a letter by air mail

1950] 

 protesting the decision, that on February 23, the bureau then sent claimant a form of petition for appeal, which form was completed by claimant and filed March 1; under the circumstances, the action of the bureau in sending the claimant an appeal blank in response to her letter misled her into assuming that the letter had been accepted as an appeal, the filing date of which expired on February 26. 

Appeal from decision of the Unemployment Compensation

Board of Review, dated July 22, 1949, Appeal No. B-44-IB- 

97-J-3420. Reversed and record remitted. January 12, 1950. 

Superior Court of Pennsylvania, April term, 1949, no. 167. 

Robert Engel, for appellant. 

Richard H. Wagner, William L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, and T. McKeen Chidsey, Attorney 

General, for appellee.

DITHRICH, J.: 

Claimant was employed for some eight years as the manager of a Pittsburgh beauty shop. Having suffered for some time from a sinus condition, she resigned her position and, on the advice of her physician, moved to California. There she registered for work and filed a claim for unemployment benefits against the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania. The

Inter-State Benefit Section of the bureau denied her claim by a decision of February 16, 1949. The Notice of Decision (UC 44) was that day mailed to claimant. On its face was stated: "Last day to file appeal February 26, 1949."

On the reverse side there was an explanation of the method for filing an appeal which contained, inter alia, the following: "If you write a letter and intend that it be accepted as an appeal, be sure to specifically state that 'I hereby request a review of decision' or 'I hereby file an appeal'. Letters which contain only general statement [s] of dissatisfaction or complaint cannot be accepted as an appeal within the ten day appeal period." 

No blank form for appeal was enclosed with that letter. 

On February 21 (five days after the letter was sent from

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Harrisburg) claimant wrote and air mailed from California a letter which was received by the bureau on February 23. 

This letter said, in part: ". . . If you will please carefully examine my particular case I am sure you will agree with me, that I am entitled to the full benefits . . . In the meantime, I have been very assiduous in fulfilling all my obligations pertaining to these benefits. . . I have no alternative but resort to this demand . . ."

Not choosing to accept this letter as an appeal, the bureau sent claimant a blank form of Petition for Appeal (UC 46) and instructions (UC 46 FI). These were mailed to California on February 23—just three days before the last day for filing an appeal. The form was completed by claimant and filed March 1.

On the ground that a timely appeal had not been instituted, the referee dismissed her petition, and, on further appeal, the board affirmed the referee's decision. 

On claimant's appeal from the decision of the board we must determine: first, whether claimant's letter of February 

21 should have been accepted as an appeal; and second, if not, whether the bureau unintentionally misled claimant by mailing the blank forms on February 23.

Section 501 of the Unemployment Compensation Law of 

December 5, 1936, P. L. (1937) 2897, as amended, 43 PS §821, provides, in part: "(e) Unless the claimant . . . files an appeal with the board, from the determination contained in any notice required to be furnished by the department . . . within ten (10) calendar days after such notice . . . was mailed to his last known post office address, and applies for a hearing, such determination of the department, with respect to the particular facts set forth in such notice, shall be final . . ."

In Layton v. Unemployment Compensation Board of Re view, 156 Pa. Superior Ct. 225, 40 A. (2d) 125, this court said, in an opinion by President Judge Keller, pages 230, 231: "Claimants in these cases are not usually represented by counsel and are not conversant with intricate rules of pro-

1950] 

 cedure. If they are expected to comply with formal rules of procedure for claims appeals, fair, correct and reasonable notice of such rules should be sent them with the notice of the department's ruling or decision."

In accordance with that ruling claimant, who was not represented by counsel and who was known to the bureau to be residing in California, should have been sent the form of Petition for Appeal with the notice of adverse decision mailed her on February 16. We agree with appellant that "It should have been manifest to the Bureau that the Claimant, who resided in California, could not receive the adverse Notice of Decision mailed her on February 16, write to the Bureau for specific instructions as to appeal, receive them back, and comply with the instructions-all before February 

26th, the last date for appeal." But instead of sending the form for appeal together with instructions to the claimant with the notice of adverse decision, the bureau did not send them to her until February 23, after the receipt of claimant's letter dated February 21. Since the appeal form and the instructions for completing the same were not mailed until February 23, it was physically impossible for the claimant to have received them in the ordinary course of the mails, completed the form, and have it in the return mail by February 26, the date on which the time for taking the appeal expired.

In reversing the order of the board of review in the 

Layton case, supra, this court said, pages 226, 227: "The general rule is in accord with the board's action, which followed the strict wording of the statute. But there is a well recognized exception to it, namely, that where a person is unintentionally misled by an officer who is authorized to act in the premises, courts will relieve an innocent party of injury consequent on such misleading act, where it is possible to do so."

When claimant received the form of Petition for Appeal with instructions as to completing it, after she had written the letter dated February 21, it would be only natural for her to conclude that the bureau had accepted her letter as

[36 Dep. Rep. 

the equivalent of saying "I hereby request a review of decision." She then promptly completed the form and returned it to the bureau on March 1, 1949, three days after the last day for taking an appeal.

We conclude that claimant was unintentionally misled by the officer of the bureau who sent her the claim petition into assuming that her letter had been accepted as an appeal which was to be perfected by the completion of the appeal form.

The order is reversed at the cost of the appellee and the record remitted for a hearing and determination by the board of review on the merits.

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA SAFIN v. JONES & LAUGHLIN STEEL CORPORATION ET AL. 

Workmen's compensation—Occupational disease—Death from lobar pneumonia - Presence of silicosis by the board. 

Finding

An award of compensation for death resulting from silicosis will be reversed, where the board found that the immediate cause of death was lobar pneumonia but that the primary cause of death was silicosis, the only medical evidence to support the board's finding however, being to the effect that silicosis lowered decedent's vitality and powers of resistance and rendered him less able to combat the pneumonia; such evidence was not sufficient to sustain an award. 

Appeal from judgment of C. P. of Washington County, February term, 1949, no. 132. Reversed. January 12, 1950. 

Superior Court of Pennsylvania, April term, 1949, no. 74. 

Joseph C. Spriggs, Robert Strub, William A. Challener, Jr., and Challener & Challener, for appellants. 

Samuel Krimsly, for appellee.

1950] 

RENO, J.:

In this occupational disease case two facts were incontrovertibly established: first, claimant's husband died of lobar pneumonia; and second, he had been exposed to a silica hazard and had contracted silicosis. The compensation authorities were required to decide whether his death was "caused primarily (as definitely distinguished from a contributory or accelerating cause) by silicosis," within the meaning of the Act of July 2, 1937, P. L. 2714, sec. 

5(b), 77 PS §1105. Twice the referee disallowed the claim.

In the first instance the board vacated his order and appointed an impartial physician. On the second appeal the board reversed the referee, found that "the immediate cause of death was lobar penumonia but that the primary cause of death was third stage silicosis," and awarded compensation. The court below affirmed. The employer and the 

Commonwealth appealed. In this court the question is whether the finding of the board is supported by substantial competent evidence.

There is virtually no dispute about the basic facts. Mr. 

Safin had been employed as a motor operator and coal loader in the employer's mines since 1914 and was there exposed to a silica hazard. About three years before his death he developed a cough and shortness of breath, but he continued at work until March 31, 1939. Two days later he became ill, and on April 10, 1939, his attending physician sent him to a hospital "with the diagnosis that he was developing pneumonia." He died April 20, 1939, and the immediate cause was pneumonia. An autopsy revealed the silicosis.

The conflicts occurred in the medical testimony. Four physicians, including one called by claimant and also the impartial expert, testified that the primary cause of death was penumonia with the silicosis a contributory factor. 

Dr. C. Howard Marcy, the impartial physician, testified that the type of pneumonia which attacked the deceased "is a specific infection and is not definitely related to silicosis.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

There is no reliable medical information that silicosis per se produces any susceptibility to pneumonia." 

For the claimant Doctors Fetterhoff and Alpern testified that silicosis was the primary cause of the death, and the board, resolving the conflict, accepted parts of their testimony. Their testimony, however, is open to the same critical objection which this court made to the medical witnesses in Stauffer v. Hubley Mfg. Co., 151 Pa. Superior Ct. 322, 324, 30 A.(2d) 370, (allocatur refused, 151 Pa. Superior 

Ct. xxiii): "But a reading of their testimony shows that they did not understand what was meant by the phrase 'total disability or death caused primarily as distinguished from a contributory or accelerating cause-by silicosis,' as used in the Act of 1937." 

Dr. Fetterhoff, like Dr. Boughton in the Stauffer case, defined primary cause as the "underlying factor", and concluded that deceased died primarily of silicosis because "the pneumonia placed an additional burden on his lungs to such an extent that death ensued, ... pneumonia was an accelerating condition . . . it was the final blow that really caused his death." He would not agree that penumonia was the immediate cause of death, calling pneumonia the terminal or ending condition. (The board did not accept this part of his testimony; it specifically found that pneumonia was the immediate cause of death.) The tenor of his answers on cross-examination indicated that actually the characteristic effect of the silicosis was to reduce the employe's power to resist the attack of pneumonia.

Dr. Alpern's testimony followed the same pattern. He testified in chief that silicosis complicated by terminal pneumonia caused the death, but his cross-examination revealed that his statement on direct examination meant no more than that the silicosis had diminished or lessened deceased's power to resist a disease.

Although they testified that silicosis was the primary cause of death, it is altogether clear that their opinions rested wholly upon the circumstance that the employe's

1950] 

 silicotic condition reduced his power of resistance so that he could not combat the additional pneumonic burden. They did not deny that pneumonia is a germ disease, and that it bears no relation to silicosis. They did not testify that silicosis causes pneumonia, or that the deceased's silicosis produced or brought about the pneumonia. His silicosis was, according to a fair reading of their testimony, only a weakening process, an accelerating, a contributory, a complicating, a secondary, and, notwithstanding their positive assertions, not the primary cause of the death. "A silicotic condition that merely contributes to death along with other cooperating infirmities, or which simply serves to aggravate a preexisting disease or state of physical weakness into fatal consequences would not be sufficient to sustain an award under the Act": Treaster v. North American Refractories Co., 156 Pa. Superior Ct. 567, 572, 41 

A. (2d) 53.

Claimant's main reliance is the Treaster case, but it and the other cases which she cites do not support her claim. 

In the Treaster case pulmonary silicosis was the active agency which terminated decedent's life and brought about the coughing spasm which so strained his weakened valvular heart condition as to cause his death. In Roschak v. 

Vulcan Iron Works, 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 227, 42 A. (2d) 

280, death was "predominantly" caused by anthracosilicosis and tuberculosis played only a very small part. 

In Anderson v. Schroeder Monumental Works, 159 Pa. Superior Ct. 620, 49 A. (2d) 631, the inhalation of tetryl powder merely irritated and hastened death, which was inevitable because of the silicotic condition, but such inhalation was only a passive factor and did not deprive the silicosis of its character as the sole producing cause of death. In Oskin v. Wheeling Steel Corp., 162 Pa. Superior Ct. 568, 60 A. (2d) 424, although claimant had arthritis the consensus of the medical testimony was that his total and permanent disability was due to silicosis. In Holahan v. Bergen Coal 

Co., 164 Pa. Superior Ct. 177, 63 A. (2d) 504, the immediate cause of death was a coronary embolism which had been produced by anthraco-silicosis.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

This appeal is ruled by the Stauffer case, supra. True, that case was an adjudication under the Act of June 21, 1939, P. L. 566, sec. 301 (e), 77 PS §1401, wherein "solely" was substituted for "primarily." However, the case had been tried before the referee as though the Act of 1937 were applicable, and the opinion declares the principles that apply to both "solely" and "primarily." There the employe contracted silicosis while employed as a moulder. He died as a result of a streptothricial infection. Claimant's medical witnesses testified that the silicosis from which claimant's husband suffered, although not the immediate cause, was the primary cause of his death. Reversing an award for compensation, this court defined primary and immediate cause, and held (p. 325): "Where the primary cause and the immediate cause are not the same, in order to be the primary cause of the death it must have produced or brought about the immediate cause which resulted in the death."

Here the board found that the immediate cause was pneumonia and that the primary cause was silicosis. Thus, the primary cause and the immediate cause were not the same, and the board did not and could not find from the evidence that what it called the primary cause, silicosis, produced or brought about the immediate cause, pneumonia, which resulted in the death.

The most that claimant's medical witnesses established is that the silicosis lowered deceased's vitality and powers of resistance and rendered him less able to combat a germ disease, and that was the cause of his death. Under the 

Stauffer case that testimony will not sustain an award.

Accordingly, there is no competent evidence to support the board's finding that silicosis was the primary cause of death. As silicosis does not generate pneumonia, its presence in rendering a debilitated system less able to withstand an unrelated disease does not create liability under the Occupational Disease Act, and compensation must be denied. 

The judgment is reversed, and is here entered for the appellants.

1950] 

 SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA HEINRICH v. COMMONWEALTH OF PENNSYLVANIA Workmen's compensation Occupational disease-Award by board- 

Reversal on appeal by employer-Failure of Commonwealth to appeal.

Where the Workmen's Compensation Board entered an award against the employer and the Commonwealth, for disability resulting from silicosis under the Occupational Disease Act of 1937 and on appeal by the employer, the award was set aside and judgment entered in favor of the employer and his insurance carrier, there was no liability on the part of the Commonwealth to pay compensation because it failed to appeal from the award; the liability of the Commonwealth under the act is solely derivative being predicated upon a final award. 

Appeal from judgment of Court of Common Pleas No. 

2 of Philadelphia County, September term, 1943, no. 2303. 

Affirmed, January 12, 1950. Superior Court of Pennsylvania, October term, 1949, no. 26. 

A. F. Barbieri, for appellant.

S. H. Torchia, Ralph H. Behney and T. McKeen Chidsey, 

Attorney General, for appellee. 

HIRT, J.:

After hearing, on the claim petition of plaintiff's decedent during his lifetime, the referee found him totally disabled from silicosis and entered an appropriate award. On appeals of both the employer and the Commonwealth, the award was affirmed by the Workmen's Compensation Board. 

Thereafter, defendant employer alone appealed from the award to the common pleas. In disposing of that appeal the lower court, by order dated February 3, 1942, set aside the award and entered judgment in favor of defendant employer and its insurance carrier. Based upon the Common-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

wealth's failure to appeal to the common pleas of Philadelphia County from the affirmance of the award by the Board, claimant, on November 24, 1943, entered judgment against the Commonwealth ostensibly under section 428 of the then applicable Act of June 4, 1937, P. L. 1552, 77 PS §921, in that court. The Commonwealth thereupon, admitting that it had not appealed, moved in the lower court to strike off the judgment on the ground that its liability could be predicated only upon the liability of the employer, and since final judgment had been entered in favor of defendant employer, there could be no liability on the Commonwealth. 

After argument, the judgment was stricken off. On suggestion of the death of claimant, his widow as statutory dependent was substituted as plaintiff of record, and she then took this appeal. The order must be affirmed. 

The precise point here raised has not been squarely decided, but from the import of our decisions it is clear that the Commonwealth's liability on a claim resting upon occupational disease, is purely derivative, since in every case it is predicated upon a final award, or judgment thereon, entered against the employer. That is the necessary conclusion from this language of Keller, P. J., in Weyant v. 

General Refractories Co., 150 Pa. Superior Ct. 502, 29 

A. (2d) 100. "The Commonwealth had no part in causing or continuing the conditions responsible for the occupational diseases which disabled workers in certain industries. The employers alone were responsible for them. When an enlightened public opinion demanded that employees suffering disability as a result of such diseases should be compensated by their employers for their inability to work, as well as employees disabled by accident, the Commonwealth, recognizing the justice of the demand, but apprehensive of the effect of its immediate enforcement on many of its great industrial operations, already hard hit by outside competition, provided for a method of easing the employer's burden of compensation by fixing a maximum of $3600 in certain claims and by assuming payment of a part of the compensa-

1950] 

 tion, decreasing proportionately for disability arising in subsequent years, when the employers would be able to adjust their operations to the new burden; but there is nothing in the act that shows any intent on the part of the Commonwealth to assume any part of the costs of the proceedings incidental to establishing the employer's liability. 

To demand it smacks of looking a gift horse in the mouth." 

There is no inconsistency in Jones v. Philadelphia & Reading C. & I. Co. et al., 154 Pa. Superior Ct. 465, 36 A. (2d) 

252, with that conclusion. The Commonwealth's obligation to pay a part of the compensation in this class of cases is an assumed gratuity in relief of an employer, primarily liable, and does not otherwise become a liability on the Commonwealth. Nevertheless, for its protection the Commonwealth has the status, wherever it chooses to assert it in such compensation proceedings, of a codefendant in the action and, as such, has the right of appeal. In the Jones case the Board had erroneously affirmed an award which had been barred by the occupational disease statute because based on a claim for compensation filed more than one year after the death of the deceased workman. Although the employer did not contest that award, the Commonwealth properly exercised its right of appeal from the order directing it to pay 8/10 of the award. The right of appeal under such circumstances is clear, for otherwise the Commonwealth could not protect itself against an award entered by collusion between a claimant and an employer willing to bear the small percentage of the award imposed on the employer by the act, with the Commonwealth paying the rest. 

Creighton v. Continental Co. et al., 155 Pa. Superior Ct. 

165, 38 A. (2d) 337, also relied upon by appellant decided merely that voluntary payment by an employer, in excess of statutory requirements, has no effect upon the separate and distinct liability of the Commonwealth. 

Certainly the Commonwealth is bound, under liability which it assumed in the act, where the employer alone appeals from an award to the common pleas and the award in claimant's favor is finally sustained. Agotin v. Pittsburgh

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Steel Foundry Corp., 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 322, 43 A. (2d) 

604, affirmed in 354 Pa. 543, 47 A. (2d) 680. But if, in such circumstances, the employer alone appeals and the award is set aside by the common pleas as to the employer, that ends the legal obligation of the Commonwealth also, for it is not liable at all except as a contributor to the compensation payable to an employe under an award or judgment against the employer. 

Order affirmed.

COMMON PLEAS OF LUZERNE COUNTY TOMALINAS v. KELLER ET AL. 

Workmen's compensation-Dependency, total or partial Finding by board.

An award of the workmen's Compensation Board to claimant for partial rather than total dependency will be set aside, and judgment for total dependency entered, where it appears that claimant, mother of decedent, was a widow who resided with decedent prior to his death; that decedent had turned his entire pay averaging $38 per week over to claimant, who in turn gave him $6 or $7 per week for his personal needs and that claimant had no other source of support excepting a married son with a family of his own, who, according to the evidence, gave her no support being unable to do so, and a nephew who boarded with the claimant who had irregular employment in the mines, and who paid her $40 per month when he worked. 

In the Court of Common Pleas of Luzerne County, October term, 1949, no. 776. Appeal by plaintiff from decision of the Workmen's Compensation Board. Reversed. Decem ber 8, 1949. 

Max Rosenn, for claimant. 

James P. Harris, for defendant.

1950] 

 FLANNERY, J.: 

This is an appeal by claimant from the action of the 

Workmen's Compensation Board affirming an award by the referee for partial rather than total dependency. 

The record discloses the following factual situation: "Claimant, decedent's mother, is a widow 63 years of age who resided with the decedent prior to his death. At the time of his death, decedent was 40 years of age and unmarried. The decedent turned his entire pay, averaging $38.00 per week, over to the claimant who, in turn, gave him $6.00 or $7.00 for his personal needs. The claimant and decedent were living in a home which had been owned by claimant's husband in his lifetime. Claimant's husband died intestate, leaving to survive him, claimant, the decedent and two other children. (Claimant's interest in the property, which was free and clear of encumbrances was thus a third interest plus an additional 2/9 interest upon the death of decedent. Claimant paid no rent for her occupancy of the building, but did pay taxes, insurance and for necessary repairs. "At the time of decedent's death, Anthony Bajas, a nephew of claimant, 52 years of age, was also living with claimant, and when he was working, paid claimant the sum of $40.00 per month for board. "Further, claimant had two other children, one, William 

Tomalinas, 34 years of age, married and the father of two children, and a daughter, Mrs. Anthony Skarsulis, 42 years of age, married. Neither of these two children contributed to claimant's support. "Further, upon the death of the decedent, claimant received the proceeds of insurance policies on the decedent in the sum of $3,000."

The fact that plaintiff was the beneficiary of a life insurance policy was held to have no bearing on the question of dependency; the board followed the authority of Norris v. Philadelphia Life Insurance Co. et al. 137 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 293.

Her interest in the real estate was likewise ex-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

cluded from consideration on the basis of the authority of 

Kloskowski et al. v. Hudson Coal Co., 144 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 393.

There remained two factors on which the board based its conclusion denying total dependency. It found the son, William Tomalinas, was a source of income available but not tapped. And with reference to the nephew, the irregular boarder, the Board said: "Since it was admitted by her that part of the income received by her from her nephew was used for her maintenance, it cannot then be said that she was totally dependent upon her deceased son."

The extent of dependency, i.e., whether partial or total, is a mixed question of law and fact and involves the interpretation of the phrase "totally dependent", within the meaning of the Act. Petty v. R. & P. Coal Co., 4 P. C. R. 55; 

Cardone v. Jennings, 133 Pa. Superior Ct. 318; Makar et ux. v. Sullivan Trail Coal Co., 135 Pa. Superior Ct. 317.

We will consider first the son, William, as bearing upon the issue. He, 34 years of age, is married, the father of two children and lives apart from the mother. There is no evidence as to his employment, income, or the condition in his home. 

Claimant was asked concerning William and testified: "Q. Mrs. Tomalinas, is William's wife living? "A. (Through Interpreter) Yes. "Q. Does he maintain a home for his wife and two children? "A. (Through Interpreter) Yes, he has his own family. "Q. Have you been able to secure any support from him? "A. (Through Interpreter) I asked him. He told me he can't, he needs it for his own family." 

And again: "Q. Does William ever contribute anything to you? Your son William? "A. (Through Interpreter) No, he only keeps everything for himself."

1950] 

Beyond these two references the testimony is bare as to William and we have no hesitancy in saying, as a matter of law, that such evidence does not support the conclusion of "another source of income 'available, but not active, which could be tapped.'" On the contrary it supports the appellant's contention of total dependency upon the deceased. 

We come now to the nephew, Anthony Bazas. He is 52 years of age, employed in the mines and when working paid $40.00 per month. Claimant testified as to him: "Q. Who is Anthony Bazas? "A. (Through Interpreter) He is my cousin and relation. 

"BY THE REFEREE:- 

"Q. He also lived in the house? "A. (Through Interpreter) Yes, he lived in the house. 

"BY MR. ROSENN:- 

"Q. Is he your sister's son? "A. (Through Interpreter) My sister's son, my nephew. "Q. And how was he maintained, how did he keep himself? "A.
