(Through Interpreter) Well, when he worked, he earned pay. He gives me and if not. . . . "Q. Did you get any income out of that pay or was it only used for his own support? "A. (Through Interpreter) He gave me some before. 

At the present time, he gives me little bit, but not much because he doesn't work steady. "Q. What do you do with that money? "A. (Through Interpreter) Well, I have to buy something to eat and pay other expenses. "Q. Do you use the money you get from him to buy food or for yourself? "A. (Through Interpreter) Oh yes for both of us. "Q. Did you depend upon that income for your support? 

Did you at the time of your son's death? "A. (Through Interpreter) No, never. "Q. All of your support was depended upon John?

[36 Dep. Rep. 

“A. (Through Interpreter) That's correct." (Italic supplied) 

And again: "Q. How long did your nephew live there? "A. (Through Interpreter) Fifteen years. "Q. Fifteen years? "A. (Through Interpreter) Yes. "Q. Where does he work when he works? "A. (Through Interpreter) In the mines. "Q. Did he turn over all his pay to you the same as your son? 

"A. (Through Interpreter) No. 

"Q. What is the arrangement that you have with him with respect to board? "A. (Through Interpreter) As much as he can pay me, that's how much he pays me. "Q. Is he supposed to pay so much a week? "A. (Through Interpreter) He gives me sometimes maybe twenty or thirty dollars a month. 

"BY THE REFEREE: 

"Q. When he works regularly every day, how much does he pay you a month or two weeks? "A. (Through Interpreter) He gives me about twenty dollars every two weeks, about twenty dollars every two weeks if he works steady."

From this the board, and perhaps the referee, lifted the phrase we have underscored: "Oh yes for both of us" and from it construed a partial dependence. But that ignores all the rest of the testimony; it ignores not only the plain import of what she said but her explicit assertion. It ignores the very obvious consideration that a healthy working man paying, at the most, occasional board of $40.00 per month when he works cannot be maintained, fed, housed, etc., except at a financial sacrifice which claimant here was willing to make because he was her nephew. We have no hesitancy concluding that Anthony Bazas, rather than a source of dependence was a drain on her slender assets.

1950] 

If we look upon the conclusion of partial dependence found by the referee and affirmed by the board, as a question of fact, it becomes obvious that there is not sufficient competent evidence to sustain it and it cannot stand. 

If we look upon the conclusion of partial dependence found by the referee and affirmed by the board as a mixed question of law and fact as held in Cardone v. Jennings, supra, it becomes obvious that the proof here lends itself to only one interpretation under the law, to wit, that claimant was totally dependent within the act. 

In either event the appeal must be sustained. 

Wherefore, now, December 8, 1949, appeal is sustained; the order of the Workmen's Compensation Board is overruled; judgment is directed to be entered in favor of claimant and against defendant in the sum of $3300.00-which amount is determined as follows: Compensation at the rate of $11.00 per week, beginning April 7, 1947, and continuing within the 300 week limitation of The Pennsylvania 

Workmen's Compensation Act; with interest at the rate of six percentum on deferred payments, less such payments, if any, as may have been made on account. 

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA DEMIN v. YELLOW CAB COMPANY OF PITTSBURGH Workmen's compensation-Injury-Loss of finger-Effect on gripping power of other fingers-Compensation under section 306 (b) or (c).

An award of the compensation board to claimant for the loss of use of a finger rather than for partial disability based on its findings that claimant had no disability separate and distinct from his specific loss will be affirmed, where claimant's injury was to his hand but the hand injury fully healed, leaving the middle finger somewhat stiffened and an impaired grip due not to any organic impairment of the remaining fingers but solely to the stiffness of the middle finger.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Appeal from final judgment of C. C. of Allegheny County, no. A 918, of 1948. Affirmed. January 12, 1950. Superior 

Court of Pennsylvania, April term, 1949, no. 73. 

Robert L. Prior, John L. McGrath and McGrath, McGrath & McGrath, for appellant. 

Daniel S. Newman and Dickie, Robinson & McCamey, for appellee.

DITHRICH, J.: 

Claimant's hand was crushed by a blow from a pressure hammer on May 24, 1945, in the course of his employment as a mechanic or repairman for defendant. The third metacarpal of his left hand sustained a compound fracture. 

He was hospitalized and treated, and the parties entered into a compensation agreement calling for indefinite payment of $18 a week. About a year later the employer filed a petition for modification, alleging that the injury was then limited to loss of the middle finger. After hearing, the referee found claimant's disability to be a 15 per cent partial one. Claimant appealed, but—as he had gone back to work for the employer at an equal or better wage-the parties agreed to a suspension of payments until loss of earning power could be shown. Claimant was later laid off, and on October 9, 1947, filed a petition for reinstatement. The referee heard claimant and a medical witness for each party, found that claimant's disability had become a 25 per cent partial one, and ordered reinstatement. On appeal the board reversed the referee and concluded that the injury had resolved itself to the loss of the middle finger and that there was no other separate and distinct disability. On appeal to the Allegheny County Court the board was affirmed. Claimant then brought this appeal. 

The question is: Does claimant's condition amount to more than the loss of his finger so as to qualify him for compensation under section 306 (b) rather than section 

306 (c) ?

1950] 

These sections provide, in part: "Section 306 (b). For disability partial in character (except the particular cases mentioned in clause (c)) sixty-six and two thirds per centum of the difference between the wages of the injured employe, as defined in section three hundred and nine, and the earning power of the employe thereafter; (c) For all disability resulting from permanent injuries of the following classes, the compensation shall be exclusively as follows: . . . For the loss of a second finger, sixty-six and two-thirds per centum of wages during thirty weeks. . . . Permanent loss of the use of a . . finger, shall be considered as the equivalent of the loss of such . . . finger, . . .": Workmen's 

Compensation Act of June 2, 1915, P. L. 736, as reënacted and amended, 77 PS §§512, 513. 

When the metacarpal healed there was a good, solid union. 

About one-fourth inch shortening and some lateral displacement of the bone resulted. The adjacent metacarpophalangeal joint is partially ankylosed and that seems to be what is bothering the claimant. It is agreed that the finger has 90 degrees of flexion. Normal flexion is 90 degrees for each joint of a finger. Since some impairment of flexion seems to be admitted by all parties, it may be that the 90 degrees refers to the combined flexion of all of the finger joints. Due to the interrelation of the normal mechanical aspects of finger flexion the stiffness of this finger is reflected in the other fingers by a limitation of flexion and weakening of gripping strength. Just how much the rest of the fingers are affected is not entirely clear from the record.

Claimant's theory is based on this line of reasoning: 

The injury was to a bone of the hand. The middle finger is impaired and the grip is weakened. Therefore the hand injury has caused a partial disability which is compensable under section 306 (b). The board's counter-theory is: The hand injury has fully healed leaving the middle finger somewhat stiffened. The impaired grip is not due to any organic impairment of the remaining fingers but solely to the stiffness of that finger. Therefore compensation is

[36 Dep. Rep. 

due only under section 306 (c) and only for the loss of the middle finger.

To accept claimant's theory would require, at least, a conclusion that he was worse off with this finger than without it. That is often the case. Dr. Earl D. McBride in his book, Disability Evaluation,¹ suggests that amputation of a middle or ring finger which is stiffened in extension is frequently the preferred treatment. Yet claimant's own medical witness testified that he would hesitate to recommend amputation. 

In Pellegrino v. Consumers Mining Co., 162 Pa. Superior Ct. 119, 56 A. (2d) 267, a similar injury was considered

There claimant suffered amputation of the index finger and stiffening of the middle finger which affected the grip of the hand as a whole. The referee found 20 per cent partial disability under section 306 (b). The board reversed and awarded compensation for the loss of the two fingers under section 306 (c), finding that the disability to the ring and little fingers was a result of the injury to the second finger and failure to have it amputated. This court affirmed the board, citing Bordick v. John Conlon Coal Co., 114 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 522, 529, 19 A. (2d) 536, and Hayden v. Stony Spring 

Coal Co., 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 423, 43 A. (2d) 384, as authority for the proposition that section 306 (c) determines the compensation to be paid for specific losses of members and all injury or disability directly resulting from such loss. 

In the instant case the board found "that claimant has no disability separate and distinct from the specific loss."

Since this finding is based on competent medical testimony, we are bound by it, as was the learned court below. 

Judgment affirmed. 

1 McBride, Disability Evaluation (4th ed., 1948) 

Page 204: "One finger that is straight and stiff will be greatly in the way of grasping objects with the other fingers. It is for this reason that a severely flexed or extended finger often deserves the consideration of amputation.” ... 

Page 212: "When one finger is fixed in full extension or full flexion the person usually prefers amputation. It is easier to overcome the handicap of fixed extension in the first finger than in the second or third fingers because a finger that stands straight out between the other fingers cannot be gotten out of the way in closed fist action."

1950] 

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD JONES v. RICHMOND RADIATOR COMPANY Workmen's Compensation - Occupational disease - Earning power-Employment of claimant as janitor during period of total disability.

A referee's award of compensation to claimant on account of total disability resulting from silicosis will be set aside to the extent that such award included payment of compensation for a period during which claimant was employed as a janitor and received wages substantially the same as those received when he first became totally disabled. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Foust, District No. 9, claim petition no. 110,953, accident no. 5117045. 

Reversed. January 11, 1950. 

Travelers Insurance Company, Pittsburgh, Pa. Insurance carrier. 

T. A. Waggoner, Jr., for claimant. 

John J. Schoenberger, for defendant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for Commonwealth of Pennsylvania.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

It is admitted that claimant, Wilber Jones, is totally disabled from silicosis. Jones filed a claim petition on December 1, 1947, which was heard before Referee Foust on 

February 23, 1949. The delay in hearing the case was due to the fact that the question of insurance liability was raised in the case of Gaydosk against the same defendant and was decisive of the same question raised by the pleadings in the present case. The Superior Court decided the question on January 14, 1949 (164 Pa. Superior Ct. 154).

The referee entered an award on March 9, 1949, later amended on March 25, 1949. Defendant, Richmond Radi-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

ator Company, took this appeal on April 7, 1949, which was heard by the board on September 28, 1949.

The question involved in this case is whether claimant is entitled to compensation for the period from February 3, 1947, until October 22, 1948, during which time claimant received wages substantially the same as those received by him on January 6, 1947, when he first became totally disabled. The record discloses that claimant quit work on 

January 6, 1947, and he was hospitalized from that date until January 22, 1947. He returned to work on February 

3, 1947, and continued in defendant's employ until October 22, 1948, when the radiator plant closed down. Upon his return to work on February 3, 1947, he was assigned work as a janitor. Dr. W. R. McGee defendant's physician, who was called by claimant, testified that claimant was totally and permanently disabled due to silicosis. He stated that defendant favored claimant because of his long period of service and claimant's duties as a janitor consisted of sweeping and picking up paper.

It is the contention of defendant that there was no evidence in the case upon which an assumption might be made that defendant intended the payments to claimant during the controversial period from February 3, 1947, to 

October 22, 1948, as a gratuity. Furthermore, the claim is advanced that claimant was able to perform the duties assigned to him so that he was not totally disabled, within the contemplation of the act. Claimant contends that the job of janitor was specially created for claimant because of his long service and that he did not have the physical ability to perform any work requiring sustained effort. 

Whatever the requirements of the job, claimant continued in the work for approximately twenty months and received full wages. The case of Jones v. Philadelphia &

Reading Coal & Iron Co. 154 Pa. Superior Ct. 513, cited by claimant's counsel, involved the Occupational Disease Com pensation Act of 1937, P. L. 2714. Section 306 (b) of the 

Act of 1939, P. L. 566, defines "the term 'earning power', as used in this Section shall in no case be less than the

1950] 

 weekly amount which the employee receives after disability begins". We are of the opinion that the legislature intended to exclude compensation payments, where the employe was already receiving full wages and no loss in wages was reflected as a result of the accident. This opinion is buttressed by the language of the Superior Court in the case of Scipani v. Pressed Steel Car Co., 150 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 410, where the court said (p. 414): "The amendment goes no further than to say that no compensation is payable, regardless of loss of earning power (as formerly, e.g. Weinstock v. United Cigar Stores Co., 137 

Pa. Superior Ct. 128, 8 A. (2d) 799), when an employee receives as much for his services as he earned before his injury."

We feel duty bound to limit the payment of compensation benefits from the date of October 22, 1948, when claimant's disability became total in fact. Claimant is precluded from compensation for the period from February 3, 1947, to October 22, 1948, during which period claimant was employed as a janitor and received full wages. It is therefore, necessary to modify the referee's order accordingly. 

We hereby vacate and set aside the referee's third finding of fact and in lieu thereof, we substitute the following: 

3. That over the period of his employment with defendant, claimant was exposed to a silica hazard and that he became totally disabled solely the result of silicosis on January 6, 1947. Claimant returned to defendant's employ on 

February 3, 1947, as a janitor and received wages therefor equal to that which he had earned and received prior to his becoming totally disabled, until October 22, 1948, when he again became totally disabled. Claimant has been totally disabled since the latter date.

The referee's other findings of fact and conclusions of law are hereby affirmed.

We hereby vacate and set aside the referee's award and in lieu thereof, we substitute the following:

[36 Dep. Rep. 

32 Award

Compensation for total disability is hereby awarded to claimant, Wilber Jones, and against defendants, Travelers Insurance Company and the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, respectively, 80 per cent and 20 per cent, or at the weekly rate of $16.00 and $4.00 beginning January 13, 1947, to February 3, 1947; compensation payments are suspended as of February 3, 1947, until October 29, 1948; compensation payments are reinstated as of October 29, 1948, at the same rate respectively as hereinbefore specified, to the present date and to continue for such period as the status of disability remains unchanged, not to exceed, however, the total sums, respectively, of $3200 and $800 or a grand total award of compensation against all defendants of $4000.

Defendants are directed to pay interest on accrued compensation in accordance with section 410 of the act; and as well directed to pay their proportionate share of the medical and hospital expense incurred to wit: $51 due and owing 

Dr. W. R. MaGee, Uniontown, Pa., and $99 due and owing the Uniontown Hospital. 

The appeal of the defendant is sustained.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD HECKER v. ALLEGHENY INDUSTRIAL ELECTRICAL COMPANY Workmen's compensation-Dependents and dependency- 

Mother of decedent-Partial dependency.

A referee's order denying claimant's claim for partial dependency upon her son will be affirmed, where it appears that decedent paid claimant out of his wages $10 weekly for his food and lodging, that

53 

 he made no other contributions to claimant with the exception of occasional purchase of groceries, that claimant was employed at an average weekly wage of $25 to $35 out of which she paid $25 per month for rent and $5 for gas and electric service, it further appearing that she had a substantial bank deposit; under the circumstances, contributions made by decedent were not required to permit claimant to meet the ordinary needs of life, hence her claim for partial dependency was not sustained. 

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Smith. 

District No. 10, claim petition no. 108,246, accident no. 

4,950,419. Affirmed. February 1, 1950. 

Stuart A. Culbertson, for claimant. 

Irwin M. Ringold, for defendant. 

Hartford Accident and Indemnity Company, insurance carrier. 

KNOLL, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by claimant from the order of disallowance of Referee Jesse G. Smith, of Compensation District 

No. 10.

On March 31, 1947, claimant filed a petition, averring, inter alia, that her son, John Hecker, sustained certain injuries on December 20, 1946, while acting within the course of his employment with defendant, which injuries resulted in his death the same day. Claimant further alleged that she was partially dependent upon her son for support. To this petition a responsive answer was filed by defendant, denying the dependency of claimant on her son. 

The testimony in this case discloses that John Hecker, son of claimant, was 26 years of age at the time of the accident and death; that he obtained employment with defendant company on September 1, 1946, and moved to Meadville to reside with his mother. Prior thereto, he had resided with his father, who was divorced from claimant, and, before moving to Meadville, was in the Army of the United

[36 Dep. Rep. 

States, but sent no moneys whatsoever to claimant herein. 

The testimony further establishes that claimant has two other sons who had resided with her at some previous time and who, at the time of the injuries of John Hecker, were not residing with claimant and neither of whom was contributing to the support of claimant. 

It also appears from the testimony that when decedent obtained employment with defendant herein, he was paid an average weekly wage of $65, of which sum he paid $10 weekly to claimant for food and lodging, and made no other contributions to claimant, with the exception of occasional purchases of groceries; that claimant has been employed by the Talon Company for about 20 years at an average weekly wage of $25 to $35 and that she is still employed by that company; that she pays the sum of $25 per month for rent, $5 for gas and electric and there is no testimony whatsoever as to what other expenses claimant incurs for food, clothing and other incidental expenses, and that on October 3, 1947, claimant had on deposit in the First National Bank of Meadville the sum of $3,567.

The referee concluded as a matter of law that claimant was not partially dependent upon her son for support and dismissed the claim petition. From this ruling claimant has appealed.

We have made a careful study of the record in this case and are of the opinion that the ruling of the referee should be affirmed.

The testimony in this case discloses that when the son of claimant, decedent herein, obtained employment with defendant company, he was paid an average weekly wage of $65, of which sum he paid to claimant $10 a week for board and lodging, and that the balance of $55 per week was retained by decedent for his own uses and purposes, although decedent bought some groceries on several occasions.

It further appears from the testimony that claimant has been employed by the Talon Company for a period in excess of 20 years and that she is still employed by that company earning $25 to $35 a week of which she pays $25 monthly

1950] 

 for rent and $5 a month for gas and electric. In addition thereto, the testimony discloses that, on October 3, 1947, claimant had a balance of $3,567 in the First National Bank of Meadville, Pennsylvania. It is apparent, therefore, that the contributions made by decedent were not required in order to permit claimant to meet the ordinary needs of life for a person of claimant's position, and that claimant was not partially dependent upon the decedent for support. 

Accordingly, claimant is not entitled to the payment of compensation and the referee properly dismissed the claim petition. See Fitzpatrick v. Hudson Coal Co., 159 Pa. Superior Ct. 53; Yurski v. Continental-Archbald Coal Co., 157 

Pa. Superior Ct. 201; Washco v. Wyoming Seminary, 88 

Pa. Superior Ct. 470.

We, therefore, affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of disallowance. 

The appeal of claimant is dismissed. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD KENEWELL v. BERWIND WHITE COAL MINING COMPANY 

Workmen's compensation-Accidental injury-Pain in the back while lifting-Absence of other unusual circumstances. 

Testimony by claimant that in the course of his employment as an electric welder and while engaged in placing sills in a gondola car he lifted a clamp, when a sharp pain hit him in the back, and that he thereafter continued his work for a period of several months before losing any time, would not support a finding of accidental injury where the lifting operation was a normal part of plaintiff's work and was not accompanied in this instance by any unusual circumstances, except the actual occurrence of the pain in the back; hence a referee's order dismissing the claim will be affirmed. 

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Patterson. District No. 6, claim petition no. 117,238, accident no. 

5416306. Affirmed. February 1, 1950.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Ashton Gardner, for claimant. 

Francis A. Dunn, for defendant. 

Berwind Exchange, insurance carrier.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal from an order of disallowance entered by Referee Patterson September 7, 1949. Claimant, Ralph

C. Kenewell, filed his claim petition July 11, 1949, alleging that he wrenched his back September 1, 1948, in the course of his employment with defendant, Berwind White Coal Company. An answer in the nature of a general denial was filed in due course and the referee heard all the testimony in the case on August 26, 1949.

Claimant was employed by defendant as an electric welder. 

In his testimony he has related that on September 15 or 16, 1948-a change from one of the material allegations contained in his petition-he was engaged in placing center sills in a gondola car. This operation entailed raising the middle of the sills with the use of jigs in order to bow them and then clamping them together before going ahead with the welding. On the occasion in question everything went well until claimant reached for the clamp, weighing from 40 to 50 pounds, to throw it up on the sills, preparatory to fixing them in position. The lift was 412 feet at the most but, as claimant complains, a sharp pain hit him in the back when he performed the operation on the instance under consideration. He admitted that he had done the same thing for several years without incident and that nothing unusual, such as slipping or losing his balance accompanied the act. He kept on working and according to his testimony notified his foreman, W. L. Brenner, when the latter made his rounds a few hours later. According to Brenner's notes the notice was given to him September 23, 1948, and referred the alleged accidental injury to the previous day.

In any event claimant continued to work regularly until 

March 28, 1949. He did not work on the latter date and

1950] 

 also lost March 2 and 3, 1949. The following day he lost four hours. He then worked through to the fourteenth of the same month at which time a mine holiday shut down operations for two weeks. Thereafter claimant lost four more days but that is all. He has worked regularly without loss of wages ever since. In the meantime, however, after trying home remedies claimant was attended by Dr. O. E. 

Bailor at regular intervals beginning January 18, 1949, and continuing to the time of the hearing. He was also put to the expense of examination by specialists, viz., Dr. J. C. 

Mattas, Dr. O. F. Andrew and Dr. W. E. Preston. All of this was expensive and at the time of the hearing he faced the prospect of paying out more money for recommended hospitalization. 

The question before us on appeal is whether or not there is evidence sufficient in quantity and quality to justify a finding that claimant sustained an accidental injury within the contemplation of the law. The referee found that he did not and we are of the same opinion. The foremost authority which comes to mind under the circumstances is Buck v. 

Arndt et al., 153 Pa. Superior Ct. 632. Both of the parties hereto cite excerpts from that case, claimant calling attention to the courts' declaration that "the right to compensation for an unexpected injury caused by the performance by the employee of his usual work in the usual manner is clear" (Good v. Penna. Dept. Prop. and Sup., 346 Pa. 151) and defendant pointing to the courts' more pertinent statement that "Even where there is no evidence of a preexisting condition, the uncorroborated post hac testimony of an employee that while performing his usual work in the usual manner he felt a sharp pain', accompanied by an opinion of a physician based upon such assumed history, will not support an award": Toohey v. Coal Corp., 150 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 297.

The last quoted principle is more in line with the other evidence we have reviewed in this case and some of that evidence is extremely significant to us as the finders of the facts. Considering the testimony of Brenner and claimant and the allegations of the petition filed, the happening of

[36 Dep. Rep. 

the supposed accident is very indefinite in point of time. 

Dr. Bailor, called as a witness, did not in his opinion give the connection between the lifting of the clamp and the precipitation of claimant's back pain any greater status than possibility. J. F. McClain, a fellow workman of claimant's who was supposed to have been working with him and to have had first hand knowledge of claimant's mischance, denied that he knew anything about it in particular in his testimony in defendant's behalf. Thus there is other substance to consider aside from claimant's "uncorroborated post hac testimony" and it is of no benefit to his case. 

The findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of 

Referee Patterson dated September 7, 1949, are affirmed. 

The appeal is dismissed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD GOLOBISH v. WEIRTON COAL COMPANY Workmen's compensation-Death Suicide Causal connection with prior injury.

A referee's award of compensation to claimant on account of the death of her husband, who committed suicide, will be reversed, where the award was based on the referee's findings that an accidental injury sustained by decedent was the proximate cause of his suicide but the only medical testimony as to any causal connection between the injury and the suicide was to the effect that conditions resulting from the injury might have affected decedent's mental serenity; such proof was insufficient to support an award, especially so where other medical testimony was to the effect that decedent's injury did not so derange his mind that he was incapable of understanding the consequences of his final act. 

Commissioner Knoll dissents. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Foust. District No. 9, claim petition no. 107,031, accident no. 4664827. 

Reversed. February 1, 1950.

1950] 

James A. Reilly, for claimant. 

I. Burdette Coldren, for defendant.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal from a death award entered by Referee Foust February 2, 1949. The claim petition in the case was filed December 9, 1946. The referee held the first hearing 

April 22, 1947. Following this there were quite a number of continued hearings and postponements, the case finally being closed January 31, 1949.

Claimant, Mary Golobish, is the widow of Mike Golobish, who committed suicide by hanging himself with his belt in a rather secluded and little used court in the Mercy Hospital in Pittsburgh. He was last seen in the ward where his bed was located October 17, 1946. His body was found two days later. It is not disputed that he took his own life or that claimant was dependent upon him. The issue is whether or not an accidental injury which occurred May 23, 1945, was the proximate cause of his self-destruction. 

On the last mentioned date, decedent was working as a miner for defendant, Weirton Coal Company. He was struck and dragged by a loading machine, sustaining a laceration of the scalp, a contusion of the left wrist and fractures of the fourth and sixth dorsal vertebrae. He immediately came under the care of Dr. George H. Robinson at the Uniontown Hospital and was discharged back to work July 27, 1945. Dr. Robinson again attended him for pneumonia in the same hospital from August 19 to September 14, 1945, and after that checked him at two-month intervals because of complaints of pain in the back on bending. In 

Dr. Robinson's judgment, decedent was ready for rehabili tative work July 1, 1946. Then Dr. Joseph B. Brain, physi cian for the workmen at Weirton Coal Company, took over. 

Dr. Brain was not sure about decedent's return to work. 

He thought it better to send decedent to Mercy Hospital for orthopedic consultation on the service of Dr. Harold C. 

Kuehner. This last hospitalization covered the period from 

September 18, 1946, until the date of the suicide and the

[36 Dep. Rep. 

treatment given was physiotherapy. During all the time covered by the events above set forth, defendant remained liable for compensation under a total disability agreement dated June 11, 1945.

The particular circumstance which claimant apparently relies upon to establish a prima facie case is that when Dr. 

Kuehner last saw decedent at 5:15 o'clock on the afternoon of October 17, 1946, he noted the presence of worry and excitement. This suggested to him that a psycho-neuropathic examination should be made but he did not think the necessity was immediate. In his testimony he has said that the symptoms were not sufficiently alarming to warrant the administration of sedatives or the resort to measures of restraint. But with such a preliminary showing in the record, the question rises whether or not the aftermath of the decedent's accidental injury so disturbed his mental balance "that he killed himself while possessed by an uncontrollable insane impulse or while in a delirium, or frenzy, without rational knowledge of the physical consequences of his act": 

Lupfer v. Baldwin Locomotive Works, 269 Pa. 275; Gas perin v. Consolidated Coal Co., 293 Pa. 589; Cubit v. Philadelphia, 138 Pa. Superior Ct. 325. The burden of proving this was upon claimant in the instant case. Section 301 of the Workmen's Compensation Act, as amended by the Act of June 21, 1939, P. L. 520, places the onus of proving intentional self-infliction of injury or death on defendant but where suicide is admitted the burden of proof shifts to claimant: Kasman v. Hillman Coal & Coke Co., 149 Pa. Superior

Ct. 263; Vadnal v. Krsul-Kutchel Coal Co., 149 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 269. With these principles in mind, we have reviewed the evidence in the case before us and have reached the conclusion that it does not warrant the effect that the referee has given it.

Dr. Kuehner was claimant's only medical witness. He stated that his patient had ordinarily been quiet, taciturn and coöperative and that there had been no departure from this normal behavior except at the time of his last visit. He could not account for that departure "unless it had been due to a depressional psychotic state" and he felt that the

1950] 

 mental condition so described "must have developed to the point of suicide". However, he called attention to the fact that he was a surgeon and not a psychiatrist and admitted that he was reasoning from the effect back to the cause. 

The most that he could be pinned down to by way of a competent opinion was that the conditions due to the injuries which he treated might have affected decedent's mental serenity. He did not know "specifically in any instance that they did. . .". Such medical proof does not comply with the standard required by law: Elonis v. Lytle Coal 

Co., 134 Pa. Superior Ct. 264.

Defendant introduced its evidence through Dr. Brain, Dr. Robinson, Dr. Harold L. Mitchell, a neuro-psychiatrist; 

Joseph Maraney, decedent's foreman; John Donelick, a fellow workman who was in the Mercy Hospital at the same time as decedent; Charles Lotz, an engineer, and Paul C. 

Sheahan, defendant's compensation agent. Dr. Robinson did not detect anything unusual about decedent's behavior while he was dealing with him. Dr. Brain, Sheahan, Maraney and Donelich noted decedent's tendency to be noncommunicative. Dr. Mitchell, in answer to a hypothetical question, expressed a definite opinion against the theory of the case claimant has attempted to propound. Lotz, by blue print, showed that decedent would have to walk 270 feet along hospital hallways in the trip he made from his bed to the secluded spot where he ended his life. All of this is as convincing as claimant's evidence is not and we, therefore, find that we must disagree with the referee as to the ultimate fact in the case. Our conclusion is that decedent did know what he was doing and that the sequelae of his accidental injury did not so derange his mind that he was incapable of understanding the consequences of his final act. 

The first, second, third, fifth and seventh findings of fact and first conclusion of law of Referee Foust dated February 

2, 1949, are affirmed. 

Referee Foust's fourth finding of fact of the same date is modified changing the last word thereof, viz., "bedclothes" to the words "a belt" and as so modified is affirmed.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

The sixth finding of fact, second and third conclusions of law, award and bill of expense of Referee Foust of the same date are vacated and set aside and the following substituted therefor: 

Finding of Fact 

6. Decedent's taking of his life was a willful act of his own and was not an irresponsible act due to mental derangement flowing from the consequences of his accidental injury sustained in the course of his employment with defendant. 

2. Since decedent's suicide was not in any way related by pathology to his accidental injury in the course of his employment with defendant, claimant, his widow, is not entitled to compensation.

Order The claim petition filed in this case December 9, 1946, is hereby dismissed. 

The appeal is sustained.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD JOHNSON v. PYLE Workmen's compensation—Dependency—Widow living apart from decedent.

A referee's order disallowing a death claim will be affirmed, where it appeared that after claimant and decedent were married she went to another State to take a job and never returned to Pennsylvania where decedent remained, although she claimed decedent visited her a few times, it further appearing that claimant received wages that enabled her to be self-supporting.

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Graybill. District No. 4, claim petition no. 112,212, accident no. 

5009480. Affirmed, February 1, 1950.

1950] 

Marshall M. Cohen, for claimant. 

John F. Donohue, for defendant. 

Royal Indemnity Company, insurance carrier.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal from an order entered by Referee Graybill June 30, 1949, disallowing a death claim. The claim petition in the case was filed March 22, 1948, and the referee heard the evidence February 16, 1949. 

Leslie J. England was fatally injured on April 9, 1947, when a truck he was driving in the employ of defendant, A. Duie Pyle, was forced off the road at Whitford. At the time he was legally married to claimant, Genevieve England, who married again on December 24, 1947, and is now known as Genevieve Johnson. These facts are not disputed. 

The issue in the case is raised by the provision of the statute which sets forth that "no compensation shall be payable under this section to a widow, unless she was living with her deceased husband at the time of his death, or was then actually dependent upon him and receiving from him a sub stantial portion of her support": Section 307 of the Work men's Compensation Act, as amended by the act of May 

18, 1945, P. L. 671, sec. 1. The referee found that the facts regarding the marital relationship made it necessary to apply the bar of the quoted provision.

From the evidence it appears that claimant and decedent were married at Elkton, Md., April 13, 1945. They then stayed at the home of decedent's mother in Oxford, Pa., for the ensuing week-end after which claimant left for Cleveland, Ohio, where she had found employment. Decedent remained at home in Oxford staying some of the time in quarters he maintained in Coatesville. According to claimant's testimony she never returned to Pennsylvania. 

She has stated, however, that decedent visited her from month to month in Cleveland in 1945, three times at the most in 1946 and not at all in 1947. Her explanation of this

[36 Dep. Rep. 

is that they were making arrangements to go to housekeeping but that decedent wanted to keep his job as a truck driver with defendant. Further in this phase of the matter she insists that she received money from decedent to put down as hand money on a three-room apartment just prior to her husband's death. All that has been said in this connection would, if accepted as offered, indicate a possible separation because of necessity or convenience but the possibility is not substantiated by the whole picture: 

Sheaffer v. Penn Dairies, 161 Pa. Superior Ct. A careful review of everything in the record points to the fact that there was little or no excuse for claimant and decedent to live apart. The most reasonable inference to be drawn is that an estrangement developed. Claimant's protestations to the contrary simply do not ring true. 

As to the provisions of the section, supra, having to do with dependency and the receipt of a substantial portion of support, the earnings of claimant and decedent as shown by the evidence are elucidating. Claimant at first earned about $20 take home pay per week from the R. B. Biscuit 

Company in Cleveland. She progressed to forelady however and received substantial increases in pay. The expenses she has listed in her testimony were well within her income and show that she was self-supporting. The referee's finding as to decedent's average wage was $50 per week. Claimant stated, however, that to her knowledge it was $70. Of this amount she says that decedent sent her $40 per month in cash through the mail, though she had no letters or envelopes to back her statement up. She also claims that decedent brought her some clothes and a few furnishings and turned some money over to his mother to save for him and his wife. She did not receive any of the latter from her mother-in-law and significantly did not call Mrs. England as a witness. There is an unfavorable inference to be drawn from this circumstance: Mosley v. Jones & Laughlin Steel Corp., 155 Pa. Superior Ct. 598. 

Defendant produced as lay witnesses officials familiar with its traffic to negate some of claimant's statements

1950] 

 about visits her husband made to Cleveland while on trucking trips but the point so made is of little importance considering the situation discernible from all the other evidence. From our reading of the record claimant's testimony as to living together and dependency does not warrant the stamp of credibility. It is true, as claimant contends, that in some cases widows living apart from their husbands have been able to prove some support and received awards: 

Berman v. George J. Blair Co., 137 Pa. Superior Ct. 193;

Icenhour v. Freedom Oil Works, 145 Pa. Superior Ct. 168. 

But the referee has not found the proof to the same effect acceptable in the present case and we find ourselvs in agreement with him.

The findings of fact, conclusion of law and order of disallowance of Referee Graybill dated June 30, 1949, are affirmed. 

The appeal is dismissed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD O'TOOLE v. PHILADELPHIA TRANSPORTATION CO. 

Workmen's compensation—Injury in course of employment— 

Motorman on car line-Injury while riding home after day's work.

A referee's award based on his finding that claimant at the time of his injury was engaged in the course of his employment by defendant transportation company will be set aside, where it appears that claimant was employed as a motorman-conductor on defendant's elevated line, that he had free transportation in going to and from his work, that on the date in question he had completed his day's work, had gone to the locker room to change his train hat for his street hat and had then taken a train for transportation to his home when he suffered a cut resulting from the breaking of a door glass as the train started; under the circumstances, claimant was not in the course of employment when injured and hence was not entitled to compensation.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Appeal by defendant from award of referee Alessandroni, District No. 1, claim petition no. 116,346, accident no. 

5392241. Reversed. February 15, 1950. 

Frank J. Eustace, for claimant. 

George H. Detweiler, for defendant.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

Claimant, John O'Toole, suffered accidental injuries on April 23, 1948. He filed a claim petition on April 21, 1949, which was heard by Referee Alessandroni on July 19, 1949. 

The referee entered an award on September 9, 1949, and defendant, Philadelphia Transportation Co., took this appeal on September 29, 1949. The appeal was heard by the board on November 30, 1949.

The facts are not in serious dispute. The record discloses that claimant was employed as a motorman-conductor by defendant on a run from 69th Street to Frankford. His daily run on the elevated line began and ended at the 69th Street terminal. He had free transportation through an employe courtesy pass on its lines and used this pass daily in going from 60th Street to 69th Street and return. On the date in question, O'Toole had completed his day's work. 

He visited the locker room, descended and crossed over to the east-bound platform and boarded a train about 9:05 p.m. for transportation to his 60th Street destination. As the train started, a fellow employe hit the door glass, breaking it and claimant suffered a cut tendon of the left leg. 

The basic question raised in this appeal is whether claimant was in the course of employment when he was injured. 

In the course of the hearing, testimony was given by claimant relating to certain rules and regulations of defendant employer in an effort to establish that claimant, as a free passenger, still continued as an employe of the company. The printed rules require claimant to report any accident or improper conditions and to assist the regular train crew. Counsel for both parties have submitted able

1950] 

 briefs and we have carefully reviewed all of the cited cases. 

The general proposition of law is that an employer is not an insurer and that where claimant abandons his employment or steps entirely aside from duty, there is a severance of the employer-employe relationship and the status of the employe rises no higher than that of a stranger, volunteer or licensee: Shoffler v. Lehigh Valley Coal Co., 290 Pa. 480. 

Here, claimant had fully completed his duties for the day. 

He went to the locker room to change his train hat for his street hat. He was not required to take the particular train which he did, nor any train at all, nor were any duties assigned to him. He could have adopted a different medium of transportation, had he so elected, and clearly there would have been no compensable liability had he suffered an accident en route home. When O'Toole embarked upon the particular train, he was not distinguishable from any civilian passenger. Without any insignia of his employment visible, it is doubtful if he could have asserted any commands or direction in case of any dispute or incident involving his fellow travelers. In any event, at the time of the accident, claimant was not then engaged in furthering his employer's business or interests. The leading case of 

Knorr v. Central R. R. of N. J., 268 Pa. 172, is not analagous and, therefore, not controlling. In that case Knorr was employed as an extra fireman. He lived at Mountain Top, 16 miles from Ashley, where he reported for work. A plane train (an independent operation of the company) was used to take claimant to and from his work. On the particular date when the fatal accident occurred, deceased was called for a run from Ashley to Taylor. When he completed the run at Taylor, he returned to Ashley the same day with a train made up partly of cars shipped from outside the State and he was thus engaged in interstate commerce. After returning to Ashley, he later boarded the plane train for his home and he was killed en route. The Supreme Court affirmed the lower courts' award, saying (p. 175): "In accepting and using free transportation to and from work, Knorr was not to be regarded as a passenger but as

888 

 [36 Dep. Rep. 

an employee, in this case under general employment, which began when he boarded the train at Mountain Top and continued until he entered the particular premises where he was to perform the special work designated by the employer. This service from the place of residence to the place of work, using the facilities furnished by the company, was useful to the company; and the place of residence, while a matter of convenience to him, was nevertheless beneficial to the employer, being within easy reach of his place of work, and appellant would require it to be so. His transportation may be regarded as a privilege incidental to the contract of service, if it is not a part of his compensation."

Transportation, in that case, was a necessary adjunct of the employment and “tied in” with it. In the metropolitan area of Philadelphia, no special transportation was furnished any of defendant's army of employes. In our opinion, claimant would not be entitled to a recovery if the injury occurred under the same circumstances on his day off, or when he might be temporarily laid off from his regular work. There should be no distinction, then, if the injury occurred shortly after claimant's regular day's work had been completely terminated. Nor is the case of Combs v. 

Cole Bros. Circus, 165 Pa. Superior Ct. 346, likewise involving the question whether decedent was injured in the course of his employment, analagous. Describing the nature of deceased's duties, the court said (p. 352): "Although assigned specific tasks on the circus grounds, he was subject to call. He responded to call when asked to assist in 'braking' the employer's wagons after completing his actual work. Such services were not rendered as mere gratuities, as where one assists a stranger. Combs was strictly a traveling worker. His movements were directed, if not entirely controlled, by his employer who provided him with transportation, food and lodging. The course of employment of a traveling worker is necessarily broader than that of an ordinary employee and it should be liberally construed to effectuate the purposes of the Compensation Act."

1950] 

In the case of Strohl v. Eastern Pa. Rwys. Co., 270 Pa. 

132, plaintiff was an employe of the Lehigh Coal & Navigation Co. Defendant requested "plaintiff to examine the meters at the end of each day he was working for the navigation company, to enter the amounts thus ascertained upon blanks furnished by defendant, and to deliver them to any conductor of its trolley cars. As a consideration for this, plaintiff was given an employe's pass, with individual ticket attached, each of which entitled him to a ride, either way between his home and the substation". Plaintiff was injured due to the derailment of the car in which he was returning home. The court said (p. 135): "The tickets he received were his pay for what he did, and the pass was not a free pass, but one issued for a valuable consideration, entitling him to claim for any damages suffered by defendant's negligence, exactly as any other passenger was entitled to do."

The court then held that plaintiff, being a passenger, could not recover an award in a proceeding under The 

Workmen's Compensation Act. 

To permit the referee's award to stand in this case would be tantamount to saying that every traction or transportation company employe was entitled to the benefits of the compensation laws for any accidental disability while riding gratis in a train, although not directly engaged in the performance of regular duties or the positive furtherance of the employer's interests. We do not interpret the act or construe the language of any appellate authority as countenancing such an extension of what constitutes injury in the "course of employment”.

We hereby amend the referee's findings of fact, by adding the following

7. We find as a fact that at the time of claimant's accident on April 23, 1948, he was not engaged in the course of his employment by the defendant.

The referee's other findings of fact are affirmed.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

4 We hereby affirm the first paragraph of the referee's conclusions of law. We vacate and set aside the referee's other conclusions of law. In lieu thereof, we substitute the following

Having found that claimant was not engaged in the course of his employment at the time of the accident in question, he is not entitled to compensation.

We hereby vacate and set aside the referee's award and in lieu thereof, we substitute the following

Order of Dismissal 

Claim Petition No. 116,346, filed by John O'Toole, claimant, is hereby dismissed. 

The appeal of defendant is sustained.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD SARACINI v. PHILADELPHIA GAS WORKS Workmen's compensation-Death-Asphyxiation-Carbon monoxide poisoning-Sufficiency of evidence. 

A referee's award of compensation to claimant based on his finding that decedent died of asphyxiation caused by inhalation of carbon monoxide during the course of his employment digging a ditch will be reversed, where claimant's doctor testified that the carbon monoxide contributed to the death but admitted that he found no autopsy evidence of carbon monoxide poisoning, and that death could have resulted from natural causes, there being other evidence that decedent had been in ill health and no proof that decedent inhaled carbon monoxide during the course of employment with defendant. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Clayton. 

District No. 1, claim petition no. 114,924, accident no. 

5,308,153. Reversed. February 1, 1950.

1950] 

Herman H. Greenberg, for claimant. 

Martin P. Snyder, for defendant.

KNOLL, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by defendant from an order of the referee awarding compensation payments to the widow and minor dependent children of Carmen Saracini, deceased.

Claimant's petition, filed December 20, 1948, alleged that her husband died on November 1, 1948, as the result of an accident. The nature and cause of the accident were described as asphyxiation caused by the inhalation of carbon monoxide and the cause of death was attributed to asphyxia with inhalation of carbon monoxide as a contributing factor. 

Defendant filed an answer averring that death was due to heart failure and not to any accident occurring in the course of decedent's employment.

The referee, after hearing, found that decedent died of asphyxia due to carbon monoxide inhalation during the course of his employment with defendant, and that his death was caused by the employment. 

The undisputed facts of record established that decedent was employed by defendant as a laborer. On November 1, 1948, he was digging a trench, together with a fellow employe, preparatory to the laying of gas pipes. He began work at 8:00 a.m. and worked until lunch time at 12 noon. 

He returned to work at 12:30 p.m. and continued at work until he collapsed at 2:15 p.m. He was taken by automobile to a hospital where he was declared dead on arrival. 

Dr. Benjamin A. Gouley, coroner's physician, called by claimant, testified that he performed an autopsy on decedent's body on November 2, 1948, and that his findings, as to the cause of death were: First, asphyxia caused by bronchitis, pneumonitis and myocarditis; and second, carbon monoxide inhalation, contributing factor. He said that blood tests, made by coroner's chemists, showed the pres-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

ence of carbon monoxide to the degree of 15 percent; that this carbon monoxide was a factor that brought about the fatality, although in a person of normal health there might have been a question as to causation. Dr. Gouley testified further that decedent's blood was dark and not cherry red as is characteristic of carbon monoxide inhalation and that decedent must have inhaled the gas after he had left for work in the morning. He described his autopsy findings as indicative of an acute catarrhal bronchitis, some changes of myocardial fibers, congestion of the lungs and of the gastro-intestinal tract and said that death would have been caused independently by either of these conditions or by the carbon monoxide.

Willis Floyd, claimant's witness, testified that he worked with decedent on November 1, 1948; that he was loosening the earth in the ditch and decedent was shoveling; that decedent had charge of an air compressor on the job and his duties required him to start and stop the compressor twice daily; that the compressor was located about 200 feet away from the ditch and on the far side of a house near the ditch, and that the compressor started on gasoline by push button and was operated by oil. The witness said decedent complained of feeling ill all morning and, upon cross-examination, he testified that decedent had complained of ill health after they left a bus on which they had ridden to work that morning as well as on other occasions during the previous week. Further, upon crossexamination and later upon redirect, claimant's witness said that they had worked in the open air; that the earth in the ditch was dry and he had smelled no odor of any nature; that there was no pipe in the ditch, and that he worked in the ditch all day without feeling any ill effects. 

Dr. Marshall M. Lieber, a pathologist, called on behalf of defendant, testified, in answer to a hypothetical question which fairly included the facts previously testified, that he was of the opinion that carbon monoxide was not a factor contributing to decedent's death because the post-mortem examination showed no evidence of changes due to carbon

1950] 

 monoxide and decedent's pneumonitis and myocarditis could account for the death.

Dr. Henrich Brieger, a toxicologist and expert of carbon monoxide poisoning, testified that, in his opinion and experience, a saturation of 15 percent carbon monoxide in the blood does not cause any recognizable symptoms of asphyxia.

Dr. Abraham Cantarow, a specialist of internal medicine, biochemistry and toxicology, testified that 15 percent carbon monoxide in the blood does not cause any recognizable asphyxia and that a 20 percent saturation would cause only mild symptoms such as headaches or dizziness. In his opinion, death in this case was not contributed to by carbon monoxide asphyxia since there was no established antecedent asphyxia. He testified further that there was no relationship between the presence of the carbon monoxide in decedent's blood and the operation of the compressor about 200 feet away from decedent.

Defendant offered testimony by other employes that claimant had complained of ill health and pain of his chest and shoulder on the morning before he died.

It is our opinion that claimant had not presented sufficient credible evidence warranting the referee's finding of fact that her husband, "Carmen Saracini died as a result of asphyxia due to carbon monoxide inhalation, which carbon monoxide was inhaled during the course of his employment with defendant, and thus his death was caused by his employment."

Claimant's physician testified that the carbon monoxide contributed to the death. However, he also testified that death could have resulted from natural causes and he admitted that he found no autopsy evidence of carbon monoxide poisoning and that only the unusual circumstances of the death, namely, the unusual degree of asphyxia of the blood, that decedent had been working in a ditch and for a gas company, prompted him to have the blood tested. Considering the fact that decedent had been in ill health on

[36 Dep. Rep. 

November 1, 1948, and the testimony of the medical experts that the carbon monoxide did not contribute to the death, we believe that decedent died from natural causes unrelated to his employment.

Further, there is no proof that decedent inhaled carbon monoxide during the course of his employment with defendant. We are not convinced that decedent could have inhaled the gas only while at work in view of the fact that the fellow employe who worked with him and another employe who worked nearby were not in any way affected nor felt any illness then or later. The air compressor, as testified by claimant's witness, was not run on gasoline, it was located over 200 feet away from decedent and it can, therefore, be discounted as the cause of the carbon monoxide inhalation. We submit that any finding that the inhalation occurred during working hours would be conjectural since even claimant's physician could state only that decedent inhaled the gas after leaving his home that morning and the record shows that decedent rode to work in a bus where he may have been exposed to the carbon monoxide. 

Accordingly, the referee's fourth finding of fact, the second conclusion of law and order of award are set aside and we substitute, in lieu thereof, the following 

4. That decedent died on November 1, 1948, from natural causes not related to, nor caused by, any accident during the course of his employment with defendant and death was not due to, nor contributed to, carbon monoxide which was present in decedent's blood to the degree of 15 percent.

2. Since decedent's death was from natural causes and was not due to any accident during the course of his employment with defendant, claimant is not entitled to compensation payments under the provisions of The Pennsyl vania Workmen's Compensation Act.

1950] 

Order Claimant's claim petition no. 114,924 is hereby dismissed. 

Defendant's appeal is sustained.

COUNTY COURT OF ALLEGHENY COUNTY SIMON v. FINE Workmen's compensation-Accidental injury-Disease from eating food contaminated by rats.

An order of the Workmen's Compensation Board disallowing a claim for compensation based on the board's conclusion that there was no accident within the meaning of the compensation law will be reversed, and an award entered where claimant a meat inspector in the employ of defendant became violently ill suffering from a malady known as Weil's disease, as the result of eating a lunch that had been contaminated by rats while he was at work; the board having found the facts, the question whether they constituted an accidental injury was one of law. In the County Court of Allegheny County, no. A 968 of 

1949. Appeal by claimant from the Workmen's Compensation Board. Reversed January 16, 1950. 

Esther Kochin, for plaintiff. 

O. W. T. Peterson, for defendant.

LENCHER, P. J.: 

It is clear under the provisions of the Workmen's Compensation Law of Pennsylvania that injury from germ disease or infection, whether developing within the body or entering from without, is not compensable unless such

[36 Dep. Rep. 

injury and subsequent disability are shown to have come out of a sudden development, from trauma, from violence in some form, unexpected, to the physical structure of claimant's body; such ailment or illness is not compensable where the employe suffers it as a gradual development from long continued exposure to dangers that are naturally incident to his work, and in this connection, the term, "injury", means violence to the physical structure of the body, either external or internal: McCauley v. Imperial W. Co. et al., 261 Pa. 312, 328, 104 Atl. 617; Lacey v. Washburn & 

Williams, 309 Pa. 574, 164 Atl. 724. At page 577 of the case last cited, Mr. Justice Drew, speaking for our Supreme

Court, reviews the legal necessity for proof of "an event that takes place without one's foresight or expectation; an undesigned, sudden and unexpected event; a mishap, a fortuitous happening", and cites various cases of illness caused or manifested by germs, pneumonia, inter alia, which were declared compensable because there was violence in some form, some trauma, some blow or fall, some unusual factor inadvertently or unexpectedly started the chain of circumstances which, taken together, could be legally declared to be an accident.

Such is the basic problem posed by the appeal of this claimant from the refusal of the Workmen's Compensation 

Board to award him compensation following his illness in the summer of 1948. The facts are undisputed. The problem is that of the legal effect of the facts, circumstances and inferences arising therefrom. A paper covering in which claimant carried his lunch had been torn by-and the food therein contaminated by-rodents or rats on the premises of the employer. Claimant ate that lunch, having seen the disturbed condition of the lunch bag, later became ill as hereinafter discussed. The referee declared the facts and circumstances "failed to prove the occurrence of accidental injuries." The Workmen's Compensation Board affirmed the referee's findings of fact, dismissed claimant's appeal, agreeing with the referee that this is a case where there was no violence to the physical structure, that there was

1950] 

 only the contracting of some infection or disease at work, and that there was therefore no accident within the meaning of the law. From the board's order refusing an award, claimant has appealed to the County Court of Allegheny County in accordance with the jurisdiction in such cases give us by the Act of May 27, 1943, P. L. 691, sec. 177,

PS §872. Had the record shown only the bringing of lunch by claimant from his home, and no intrusion or any other untoward fact, we would sustain the board's order; however, at least two major factual developments are uncontradicted and so found by the board constrain us to reverse the board's conclusion of law.

Where the triers of facts, here the Workmen's Compensation Board, refuse to find the facts in favor of the party having the burden of proof, here claimant, the question on review before us is not whether competent evidence would sustain such a finding if made, but whether there was a capricious disregard of competent evidence in the refusal so to find: Kline v. Kiehl, 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 392, 43

A. (2d) 616. Our problem in this case is not a refusal of the board to accept uncontradicted oral evidence; that they need not do in workmen's compensation cases any more than they can be compelled to accept oral evidence in other areas of the law: Kostello v. Kostello, 159 Pa. Superior Ct. 

194, 196, 48 A. (2d) 25. That claimant is a meat inspector in defendant's employ, brought his lunch with him to defendant's premises sometime in the middle of July 1948, that there are rodents and rats on the premises of all or most meat provision plants, and certainly so on the premises of defendant, that claimant saw rats frequently, that his lunch paper or bag had been tampered with or touched or molested, that he ate the lunch nevertheless, not having actually seen a rat in it or upon it, that in the period of incubation necessary for the particular sickness involved, he took sick, developed Weil's disease, that the malady invariably comes from oral ingestion of material contaminated by rats, an expert physician testifying unequivocally as to the causal relationship (Bunnell v. State Workmen's

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Insurance Fund et al., 124 Pa. Superior Ct. 171, 188 Atl. 

411); all this is clear in this record, the facts are so found, the Workmen's Compensation Board declaring however that "the trouble with the claimant's case is that he has not in law proved a compensable accident". We are constrained to disagree with the board.

Let us first make clear that this is the only legal point before us; no other legal difficulties are suggested. Thus, if the evidence believed by the board spells out an accident within the meaning of the Workmen's Compensation Law, it is of no concern that at the moment that the deleterious substance entered claimant's body he was not actually at his work inspecting food or meat, that he was eating lunch, since the consumption of food or drink or refreshment enjoyed on the premises of the employer does not break the course of employment in a compensation case in our 

Commonwealth: Baumann v. Howard J. Ehmke Co., 126 

Pa. Superior Ct. 108, 116, 190 Atl. 343. Nor is it fatal to the claim that in picking up his lunch bag, claimant clearly saw that it had been touched or tampered with; claimant may have been guilty of negligence or even gross negligence in consuming his lunch under such circumstances, but negligence in no form bars the right of a workingman to compensation where there is competent proof of disability following accidental injury: Delbene v. Pine, 144 Pa. Superior Ct. 353, 356, 19 A. (2d) 533. Coming closer to the specific issue here involved, we must make some distinction even as to so-called germ or infection cases. Germs may indeed be inhaled through the nose or mouth or absorbed into the system through the normal channels of entry. In such cases their inroads will seldom, if ever, be assignable to a determinate or single act, identified in space or time. 

For this as well as for the reason that absorption is incidental to a bodily process both natural and normal, their action presents itself to the mind as a disease and not an accident. Our mental attitude is different when the channel of infection is abnormal or traumatic, a lesion or a cut.

1950] 

If these become dangerous or deadly by contact with infected matter, we think and speak of what has happened as something catastrophic or extraordinary, a mishap or an accident though very likely a disease also. A common sense appraisement of everyday forms of speech and modes of thought must tell us when to stop: Gerst et ux. v. Smith- 

Faris Co. et al., 107 Pa. Superior Ct. 30, 162 Atl. 490, monoxide gas; Sinkiewicz v. Susquehanna Collieries Co., 115 

Pa. Superior Ct. 377, 175 Atl. 757, inhalation of poisonous gases; Clancy v. Booth & Flinn Co. et al., 109 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 452, 167 Atl. 393, sunstroke; Johnson v. Valvoline Oil 

Co., 131 Pa. Superior Ct. 266, 211 Atl. 224, inhalation of ammonia fumes; Bloom v. Brotherhood Accident Co., 85

Pa. Superior Ct. 398, an action for damages involving the drinking of poison by mistake. Other cases in Pennsylvania sustain claimant's contention of an unexpected and unusual happening at a particular, definite time, the attack first or intrusion of the rats upon the paper bag containing the food, and possibly the food itself, the absorption of the food into the body, the development then of the specific ailment invariably caused by rodent contamination, and the resulting sickness and disability: Loudon v. H. W. Shaull & 

Sons, 140 Pa. Superior Ct. 106, 114, 13 A. (2d) 129. Courts should declare the legal conclusion which rationally follows such a chain of events, the evidence pointing thereto coming from a chain of inferences, each of which is reasonably and sensibly established to the exclusion of any other reasonable theory rather than merely by a probability. It is so in civil cases in Pennsylvania. Henderson v. National Drug Co., 343 Pa. 601, 23 A. (2d) 743. And under our Workmen's

Compensation Law, even an accident from an unexplained cause may be compensable if the circumstances establish a resulting disability: Gaffey v. John J. Felin Co., 162 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 222, 57 A. (2d) 432.

True it is that conjectures and guesses will no more be permitted in these cases than in any other branch of the compensation law. Thus compensation was refused a hotel chambermaid for the loss of an eye through a gonococcic

[36 Dep. Rep. 

infection alleged to have been accidentally transferred to the membrane of the eye through the wiping of the eye by claimant while she was handling soiled and infected bed linens and towels, but because it had not been definitely shown how or when the germs entered the body, and the "might haves", the possibilities and conjectures on that basic point were obviously not obviated and overcome by claimant's testimony: Troxell v. Shirk et al., 130 Pa. Superior Ct. 40, 196 Atl. 899. Where there was no competent evidence to sustain the findings that a streptococcic germ entered through a crack in the finger of claimant's decedent or entered the body by reason of any other accidental injury, the death from disease germ was not compensable in 

Eaton v. Elk Tanning Co., 129 Pa. Superior Ct. 535, 195

Atl. 648. It is clear in all such cases that an award will be sustained in the case of infection if competent evidence connects the infection with an accidental entry of some deleterious matter into the body of the claimant: Shashura v. Vesta Coal Co., 146 Pa. Superior Ct. 161, 22 A.(2d) 441.

In addition to the other cases cited supra a strong parallel may be found in Rittenberg v. Abbott Laboratories, 158 

Pa. Superior Ct. 400, 45 A. (2d) 400, where it appeared that an employe, apparently in good spirits consumed some water in the belief that it contained powdered bicarbonate of soda, a remedy frequently taken by him on the premises of his employer. About half an hour later he died in agony in a hospital, an autopsy disclosed that cyanide poisoning, similar in appearance to powdered bicarbonate of soda was the cause of death. There was abundant evidence and no serious controversy as to these facts, the employer failed to meet the burden of showing intentionally self-inflicted injury, an award for claimant widow was sustained; it was not necessary for claimant to show the exact cause of the mistake or accident which resulted in the injury and death since competent testimony sustained the factual conclusion that the deleterious substance was taken accidentally, the working man intending and believing that he was consuming water and harmless medicine. Our case at bar has an

1950] 

 even stronger base in various particulars; the testimony here is uncontradicted on the basic point involved; the compensation authorities believed the testimony of claimant and his expert physician; there is not only the ingestion of the material later found to be deleterious, water containing poison in the Rittenberg case, the lunch later found to be contaminated in the case at bar, but a factor far better explained in the case at bar arises in the strong inference that rodents, not unusual upon the defendant's premises, intruded upon and occasioned a physical contact with the food or its container, or both, and thus initiated the contamination, an inference certainly as strong as the necessary inference which completed the chain of developing factors in the Rittenberg case, supra.

We make it clear that we may not, and do not weigh the evidence in this case. Since they are sufficiently supported by competent and substantial evidence, clearly appearing in the record, the findings of fact by the board here are conclusive, we are confined only to the question of law, and our answer to that question must be that the facts, circumstances and all the inferences present spell out an emergency situation, outside of the normal and ordinary, culminating in injury to the internal structure of claimant's body, his sickness and disability. In such case we are without authority to remit the record for further findings as to the essential facts found by the board, and we need not remit the record for any further hearing or findings: Rozauski v. Glen Alden Coal Co., 165 Pa. Superior Ct. 460, 69 A. (2d) 192.

This being the legal conclusion demanded by the factual findings discussed supra, the other facts necessary for an award in accordance with this legal conclusion appear of record, to wit, the illness of claimant from July 25, 1948, to October 27, 1948, his inability to do any work at all in that period of time, his average weekly wage of $81, the doctor and hospital bills in the record, and of course the amount of compensation due him is already set by the provisions of

[36 Dep. Rep. 

our Workmen's Compensation Act. Judgment will be entered accordingly.

Order of Court 

And now, to wit, January 16, 1950, the exceptions of claimant are sustained; the findings of fact of the Workmen's 

Compensation Board are affirmed, but its conclusion of law refusing an award is hereby reversed; and judgment is entered in favor of claimant, Aaron Simon, and against defendant, Jacob Fine, for total disability at $20 per week from July 26, 1948, to October 27, 1948, in the sum of $245.78; defendant is ordered to pay the medical and hospital bills and legal costs sec. reg. et sec. leg. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD EBERT v. THE ALDEN COAL COMPANY Workmen's compensation—Blindness causing total disability—Blind pension—Acceptance as bar to further compensation— 

Section 306(a) of the Act of June 4, 1937.

A referee's order terminating compensation to claimant will be affirmed, where it appears that claimant had lost the sight of both eyes as the result of injuries in the course of his employment, that he had received compensation for a period of 500 weeks, that he was likewise continuing to receive a blind pension from the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, at the rate of $40 per month; in such case section 

306(a) of The Workmen's Compensation Act of June 4, 1937, providing that a claimant totally disabled shall receive further compensation at the expiration of the 500-week period with the proviso that such payments shall cease if the person receiving compensation accepts other public funds contributed to by the employer which equal or exceed the compensation payments, barred claimant from further compensation where it appeared that defendant paid corporate capital stock and net income taxes into the Commonwealth's general fund.

1950] 

Appeal by claimant from order of Referee Olexy, District 

No. 2, granting petition for termination under compensation agreement no. 8,023,500. Affirmed. March 8, 1950. 

T. L. Kennedy, Jr., for claimant. 

S. Keene Mitchell, Jr., for defendant.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

The record in this case discloses that claimant, Stanley Ebert, sustained serious accidental injuries in the course of his employment by defendant, Alden Coal Company, on 

February 23, 1938. Claimant lost the sight of both eyes as a result of an explosion. The present proceedings involve a petition filed by defendant on June 24, 1949, for termination of compensation liability. Hearing was held before Referee 

Olexy on September 2, 1949, and the referee entered an order granting the petition on December 15, 1949. Claimant appealed on December 28, 1949, which was heard by the board on January 25, 1950.

Counsel for the respective parties stipulated upon the record that beginning in October 1940 claimant has received a blind pension from the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania,

Department of Public Assistance, and that he is presently entitled to a pension at the rate of $40 per month. The sole question involved in this case is whether claimant is entitled to further compensation payments in view of his pension as a blind person. No brief was filed in behalf of claimant. 

Section 306 (a) of The Workmen's Compensation Act of 

June 4, 1937, P. L. 1552, provides, inter alia, ". . . and beginning at the expiration of the five hundred weeks, the sum of thirty dollars per month shall be payable during such permanent total disability prior to death: Provided, however, that such monthly payments shall cease if the person receiving compensation accepts other public funds contributed to by the employer which equal or exceed the amount of compensation payments."

[36 Dep. Rep. 

The 500-week period has expired. Testimony offered in behalf of defendant discloses that defendant has paid corporate capital stock and net income taxes into the Commonwealth's general fund and that blind pensions are paid from the same general fund. Does the payment of corporate taxes into the general fund of the State constitute a "contribution" within the contemplation and intendment of the act? We are of the opinion that the question must be resolved in the affirmative. The payment of taxes by defendant employer into the general fund constitutes a contribution to such funds and payment of the pension to claimant is paid from the same general fund. The general fund is the public fund. 

The language of the particular section is clear and free from ambiguity: Duff v. Pennsylvania Railroad, 36 Dept. Rep. 1 (1950). The Statutory Construction Act of May 28, 1937, 

P. L. 1019, sec. 51, provides: "The object of all interpretation and construction of laws is to ascertain and effectuate the intention of the Legislature. Every law should be construed, if possible, to give effect to all its provisions. When the words of the law are clear and free from all ambiguity, the letter of it is not to be disregarded under the pretext of pursuing its spirits." 

We are in accord with the referee that the language of the act, in the present case, is a bar to further compensation to claimant under The Workmen's Compensation Act. 

We hereby affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of termination.

The appeal of claimant is dismissed.

1950]
