 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD SALINKA v. LOCUST COAL COMPANY Workmen's compensation—Occupational disease-Claim by administratrix for funeral benefits—Limitations—Payment of compensation due decedent within year after death 

-Section 315 of the Occupational Disease Act. 

A referee's order awarding funeral benefits provided for by the Occupational Disease Act to the administratrix of the estate of decedent will be reversed, where the claim petition for the benefits was not filed until subsequent to one year after the date of decedent's death, although it was filed within a year after the payment by defendant employer to the administratrix of compensation owed to decedent; the provision of section 315 of the act which extends the time for filing of a claim to one year from the date of last payment of compensation does not apply to a separate and distinct claim not dependent upon nor derivative from the claim of the employe. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Wisansky, District No. 5, claim petition no. 114,029, accident no. 

4,936,775. Reversed. March 8, 1950. 

Eureka Casualty Company, insurance carrier. 

T. L. Kennedy, Jr., for claimant. 

Henry Houck, S. P. Snyder, R. L. McLaughlin, for defendant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for Commonwealth of Pennsylvania.

KNOLL, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by defendant from the order of award of the late Referee O. A. Wisansky, of Compensation District 

No. 5.

On September 22, 1948, claimant, as the administratrix of the Estate of Peter Salinka, filed a claim petition pursuant to the provisions of the Pennsylvania Occupational Dis-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

ease Act to recover the funeral benefits provided for by the act. To this petition responsive answers were filed by the 

State Workmen's Insurance Board, custodian of the occupational disease fund, and by defendant employer, averring that the claim was barred by section 315 of the act. 

A study of the record discloses that Peter Salinka, during his lifetime, filed a claim petition alleging that he became totally disabled as a result of silicosis. The matter was duly referred to Referee Enterline, who, by decision dated 

April 29, 1947, awarded compensation to decedent. Pursuant to the award, decedent was paid compensation to May 

27, 1947. On June 2, 1947, decedent died without dependents surviving him and on June 9, 1947, his sister-in-law, Frances Salinka, was appointed administratrix of his estate. Mrs. 

Salinka notified defendant company of her appointment as administratrix on March 4, 1948. She was paid the compensation owed to decedent from May 28, 1947, to June 1, 1947. On September 23, 1948, the administratrix filed a claim petition for burial expenses, forming the basis of the present action. The testimony also establishes the fact that the death of Peter Salinka, on June 2, 1947, was caused solely by anthraco-silicosis.

The referee found as a fact that the claim petition was filed within the requisite period and awarded to claimant, as administratrix of the estate of Peter Salinka, deceased, the sum of $200. From this ruling, defendant and the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania have appealed. 

We have made a careful study of the record in this case and are of the opinion that the ruling of the referee should be reversed.

It is to be noted that decedent died on June 2, 1947, and that the claim for burial benefits was not filed by the administratrix until September 23, 1948, or more than one year subsequent to the date of death. Section 315 of the act provides that, in case of death, all claims for compensation will be forever barred unless, within one year after the death, the parties shall have agreed upon the compensation payable, or one of the parties shall have filed a petition as provided by the act. The act also provides that where pay-

1950] 

 ments of compensation have been made in any case, said limitations shall not take effect until the expiration of one year from the time of the making of the most recent payment prior to the date of filing of such petition. 

The issue in this case, therefore, is whether the one-year period runs from June 2, 1947, the date of death, or from

March 4, 1948, the date that payment was made by defendant employer to claimant, as the administratrix of the estate of 

Peter Salinka.

In Lambing v. Consolidation Coal Co., 161 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 346, 354, the court cites, with approval, Rossi v. Hillman 

Coal & Coke Co., 145 Pa. Superior Ct. 108, as follows: 66 . . the right of the widow to secure for herself and dependent children compensation for the death of the employee is, under the act, entirely independent of the right of the employee. It is not derivative and, therefore, not conditioned on the employee having a right at the time of death. This was settled by the Supreme Court in Smith vs. 

Primrose Tapestry Co., 285 Pa. 145, 131 A. 703 (1926), where an injured employee had prior to his death lost his right to compensation by failing to petition for or receive compensation within the statutory period and it was held that the widow was nevertheless entitled to compensation on a showing that the death was caused by the original injury."

It is, therefore, our opinion that the rights of the administratrix are separate and distinct from that of the employe and are not derivative or conditioned upon any rights of the employe, but are a separate and distinct claim which came into existence for the first time upon the death of decedent on June 2, 1947. We are also of the opinion that the provision of section 315 of the act, which extends the time for filing of a claim to one year from the date of the last payment of compensation, applies only where the rights of the employe are involved and is not applicable where there is a separate and distinct claim which is not dependent upon nor derivative of the claim of the employe. 

It was, therefore, necessary for the administratrix to have filed her claim within a period of one year from the date

[36 Dep. Rep. 

of death and, having failed to do so, the claim is barred by section 315 of the act.

We, therefore, set aside the referee's second and third conclusions of law and order of award and, in lieu thereof, substitute the following

2. That the rights of claimant, Frances Salinka, as administratrix of the estate of Peter Salinka, are independent of any rights of decedent employe; are not derivative in character, and arose as a separate and independent claim at the time of the death of decedent on June 2, 1947. 

3. That since the claim petition was not filed within one year from the date of death, the claim is barred by reason of section 315 of the act and the claim petition must be dismissed.

Order 

Claim petition no. 114,029, filed on behalf of Frances

Salinka, as administratrix of the estate of Peter Salinka, be and the same is herewith dismissed.

The findings of fact and the first conclusion of law of the referee are herewith affirmed.

The appeals of defendant employer, Locust Coal Company, and the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, are sustained.

COURT OF COMMON PLEAS OF DELAWARE COUNTY MILLER v. POWERS, etc. 

Turkey raising

Workmen's compensation — Agricultural employe 

Injury in performance of work incidental thereto. 

An order of the Workmen's Compensation Board dismissing a claim for compensation on the ground that claimant at the time of his accidental injury was an agricultural worker engaged in agriculture will be affirmed, where it appears that he was employed by defendant, who operated a farm under the trade name of "Drexel Turkey Farm",

1950] 

 though in addition to raising and selling turkeys the farm was used for the production of crops and other live stock, that claimant's duties embraced general farm work, and that at the time of his injury he was riding in a jeep on the public highway hauling screen doors in the performance of his duties; the fact that defendant's main business was turkey raising and his other farming activities merely incidental thereto, did not affect claimant's status as an agricultural worker; nor did the fact that the particular employment in which he was engaged at the time of the accident was not strictly speaking farming affect his status since the hauling was incidental to the carrying on of defendant's activities as a farmer.

In the Court of Common Pleas of Delaware County, June term, 1949, no. 1600. Appeal by claimant from Workmen's 

Compensation Board. Claim petition no. 113,877. Affirmed. 

February 9, 1950. 

Samuel Lichtenfeld, Norman Snyder and Herman Moskowitz, for appellant. 

Lindenmuth and Class, for appellee.

BRETHERICK, J., February 9, 1950: 

The sole question raised by the appeal in this workmen's compensation case is whether claimant, at the time of the injury, was engaged in agriculture and thereby excluded from the protection of our Workmen's Compensation Act. 

The Act of June 21, 1939, P. L. 565, 77 PS §la, provides that the Compensation Act shall not "apply to or in any way affect any person who at the time of injury, is engaged in domestic service or agriculture." The title of this act reads as follows: "A Supplement to . . . (the Workmen's 

Compensation Act of 1915) . . . as reënacted and amended, to exempt domestic servants and agricultural workers from the provisions thereof, except in certain cases." The title of this act must be taken into consideration in determining its scope: Matis et al. v. Schaeffer, 270 Pa. 141; Hein v. 

Ludwig, 118 Pa. Superior Ct. 152.

There is no dispute with respect to the material and controlling facts of the case. At the time of claimant's injury, defendant operated a farm under the trade name of "Drexel

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Turkey Farm", located at Edgemont, this county. The farm consisted of what were originally three separate parcels of real estate. Defendant testified: "One was the Hancock; one was the Shay property; and the other was the Menden 

Hall. They are all one parcel now and used for one general purpose." 

On this farm, defendant raised turkeys, chickens, pigs, sheep and cattle. According to defendant's testimony, he did not have milk cows; but he did "fatten steers". He said that the farm included 44 acres of pasture, and that the steers are "brought in" in the fall and fed with grain. The over-all acreage of the farm does not appear in the testimony.

Part of defendant's farm was used for the production of oats, hay, wheat, corn, soy beans and barley. Practically all the crops raised were consumed on the farm. In addition, defendant bought "mash" for the turkeys, and, when the crops were insufficient, other types of feed for the livestock. Defendant testified that his feed bills average about $5,000 or $6,000 a year.

Defendant sells his turkeys only at retail, and to purchasers who come to the farm. From defendant's testimony, it would appear that he sells only turkeys. What he does with the chickens, pigs and sheep, does not appear in the testimony.

Claimant had been employed by defendant for approximately one year and three months previous to the date of his injury. Claimant's duties embraced general farm work. 

He fed and watered the turkeys, chickens and livestock; cleaned the barn and poultry houses; brought in the corn from the field to store in the crib, cut the alfalfa four times a year and ground it up for feed. 

On November 26, 1947, at about eleven a.m., claimant fell from a jeep driven by a fellow employe, and sustained compound fractures of the left leg involving the ankle joint, and other injuries. The jeep was proceeding from the turkey house to the barn, and appears to have been on the public highway at the time of claimant's fall, although the evidence is not at all clear on the latter point. The evidence

1950] 

 also fails to disclose the precise nature of the work claimant was performing at the time of the accident, but it is averred in the petition and admitted in the answer that "the accident occurred while hauling screen doors." 

The referee disallowed compensation, holding in his fifth fiinding of fact as follows: "That claimant did suffer an accidental injury but is an agricultural worker, and not covered by the Pennsylvania Workmen's Compensation Act.” 

The board affirmed the referee. We are of opinion that the case was rightly decided by the compensation authorities, and that their action must be affirmed. 

In Bucher v. American Fruit Growers Co., 107 Pa. Superior Ct. 399, 403, the court said: "We think the legislature used the words 'agricultural workers' and the phrase 'engaged in agriculture' in their comprehensive, usual and commonly accepted sense; 

Agriculture as defined by Webster is the 'art or science of cultivating the ground, especially in fields or in large quantities, including the preparation of the soil, planting of seeds, the raising and harvesting of crops and the rearing, feeding and management of livestock.' In Funk & Wagnall's dictionary agriculture is defined as 'a science that treats of the cultivation of the soil,' and under this definition it is stated 'agriculture as a generic term includes at once the science or art and process of supplying human wants by raising products of the soil and by associated industries."" 

We think the disposition of this case is controlled in principle by Hardy v. Gapen, 141 Pa. Superior Ct. 101. In that case, the employers' main activity was the conduct of a dairy farm. The entire farm contained about 600 acres; on it they raised the usual crops and livestock. The dairy farm proper contained 68 acres, and the milk from about 35 cows. was sold as raw milk under a license from the Milk Control Board. Practically all the crops raised were consumed on the farm. Claimant sustained an injury when he was accidentally jolted to the ground from an empty hay wagon. 

The compensation authorities concluded that because the farm work in which claimant was engaged when injured was merely incidental to the operation of a dairy, he was

[36 Dep. Rep. 

not engaged in agriculture. Accordingly, an award was made, and was affirmed by the lower court. In reversing, and holding that claimant when injured was "engaged in agriculture", within the intendment of the Act of 

June 3, 1915, P. L. 777, the court said, in part (p. 104): "Surely our legislature never intended to declare that every farmer who decides to transform the grain, grass and hay raised upon his farm into wool, eggs or milk by feeding these crops to his sheep, chickens or cattle, and selling the resulting commodities at wholesale or retail, has abandoned agriculture and entered upon an industrial business." 

While it may be true, as claimant insists, that defendant's business was "turkey raising", and his other activities were merely incidental thereto, still we can perceive no distinction in principle between the transformation of agricultural products into milk, and their transformation into turkeys. 

The one operation seems to us to be as closely allied with agriculture as the other.

Claimant also argues that he was not engaged in agriculture at the time of injury, because he was then "riding in a motor vehicle, off defendant's premises, upon the public highway, engaged in transportation". The important words from claimant's viewpoint are, "at the time of injury". He contends that these words, which appear in the 1939 Act, were not contained in the 1937 Act, and that, consequently, decisions under the prior act are not here controlling. A review of the legislation exempting persons engaged in domestic service and agriculture from the benefits of The Compensation Law, will disclose the fallacy of claimant's argument.

In Skinner's Pennsylvania Workmen's Compensation 

Law, 4th ed., vol. 1, p. 115, it is said: "Persons engaged in domestic service or agriculture were exempted from the original Compensation Act of June 2, 1915, by an Act approved the following day entitled 'A supplement to an act entitled "The Workmen's Compensation Act of 1915" to exempt domestic servants and agricultural workers from the provisions thereof.' It read, 'nothing contained in any article or any section of the Act

1950] 

333 

entitled the Workmen's Compensation Act of 1915, shall apply to or in any way affect any person who, at the time of injury, is engaged in domestic service or agriculture.' 

This exemption was created in this way probably because of doubt as to the constitutionality of the exemption. The 

Act however was upheld. The exception was retained in the Act of 1937 in Section 104 defining employes by excepting those who perform 'agricultural services or domestic services performed in a private home'. In 1939 the Legislature, instead of exempting agricultural workers or “domestic service performed in a private home' in the body of the Act, followed the method adopted in 1915 and excepted them by a separate act approved the same day, June 21, 1939, P. L. 565, 77 PS. Sec. la." (This act was cited in the opening paragraph of this opinion. 

It will be noted that the language of the 1915 Act is precisely the same as that of the 1939 Act, in all material respects. Both acts contain the phrase "at the time of injury”. 

The phrase does not appear in the 1937 Act. Claimant argues that the test of whether or not he was engaged in agriculture, is the nature of the work he was performing at the precise instant of the accident. He refers us to the long line of cases, involving injuries to railroad workers and similar employes, which establishes the rule that the status of the employe (as engaged in interstate or intrastate commerce) is to be tested by the work he is actually performing at the time of the accident.

A brief reference to the cases will show that claimant's argument is without merit, under the facts of this case. "Agriculture" covers all things ordinarily done by the farmer and his servants incidental to the carrying on of his branch of industry: Warner v. Longstreth, 108 Pa. Superior Ct. 124, 126. In Weller & Weller v. Grange Co., 105 

Pa. Superior Ct. 547, 551, the court said: "There can be no question, however, that a certain amount of hauling is always incident to agriculture. It is inconceivable that one may conduct a farm without doing some hauling." 

In Bucher v. American Fruit Growers Co., 107 Pa. Superior Ct. 399, a truck driver was injured while returning

[36 Dep. Rep. 

from a railroad station after delivering a load of barrelled apples from a fruitgrowers farm. It was held that the employe was engaged in agriculture.

Of course, if a farmer chooses to engage also in outside industrial operation, his employes in such outside transactions are within the protection of The Workmen's Compensation Act: Strunk v. Keller, 75 Pa. Superior Ct. 462, 466; 

Warner v. Longstreth, 108 Pa. Superior Ct. 124, 127. No such situation is presented by the present record, however. 

We conclude that, although the particular employment in which claimant was engaged at the time of the accident was not strictly speaking farming, he must still be regarded as engaged in agriculture, because the hauling was merely incidental to the carrying on of defendant's activities as a farmer and connected therewith.

Order 

And now, February 9, 1950, upon consideration of the foregoing case, it is ordered, adjudged and decreed that claimant's exceptions are dismissed, the disallowance of compensation is affirmed and judgment is entered for defendant.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD AMERINE v. SYLVANIA CORPORATION Workmen's compensation—Accidental injury—Performance of reg ular duties-Numbness in hand.

Testimony by claimant that she had been employed by defendant doing spot welding on mountings, that her duties consisted of picking up parts with a pair of tweezers, placing a part on a mounting and welding the part to the mounting and that in the performance of such duties on a certain date her hand went numb, causing disability, was insufficient to support a finding of an accidental injury and therefore a referee's order disallowing her claim will be affirmed. 

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Patterson, District No. 6, claim petition no. 116,489, accident no. 5,392,339. Affirmed February 23, 1950.

1950] 

Liberty Mutual Insurance Company, insurance carrier. 

Sidney Rosenblum, for claimant. 

J. Paul Erwin, for defendant.

KNOLL, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by claimant from the order of dis allowance of Referee P. A. Paterson, of Compensation Dis trict No. 6.

On April 28, 1949, claimant filed a petition, averring, inter alia, that she sustained injury to a nerve of her right hand on April 21, 1949, while acting within the course of her employment with defendant herein. To this petition a responsive answer was filed by defendant, denying the allegations of the petition. 

Claimant, on her own behalf, testified that she had been employed by defendant for a period of five years doing spot welding on mountings; that her duties consisted of picking up parts, approximately a quarter of an inch to one-half inch, with a pair of tweezers, placing the part on the mounting, and welding the part to the mounting; that, on April 

21, 1949, while engaged in the performance of her usual duties, at or about 3:15 o'clock, p.m., her right hand went numb. She was asked: "Q. Will you describe to us what, if anything, happened to you physically on April 21, 1949? "A. Nothing outside of my hand going numb. "Q. When you say your hand went numb; when did you first notice that? "A. About 3:15 when I gradually slowed down in working."

Claimant also testified that she noticed nothing unusual before the occurrence; that she completed her duties and the following day reported the matter to her supervisor and I went to the first aid station where she told the nurse in charge, "I just told her my hand went numb"; that she consulted Dr. Heimbach, to whom she gave the same history; and that she has been under the care of Dr. Heimbach to the

[36 Dep. Rep. 

present time. It is the contention of claimant that, by reason of the condition of her right hand, she is unable to do any work whatsoever. 

No testimony was offered on behalf of defendant. 

The referee concluded, as a matter of law, that claimant had failed to establish the happening of an accident and dismissed the claim petition. From this ruling claimant has appealed and has submitted a statement of her claim wherein she set forth that small pieces of glass began to work their way out of her finger after she came under the care of Dr. Heimbach. Claimant further set forth in this statement that, while working on her regular job, she felt nothing wrong physically and that she could not have injured herself while away from her place of employment. 

Claimant then requested that the board consider these facts and award "a new hearing".

We have carefully considered the testimony in this case and the statement submitted by claimant, and are of the opinion that the ruling of the referee should be affirmed. 

The testimony in this case clearly establishes that, at the time the right hand of claimant became numb, she was performing her usual duties in the usual manner and there is no evidence whatsoever of a fortuitous or unforeseen event taking place other than the right hand of claimant becoming numb. There is, therefore, no evidence whatsoever that would warrant a finding that claimant sustained an accident within the meaning of the Compensation Act. 

It must be remembered that there must be proof both of an accident and injury. There must be some evidence of an accident, either direct or circumstantial, in the latter instance clearly and logically indicating it. In the case at issue, there is no evidence whatsoever of the happening of an accident. See Adamchick v. Wyoming Valley Collieries

Co., 332 Pa. 401; Collins v. State Workmen's Insurance 

Fund, 145 Pa. Superior Ct. 182.

We, therefore, affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of dismissal. 

The request of claimant for a rehearing is denied. 

The appeal of claimant is dismissed.

1950] 

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD KAECHER et al. v. TERMINAL WAREHOUSE COMPANY Workmen's compensation 

Death - Dependency — Minor In loco parentis-Grandparent.

A referee's order denying compensation on behalf of a minor claimant on the ground that decedent did not stand in loco parentis to said minor will be reversed, and an award entered where it appears that decedent was the grandfather of the minor, that when the minor was an infant, her mother became mentally ill and the minor was brought to the home of the decedent where she resided up until the time of his death; the fact that the father of the minor established a residence with decedent and continued to live in the latter's household for a period of a year and a half prior to decedent's death during which time, however, he exercised no parental supervision over his child and was employed only periodically so that he contributed nothing toward the support of the child, did not alter decedent's status of in loco parentis.

Appeal by claimant from order of Referee Gibb, District 

No. 8, dismissing petition to review under compensation agreement no. 5328241. Reversed. March 15, 1950. 

American Automobile Insurance Co., insurance carrier. 

Martin E. Geary, for claimant. 

Irwin M. Ringold, for defendant.

KNOLL, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by claimants from the order of Referee 

Laurence V. Gibb, of the Eighth Compensation District, dismissing a petition for review filed on behalf of the claimants. 

On November 9, 1948, Clarence R. Kaecher, husband of claimant May Lloyd Kaecher, sustained certain injuries while acting within the course of his employment with defendant herein, which injuries resulted in his death. By

[36 Dep. Rep. 

888 virtue of the same, compensation agreement no. 5,328,241 was entered into between claimant, May Lloyd Kaecher, and defendant, providing for the payment of compensation to claimant in the sum of $3,600.00.

On March 24, 1949, claimants filed a petition for review of the aforesaid agreement, averring, inter alia, that minor claimant, Bernice May Kaecher, was supported by decedent, Clarence R. Kaecher, and that decedent stood in the status of loco parentis to said minor claimant. To this petition a responsive answer was filed by defendant, denying the allegations of the said petition. 

The facts in this case, which are not in dispute, disclose that when minor claimant was four weeks of age, her mother became mentally ill and she was brought to the home of claimant and decedent on July 23, 1945, who were the grandparents of the said minor child (parents of minor claimant's mother). On the same day, minor claimant's mother was confined to a mental institution and continued to be confined to said institution at all times material to this issue. From July 23, 1945, to March 15, 1947, for a period of approximately twenty months, the father of said minor child lived separate and apart from the said child. 

On March 15, 1947, the father of the said minor child established a residence with claimant and decedent and continued to live at their home thereafter, and was living at the home of the child's grandparents at the time of the death of decedent, November 9, 1948. From July 23, 1945, to November 9, 1948, the minor child lived continuously with her grandparents and all expenses incident to the maintenance and support of the said minor child were paid by the decedent grandfather. During the said period of time, the grandparents also exercised direct control over the said minor child and no supervision was exercised by the father, even though he resided at the home of the grandparents for a period of time. It also appears from the testimony that when the father of the child established his residence with the child's grandparents, he was employed only periodically and, as a result, contributed nothing toward the support

1950] 

66 of the said child, but paid the sum of $10.00 for his personal food and lodging.

The sole issue in this case is whether or not the decedent grandfather stood in the status of loco parentis to the said minor child at the time of his death.

The referee, by decision dated June 8, 1949, found as a fact that claimant had failed to prove, by competent evidence, that Clarence R. Kaecher, deceased, stood in "loco. parentis" to Bernice May Kaecher, granddaughter, on November 9, 1948, the date of death of decedent. Based upon the said finding of fact, the referee dismissed the claim petition. From this ruling claimants have appealed. 

We have made a careful study of the record in this case and are of the opinion that the ruling of the referee should be reversed.

The testimony in this case discloses that, when the minor child was four weeks old, she was brought to the home of her maternal grandparents due to the fact that her mother became mentally ill and was confined to a mental institution on the same day that the minor child was brought to the home of her grandparents and that the mother was confined to the institution continuously thereafter and at all times material to this case.

It also appears from the testimony that, from the time. the minor child was brought to the home of her grandparents, on July 23, 1945, to November 9, 1948, the date of the death of the grandfather, the grandparents assumed the duties and obligations of parents to the said child, expended all moneys necessary for the proper care and maintenance of the said child and exercised exclusive control as to the raising and care of the child. While the father of the child, on March 15, 1947, established a home with the child's grandparents, the testimony discloses that the father exercised no control whatsoever as to the raising of the said child. In fact, the testimony establishes that the father was only periodically employed and was able to contribute only the sum of $10.00 toward his own board and lodging and was unable to contribute anything toward the main-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

tenance and support of the minor child. Moreover, the testimony discloses that the father of the child expressed the desire that the grandparents should raise the child. 

In view of the foregoing, we are of the opinion that decedent put himself in the status of a lawful father to the minor child with reference to the office and duty of making provision for the child. We are not unmindful of the purpose of the compensation act to provide compensation as a substitute for the support of dependents by a decedent in his lifetime, and that where a natural father is physically and financially able and is amenable to process adequately, to compel him to discharge his legal duty, the undertaking of decedent voluntarily assumed during his lifetime may not be passed on to the employer of decedent under the compensation law to the further relief of the natural father. 

The answer to this purport of law is that the father did not earn sufficient money to maintain a home or support the child and an order of support would have served no useful purpose. Under the circumstances, we are of the opinion that the father, having given up all control as to the raising and care of the child and having transferred the same to the grandparents of the child, who cared for and raised the child, expended the necessary moneys for the support of the child and exercised sole control of the raising of the child, were, for all intents and purposes, the parents of the said child and decedent stood in the position of loco parentis to the said child. This being so, the minor child is entitled to the payment of compensation by reason of the death of Clarence R. Kaecher. See Fitzpatrick v. Hudson Coal Co., 159 Pa. Superior Ct. 53; Renovich v. Bethlehem Mines Corp., 131 Pa. Superior Ct. 354; Leyerly v. 

United States, 162 F. (2d) 79.

We, therefore, set aside the referee's seventh finding of fact, conclusions of law and order of dismissal and, in lieu thereof, substitute the following

7. We find as a fact that, as of November 9, 1948, the date of death of Clarence R. Kaecher, the said Clarence R.

1950] 

Kaecher stood in the position of loco parentis to the minor child, Bernice May Kaecher.

That since decedent, Clarence R. Kaecher, stood in loco parentis to his granddaughter, Bernice May Kaecher, at the time of his death, the said minor child is entitled to the payment of compensation.

Order 

Compensation agreement no. 5,328,241 entered into between May Lloyd Kaecher and Terminal Warehouse Company, defendant, and the American Automobile Insurance Company, the insurance carrier, is herewith modified, and defendant, Terminal Warehouse Company, and/or its insurance carrier, American Automobile Insurance Company, is herewith directed to pay to the claimant, May Lloyd Kaecher, compensation at the rate of $15.00 per week for the period beginning November 16, 1948, and to continue thereafter for a period of 300 weeks, for the sum of $4,500.00.

In addition thereto, defendant, Terminal Warehouse Company and/or its insurance carrier, is directed to pay to 

May Lloyd Kaecher, as the natural guardian of Bernice 

May Kaecher, a minor, compensation at the rate of $4.50 per week, being the minimum payable under the act, commencing at the expiration of the 300-week period, to wit, August 10, 1954, and to continue thereafter until the said minor child shall attain the age of 16 years, to wit, June 26, 1961.

This award to bear interest at the rate of 6 percent per annum on all deferred payments.

The remaining findings of fact of the referee are herewith affirmed.

The appeal of the claimants is sustained.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD KALE v. COOPER-BESSEMER CORP. 

Workmen's compensation-Eye injury-Total disability through subsequent injury-Act of June 1, 1945-Application to accident occurring prior to effective date.

Where claimant had lost the vision of his right eye prior to an accident in February of 1945, at which time he suffered an injury to his left eye, as the result of which he lost the vision of that eye in May of 1946 causing him to be totally blind, his right to compensation was governed by the act in force at the time of the accident and not the Act of June 1, 1945, known as the Second Injury Reserve Act, providing for the payment of additional compensation out of the second injury reserve fund set up by the Commonwealth. 

Appeal by Commonwealth of Pennsylvania from order of Referee Moore, district no. 8, awarding compensation under agreement no. 4620856. Reversed. April 19, 1950.

Pennsylvania Manufacturers' Association Casualty In surance Co., insurance carrier. 

Ralph F. Smith, for claimant. 

John V. Wherry and H. M. Engell, for defendant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for Commonwealth of Pennsylvania.

KNOLL, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania from the order of award of Referee Harvey E. Moore, of the eighth compensation district.

On February 24, 1945, while acting within the course of his employment, claimant sustained certain injuries. By virtue of the same, compensation agreement no. 4620856 was entered into between claimant and defendant, providing for the payment of compensation at the rate of $18 per

1950] 

 week for an indefinite number of weeks. The agreement described the accident and injury as: "Employe was breaking scrap when a piece of scrap flew up and struck left eye causing laceration."

On March 8, 1946, a supplemental agreement was entered into between the parties, setting forth, inter alia, that, as of March 10, 1946, claimant had sustained the industrial loss of use of the left eye and providing for the payment of compensation at the rate of $18 per week for 

125 weeks.

On August 15, 1946, a petition for review was filed by claimant against the Cooper-Bessemer Corporation, setting forth that claimant was permanently and totally disabled and that the agreement should be amended to provide for permanent and total disability. To this petition a responsive answer was filed by defendant, denying the material allegations of the petition. Prior to the determination of the petition, claimant filed an amended petition for review against defendant employer and the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, setting forth, in the amended petition, facts similar to the first petition. To this petition responsive answers were filed by the State Workmen's Insurance Board, custodian of the second injury reserve account, and defendant employer, denying the allegations of the petition. 

Claimant, on his own behalf, testified that, while acting within the course of his employment on February 24, 1945, a piece of steel flew up and hit him in the left eye, causing injury to the eye; that in 1909 he had sustained injury to the right eye, but he was able to see out of the right eye until the accident of February 24, 1945, at which time the right eye began to disintegrate; and that in November 1945 he lost all sight in the right eye. Claimant also testified that he has no vision whatsoever in the left eye, as a result of the accident heretofore referred to, so that, for all intents and purposes, he is totally blind; also that he was employed by defendant employer for a period of 18 years, and that he is 61 years of age.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Dr. W. F. Rossman, a specialist in treatment of diseases of the eye, called on behalf of claimant, testified that he examined claimant on September 3, 1946, and on November 22, 1946; that his examination revealed that claimant was blind in both eyes and that the condition was permanent, and that his examination of September 3, 1946, disclosed that claimant, as of that date, had lost the vision of his right eye. 

Dr. S. L. Koch, an ophthalmologist, called on behalf of defendant, testified that he treated claimant for injuries to his left eye immediately following the accident; that the left eye gradually degenerated until claimant lost all vision of the left eye; that he examined the right eye of claimant at the time, and that, while there was light perception of the right eye, claimant had sustained the loss of the industrial vision of the right eye. The doctor was of the opinion that claimant had sustained the loss of vision of both eyes. 

The referee found as a fact that, prior to the accident of February 24, 1945, claimant had lost the vision of the right eye and that as of May 20, 1946, claimant had suffered the loss of the industrial vision of the left eye, thereby causing claimant to be totally blind, which condition would continue to be permanent. Based upon these and other findings of fact, the referee awarded compensation to claimant to be paid by the second injury reserve fund set up by the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, Department of Labor and Industry, beginning July 27, 1947, and to continue to the end of the 500-week period. From this ruling the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania has appealed, contending that, since the injury occurred on February 24, 1945, which was prior to the effective date of the Second Injury Reserve Act, namely, July 1, 1945, the act did not apply to this claimant. 

We have made a careful study of the record in this case and are of the opinion that the ruling of the referee should be reversed.

The testimony in this case discloses that, as a result of the accident of February 24, 1945, claimant sustained a

1950] 

 laceration of the cornea and iris of the left eye and that, by reason thereof, compensation agreement no. 4620856 was entered into between claimant and defendant, providing for the payment of compensation at the rate of $18 per week for an indefinite number of weeks; and that the left eye began to degenerate and that, as a result thereof, claimant suffered the loss of use of the left eye on or about March 10, 1946, at which time a supplemental agreement was entered into between the parties.

The Act of June 1, 1945, provides in section 306.1 as follows: "If an employe, who has incurred (through accident or otherwise) permanent partial disability, through the loss, or loss of use of, one hand, one arm, one foot, one leg or one eye, incurs total disability through a subsequent injury, causing loss, or loss of use of, another hand, arm, foot, leg or eye, he shall be entitled to additional compensation as 

"After the cessation of payments by the employer for the period of weeks prescribed in Clause (c) hereof, for the subsequent injury, additional compensation shall be paid during the continuance of total disability, at the weekly compensation rate applicable for total disability, for the remainder of the five hundred week period provided in 

Clause (a) hereof. This additional compensation shall be paid by the Commonwealth only upon an award by a compensation referee or the Board. All claims for such additional compensation shall be forever barred unless the employe shall have filed a petition therefor with the Board within one year after the last payment made under Clause (c) hereof. The sum of one hundred thousand dollars ($100,000) is hereby appropriated to the Department of Labor and Industry for compensation payable, by the Commonwealth, under this Section for the biennium one thousand nine hundred and forty-five-one thousand nine hundred forty-seven."

It is our opinion that the rights of claimant are governed by the statutes in effect at the time of the accident of Feb-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

ruary 24, 1945. Where employer and employe accept the provisions of the Compensation Act, their relations become contractual. The statute in force at the time of the accident forms a part of the contract of employment and governs the rate of compensation payable to the employe: Polk v. 

Western Bedding Co. et al., 145 Pa. Superior Ct. 142. The 

Act of June 1, 1945, heretofore referred to, did not take effect until July 1, 1945, or approximately five months subsequent to the accident in which the injuries were sustained by claimant. The rights of claimant to compensation are substantive, and he is bound by substantive law in effect at the time of the accident. Since the Act of 1945 was not in effect at the time of the accident, claimant is not entitled to the benefits of the act. Moreover, the Act of 

1945 provides that where an employe is partially disabled by reason of accident or otherwise and incurs total disability through a subsequent injury, he is entitled to additional compensation as therein set forth. The "subsequent injuries" thereto referred to must of necessity relate to accidents and injuries subsequent to the effective date of the act. While we regret that we are unable to sustain the award of additional compensation to this claimant, we are bound by the statutes in effect at the time of the accident of 

February 24, 1945, and the interpretation of our courts fixing the rights of claimant as of the date of the accident. 

We, therefore, set aside the referee's conclusions of law and order of award and, in lieu thereof, substitute the following

1. That since the injuries, forming the basis of claimant's claim, are the result of an accident which occurred on 

February 24, 1945, prior to the effective date of section 

306.1 of the Act of June 1, 1945, claimant is not entitled to the benefits of the Act of June 1, 1945, and his petition for review must be dismissed.

1950] 

Order Amended review petition no. 88,492 filed on behalf of claimant, James Kale, be and the same is herewith dismissed.

The findings of fact of the referee are herewith affirmed. 

The appeal of the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania is sustained.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD NORRIS v. LEE'S FURNITURE STORE Workmen's compensation-Wages-Veteran's allowance. 

A war veteran's monthly allowance of $90 for training and subsistence under the provisions of the Servicemen's Readjustment Act paid to him by the Federal Government, does not constitute wages within the meaning of the Pennsylvania Workmen's Compensation 

Act, hence it may not be included in the computation of weekly wages in determining the amount of award to which claimant was entitled as the result of an injury. 

Appeal by claimant from award of Referee Phillips, district no. 3, claim petition no. 115,840, accident no. 5328676. 

Affirmed as modified. April 19, 1950.

Pennsylvania T. & F. Mutual Casualty Insurance Com pany, insurance carrier. 

P. T. Lonergan, for claimant. 

J. G. Feinour and E. C. Marianelli, for defendant.

KNOLL, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by claimant from an award by Referee 

David W. Phillips of the third compensation district.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

The facts in the case were agreed upon by the parties and the sole issue relates to the computation of claimant's average weekly wages.

On November 9, 1948, claimant was in the employ of defendant at a monthly wage of $110. As a veteran, he was receiving monthly payments of $90, for training and subsistence, under the provisions of the Servicemen's Readjustment Act, paid directly to him by the Federal Government. 

On November 9, 1948, claimant suffered an accidental injury to his right eye which resulted in the permanent loss of the eye.

The referee awarded compensation payments at the rate of $16.92 for 125 weeks, based upon claimant's average weekly wage of $25.38 received from his employer.

Claimant, on appeal, contends that the referee erred in his computation of the weekly wages since he had not included the Government payment of $90 as wages. 

It is our opinion that, under the provisions of the Workmen's Compensation Act, sec. 309, we may not regard claimant's training and subsistence payments as wages. This allowance under the Federal law is a gratuity and not a wage. Further, claimant was not an employe of the Federal Government on November 9, 1948, and we cannot consider him as working under concurrent contracts with two or more employers.

In addition to the referee's conclusions of law, we make the following

4. That the monthly allowance of $90 received by claimant from the Federal Government for training and subsistence does not constitute wages within the meaning of the Pennsylvania Workmen's Compensation Act. 

The referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of award are affirmed. 

Claimant's appeal is dismissed.

1950] 

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD DeMURO v. ANCHOR DYEING & FINISHING COMPANY Workmen's compensation—Partial disability—Suspension of payments 

-Leaving employment to attend college. 

Where claimant sustained a partial disability as the result of an injury but subsequently became employed as a result of which the referee suspended compensation, it was error for the referee to lift the suspension and enter an award for partial disability where claimant voluntarily left his employment and returned to college; in such case compensation payments will be suspended during such period as claimant voluntarily abstains from employment. 

Appeal by defendant from order of Referee Sheldrake, district no. 1, awarding compensation under agreement no. 

5286127. Reversed. April 19, 1950. 

Maryland Casualty Company, insurance carrier. 

Philip J. Franzese, for claimant. 

Louis G. Wagner, for defendant.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

Claimant, Francis DeMuro, suffered admitted accidental injuries on August 25, 1948, in the course of his employment by defendant, Anchor Dyeing & Finishing Company.

The parties entered into an open compensation agreement. 

On January 3, 1949, defendant filed a petition for suspension of payments. Upon hearing held before Referee Sheldrake on September 27, 1949, the referee entered the following order: "It is hereby ordered and decreed that defendant, and/or insurance carrier defendant is ordered and directed to pay to claimant compensation for 20 percent partial disability, at the rate of $5.10 per week, beginning December 22, 1948,

36 Dep. Rep. 

and continuing to July 28, 1949, or a period of 31-1/7 weeks, or a total sum of $158.83; that on July 28, 1949, claimant became employed as an attendant at the Byberry State Hospital. "That claimant worked at the Byberry State Hospital from July 28, 1949, and continued working until September 18, 1949, during which time he was still suffering a residual disability in the amount of 20 percent partial, and compensation should therefore be suspended during this period of time. "That on September 18, 1949, claimant stopped working, and went back to school, and again suffered his 20 percent partial disability and is to be compensated for same, in the amount of $5.10 per week, continuing from September 18, 1949, until such time as his disability shall change or cease, within the meaning of the Workmen's Compensation Act." 

The parties were notified on December 29, 1949, and this appeal by defendant followed on January 4, 1950, which was heard by the board on February 15, 1950.

The appeal poses the question whether all compensation payments should be suspended while claimant attended college. Defendant concedes that the injury to the left hand constitutes a permanent 20 percent partial disability. Claimant's testimony discloses that he has not returned to his former employment although he admitted that he thought he would be able to perform his regular duties. DeMuro stated that he has been attending LaSalle College as a full time day student for the past two years. After the first academic year, claimant obtained summer employment as an attendant at the State Hospital at Byberry, at $144 per month and left this work in September 1949 to resume his studies.

We are of the opinion that defendant's contention is sound and that compensation should be suspended until claimant's residual disability is reflected in an actual loss of earning power. It is significant that claimant, familiar with his duties in the employ of defendant, felt that he was capable of discharging the same. It is also important that claimant

1950]  discharged his duties as a hospital attendant, and voluntarily left that employment in order to continue his academic studies. Had he returned to defendant's employ or continued in his work as a hospital attendant, a suspension order would be proper as claimant demonstrated his ability to perform such work, without any appreciable loss in earnings. 

Thus, his earning power has not been diminished, even though there has been an impairment in physical function. 

An employer is not required to subsidize an employe who pursues a college training. We believe that the equities in the case require us to suspend compensation payments while claimant was in school. 

We affirm the referee's findings of fact. The referee has failed to enter any conclusions of law, so that we now enter the following

1.

On August 25, 1948, both parties were bound by the provisions of article III of The Workmen's Compensation 

Act of 1915, as reënacted and amended. 

2. Since claimant suffers from a 20 percent partial disability, beginning December 22, 1948, he is entitled to compensation for any loss of earning power on that account, but that compensation should be suspended unless said partial disability is actually reflected in a loss of earning power. 

3. Having found that claimant left employment on September 18, 1949, to attend college, we conclude that compensation payments should be suspended while he attended such college.

We hereby amend the second paragraph of the referee's order by adding:

Compensation payments are also suspended during the period claimant attends college and voluntarily abstains from employment.

We vacate and set aside the third paragraph of the referee's order. As amended, the order is now affirmed. 

The appeal of defendant is sustained.

36 Dep. Rep. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD JOBACK v. HILLMAN COAL & COKE CO. 

Workmen's compensation · 

Occupational disease - Dependency claim by widow-Limitations—Section 315 of Occupational Disease Act. 

A referee's award of compensation to claimant as dependent widow under the provisions of the Occupational Disease Act of 1939 will be reversed where the claim which was based upon death resulting from anthraco-silicosis was not filed within one year after the date of death; since the right of claimant to compensation for the death of her husband was entirely independent of his right, her claim could not be treated as a claim for the continuance of payments of compensation which had been awarded to him in his lifetime for disability resulting from the disease which subsequently resulted in his death. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Schwing, district no. 7, claim petition no. 115,389, accident no. 

4,798,236. Reversed April 5, 1950. 

J. W. Stephenson, for claimant. 

R. M. Steffler, for defendant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for Commonwealth of Pennsylvania.

MURPHY, Chairman: 

This is an appeal by defendant and the Commonwealth from an award of the referee.

Decedent's widow, Mary Joback, filed her petition on 

January 31, 1949, to recover compensation as a dependent widow under the provisions of the Occupational Disease Act of 1939, alleging that the death of her husband on August 27, 1947, was the primary result of anthraco-silicosis which decedent contracted in the course of his employment with defendant. Defendant's answer, filed on February 9, 1949, denied dependency of claimant and set up as a defense the statute of limitations, under section 315 of the act, the

1950] 

 claim not having been filed within one year of decedent's death. On February 8, 1949, an answer was filed by the State 

Workmen's Insurance Board denying claimant's dependency and denying that decedent's death resulted from an occupational disease. After hearings, the referee found that claimant was the dependent widow of decedent, that the claim was not barred by section 315 of the act and that decedent's death did result from an occupational disease and, in accordance with such findings, entered an award in favor of claimant. This appeal followed. 

We are of the opinion, after a careful review of the entire record, that the award of the referee must be reversed.

The chronology of events in this case was as follows: 

July 15, 1946-Decedent filed his petition alleging that he became totally disabled as a result of anthraco-silicosis on July 17, 1945. 

May 14, 1947-An award was entered in favor of decedent. 

August 27, 1947-Decedent died. 

November 12, 1947-Upon appeal by defendant, the findings, conclusions and award to decedent were set aside and the matter remanded to the referee. 

December 15, 1947-An award was entered in favor of decedent. 

January 31, 1949-Claimant widow filed her petition.

The substantive rights of the claimant widow, as well as the procedure for the enforcement of them, are controlled by section 315 of the act which provides that, in the case of death, all claims for compensation shall be forever barred. unless, within one year after the death, one of the parties shall have filed a petition as provided in article IV of the act: Jones v. Philadelphia & Reading Coal & Iron Co., 154 

Pa. Superior Ct. 465.

The right of claimant widow to secure for herself and minor child compensation for the death of her husband is, under the act, entirely independent of the right of her husband. It is not derivative: Rossi v. Hillman Coal & Coke Co., 145 Pa. Superior Ct. 108. Until the death of decedent, claim-

36 Dep. Rep. ant had merely an inchoate right to compensation. At the time of his death, her right ripened or accrued and it was upon that date that her substantive rights under the act came into existence.

The filing of a claim petition, within a specified time, is an express condition of the right to obtain an award and failure so to do operates as an absolute bar in the absence of fraud or its equivalent. In the absence of testimony justifying a delay, courts are powerless to extend the time for filing a petition beyond the limitation of section 315 as a matter of indulgence, even in a meritorious case such as this. Decedent's death, as a result of the occupational disease suffered by him, occurred on August 27, 1947, and claimant's petition was not filed until January 31, 1949. If the right of claimant widow was derivative then, his right to an award having been determined on December 15, 1948, the filing of her petition by the widow six weeks later would be sufficient. However, since her right to secure compensation for the death of her husband is entirely independent of his right (Rossi v. Hillman Coal & Coke Co., supra) she is bound by the provisions of section 315 of the act: Holahan v. Berger Coal Co., 164 Pa. Superior Ct. 177; Polk v. Western Bedding Co., 145 Pa. Superior Ct. 142.

Claimant cites the case of Toffalori v. Donatelli Granite 

Co., 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 311, as a basis for the theory that the claim of present claimant is not an original claim but a claim for the continuance of the payments of compensation which have already been awarded. A careful review of the facts and opinion in that case leads to the conclusion that the basic facts there were not similar to those in the instant case and the law enunciated there is not controlling in this case.

In the Toffalori case, decedent had been employed by defendant from 1919 to December 23, 1939, on which latter date he became totally disabled as a result of an occupational disease contracted in the course of his employment. 

After the filing of his claim petition, an award was entered, commencing December 30, 1939. Decedent died of his occupational disease on January 23, 1943, and on February 3,

1950] 

1943, his widow filed her petition. The court held that, under the facts of that case, even though decedent's death occurred more than three years after decedent's total disability, the claim of his widow was not barred under section 301 (c) of the act, since claimant's petition was not an original claim and death was not the cause for compensation and decedent had made claim for compensation in his lifetime.

It is important to note that, in that case, the single question of law raised was an interpretation of the limitations set forth in section 310 (c) of the act, while in the instant case the single question of law raised is the interpretation of the limitation set forth in section 315 of the act. In the 

Toffalori case, decedent's widow did file her claim within the one-year period prescribed by section 315 of the act while, in the instant case, claimant widow did not file her petition within the time prescribed by section 315 of the act. 

When the factual situation is as in the instant case, the law is clearly settled that the rights of the widow and minor child are independent of that of the deceased employe and not derivative and, therefore, in the event of failure to file a claim petition within one year after death of decedent, the claim is barred.

Neither defendant nor the Commonwealth having taken an appeal from the award of December 15, 1948, to decedent, claimant and her minor child are entitled to the compensation due decedent from July 24, 1945, to the date of his death on August 28, 1947.

We, therefore strike out the referee's fifth and sixth findings of fact and, in lieu thereof, substitute the following 

5. That compensation was awarded in this case to Mike 

Joback under date of December 15, 1948, and no appeal has been taken from the award. No compensation has been paid under the award but claimant has petitioned the Workmen's Compensation Board to modify the referee's award in order that payment of same can be made to her and the minor child of the parties.

36 Dep. Rep. 

6. That decedent died on August 27, 1947, and claimant did not file the present claim petition until January 31, 1949, more than one year subsequent to the death of her husband. 

All other findings of fact are affirmed. 

We strike out the referee's conclusions of law and, in lieu thereof, substitute the following

1. In view of the foregoing facts we conclude as a matter of law that since claimant widow did not file her claim petition within one year of the death of her husband, her claim is barred under section 315 of the act. 

2. Claimant widow and minor child, as a matter of law, are entitled to have the award of the referee under date of December 15, 1948, amended in order that payment of the award can be made to her and the minor child of the parties. 

We strike out the referee's award and, in lieu thereof, substitute the following

Order The claim petition of claimant widow is hereby dismissed. 

In accordance with the above findings of fact and conclusions of law, compensation is awarded to Mary Joback, widow of Mike Joback and Mary Ann Joback, minor daughter of the parties, in the sum of $2,185.71 representing the total sum due decedent under the award of December 

15, 1948.

Defendant company, Hillman Coal and Coke Company, is directed to pay 70 percent of the above stipulated sum and 30 percent is to be paid by the Commonwealth of Pennsyl vania, Department of Labor and Industry, Bureau of Work men's Compensation.

The above award is to bear interest at the rate of six percent per annum in accordance with the provisions of the 

Occupational Disease Act, sec. 410. 

The appeals of defendant and the Commonwealth are sustained. 

Affirmed as amended.

1950] 

 COMMON PLEAS OF DAUPHIN COUNTY CROSS COUNTRY SPORTSWEAR CORPORATION APPEAL Unemployment compensation-Contribution rate-Sale of business— 

Right to claim predecessor's lower experience rating-Change of rate during calendar year—Purchase by one having previous rating of own. Unemployment Compensation Law of 

May 26, 1943, as amended May 29, 1945—Right to rely on advice of field representatives of bureau. 

1. Where an employer who has been assigned an unemployment compensation contribution rate for a calendar year thereafter acquires the business of another employer to whom a lower rate has been assigned for such calendar year, he may, under section 301 (g) of the 

Unemployment Compensation Law of May 26, 1943, P. L. 639, as amended by the Act of May 29, 1945, P. L. 1145, claim the reduced rate assigned to the predecessor employer for the balance of the calendar year provided the prior experience is indicative of future experience. 

2. Nothing in the Unemployment Compensation Law prohibits changing a contribution rate during a calendar year in order to give a purchasing employer the benefit of his predecessor's more favorable experience rating. 

3. Section 301 (g) of the Unemployment Compensation Law, as amended, giving a successor employer a right to claim the benefit of a lower experience rating given his predecessor, applies regardless of whether the purchaser was previously an employer within the meaning of the Unemployment Compensation Law or not. 

4. An employer is not entitled to rely upon the advice of field representatives of the Bureau of Employment and Unemployment Compensation respecting his right to benefit by a predecessor's lower experience rating. 

Appeal from Bureau of Employment and Unemployment

Compensation. C. P. of Dauphin County, Commonwealth dkt., 1948, no. 32. Appeal sustained. August 10, 1949. 

Manuel Kraus, for appellant. 

T. McKeen Chidsey, Attorney General, and Morley W. 

Baker, Special Deputy Attorney General, for Commonwealth.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

RICHARDS, P. J.: 

This is an appeal by the Cross Country Sportswear Corp., from a determination of the Bureau of Employment and 

Unemployment Compensation, of the Department of Labor and Industry, of the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania. The determination appealed from relates to a change of the rate of contribution for the calendar year 1947. The appeal was brought under the provisions of the Unemployment Compensation Law of December 5, 1936, P. L. (1937) 2897, sec. 301 (h), as amended by the Act of May 29, 1945, P. L. 

1145, sec. 5. At the hearing before the court, the parties filed a stipulation of facts, and in due time the case was argued before the court en banc. Facts Appellant is a New York corporation, duly authorized to carry on business in Pennsylvania. The date on which it began to do business here was November 1, 1946. For that year its rate of contribution under the act was fixed at 

2.7 percent, which was also the rate fixed for 1947. On

July 1, 1947, it acquired two of the three plants formerly owned and operated by the Universal Pants Company, a partnership, which was a contributing employer under the 

Pennsylvania Unemployment Compensation Law. The partnership, for 1947 and for previous years, had been assigned a contribution rate of one percent. The partners of the Universal Pants Company, and the stockholders of the appellant, were the same individuals and their respective interests in each organization were the same. 

On December 11, 1947, an application for predecessor's employer experience was filed. This application was dated 

July 30, 1947, and was executed by appellant and by the Universal Pants Co. Although advised that the rate of appellant would be 2.1 percent for the balance of the calendar year, appellant filed returns for the third and fourth quarters of 1947 in which it computed its contributions at the rate of one half of one percent. The bureau

1950] 

 on January 14, 1948, finally determined the rate of contribution to be 2.7 percent.

Appellant, before acquiring the two plants aforesaid had 

38 employes and thereafter had 500 additional employes.

The third plant of the Universal Pants Company, a partnership, was transferred to the Universal Pants Co., Inc., a newly formed corporation, on July 1, 1947. The new company was given a contribution rate of one half of one percent.

The stipulation provides that if the appeal is sustained, the rate of contribution of appellant for the last two quarters of 1947, shall be one half of one percent; otherwise it shall remain 2.7 percent. This proviso of the stipulation results from the fact that the contribution rate of the Universal Pants Co., was reduced from one percent to one half of one percent by virtue of the provisions of the Act of June 10, 1947, P. L. 1469, sec. 313.

The nineteenth paragraph of the stipulation states the legal question involved to be: "Where an employer has been assigned an unemployment compensation contribution rate of 2.7 percent for a calendar year, and thereafter such employer acquires the business of another employer to whom an unemployment compensation contribution rate of one-half percent has been assigned for such calendar year, is the successor-employer, under the provisions of the Unemployment Compensation Law, entitled to the reduced rates assigned to the predecessoremployer for the balance of such calendar year?" Discussion The Bureau of Employment and Unemployment Compensation, hereinafter referred to as the bureau, contends that where an annual rate of contribution has been fixed for an employer, that such rate may not be changed during said calendar year, except to maintain the safety factor as provided in section 301 (f). On the contrary, appellant contends that the rate may be changed during the calendar year when an employer acquires the business of another

[36 Dep. Rep. 

having a more favorable rate, provided that the experience of the selling employer be considered indicative of the future employer experience of the purchasing employer. 

At the outset we may say that appellant contends that it was advised, by field representatives of the bureau, before it acquired the two plants in question, that it could get the benefit of the preceding employer's experience, and have its rate of contribution reduced during the calendar year 1947. We attach no importance to the exhibit or to the advice said to have been given. Even though given, it was not an official determination, and the provisions of the law supersede any private advice, or any rules or regulations inconsistent with the law. Furthermore, in this case the provisions of section 301 (f), relating to the safety factor, are not involved, nor is the question of probable future employer experience.

The sole issue before us is one of law. May an annual rate of contribution be changed during a calendar year to give an employer the benefit of the rate of another employer whose business he has acquired? 

Experience rating was first introduced into the Unemployment Compensation Law by the Act of May 26, 1943, P. L. 639. However, the law which controls the present case is section 301 of the Act of May 29, 1945, P. L. 1145, which was reënacted and amended by the Act of June 10, 1947, P. L. 498. The section in question became effective January 1, 1946. 

The act fixes the basic rate of contribution for each calendar year at 2.7 percent. However, this rate may be reduced by a favorable experience. No claim is made in this case for a reduced rate based upon appellant's employer experience. On the contrary, appellant's claim is based solely on the provisions of subsection (g) of section 301, Act of May 29, 1945, supra. 

Subsection (g) bears the the heading: "Successor-ininterest". It contains two paragraphs. Paragraph (1) provides, that an employer who, prior to January 1, 1946, acquires the business of another employer, in whole or in

1950] 

 part, shall immediately notify the department, and for the purpose of ascertaining the rate of contribution, his employer's experience shall include that of the prior employer. 

This language suggests, in view of the heading, that the employer who acquires the business of another, is a successor-in-interest. The transfer of employer experience is mandatory for the purpose of ascertaining the rate of contribution. It will be noted that this paragraph mentions a rate of contribution, not an annual rate of contribution, or a rate for a calendar year. The requirement of immediate notice implies a right to use the acquired experience forthwith to obtain a more favorable rate, otherwise there would be an urgency. If the rate for the calendar year is fixed and immutable, and may in no case be affected by the acquisition of the predecessor employer's experience, there would be little point in requiring immediate notice of the acquisition of the business. 

The significance of these observations is enhanced by the fact that section 301 (g) of the Act of May 26, 1943, contains substantially similar provisions in force before the effective date of the Act of May 29, 1945. This militates against the idea that the purpose of immediate notice is to furnish data to be used in fixing a rate for the succeeding calendar year.

The second paragraph of section 301 (g) of the Act of May 29, 1945, is new. It provides that an employer who, subsequent to December 31, 1945, transfers his business, in whole or in part, to a successor-in-interest, may jointly make application with the successor-in-interest for transfer of the preceding employer's experience to said successor-ininterest. The department may allow such transfer only if it finds that the experience of the preceding employer may be considered indicative of the future employer's experience of the successor-in-interest. 

There was a joint application here for transfer of the preceding employer's experience. The plants involved were transferred after December 31, 1945. There was no finding that the previous employer's experience was not indicative

[36 Dep. Rep. 

of future employer's experience. The transfer of the employer experience, and the consequent reduction of rate, was refused solely upon the ground that the rate of contribution having been fixed for the calendar year could not be reduced during the calendar year. The bureau has argued that the transfer of experience and reduction of rate may be allowed only to a successor-in-interest who never theretofore had been an employer. We can find nothing in the act to justify this conclusion. While the act contains many statutory definitions, it does not define successor-in-interest. A successor-in-interest is one who succeeds to the interest of another. He may be one who previously was an employer, or one who theretofore never was an employer. The purpose of paragraphs (1) and (2) is to transfer employer experience with the consequent effect upon the rate of contribution. 

In paragraph (1) it is not only mandatory, but unilateral. 

In paragraph (2) it is discretionary and bilateral. In neither case is there any legislative prohibition against the reduction of a rate during the calendar year. The intent of the legislature, it seems to us, was just the opposite. It intended to allow a reduction of the rate, during a calendar year, upon compliance with the terms of the statute. The taxpayer should be entitled to the benefit of any doubt. He should not be deprived of an advantage by an unjustified technical construction, or by administrative rules or practice, not founded upon a legislative mandate, but rather upon what is read into the statute. It would seem to be incongruous to allow a successor with no previous employer experience an advantage denied to a successor who has had previous employer experience, especially when the successorin-interest is owned and controlled by the same parties who conducted the prior business. Yet that is exactly what the bureau contends.

It is our conclusion, therefore, that section 301 of the act in question was expressly designed to enable a successor-ininterest to acquire the predecessor employer's experience with the attendant effect upon the contribution rate; that the term successor-in-interest comprehends both those who

1950] 

 were, and those who were not, previously employers; that there is no legislative prohibition against changing a contribution rate in such cases, during a calendar year; that upon joint application the rate may be reduced during a calendar year, provided the prior experience is indicative of future experience; and that a right of appeal exists from the determination of an annual rate or any modification thereof, as provided in section 301 (g). Accordingly, the appeal must be sustained. Decree 

And now, to wit, August 10, 1949, the appeal is sustained and it is hereby ordered and decreed that the rate of contribution of appellant from July 1, 1947, to December 31, 1947, shall be at the rate of one half of one percent, as provided by the stipulation.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD SMITH v. OTIS ELEVATOR COMPANY 

Workmen's compensation · Accidental injury · Absence of unusual occurrence-Knee slipping out of place. 

Testimony by claimant that he was employed by defendant company repairing elevators and that on a certain date while climbing on top of an elevator he rubbed his leg a little bit, and heard something snap so that he couldn't put any weight on it, and that about two days after the occurrence, while engaged in the climbing of a ladder his knee again slipped out, was insufficient to establish the happening of an accident since claimant was admittedly performing his usual duties and there was no evidence that he slipped, twisted or bumped himself; especially so where claimant admitted that several years prior he had suffered from "housemaid's knee". 

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Carmalt, specially presiding in the fifth district, claim petition no. 115,934, accident no. 5,345,892. Affirmed. May 3, 1950.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Employer's Liability Assurance Corp., insurance carrier. 

Milton J. Kolansky, for claimant. 

E. Mac Troutman, for defendant.

KNOLL, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by claimant from the order of disallowance of Referee Gladstone A. Carmalt, specially presiding in the fifth compensation district.

On March 16, 1949, claimant filed a petition, averring, inter alia, that he sustained certain injuries on December 7, 1948, while acting within the course of his employment with defendant herein. To this petition a responsive answer was filed by defendant, denying the allegations of the petition.

Claimant, on his own behalf, testified that he was employed in the service department of defendant company, repairing elevators for a period of five years, and that on December 7, 1948, while engaged in the repairing of an elevator, he sustained injuries to the knee of his left leg. 

In reference to the alleged occurrence, claimant testified as follows: 

"Why, I was working up in Tamaqua, in Majestic Hotel, and I was climbing on top of the elevator and it is all iron work on an elevator and I just rubbed my leg a little bit and the way I was climbing and that and I heard something snap and from then on, why I just couldn't put any weight on it, had to get right down. At the time the elevator wasn't running, we were working on top of it.” 

Claimant further testified, that, following the occurrence, he consulted Dr. Davison, a chiropractor, who snapped the knee back in place; that he returned to work a few days later and then reported to the Mount Carbon Brewery and, while engaged in climbing up a ladder, the knee again went out of position and he again reported to Dr. Davison, who snapped the knee back in place. Claimant also testified that he continued to receive treatment from Dr. Davison

1950] 

 which proved to be unsatisfactory and that he then reported to Dr. Burke, who referred him to the Pottsville Hospital for treatment. It is the contention of claimant that, as a result of the aforesaid occurrence, he was disabled from 

December 7, 1948, to February 13, 1949, inclusive, with the exception of two days, namely December 8th and 9th.

On cross-examination, claimant testified that some two or three years prior to the alleged occurrence, he suffered from "housemaid's knee” as a result of his employment; that, at the time of the alleged occurrence, he was performing his usual duties in the usual manner, and that climbing on the top of an elevator is part of his regular duties. 

No medical testimony was offered on behalf of claimant to show any causal connection between the occurrence and the alleged disability.

The referee concluded that claimant had failed to establish the happening of an accident and dismissed the claim petition. From this ruling claimant has appealed. 

We have made a careful study of the record in this case and are of the opinion that the ruling of the referee should be affirmed.

The testimony in this case discloses that, on December 7, 1948, at the time of the alleged occurrence, claimant was performing his duties in a usual manner and there is no evidence that claimant slipped, twisted, or bumped himself or that any unusual or untoward occurrence occurred which would constitute an accident within the meaning of the Compensation Act; and that about two days after the alleged occurrence, claimant was engaged in the climbing of a ladder which was incident to his duties as an elevator repairman and, while so engaged, the knee again slipped out, although there is no evidence of the happening of an accident at that time. It is also established by the testimony that two or three years prior to the alleged occurrence, claimant suffered from disability of the knee, which he described as "housemaid's knee" as a result of the performance of his usual duties. We have, therefore, concluded that claimant has failed to establish the happening of an

[36 Dep. Rep. 

accident within the meaning of the Compensation Act and that there was an aggravation of a preëxisting condition. 

Accordingly, claimant is not entitled to the payment of compensation and the referee properly dismissed the claim petition. See Rupchak v. Westinghouse Electric & Manufacturing Co., 161 Pa. Superior Ct. 228; Turek v. Damalak, 161 Pa. Superior Ct. 84; Good v. Pennsylvania Department of Supplies, 346 Pa. 151; Adamchick v. Wyoming Valley 

Collieries Co., 332 Pa. 401.

We therefore affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of dismissal.
