The appeal of claimant is dismissed. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD TARASAN v. H. F. UBER & SONS Workmen's compensation—Employe or independent contractor--Con trol and direction of work-Source of wage payment. 

A referee's order disallowing a claim against defendant will be affirmed, where the order was based on a determination that claimant was not an employe of defendant but was an employe of an independent contractor in the doing of work for defendant, it appearing that the latter was a roofing contractor, that for the purpose of adding a one-story addition to its warehouse building it agreed with one Kendi, for the purpose of having him do masonry work on an hourly basis, that Kendi brought claimant and another person upon the job as helpers, both of whom were paid by defendant a specified hourly rate for their work, that defendant supplied materials and Kendi supplied the necessary tools, that Kendi exercised control and direction over claimant, that Kendi used claimant in connection with work on other jobs at the same time without seeking defendant's permission; the fact that Kendi was paid nothing beyond what he actually received as a mason for the work done for defendant was merely evidence of, but not conclusive as to, Kendi's status as an employe rather than independent contractor; likewise the source of the wage payment was not controlling.

1950] 

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Foust, district no. 9, claim petition no. 114,645, accident no. 

5,285,539. Affirmed. April 5, 1950. 

American Casualty Co. of Reading, insurance carrier. 

C. Dale Blair, for claimant. 

Irwin M. Ringold, for defendant.

C. Dale Blair, for New Amsterdam Casualty Co. 

MURPHY, Chairman: 

This is an appeal by claimant from an order of disallowance entered by the referee in claimant's claim against 

H. F. Uber & Sons. In the companion case, A-34674, which has been determined by us in a separate opinion, defendant, Stephen J. Kendi, has appealed the granting of an award against him and in favor of claimant. 

In the instant case, claimant in his petition filed on November 22, 1948, alleged that while crawling through the roof rafters to get to the scaffold, in the course of his employment on August 23, 1948, the roof rafter let go, causing him to fall upon the scaffold, then roll off to the ground, as a result of which he suffered a fracture of two vertebræ and contusions of the head. Defendant's answer, filed on 

December 2, 1948, denied that claimant was in its employ at the time of the accident and demanded proof of all other material averments contained in claimant's petition. After hearings, the referee found that claimant was not in the employ of defendant, H. F. Uber & Sons, at the time of the accident and dismissed claimant's petition. This appeal followed.

In this case we must first determine whether defendant, Stephen J. Kendi, was an independent contractor, in the doing of work for the other defendant, H. F. Uber & Sons, or an employe and, further, whether claimant was an em-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

ployee of defendant, Stephen J.

Kendi, or of defendant, H. F. Uber & Sons. 

The general rules of law applicable to the situation here presented are quite clear.

The chief things to consider in determining the relationship are the payment of wages, control over claimant, the manner of entering into the contract of hire and the general classification of claimant on the basis of the nature of the services performed: Dolan v. Franz Beverage Co.,

A-25857. 

The independent contractor usually expects some independent profit or advantage and it is because the typical employe ordinarily lacks such an opportunity that the workmen's compensation system has been devised to help him: 

Ribniseky v. Perry Coal Co., A-31533, decided December 

12, 1945.

The fact that claimant is not carried on the records as an employe is not determinative of his status. It is only a matter to be considered: Shields v. Freihofer, 147 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 455.

Where a contract is entered into and no control is reserved over the means and manner of accomplishment of the work, but merely the result, the employment is an independent one (Shields v. Freihofer, supra), and the mere fact that a party retains the right to supervise and inspect the work during performance does not change the relationship from independent contractor to master and servant where the mode and means of performance are within the control of the one designated as the independent contractor. 

The relation of employer and employe exists where the employer has the right to select the employe, to direct both what work he shall do and the manner in which it shall be done, and to remove and discharge him: Shields v. Freihofer, supra; McColligan v. Pennsylvania R. R. Co., 214 

Pa. 229.

Thus, whether one is an employe or an independent contractor often presents, as here, a close legal question which is to be determined from the facts of the case.

1950] 

The facts in the instant case are as follows:

Defendant, Kendi, was a skilled mason and had a general knowledge of building. Defendant, Uber & Sons, was a roofing contractor; it had an office warehouse building which it wished to enlarge by adding a one-story addition. It proceeded to do the necessary excavation and laying of the concrete footer and then contacted defendant, Kendi, for the purpose of having him do the masonry work (subsequently Kendi and his helper were also engaged to pour the concrete, do the steel work and lay the roof). Kendi came to Uber's place where the parties reviewed the place together and it was then agreed that Kendi would do the work on an hourly basis rather than by contract. Kendi received $2.45 an hour for the time actually spent by him working on the job and, with the approval of defendant, Uber & Sons, brought claimant and one Mehallic upon the job as helpers. These helpers, paid by Uber & Sons, received $1.25 an hour for their work. In addition, Uber's son, who was regularly employed by and paid by Uber, on occasion acted as a laborer on the job. During the progress of the work, Kendi kept his own time but it is not clear as to who kept the time of claimant and Mehallic. Defendant, Uber & 

Sons, made up the pay envelopes, gave the sealed envelopes to defendant, Kendi, who kept his own and turned the other sealed envelopes, unopened, to claimant and Mehallic respectively.

Defendant, Uber & Sons, supplied the materials and defendant, Kendi, supplied the necessary tools.

During the construction Uber was on the job several times a day but did no actual work and gave no orders directly to claimant or Mehallic. (There is testimony to the effect that, if Uber wished claimant or Mehallic to do some work other than that which they were then doing, he would ask Kendi if claimant or Mehallic could do that particular job rather than what was then being done and, if not busy, Kendi would direct claimant or Mehallic to do as requested by Uber.) Kendi exercised control and direction

[36 Dep. Rep. 

over claimant and Mehallic in the manner in which they did their work and Uber, though in the roofing business, had little or no knowledge of cement work, block laying or steel work.

Though Uber paid the wages to Kendi, claimant, and Mehallic, until after the accident, he made no deductions for tax purposes but immediately subsequent to the accident, such deductions were made by him. 

The testimony further shows that, at the time he was doing this work, Kendi was also engaged on another job as an independent contractor on a contract basis, on which job he admittedly employed claimant at an hourly rate of $1.25.

In order to determine the relationship between the two defendants, Stephen J. Kendi and H. F. Uber & Sons, an examination of the testimony presented must be made, keeping in mind the law applicable thereto. 

Defendant, Kendi, argues, first, that the mere fact that he directed the manner in which claimant did his work and told him what to do did not, necessarily, make him the employer of claimant and argues, further, that he was the foreman on the job and not the employer.

The law upon this point is clearly settled. The relation of master and servant exists when the employer has the right to select the employe, the power to remove and discharge him, and the right to direct both what work shall be done and the way and manner in which it shall be done: 

McColligan v. Pennsylvania R. R. Co., supra. On the other hand, where the contract is let for work to be done by another in which the contractee reserves no control over the means of its accomplishment, but merely as to the result, the employment is an independent one establishing the relation of contractee and contractor and not that of master and servant: Siminton v. Morton, 275 Pa. 562. A control of the work reserved in the employer which makes the employe a mere servant is a control not only of the result of the work, but also of the means and manner of the performance thereof: Balinski et ux. v. Press Publishing Co., 118 Pa.

1950] 

 Superior Ct. 89; Gravatt v. Workmen's Insurance Fund, 

140 Pa. Superior Ct. 434.

While the testimony in this case is not clear as to whether defendant, Uber, reserved the right to "hire and fire" or whether defendant, Kendi, could "hire or fire" without first securing the approval of Uber, the testimony is clear that Uber did not reserve the right to direct what work should be done and the way and manner in which it was to be done. 

The testimony further shows that while Uber, whose place of business adjoined the place where claimant was employed, visited the construction daily and observed what was being done, he gave no instructions or orders to either claimant or Mehallic. Nor, aside from telling him what materials were on hand or would be delivered, did Uber give any instructions or orders to Kendi as to the way and manner in which the work was to be done or as to the work to be done at any particular time. To the contrary, the record is clear that all of the orders to claimant were given by Kendi and that Uber knew little or nothing of the work which Kendi was engaged to do. From the record in this case, it is clear that Uber was not interested in the method or means adopted in the doing of the work but only in the result. The distinguishing criterion is the right to control the means of accomplishing the result. Where control is not reserved over the means, the relationship is that of an independent contractor, and conversely where such control is reserved, the relationship is that of servant or employe: 

Gailey v. State Workmen's Insurance Fund, 286 Pa. 311; 

Beals v. State Workmen's Insurance Fund, 131 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 418; Healey v. Carey, Baxter & Kennedy, Inc., 144 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 500.

Defendant, Kendi, further argues that since the testimony shows that he was to be paid nothing beyond what he, himself, actually earned as a mason, making no profit upon the work done by claimant and Mehallic, this is additional proof that he was not an independent contractor on this job but merely an employe. While we agree that such evidence must be considered in reaching our decision it is not a decisive factor, but must be considered with all of the elements in

[36 Dep. Rep. 

the case. This is also true of the fact that, though Kendi handed claimant his pay, Kendi actually received the amount given to claimant by him, in a sealed envelope from Uber. 

The source of wage payments is not always controlling but is merely one of the factors to be considered with all others which must be considered.

Further evidence tending to show that Kendi was an independent contractor and not an employer of Uber is the fact that, on rainy days, Kendi did no work for defendant, Uber, but took claimant back to the other job where he was working at the same time, and worked there together with him. There is nothing in the testimony from which we can infer that Kendi asked Uber's permission to stay away from the job, either for himself or for claimant. It is certainly reasonable to assume that, if the relationship existing between defendants was that of employer-employe, that the employe, Kendi, would ask permission to stay away from the job and certainly, ask permission to take a fellowemploye from that job to a job being done for another party. 

In addition, the mere fact that claimant would, without asking for and receiving the permission of Uber, follow the instructions of Kendi and leave the work he was doing for Uber to do the work which Kendi, under contract, was doing for someone else, is evidence of the fact that he, claimant, recognized Kendi and not Uber as his employer. 

Despite the fact that there are present in this case some factors which, standing alone, might reasonably result in a decision being made to the effect that Kendi was merely an employe of Uber & Sons, nevertheless, after a careful consideration of the entire testimony we are of the opinion that the great weight of the testimony presented in this case leads to the conclusion that Kendi was an independent contractor and not an employe of Uber & Sons. Since we so conclude, we further conclude that claimant was an employe of Kendi and that the decision of the referee, so finding, was proper and should not be disturbed.

We, therefore, affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order. 

Claimant's appeal is dismissed.

1950] 

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD LEACH v. MEADOW GOLD DAIRIES, INC. 

Workmen's compensation—Employer's subrogation—Funeral expenses 

-Allowance in excess of statutory amount Section 

319 of Workmen's Compensation Act 

Where claimant was awarded compensation on account of the death of her husband and she had also recovered a settlement against a third-party wrongdoer, on account of his death, a referee's order allowing a deduction of $952.12 for funeral expenses from the amount recovered before applying the balance as a credit to defendant for any compensation due claimant will be affirmed, as against defendant's contention that the proper deduction was the statutory amount of $200 allowable for funeral expenses under section 306 of The Workmen's Compensation Act; under section 319 of the act an employer's subrogation right, in case of recovery against a third party, is subject to reasonable attorney's fees and other proper disbursements, and in the absence of any evidence that the sum actually paid out by claimant for funeral expenses was unreasonable, the credit allowed by the referee was proper.

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Moore, district no. 8, claim petition no. 110,420, accident no. 4971857. 

Affirmed. April 5, 1950.

Zurich General Accident and Liability Insurance Com pany, insurance carrier. 

John V. Wherry, for claimant. 

Irwin M. Ringold, for defendant.

KNOLL, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by defendant from the order of award of Referee Harvey E. Moore of the eighth compensation district. 

On October 9, 1947, claimant, as the widow of Stanley T.

Leach, filed a petition on behalf of herself and her minor son, averring, inter alia, that decedent, Stanley T. Leach, sustained certain injuries on November 5, 1946, while acting

[36 Dep. Rep. 

within the course of his employment with defendant herein, which injuries resulted in his death on January 14, 1947. To this petition a responsive answer was filed by defendant, denying the allegations of the petition. 

The matter was duly referred to Referee Harvey E. 

Moore, before whom testimony was taken, who, by decision dated May 24, 1949, awarded compensation to claimant, from which ruling defendant has appealed.

The reporter at the hearing held before Referee Moore was Miss Ethel M. Watson. Prior to the transcription of the testimony, Miss Watson severed her employment with the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, so that at this time a transcript of the testimony taken before the referee is not available. Counsel for the respective parties have filed briefs setting forth the material facts in the case and have agreed that the matter be disposed of on the present state of the record. In accordance with the agreement of counsel, we are disposing of defendant's appeal. 

The record discloses that on November 5, 1946, Stanley T. Leach, while acting within the course of his employment, was injured in an automobile accident, sustaining injuries which subsequently resulted in his death. Legal proceedings were instituted on behalf of his dependents against the wrongdoer and settlement made in the sum of $5,500. Out of the aforesaid sum of $5,500, the sum of $600 was paid to the North American Company, who had filed a subrogation claim for damages to the motor vehicle, leaving a balance of $3,267. The referee allowed the following deductions: 

Dr. D. E. Vogan 

Mary Smith, nurse 

Dr. R. C. Vogan 

Mercer Cottage Hospital

Shelley Funeral Home 

$126.00 8.00 3.00 105.00 952.12 or a total deduction of $1,194.12, leaving a balance of $2,072.88. The referee directed that one half of the amount, or $1,036.44, be applied as credit to defendant for any compensation due to claimant and the balance of the amount,

1950] 

 or $1,036.44, be applied as a credit for any compensation due to the minor son, Ronald Stanley Leach.

The referee further directed that no payments for doctor, hospital, medical or funeral bills be made by defendant for the reason that credit had been given for which defendant might be liable under the Compensation Act.

As to the credit of $952.12, allowed by the referee for funeral expenses, defendant has appealed, contending that the proper deduction was the statutory amount of $200 allowable for funeral expenses under the Compensation Act and that defendant should be given credit for the additional sum of $742.12, as to future payments of compensation to claimant herein.

We have made a careful study of the record and are of the opinion that the referee properly allowed, as a deductible item from the moneys collected as against the third party, the sum of $952.12, being the actual amount paid to the Shelley Funeral Home for the burial of Stanley T. 

Leach.

Section 319 of the Compensation Act provides as follows: 

"When the compensable injury is caused in whole or in part by the act or omission of a third party, the employer shall be subrogated to the right of the employe, his personal representative, his estate or his dependents, against such third party for the balance of any sum recovered in litigation, or paid in compromise settlement, after subtraction of reasonable attorney's fees and other proper disbursements, but only to the extent of the compensation payable under this article by the employer. Any recovery against such third person in excess of the compensation theretofore paid by the employer shall be paid forthwith to the employe or to the dependents, and shall be treated as an advance payment by the employer on account of any future installments of compensation." (Italics supplied.)

It is to be noted that section 319 provides for the subtraction of reasonable attorney's fees and other proper disbursements and that defendant does not allege that the sum of $952.12, paid for funeral expenses, was unreasonable or improper.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

It is also our opinion that the rights of defendant, under section 319 of the act, and the liability of defendant for medical, surgical, and funeral expenses under section 306 of the act, are separate and distinct. Section 319 provides that where injury or death are the result of the wrongful act of a third person and recovery is had against that person, that the employer is entitled to credit for the amount remaining after the payment of reasonable attorney's fees and other expenses, incurred by reason of the injuries sustained by the employe. Section 306 provides that irrespective of the nature of the injuries or the costs of the funeral, the statutory liability of the employer is fixed as a specific sum. In this case, the statutory liability of the employer for funeral expenses is $200, whereas claimant paid out the sum of $952.12 for funeral expenses, recovering this sum from the wrongdoer. To give defendant credit for $752.12, would result in unjust enrichment of defendant for the reason that, if defendant were to pay the funeral expenses, the liability of defendant would be $200 and not $952.12. The fallacy of defendant's argument can be best illustrated, if one were to imagine that during the first 60 days after the accident, medical expenses were incurred in the sum of $1,000 and the sum of $1,000 was recovered by suit or settlement from the third party tortfeasor. Under defendant's contention, it would be entitled to credit for $850 even though the actual medical expenses were $1,000, and this sum was recovered from the third party. Surely the legislature never intended such a situation. We have, therefore, concluded that the referee's order of award and credit are correct.

We, therefore, affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order. 

The appeal of defendant is dismissed.

1950] 

 SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA KAROLY v. JEDDO-HIGHLAND COAL COMPANY et al. 

Workmen's compensation — Occupational disease Silicosis Time when liability attaches-Insurance-Failure to except to finding-Appellate review. 

1. In a case where the only question related to the party liable to pay compensation to claimant for disability as the result of anthracosilicosis where the controversy was between his employer, which was a self-insurer until a certain date, after which the State Workmen's Insurance Fund became the insurance carrier, the date of claimant's last exposure to the hazard before total disability ensued was the date when liability was imposed and since this preceded the time when the fund became the insurer, the employer was liable. 

2. Where an employer contended before the referee that claimant became totally disabled on a certain date but the referee found that he became totally disabled at an earlier date, and in its appeal to the board, the employer did not except to the finding but rather adopted the date found by the referee as the day when total disability occurred, the employer could not thereafter question the finding of the referee upon an appeal to the common pleas.

Appeal from judgment of C. P. of Luzerne County. 

October term, 1949, no. 1603. Affirmed. May 10, 1950. 

Superior Court of Pennsylvania, February term, 1950, no. 40. 

John H. Bigelow, for appellant. 

C. A. Whitehouse, S. H. Torchia, Ralph H. Behney, and

T. McKeen Chidsey, Attorney General, for appellee. 

RENO, J.:

Claimant Karoly was totally disabled by anthraco-silicosis on October 26, 1946. There is no dispute about that fact, and he is not a party to this appeal. The only question here presented is: Who shall assume and pay his occupational disease compensation? That controversy is exclusively between Jeddo-Highland Coal Company, appellant, his employer, which until September 1, 1946, was a self-insurer of its own compensation risks, and State Workmen's Insurance

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Fund, appellee, which on that date became the employer's insurance carrier. The Commonwealth, against whose occupational disease fund 20 percent of the compensation was assessed, did not appeal. 1 The referee and the board found the above facts ¹ and, additionally, that claimant had been "laid off" on June 10, 1946, and that "after working one shift on December 18, 1946, he finally quit and notified his boss that he was too sick to continue working." Applying Anderson v. Schroeder

Monumental Works, 159 Pa. Superior Ct. 620, 49 A. (2d) 

631, the board held that, since claimant became afflicted on October 26, 1946, after the State fund became the carrier, it was liable upon its policy. 

The court below, relying upon Gaydosh v. Richmond 

Radiator Co., 164 Pa. Superior Ct. 154, 63 A. (2d) 502, which explained the Anderson case, reversed the board, and by its judgment imposed liability upon Jeddo-Highland because the date of the last exposure to the occupational hazard was June 10, 1946, when the employer was its own carrier. The judgment will be affirmed. 

The date upon which claimant became afflicted is not decisive in a contest between successive employers or their insurers. The employe's last exposure to the occupational hazard imposes liability and his right to compensation is complete when total disability occurs: Occupational Disease 

Act of June 21, 1939, P. L. 566, as amended, sec. 301 (g), 77 PS §1401; Gaydosh v. Richmond Radiator Co., supra, followed in Holahan v. Bergen Coal Co., 164 Pa. Superior 

1 The referee's material findings of fact, confirmed by the board, are: "5. Claimant had worked in the mines of the defendant for thirtyfive years immediately preceding June 10, 1946. During this entire period, he was continuously exposed to an anthraco-silicosis hazard. "6. On December 18, 1946, claimant reported for work with defendant. He did very little work on that day and became sick. He has not worked since that time. "10. We find as a fact that on October 26, 1946, claimant became totally disabled by anthraco-silicosis with a superimposed active pulmonary tuberculosis. He had been contracting this disease for more than five years to the date of his last employment and contracted it as a result of his employment in the anthracite mines."

1950] 

Ct. 177, 63 A. (2d) 504 (allocatur refused, id. xxv). See also Agostin v. Pittsburgh Steel Foundry Corp., 157 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 322, 43 A. (2d) 604, affirmed 354 Pa. 543, 47 

A. (2d) 680; and Silva v. Erie Forge Co., 149 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 251, 27 A. (2d) 727.

The last day on which claimant worked and his last exposure before total disability ensued was June 10, 1946.

He became totally disabled on October 26, 1946, and on that day his right to compensation was fixed and complete. Work performed after that date could not deprive him of his completed right to compensation, unless it demonstrated that he was in fact not actually totally disabled before that time. As to that the referee found upon sufficient competent evidence that on December 18, 1946, claimant "did very little work . . . and became sick." His efforts on that day, far from proving ability to work, definitely established his weakness and his total disability.

Apart from its contention that the appeal is ruled by the Anderson case, which contention we have overruled, appellant mainly relies upon claimant's averment in his claim petition that he became totally disabled on December 18, 1946, a contention it made before the referee. However, upon clearly sufficient medical testimony, the referee found that he became totally disabled on October 26, 1946. In its appeal to the board appellant did not except to that finding. 

To the contrary, it expressly adopted that date as the day when total disability occurred, and its exception raised only a question of law.2 Notwithstanding the averment in the claim petition, appellant, having failed to except to the referee's finding, is bound by it, and cannot now contend otherwise. A party who fails to except to a material finding 

2 The only exception filed by appellant in its appeal to the board from the decision of the referee follows: "Claimant (if entitled to recover at all), would be entitled to recovery against the State Workmen's Insurance Fund, which became the insurance carrier for defendant on September 1, 1946; while the tenth finding of fact of the referee is to the effect that it was not until October 26, 1946, that claimant became totally disabled by anthraco-silicosis with a superimposed active pulmonary tuberculosis."

[36 Dep. Rep. 

of fact by a referee or to his failure to find a specific fact in an appeal to the board may not question the referee's findings in the common pleas or this court: Walker v. Nu-Car 

Carriers, Inc., 164 Pa. Superior Ct. 246, 63 A. (2d) 484; 

Forsythe v. Harrison Twp., 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 433, 43

A. (2d) 366; Vadnal v. Krsul-Kutchel Coal Co., 149 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 269, 27 A. (2d) 709; McDermott v. Sun Indemnity Co., 131 Pa. Superior Ct. 60, 198 Atl. 499; Nesbit v. Vandervort & Curry, 128 Pa. Superior Ct. 58, 193 Atl. 393.

Appellant not only accepted the finding but made it the premise for its argument before the board and in the court below that October 26th was the date which imposed liability. The board erroneously accepted that view; the court below properly rejected it; and, whatever may have been its position before the referee, appellant cannot now stand upon a proposition inconsistent with the theory it advanced before the board and the court. It cannot ask us, in the face of the referee's findings, to adjudicate December 18th as the date of his last exposure and the date when total disability occurred. 

Judgment affirmed.

1950] 

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD KOSIER v. ESSEX HOTEL Workmen's compensation-Final receipt-Setting aside. 

A referee's order dismissing claimant's petition to set aside a final receipt will be reversed and a rehearing granted, where it appears that at the time the receipt was executed his compensation was overdue, that he was told at the office of the insurance carrier that it would be withheld unless he signed the receipt, that he was not working at the time, was illiterate and was told he could come back for more compensation if unable to work.

Appeal by claimant from order of Referee Alessandroni, district no. 1, dismissing petition to set aside final receipt under compensation agreement no. 5,279,532. Reversed. 

May 17, 1950. 

New Amsterdam Casualty Co., insurance carrier. 

Louis M. Paul, for claimant. 

Raymond J. Porreca, for defendant.

MURPHY, Chairman: 

Claimant was injured on July 12, 1948, while in the course of his employment. On August 6, 1948, the parties executed an open agreement in which the accident and injury were described as follows: 

"Carrying cases of beer and ginger ale from basement to bar. Strained back."

Compensation for total disability was paid from July 23, 1948, to October 21, 1948, when claimant executed a final receipt. On November 9, 1948, claimant filed a petition to set aside the final receipt alleging that he did not know he was closing the case when he signed the final receipt and that he was still wearing a brace at the time. Defendant filed an answer alleging that the final receipt was valid. A hearing was held and the referee dismissed claimant's petition, finding as a fact that claimant's disability due to the

[36 Dep. Rep. 

accident of July 12, 1948, had ceased. Claimant has taken this appeal. He has filed a petition for a rehearing, alleging that the only medical evidence presented at the hearing before the referee was by defendant's medical experts and that he now has available medical evidence which will prove conclusively that claimant suffered a traumatic injury which rendered him totally disabled. The petition was supported by the affidavit of Dr. Frank Glauser, who found that claimant's disability was caused by a traumatic sacroiliac disease due to the accident of July 12, 1948. 

Defendant filed an answer denying the material allegations of claimant's petition for rehearing and alleging that claimant's disability was not caused by the accident; that claimant has a preëxisting systemic condition which caused his disability.

Claimant testified that he was not able to read; that he signed the final receipt at the office of the insurance carrier and was informed that if he couldn't work he could come back for compensation but if he signed it he could get his six weeks' compensation. Claimant was out of work to 

January 10, 1949, and then obtained work at the Warwick Hotel at a wage similar to that when injured. His back still hurt on March 29, 1949, the date of the hearing before the referee. Claimant had difficulty in speaking English and was difficult to understand.

Defendant called Dr. Harry Hudson, who examined claimant on September 9, 1948. Claimant had complained of pain in the lower back and was wearing a brace at the time. His back showed considerable muscle tension and the picture was one of an old osteo-arthritic back. Claimant was X-rayed by Dr. Hutton and the condition found was referable to a systemic disease liable to acute exacerbation and remission at times.

Dr. John P. Black, was called by defendant. He had examined claimant on October 4, 1948, and found that claimant suffered from arthritis of the spine which caused his pain. This condition was unrelated to the accident of July 

12, 1948. Claimant suffered pain in the lower back at the time of the examination.

1950] 

The referee made no findings as to whether or not the final receipt was procured by mistake of fact or improper conduct of defendant. He merely dismissed claimant because claimant had failed to show that his disability was caused by the accident. 

We have carefully reviewed the testimony and are of the opinion that on the present state of the record claimant was entitled to have the final receipt set aside. His compensation was overdue. He was told it would be withheld unless he signed the final receipt. He was not working when he signed the final receipt. He was illiterate and was told he could "come back" for more compensation if unable to work. Defendant offered no evidence to contradict claimant's testimony: Kessler v. North Side Packing Co., 122 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 565.

It is also undisputed that claimant suffered from a preexisting back condition which we believe rendered him more susceptible to injury. No medical evidence was offered by claimant. It is now available to show that his present disability is due to the accident. We feel he should be given an opportunity to establish this assertion. We also believe further medical evidence, from an impartial source, should be presented.

We therefore vacate the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order. The record is remanded to the referee for further hearing and determination. The referee is also directed to request the appointment, by the board, of an impartial orthopedic expert to examine claimant and report his findings subject to cross-examination by the parties. The referee shall then make such findings of fact, conclusions of law and order consistent with this opinion as is warranted by the evidence.

Claimant's appeal is sustained and the rehearing granted.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD WESNER v. PHILADELPHIA AND READING COAL AND IRON COMPANY Workmen's compensation—Occupational disease—Termination of employment prior to employer's acceptance of Occupational 

Disease Act-Employer's liability Claim by widow. 

Where an employe contracted anthraco-silicosis in defendant's employ but terminated his employment with defendant prior to the acceptance by the latter of the provisions of the Pennsylvania Occupational Disease Act, he was not entitled to the benefits of the act where he became totally disabled after defendant had accepted the act, and in such case where he died as a result of the disease, his widow was likewise not entitled to compensation.

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Carmalt, specially presiding in the fifth district, claim petition no. 116,471, accident no. 4,798,202. Affirmed. May 17, 1950. 

T. L. Kennedy, Jr., for claimant.

Penrose Hertzler and C. E. Bottiger, for defendant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for Commonwealth.

KNOLL, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by claimant from the order of disallowance of Referee Gladstone A. Carmalt, specially presiding in the fifth compensation district.

A study of the record discloses that William Wesner, claimant's decedent, on July 10, 1946, filed a petition pursuant to the provisions of the Pennsylvania Occupational Disease Act, averring, inter alia, that he became totally disabled as a result of anthraco-silicosis on July 8, 1946. 

The matter was duly referred to the late Referee L. E. 

Enterline, before whom testimony was taken.

The testimony disclosed that claimant was last employed by the Philadelphia and Reading Coal and Iron Company on June 21, 1946, at which time he was totally disabled as a

1950] 

 result of anthraco-silicosis, and that decedent's employer had rejected the provisions of the Pennsylvania Occupational Disease Act on December 31, 1944, and had not accepted the provisions of the act until June 30, 1946. The referee, thereupon, concluded that claimant (decedent) had no standing under the provisions of the Pennsylvania Occupational Disease Act and dismissed the claim petition. 

No appeal was taken from the ruling of the referee. 

On May 5, 1949, claimant, as the widow of William Wesner, filed a claim petition pursuant to the provisions of the Pennsylvania Occupational Disease Act, averring, inter alia, that decedent, her husband, William Wesner, died on 

October 5, 1948, as a result of anthraco-silicosis contracted as a result of his employment with defendant herein. To this petition responsive answers were filed by the State Workmen's Insurance Board, custodian of the occupational disease fund, the State workmen's insurance fund, and defendant employer, denying the allegations of the petition. 

The testimony in this case discloses that decedent, William Wesner, died on October 5, 1948, solely as a result of anthraco-silicosis contracted after an exposure of five or more years in and about the anthracite mines; that the average weekly wage of decedent was $54.99; and that decedent had been employed in and about the anthracite mines for a period of 33 years.

The referee concluded that, since decedent had been employed at a time when defendant employer had rejected the provisions of the Pennsylvania Occupational Disease Act, claimant, as his widow, had no standing under the act and dismissed the claim petition. From this ruling claimant has appealed.

We have made a careful study of the record and are of the opinion that the ruling of the referee should be affirmed. 

The testimony in this case discloses that claimant was last employed by the coal company on June 21, 1946, at which time he was compelled to terminate his employment by reason of his being totally disabled due to anthraco-silicosis; and that the sole cause of death on October 5, 1948, was anthraco-silicosis. However, the testimony further dis-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

closes that defendant employer had rejected the provisions of the Pennsylvania Occupational Disease Act on December 31, 1944, and had not accepted the provisions of the act until June 30, 1946. It appears, therefore, that, during the period of decedent's employment with the coal company, the company had rejected the provisions of the Pennsylvania Occupational Disease Act during the entire period of his employment.

It is established that where an employe has terminated his employment prior to the acceptance by his employer of the provisions of the Pennsylvania Occupational Disease Act, he (employe) is not entitled to the benefits of the act even though he becomes totally disabled after the employer has accepted the act, as the result of an occupational disease contracted during his employment: Kozak v. Lehigh Valley 

Coal Co. et al., 164 Pa. Superior Ct. 564. Since the employe was not entitled to the benefits of the act, we are of the opinion that, in a situation such as we have in the case at issue, the widow's rights rise no higher than that of her deceased husband and that, by reason thereof, she likewise is not entitled to the benefits of the act. See Polk v. Western 

Bedding Co. et al., 145 Pa. Superior Ct. 142; Stanley Kalish v. Lehigh Navigation Coal Co., Court of Common Pleas of 

Schuylkill County, January term, 1948, no. 158. We are, therefore, of the opinion that the referee properly concluded that he was without jurisdiction to award compensation in this case.

We, therefore affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of disallowance. 

The appeal of claimant is dismissed.

1950] 

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD SMITH v. PERKASIE BOROUGH Workmen's compensation—Employe-Volunteer fireman-Injury in performance of duties - Collection of waste paper to purchase fire equipment.

A referee's award will be set aside where claimant, a member of a volunteer fire company in a borough was injured when he fell from a truck while engaged in collecting waste paper throughout the borough to be sold for the purpose of buying and maintaining the equipment of the fire company, where it appeared that at the time the borough council had not authorized the fire company to make the paper collections although it had knowledge thereof and such collections had been made for several years prior to the date of claimant's injury. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Alessandroni, district no. 1, claim petition no. 115,871, accident no. 

5,316,401. Reversed. May 3, 1950. 

Maryland Casualty Company, insurance carrier. 

J. Lawrence Grim, for claimant. 

Louis Wagner, for defendant.

KNOLL, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by defendant from an award of compensation payments.

The facts are not in dispute. On October 19, 1948, claimant was a member of a volunteer fire company in Perkasie Borough and was engaged in collecting waste paper throughout the borough to be sold for the purpose of buying and maintaining the equipment of the fire company. While so engaged, he fell from a truck and suffered head injuries which disabled him from October 19, 1948, to February 1, 1949, and required hospital and medical treatments.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

The sole issue is whether claimant, as a volunteer fireman, was an employe of the borough within the provisions of the section of The Workmen's Compensation Act of June 

21, 1939, P. L. 566, as amended by the Act of July 2, 1941, P. L. 222, relating to volunteer firemen, which declared firemen to be employes: "... entitled to receive compensation in case of injuries received while actually going to or returning from any fire which the fire companies or fire department of which they are members shall have attended or while performing any other duties of such companies or fire department authorized by such cities, boroughs, incorporated towns and townships." 

We may take notice that volunteer fire companies promote numerous varied projects, ranging from the collection of waste paper to dances, ice cream socials and the sale of raffle tickets to raise funds for company purposes. However, it is not necessary for the disposition of this case, to discuss whether or not the legislature contemplated that these various activities should be regarded as duties of fire companies which, if authorized by a municipality, would place a participating fireman within the provisions of the act as an employe of the municipality. 

In the instant case, the Perkasie Borough Council had not authorized the volunteer fire company to collect waste paper, although collections had been made for some years prior to 1948. Two resolutions of the council were passed, dated May 10, 1949, and June 6, 1949, purportedly giving authority to the fire company for the collection of waste paper. It is our opinion that these resolutions, however valid as to subsequent collection, could not validate previous unauthorized activities of the fire company and could not, therefore, bring claimant within the act. We believe that official action by the borough authorities was required before the alleged accident and that such authority may not be implied from knowledge of an activity or from past accustomed activities. 

Accordingly, the referee's first, fourth, and fifth findings of fact, his conclusions of law and order of award are hereby set aside and we substitute, in lieu thereof, the following

1950] 

  

1. That on October 19, 1948, claimant was a member of the Perkasie Borough Volunteer Fire Company. 

4. That on October 19, 1948, claimant was engaged in collecting waste paper throughout Perkasie Borough for the purpose of reducing fire hazards and to raise funds for the purchase of equipment by the volunteer fire company. 

While so engaged, claimant fell from a truck and suffered head injuries. 

5. That neither claimant nor the Perkasie Volunteer Fire

Company was authorized by the Perkasie Borough Council to collect waste paper or for any related activity prior to, or at the time of, the alleged accident on October 19, 1948. 

1. That the Borough of Perkasie had not authorized the members of the Perkasie Volunteer Fire Company to make collections of waste paper throughout the borough on or before October 19, 1948. 

2. That claimant on October 19, 1948, was acting as a member of the Perkasie Volunteer Fire Company and he was not an employe of Perkasie Borough within the pro visions of The Workmen's Compensation Act. 

3. That since claimant was not an employe of Perkasie 

Borough on October 19, 1948, he is not entitled to compensation payments under the provisions of The Workmen's 

Compensation Act. 

Order Claimant's claim petition no. 115,871 is hereby dismissed. 

Defendant's appeal is sustained.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD CAMPBELL v. GREEN ENGINEERING CO. 

Workmen's compensation—Injury in course of employment—Inter viewing third party in respect to employment— 

Engaging in game of badminton.

A referee's order disallowing a claim on the ground that claimant did not suffer an accident in the course of his employment will be affirmed, where it appears that he, in the company of defendant's president, went in the company car from the president's home to interview a person in regard to employment by defendant, that after the matter of employment was concluded, the three men, at the invitation of the person they were interviewing, engaged in a game of badminton, in the course of which claimant slipped and sustained an injury. 

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee McGovern, district no. 8, claim petition no. 118,146, accident no. 

5,478,358. Affirmed. June 7, 1950. 

American Guar. & Liability Ins. Co., insurance carrier. 

Norman Landy, for claimant. 

Irwin M. Ringold, for defendant.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

Claimant, Vernon G. Campbell, filed a claim petition on 

September 26, 1949, for an alleged accident on July 22, 1949.

Hearing was held before Referee McGovern on November 9, 1949. The referee entered an order of disallowance on 

December 12, 1949, and the parties were notified on December 12, 1949. Claimant took this appeal on January 4, 1950, which was heard by the board on March 29, 1950.

The sole question involved in this case is whether claimant suffered an accident within the meaning and intendment of 

The Workmen's Compensation Act. The facts are not in dispute. Claimant's case discloses that on July 22, 1949, claimant, an engineer employed by defendant, Green Engineering Company, in company with Samuel J. Green, president of defendant corporation, went in a company car from

1950] 

Green's home in Leetsdale to White Oak Heights in Allegheny County, to interview Howard Francis in regard to employment by defendant. The men arrived about 6:30 p.m. and had a lunch at the Francis home. After the matter of employment was concluded, the men, at the invitation of Francis, engaged in a game of badminton. It was during the course of the second game with Francis that claimant slipped and sustained a fracture of the right elbow. Campbell has known Francis for about 15 years and had previously visited him socially. Green testified: "Q. The badminton games played both by you and by

Mr. Campbell were after Mr. Francis had already agreed to come to work for the company on a part-time capacity. 

Is that right? "A. That's right."

Claimant was paid his full salary until September 19, 1949, when he resumed his regular work, although there is some residual disability. Defendant offered no testimony. 

An employer is not an insurer: Gausman v. Pearson, 284 

Pa. 348; Billo v. Allegheny Steel Co., 328 Pa. 101. Under section 301 of the Act of June 21, 1939, P. L. 520, the right to recover compensation is restricted to injury by accident in cases where "the employe is actually engaged in the furtherance of the business or affairs of the employer". 

A realistic view of the facts in this case compels the conclusion that the claim must be rejected and the referee's order of disallowance affirmed. At the time of the accident, claimant was not engaged in the furtherance of defendant's business or interests. The work at hand, employment of Francis, was fully concluded. When claimant engaged in the relaxing pastime of badminton, the sport was in no way necessary to the consummation of defendant's business and in no way related to it. In a sense, it represented a deviation or abandonment undertaken for his personal pleasure and enjoyment, and as such the employer assumed no responsibility for any mishap while claimant was so engaged. Although there are appellate court cases which permit an award where injury occurs during a temporary moment of leisure, we do not believe that the instant case falls in that category:

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Oldinsky v. Phila. & Reading Coal and Iron Co., 92 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 328; Dzikowska v. Superior Steel Co., 259 Pa. 

578. There was a virtual abandonment of claimant's employment at the time of the injury: Shoffer v. Lehigh Valley 

Coal Co., 290 Pa. 480. In the case of Lewis v. Capital Bakers, Inc., 114 Pa. Superior Ct. 171, the court said (p. 177): "Decedent's action clearly brought him within the excepted class of injuries occurring on the employer's premises, as his conduct showed a virtual abandonment of the course of his employment, and that, at the time of the accident, he was engaged in something wholly foreign thereto." 

Nor do we deem it significant that Green, president of defendant company, was present and a spectator at the time of the accident. There is no testimony to the effect that Green requested claimant to engage in the match with Francis. On the contrary, the invitation was extended by the latter. Claimant's participation in the game of badminton was not an involuntary act on his part, nor can we say from the years of friendship existing between claimant and Francis, that it was induced because of his employer's interests: Gibson v. Blowers, 140 Superior Ct. 216; Krehmar v. 

Oakland Beach Co., 155 Pa. Superior Ct. 430.

We hereby affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of disallowance.

The appeal of claimant is dismissed.

1950] 

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD FERGUSON v. STINSON MOTOR SALES Workmen's compensation—Accidental injury—Back sprain in performance of customary duties. 

A referee's award based on a finding of an accidental injury will be affirmed, where it appears that claimant, a person in sound health at the time of the alleged injury, was lifting one of the four corners of a crated engine weighing 350 pounds to get it over the tail gate and on to the floor of a truck when he felt a distinct twitch in his back, that he ceased work the following day because of the pain in his back and subsequently was fitted with a back brace returning to lighter work with defendant; although there was no evidence that claimant slipped, fell or twisted himself, the accident consisted in the unforeseen injury resulting from the performance of claimant's usual duties. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Foust, district no. 9, claim petition no. 115,772, accident no. 5,329,387. 

Affirmed. June 7, 1950. 

American Farmers Mutual Insurance Company, insurance carrier. 

Wm. T. Dom and D. J. Snyder, Jr., for claimant. 

Francis S. McQuilkin, for defendant.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal from an award entered by Referee 

Foust on December 7, 1949. Claimant, Wayne W. Ferguson, filed his claim petition March 4, 1949, alleging that on 

November 18, 1948, he sustained an accidental back injury while helping with a heavy lift in the course of his employment with defendant, Stinson Motor Sales. Pursuant to a responsive answer, hearings were held beginning July 1, 1949, and ending November 14, 1949.

The main issue in the case rises out of the effect to be given to the purport of the proved facts under the construc-

154.

 [36 Dep. Rep. 

tion given by the courts to the term "injury by an accident" 

in The Workmen's Compensation Act as amended by the 

Act of June 21, 1939, P. L. 520, sec. 301. There are two other points raised on appeal: Viz., the lack of a finding sufficient to support an order of suspension and the question of the sufficiency of medical opinion. But our review of the record indicates that the exceptions as to these points may be dealt with as essentially subordinate to the principal issue. With regard to suspension, it appears that a finding of indeterminate residual disability has not been made and that the order of suspension itself might be cast in more appropriate form: Holtz v. McGraw and Bindley, 161 Pa.

Superior Ct. 371, 379. We are in a position to take care of defendant's objections in these phases of the matter. It appears that a finding may be supplied on the evidence adduced showing the necessity for continuous use of a back brace and employment at lighter work indicating protracted physical disability after January 20, 1949, not however, reflected in loss of earnings. Then, wherein defendant objects to competent medical proof, it is to be noted that one of the physicians testifying inthe case, Dr. George V. Foster, referring to the accident, said: 66 I would think that this was the causative factor which occurred on October 19, 1948."

This was a sufficient expression of opinion (Pelosi v. Overbrook Tile Co., 138 Pa. Superior Ct. 30) in consonance with the pertinent rule of evidence (Elonis v. Lytle Coal Co., 134 Pa. Superior Ct. 264) and we do not feel constrained to disagree with the referee's acceptance of it as such at this point in the proceedings.

We now come to the most important mixed question of law and fact in this appeal and we have purposely reserved our review of the testimony as to what happened to point up the issue which defendant pursues most assiduously. 

There is very little disagreement as to the factual situation, but considerable misunderstanding as regards its legal significance. Claimant, at the time of alleged accidental injury, was lifting one of the four corners of a crated engine weighing about 350 pounds to get it over the tail gate and

1950] 

 on to the floor of a truck. The lift was just a little over three feet and there is nothing to show that the three other men who were helping him, including his employer, put forth any less than their share of the effort required or did anything which would have the effect of casting more strain or burden upon claimant. Furthermore, it seems established that claimant did not slip, fall or twist himself unduly as the chore was being accomplished. Nevertheless, he felt a distinct twitch in his back. This is shown by the evidence to have occurred on October 19, 1948, rather than on November 18, 1948, as alleged in the petition. Notice was given the following day and claimant laid off until November 1, 1948, when he returned to lighter work. Then on 

December 2, 1948, he was admitted to the West Penn Hospital where tests failed to confirm a suspicioned dislocation of an intervertebral disc. However, by reason of a radiating sciatic pain, claimant was fitted with a back brace and returned to lighter work with defendant, February 21, 1949. His average weekly earnings showed decreases from this point but later increased to show an excess over his prior earnings after he secured employment with the Linhart Chevrolet Company beginning May 17, 1949. He insisted that at the time of the hearings he still felt sufficient discomfort as the aftermath of his injury to prevent him from doing the heavier work required of him as an automobile mechanic. In this connection, defendant admits on appeal that it does not dispute the referee's calculations of compensation based on loss of earnings as the same appear in the award up to the point of suspension agreeing in effect that it could not contradict Dr. Foster's estimate of 50 percent disability beginning January 21, 1949. Beyond this point, Dr. C. C. Crouse diagnosed sciatic tenderness in an examination conducted June 22, 1949, but did not convert the same into terms of disability. 

On the basis of the facts reviewed having to do with the incurring of an accidental injury, defendant cites a well-known line of cases (Turek v. Damalak, 161 Pa. Superior Ct. 84; Crispin v. Leedom & Worrall Co., 341 Pa. 325;

Good v. Penna. Dept. of Property and Supplies, 346 Pa.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

151; Garver v. B. K. Elliott Co., 155 Pa. Superior Ct. 511; 

Baston v. Stoehr & Fisher, 151 Pa. Superior Ct. 618; Prior v. Sweets Steel Co., 151 Pa. Superior Ct. 99; Mancusco v. 

Mancusco, 150 Pa. Superior Ct. 22; Hoon v. S. W. I. F., 149 

Pa. Superior Ct. 236; Wilcox v. Buckeye Coal Co., 158 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 264; Adamchick v. Wyoming Valley Collieries 

Co., 332 Pa. 401, and Lacey v. Washburn & Williams, 309 

Pa. 574) and argues that in view of the principles followed in those cases the courts have now gone too far afield in construing injuries in the normal course of work as accidental in nature. But the cases relied upon do not cover the situation before us except wherein the principles announced trace the line of demarkation between compensable and noncompensable disabilities. In most of them, as claimant points out, there was a preëxisting condition aggravated by the strain of a customary task. Such is not the case in the present instance. There is other uncontradicted evidence in the record to show that prior to the date of the injury claimant had enjoyed sound physical health, and that during the late war he had served with the ordnance department, handling heavy weights as a part of his duties and had never suffered any ill effect. The only trouble he had experienced in recent years came out of an accident in his home which resulted in a sore shoulder. 

This history of uneventful physical health signalizes the element of distinction in the interpretation of the law which has been handed down to us by the courts. It is set forth in Good v. Penna. Dept. of Property and Supplies, supra, in these words: "The factor which is unexpected may be either in the circumstance causing the injury or in the nature of the injury itself: Parks v. Miller Printing Machine Co., 336 Pa. 

455, 458-9. In the first type, a fortuitous happening occurs, but the injury is the natural consequence of that happening. In the second type an ordinary event takes place, but results in an unforeseen injury, as for example, in Wolford v. Geisel M. & S. Co., 262 Pa. 454, where a healthy piano mover, while lifting a piano, suffered an abscess culminating in pneumonia."

1950] 

That pronouncement explains the action taken by the court in Buck v. Arndt, 153 Pa. Superior Ct. 293, and in our opinion delineates the course we must follow in such twilight zone cases as the one which is before us on this occasion. Claimant was sound in bodily health. He sustained a lesion from exertion to which he was accustomed in his usual line of hard work. The accident therefore consisted in the unforeseen result of a known cause.

Cases may arise in which it might seem that the principle, as we understand it, would not seem to be applied. 

But the answer in such instances must be found in the acceptable proof. In Kannewell v. Berwind-White Coal Mining 

Co., 36 Dept. Rep. 55, we had before us a case in which a claimant sustained a disability allegedly from lifting a 45pound weight. It was a regular part of his work and he was accustomed to it. Neither was there any evidence that the disability resulted from the aggravation of a preëxisting condition. The crux of the case was the proof. It was indefinite and inconsistent (Seitzinger v. Fort Pitt Brewing 

Co., 294 Pa. 253), and we therefore sustained the referee's refusal to accord it the necessary credibility. Incidentally, it is interesting to note that the same question of acceptable proof was of prime importance in the Good case, supra. 

We trust we have fully explained our understanding of the law which the courts have interpreted for our edification, particularly as it applies to the situation which confronts us in this case. Howsoever, defendant may then complain about the undue extension of compensation coverage by the courts, our answer is that we are amenable to the construction of the law which they propound. 

The findings of fact, conclusions of law, and award and bill of medical expense entered by Referee Foust, December 7, 1948, as hereinafter supplemented and reformed, are affirmed.

Finding of Fact 

9. Subsequent to May 17, 1949, claimant suffered an indeterminate degree of physical disability, not reflected in loss of earnings.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Order The portion of the award directing suspension of compensation payments is reformed to read as follows:

All payments under the award are hereby suspended as of May 17, 1949; said suspension to continue as long as claimant receives wages in a weekly amount in excess of or equivalent to, the wages received at the time of injury, viz., $50.77 per week. 

The appeal is dismissed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD HEATH v. O. H. MARTIN CONSTRUCTION CO. 

Workmen's compensation-Accidental injury-Death-Causal connection-Absence of expert medical testimony. 

A referee's award to claimant as dependent widow of a deceased employe of defendant will be affirmed, on a finding of an accidental death, where there was evidence that decedent had been in good health, that on a certain date he complained to a fellow employe that he had hurt himself when he had been shoveling in an excavation, that a doctor was called in attendance upon him the same day, that the deceased complained to him of pain in the abdomen, left leg and chest, that the doctor observed symptoms of a sprain of the left knee, and contusions of the left leg and thigh, that he obtained from the deceased a history that as he attempted to throw some dirt, severe pain struck him in the abdomen, and that death followed four days later; although the cause of death was undetermined, evidence of an accidental injury while at work, coupled with the fact that decedent had previously enjoyed excellent health, and that his disability without interruption culminated in death, was sufficient to sustain an award, even in the absence of expert testimony as to the cause of death.

1950] 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee McGovern, district no. 8, claim petition no. 115,537, accident no. 5,369,- 

758. Affirmed. June 7, 1950.

Employers Mutual Liability Ins. Co. of Wisconsin, insur ance carrier. 

Samuel J. Goldstein, for claimant. 

H. Y. Crossland, for defendant.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

Claimant, Mrs. Eleanor Heath, filed a claim petition on 

February 10, 1949, as the dependent widow of James Heath, who died on January 29, 1949. Hearings were held before 

Referee McGovern on July 20, 1949, October 19, 1949, and 

October 27, 1949. On December 14, 1949, the referee entered an award. Defendant, O. H. Martin Construction

Company, took this appeal on December 27, 1949, which was heard by the board on March 29, 1950. 

The appeal poses two questions: (1) Did decedent suffer an accidental death?, and (2) Is claimant the legal widow of decedent? In support of her affirmative burden of proof, claimant called William Roberts, a fellow employe of decedent at the time of the alleged accident on January 25, 1949. 

Roberts testified that he had known decedent for about two and a half years prior to the accident in question, and that as far as he knew Heath was always in excellent health. On 

January 25, 1949, about 4 p.m., the witness was working with an air hammer, about 50 to 75 feet distant from decedent, who was working as a laborer on a shoveling job. Roberts stated that he did not see the accident proper but shortly thereafter he saw decedent in the shanty where the men change clothes. The witness testified: "He says 

I asked him, 'What's the trouble, James?'

He says, 'While shoveling, I hurt myself down in the hole where I had been working-in the excavation where I had been working"."

[36 Dep. Rep. 

The witness assisted in taking Heath home and carried him up to his third floor quarters. He said he was present when Dr. Charles L. Mellon, who was summoned, arrived and obtained a history "that he had hurt himself while shoveling while on the job." Under cross-examination, the witness was asked about a statement given to a Mr. Williams, an investigator, on January 31, 1949, that the only comment he heard decedent make to Dr. Mellon was that "he was shoveling dirt and got a pain in his chest and in the leg". The witness admitted the statement and an additional statement that "he still had indigestion when we got him home". Roberts stated that decedent was working in an excavation hole removing mud, concrete and dirt, which was decedent's regular work.

Dr. Mellon, claimant's witness, testified that he was called in attendance upon Heath on January 25, 1949, who was complaining of excruciating pains in the abdomen, pains in the left leg and chest. The doctor observed symptoms of a sprain of the left knee and contusions of the left leg and thigh. The doctor obtained a history from the patient that "just as he attempted to throw this dirt. . . severe pain struck him in the abdomen and then of course he had pain in his limbs, too". The doctor saw the patient daily for the next three days and then had him admitted to the Columbia 

Hospital on January 28, 1949. Asked to examine the hospital records, the doctor stated that they showed the cause of death was undetermined, and he also was uncertain as to the cause. Dr. Mellon stated that, without an autopsy, it would be almost impossible to determine the exact cause of death. However, the doctor felt that the "immediate cause of death was the accident because he was well and hearty and that happened all at once".

Claimant testified that when decedent was brought home she had "to split his clothes off him" as his leg was swollen. 

She stated that decedent told her what happened, as follows: "Q. And what did he tell you as to what had happened? "A. He said that he was moving concrete and wet dirt. "Q. And what kind of dirt? "A. Dirt, clay.

1950] 

"Q. Yes. "A. And he said that in moving it he struck a piece of concrete and he went to pick up his shovel and a pain hit him in his stomach like he pulled something loose in there. "Q. Like he pulled something loose in there? "A. Yes. He felt something slip loose in his stomach and went down his leg and he fell."

Claimant stated that Heath had been previously in generally good health, had never been under medical care, and had worked steadily and continuously prior to the accident. 

Defendant called Phillip J. Howells, labor foreman, who testified that he saw decedent on January 25, 1949, at about 

3:30 or 4:00 p.m. and that he complained of chest pains. 

The foreman thought that maybe he had "some indigestion" and suggested that the employe see the plant nurse at the Wiggins Company, for whom the construction job was being done, or that he stop at a drug store or see a doctor on his way home. He saw decedent shortly after he made his way into the shanty, where he was having difficulty in removing his boots. Howells stated that he asked Heath whether he had hurt himself and "he said that he didn't know that he had". The foreman then made arrangements to take the man home. The foreman testified that claimant did general labor work and used a pick and shovel. He described decedent's work at the time, as follows: "Q. Was he doing anything unusual that day, different from what he had been doing? "A. No, just we had a cave-in of a wall-dirt wall, and he was shoveling that dirt out of the way so we could put this wall up. "Q. Now, was he down in the hole shoveling dirt up or how was he shoveling? "A. No, he was just shoveling it out to his right-throwing it out to his right. "Q. And was that any different from what he would ordinarily do? "A. No.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

"Q. What kind of worker was James Heath? "A. He was a very good worker-very good worker."

Under cross-examination the foreman testified that he never knew Heath to be in poor health. Joseph I. Forsythe, defendant's carpenter foreman, testified that he saw Heath at work "off and on" all day of January 25, 1949. He then saw him in a disabled condition in the shanty and he drove decedent home. Forsythe stated that he asked Heath on the way home whether "he had fell or bumped himself or twisted himself in any way" and "he tole me no, he didn't that he knew of; he hadn't either fell, bumped himself or twisted himself as he knew of". The witness described the work being done as "there was ground caved in from the rain. We had a wall to go in, and they were making way for the wall, from which they were shoveling that dirt, broken concrete, what have you, out of the road". 

Dr. Edward L. Waisbrot, called by defendant, testified that he examined decedent in the emergency room of the 

Columbia Hospital on January 28, 1949, and that the patient died the next morning from an undetermined cause. 

The Columbia Hospital records of decedent show a past history completely negative for accidents, injuries or illnesses.

Dr. Waisbrot's impression noted upon the hospital records is "hypertensive cardiovascular disease with possible aneurysm of aorta or thrombus left iliac artery". The certificate of death states that the immediate cause of death was "undetermined". No autopsy was performed as claimant refused permission.

It is clear that the exact cause of the death is not disclosed by medical opinion. It is likewise certain that decedent was clearing away mud, dirt and concrete when he was stricken at work. While there was no testimony offered, or possibly available, as to what actually occurred, we have Dr. Mellon's testimony that decedent had an injured knee and a laceration of the left leg and thigh. These objective findings bespeak an accident. Our conclusion is buttressed by the testimony not only of claimant, but of that of several witnesses for defendant, that decedent previously enjoyed excellent health and was a good steady worker. Dr. Mellon

1950] 

 deemed decedent's prior health significant and we likewise consider it important.

Counsel for the parties have submitted able briefs. We have reviewed the authorities cited. Our appellate courts have held in many cases that where injury is followed by a result that is so directly and immediately or naturally and probably attributable to an injury, an award does not necessarily depend upon expert testimony: Mohr v. DeSimone & Sayers, 110 Pa. Superior Ct. 44. What the court said in the Mohr case applies effectively in the instant case. At page 49 the court said: "It has frequently been held that where the disability complained of is the natural and probable result of the injuries, the fact-finding body may be permitted to so find, even in the entire absence of expert opinion: Utzman v. 

Penna. Rubber Co. et al. 96 Pa. Superior Ct. 250; Paul et al. v. Atlantic Ref. Co. 304 Pa. 360, 156 A. 94.”

Here, some unusual incident at work started a series of events, which, without interruption, culminated in the death. 

It is our considered opinion that the death following the shoveling incident at an early date was more than merely a singular coincidence. In the case of Banks v. Brazee, 165 

Pa. Superior Ct. 268, the court said (p. 271): ". . . and it now must be accepted as settled law that disability of a normal healthy workman, with no physical weakness other than is common to all men, may be compensable, though the result of muscular strain or internal lesion in the performance of ordinary manual labor usual to the employee's work." 

See Rovere v. Interstate Cemetery Co., 164 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 233, 237. An accident may cause violence internally as well as produce external manifestations, readily visible upon examination. It should also be kept in mind that compensation legislation should be liberally construed: Roschak v. 

Vulcan Iron Works, 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 227; McGarvey v. 

Butler Consolidated Coal Co., 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 353.

Under all the circumstances, we believe decedent suffered a compensable accident.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Was claimant the dependent widow of decedent? Claimant relies upon a common-law marriage. Her claim petition avers that she and decedent entered into a common-law marriage on February 1, 1946, at Youngstown, Ohio. At the hearing claimant amended the petition to fix the marriage date as February 1, 1947, at Dayton, Ohio. There is cumulative and corroborative testimony which establishes that the parties lived together as husband and wife and were known as such. An insurance policy carried by decedent named Eleanor Heath as the beneficiary. While there are some inconsistencies and contradictions in claimant's testimony, her over-all testimony is credible and was accepted by the referee. We are of the same opinion. 

We hereby affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and award. 

The appeal of defendant is dismissed.

1950] 

 COURT OF COMMON PLEAS OF FRANKLIN COUNTY CRIDER v. HOCK Workmen's compensation-Subrogation-Employer's insurance carrier 

-Notifying defendant of claim to subrogation-Necessity for notifying employe-Right of defendant to pay judgment into court for interpleader. 

1. A workmen's compensation insurance carrier is, under section 

319 of The Workmen's Compensation Act of June 2, 1915, P. L. 736, subrogated to the rights of an injured employe to the extent of the compensation paid. 

2. A workmen's compensation insurance carrier need not notify an injured employe that it intends to assert its right of subrogation. 

3. Where a workmen's compensation insurance carrier has notified the wrongdoer of its intention to enforce its right of subrogation, the wrongdoer is entitled to pay the amount of the judgment recovered against it into court for the carrier and the injured employe to interplead. 

4. The right of a wrongdoer against whom a judgment has been entered at the suit of an injured employe to be released from further liability upon payment of the costs and of the amount of the judgment into court for the carrier and the employe to interplead, cannot be defeated by the carrier's failure to notify the employe of its intention to assert the claim to subrogation and the employe's contention that he would not have accepted the amount of the verdict had he been aware that such a claim would be made. 

In the Court of Common Pleas of Franklin County, April term, 1948, no. 109. February 3, 1950. 

Edwin D. Strite, for plaintiff. 

John McD. Sharpe, for defendant.

WINGERD, P. J.: 

Plaintiff sued defendant in trespass for personal injuries caused by defendant, in that defendant's automobile, while he was driving, ran into plaintiff. Plaintiff is a borough policeman of the Borough of Chambersburg, Franklin

County, Pa. The accident occurred while plaintiff was directing traffic in his capacity as a borough policeman. The

[36 Dep. Rep. 

borough carried compensation insurance with the Employers Liability Assurance Corp., Ltd., and plaintiff was paid compensation insurance in the amount of $226.41, being $109.25 medical and hospital expenses and $117.16 weekly compensation payments. Plaintiff recovered from defendant in a trial before a jury the sum of $525; for which judgment is entered. The Employers Liability Assurance Corp., Ltd., notified defendant by letter of January 26, 1948, that it intended to enforce its right of subrogation as provided by section 319 of The Workmen's Compensation Act of Penn sylvania. Judgment was entered on the verdict, on December 10, 1948. Defendant's insurance carrier issued a check, on December 29, 1948, for the amount of the judgment, payable to plaintiff and the Employers Liability Assurance Corp., Ltd. The check was refused by plaintiff's counsel on the ground that it bore the name of the Employers Liability Assurance Corp., Ltd., as one of the payees. Defendant's insurance carrier then paid the costs of record and presented its petition to the court for a rule to show cause why the amount of the judgment, $525, should not be paid into court and an order thereupon made by the court directing that the prothonotary mark the judgment satisfied. An answer was filed by plaintiff denying the right of the Employers Liability Assurance Corp., Ltd., to subrogation, on the ground that it had never notified plaintiff of its intention to claim subrogation and, for that reason, plaintiff had accepted, what he considers, an inadequate verdict, whereas if he had known of the intention of the insurance company to insist on its right of subrogation he would have moved for a new trial on the ground of inadequacy of the verdict, and insisting that the whole amount of the verdict be paid to him as plaintiff.

Article VI, sec. 319 of The Workmen's Compensation Act of June 2, 1915, P. L. 736, 77 PS §671, as last amended by the 

Act of May 18, 1945, P. L. 671, provides as follows: "Where the compensable injury is caused in whole or in part by the act or omission of a third party, the employer shall be subrogated to the right of the employee, his personal representative, his estate or his dependents, against

1950] 

 such third party for the balance of any sum recovered in litigation, or paid in compromise settlement, after subtraction of reasonable attorney's fees and other proper disbursements, but only to the extent of the compensation payable under this Article by the employer. Any recovery against such third person in excess of the compensation theretofore paid by the employer shall be paid forthwith to the employee or to the dependents, and shall be treated as an advance payment by the employer on account of any future installments of compensation."

If the Employers Liability Assurance Corp., Ltd., repre senting the employer is entitled to subrogation, that is, to share in the judgment entered against defendant in favor of plaintiff, then defendant not being informed of what expenses, such as attorney's fees and etc., may properly be deducted and what amount in the final analysis will be payable to the Employers Liability Assurance Corp., Ltd., should be allowed to pay the amount of the judgment into court so that the judgment against him can be settled and the record cleared. This seems to be the proper procedure under such circumstances: Smith v. Yellow Cab Co., 288 

Pa. 85, 90; Cooper et ux. v. Gettier, 27 D. & C. 429, 431.

The question, therefore, before the court is whether the insurance carrier of the employer is subrogated to the judgment obtained in the suit by the employe against the third person who caused the compensable injury by his act or omission. The rights of the employer and its insurance carrier as to subrogation in compensation cases are identical: Lengle et al. v. North Lebanon Township, 274 Pa. 51, 54. There is no doubt that the employer's insurance carrier is subrogated to the right of the employe and may permit the employe to sue and recover a judgment against the third person and then be subrogated to that judgment: Mayhugh v. Somerset Telephone Co., 265 Pa. 496; Cooper et ux. v.

Gettier, supra. A consideration of section 319 of the Workmen's Compensation Law brings one to the conclusion that not only is the employer given the right of subrogation "but is 'subrogated' to the extent of the compensation paid: " Smith v.
