Yellow Cab Co., supra. We must next consider the contention of plaintiff that

[36 Dep. Rep. 

the insurance carrier of the employer has waived or lost its right to be subrogated to the employe's right in this case because it at no time, either before or during the trial or prior to plaintiff's reduction of the verdict to judgment, gave any notice either oral or written to plaintiff or his counsel that it had asserted or intended to assert any right of subrogation, and further that it never has and does not now appear as a party to this proceeding. 

In the instant case the insurance carrier gave written notice to defendant, the third party, by whose act or omission the compensable injury to the employe was negligently caused, that it claimed subrogation to the extent of the payments made by it to the employe. Under these circumstances defendant was put on notice that any payments, made by him either in settlement or in payment of a judgment obtained against him, which did not recognize the claim of the insurance carrier, would be made at his own risk. In fact if defendant made a payment after having knowledge of the facts he might very well be subjected to two recoveries as the result of his neglect in not fully recognizing the subrogation rights of the insurance carrier. It is true that where an employer claims to be subrogated he must show that defendant, the wrongdoer, had knowledge or notice of the employer's right to subrogation. An explicit written notice to defendant, which was received by him, certainly was sufficient to put him on notice and to make him liable to the employer, if he failed to protect the employer's claim when settling with employe or paying the judgment recovered by the employe against him. Such a notice seems all that is necessary to preserve the employer's right. It is not necessary for the employer to intervene in the action of the employe against a third person unless it wishes to: 

Mayhugh v. Somerset Telephone Co., supra.; Wilson v.

Pittsburgh B. & I. Works, 85 Pa. Superior Ct. 537, 542. 

There was no obligation on the insurance carrier to give notice to the employe that it did not intend to waive its right to subrogation and intended to assert that right and participate in any verdict recovered by the employe. “Under the act, the employer or insurance company has the right to be subrogated in any verdict recovered for the amount

1950] 

 to be paid by virtue of the compensation agreement or order of the board": Lengle et al. v. North Lebanon Township, supra. The right of subrogation having been given by statute, plaintiff, when he instituted his action against defendant in the instant case, knew or should have known that his employer (or its insurance carrier) was entitled to subrogation in any verdict he recovered. As was stated in Wilson v. 

Pittsburgh B. & I. Works, 85 Pa. Superior Ct. 537, supra, "All parties, including the attorney instituting the action, are aware of the respective interests of employee and employer, and the power of the court to protect those interests if occasion arises". Anything which the employe decided to do in reference to his verdict against defendant was done with full knowledge of the rights of the employer and its insurance carrier and he cannot claim now that he would have acted differently if he had had notice from the employer that it intended to enforce its right of subrogation. No notice was necessary as he was bound to know of the employer's rights and was given no reason to believe that it intended in any way to waive them.

Defendant (the wrongdoer) having had notice of the employer's intention to enforce its right of subrogation must in some way protect himself in reference thereto. He can protect himself in a number of ways, one of which is that he may ask leave to pay the amount of the judgment recovered against him into the court and "for the claimants thereto to interplead": Smith v. Yellow Cab Co., supra.

This is what he has, in effect, done and we see no reason why his request should be denied. Furthermore, when the amount of the judgment is paid into the court it should be satisfied as the costs have been paid.

Now, February 3, 1950, rule is made absolute, the amount of judgment no. 109, April term, 1948, in the Court of Common Pleas of Franklin County, Pa., is allowed to be paid into court; upon such payment into court the prothonotary is directed to mark the judgment satisfied upon payment of costs, and a rule is directed to be issued forthwith upon D. 

Russell Crider, and the Employers Liability Assurance 

Corp., Ltd., claimants of the proceeds of said judgment, to interplead within 20 days from service of this rule.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD LOESCH v. DAWN DONUT COMPANY Workmen's compensation-Death-Leukemia following accidental injury-Causal connection-Evidence-Impartial witness— 

Opinion based on misapprehension of facts. 

A referee's order disallowing compensation to claimant widow will be reversed and an award entered where it appears that decedent, her husband, cut his hand while cleaning a mixing bowl in the course of his employment, subsequent to which he developed leukemia, as the result of which disease he died, where it appears that decedent was in good health on the day of his injury, that he did not work from the date thereof until his death, approximately three months later, and that there was medical testimony to the effect that trauma and infection resulting from his injury caused the leukemia which in turn caused his death; notwithstanding the fact that an impartial medical specialist appointed by the board testified that there was no causal connection between decedent's accident and his subsequent death, especially where it appeared that the opinion of the impartial witness was not based upon facts and testimony as agreed to or as uncontradicted in the record. 

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Sheldrake, district no. 1, claim petition no. 105,089, accident no. 

4,722,648. Reversed. May 17, 1950. 

Liberty Mutual Insurance Co., insurance carrier. 

Maurice S. Levy, for claimant. 

George D. Sheehan, for defendant.

MURPHY, Chairman: 

This is an appeal by claimant from an order of disallowance.

Claimant, dependent widow of John B. Loesch, filed her petition, in her own behalf and on behalf of minor children, on May 1, 1946, in which she alleged that as a result of cutting his right hand while cleaning a mixing bowl in the course of his employment on August 31, 1945, defendant developed leukemia, as a result of which he died on November 19, 1945. Defendant's answer filed on May 15, 1946, denied that decedent suffered an accidental injury in the course of his employment which caused or contributed in.

1950] 

 any way to his death. After hearings, the referee found that claimant had not proven that the leukemia of which decedent had died was related to, caused by or aggravated by any accidental injury suffered by him in the course of his employment and, in accordance with such findings, dismissed claimant's petition. This appeal followed. 

The record in this case shows that decedent was apparently well and able to work at his regular occupation as a baker until August 31, 1945, on which date he cut his right hand on a mixing bowl; that he treated himself for a period during which time his right hand became swollen and infected, with red streaks going up his right arm; that, thereafter, he was treated by a local physician until September 21, 1945, when he was admitted to the hospital where he died on November 19, 1945 from leukemia. The question for determination in this appeal is whether the finding of the referee that "there was presented no proof that the leukemia from which claimant suffered and died, was in any way related to, caused by, or aggravated by any alleged accidental injury suffered by him in the course of his employment" is correct.

As her medical witness, claimant called Dr. Max Schumann, an instructor and specialist in internal medicine who taught at Temple University Medical School. Upon being given a hypothetical question containing all of the facts agreed to by the parties, this witness stated that, in his opinion, based upon the facts so given him and an examination of the hospital records, the trauma and infection resulting from decedent's accidental injury caused the leukemia which, in turn caused decedent's death.

As its first medical witness, defendant produced Dr. Maurice J. Ward, a general practitioner who had seen decedent for the first time on September 20, 1945; had had him admitted to the hospital the following day and had treated him there until the time of his death. This witness testified that "from the actual injury I found no connection" and that, at the time of his examination and treatment of decedent, he "found no infection". In our opinion, however, the force of this witnesses' testimony is lost by his answers to questions under cross-examination by claimant's counsel. While testifying that he found no infection, hospital records made

[36 Dep. Rep. 

99 and produced by him show, "chief complaint, infected hand, right, . . When asked to explain the seeming inconsistency, the witness stated that was merely what decedent had told him; that the witness did not see an infected hand but a lacerated hand. Further, the witness admitted that he did not know whether infection would cause leukemia and that the cause of leukemia was unknown. Thus, insofar as this medical witness is concerned, the record shows a witness who, despite the fact that he does not know the cause of leukemia and does not know whether infection will cause leukemia states as his opinion that there is no causal connection between the leukemia of which decedent admittedly died and the injury he received and whose testimony that decedent suffered from no infection at the time the witness examined and treated him is denied by the witness's own records.

As its medical expert, defendant called Dr. H. W. Jones, a specialist in internal medicine, who had studied leukemia for a period of approximately 15 years. This witness testified that, in his opinion, there is no causal relation between infection and leukemia. Under cross-examination this witness however, admitted that medical books and various reports of the American Medical Association did cite many cases where leukemia followed trauma and was caused by it. However, the witness did not agree with any of these authorities, stating: "There is no evidence-I don't care what your books here say. It doesn't make any difference to me."

The witness further admitted that the exact cause of leukemia was unknown and there was one school of medicine, with which he disagreed, which held that trauma would either cause or aggravate leukemia.

The final medical witness was Dr. Rufus S. Reeves, who had been appointed by us as an impartial specialist in internal medicine. This witness testified that, in his opinion, "it is more than suggestive that the small laceration was merely incidental and had no definite bearing in this case of the acute lymphatic leukemia".

While, normally the opinion of the impartial medical witness appointed by us is carefully considered and given its proper value in reaching a determination, a review of Dr.

1950] 

Reeves' testimony reveals certain facts which make it impossible for us to attach to his testimony and opinion the weight usually attached to same. 

In the first place, Dr. Reeves testified that the fact that decedent returned to work for a few days subsequent to treatment received by him, shows that the infection had subsided. There is no testimony in the record to the effect that decedent returned to work any time after the accident and, as a matter of fact, the record shows that decedent did not work from the date of the accident until his death approximately three months later.

Further, while the statement of facts agreed to by counsel at the hearing held on March 29, 1948, set out that, at the time of the accident and prior thereto, decedent was in "apparent good health", the impartial witness refused to accept this as a fact. It is the duty of an impartial medical witness, in reaching an opinion, to accept such facts and testimony as are agreed to or as are uncontradicted and his failure or refusal so to do weakened the value which his opinion otherwise might have.

The opinion of the impartial witness to the effect that there was no causal connection between decedent's accident and subsequent death, is apparently based upon his thought that the infection from which decedent suffered as a result of his accident cleared up. However, a careful review of the record does not disclose any testimony from which it could properly be inferred that the infection suffered by decedent did clear up as a result of the sulfa which was administered. 

Under cross-examination, the impartial witness stated that leukemia is cancer of the blood, wherein white cells multiply and that, in infection, the same process results. 

He further stated that, in his opinion, decedent had a latent condition at the time of the accident and that decedent's condition increased after the accident but he refused to answer a direct question whether infection would activate the latent condition from which, in his opinion, decedent suffered prior to the accident.

Finally the impartial witness admitted that he, like defendant's medical expert, Dr. H. W. Jones, was of the school of thought in medicine which believed that no trauma will cause, aggravate or accelerate a condition of leukemia. As

[36 Dep. Rep. 

has been stated by counsel for claimant in his brief, the testimony of an impartial physician is not conclusive and the weight to which it is entitled is to be determined by the finders of fact: Wallace v. Allen, 115 Pa. Superior Ct. 347; 

Ciello v. Kennedy Co., 131 Pa. Superior Ct. 492.

Digesting the testimony as outlined above we reach the following conclusions: 

Aside from Dr. Maurice J. Ward, who frankly admitted that he did not know whether infection will cause leukemia, the opinions given by the various medical witnesses as to causal connection between decedent's injury and death depends upon whether or not they are members of the school of medicine which believes that no trauma, regardless of its extent or severity, will cause, aggravate or accelerate a condition of leukemia. Both Dr. H. W. Jones, defendant's medical expert and Dr. Rufus S. Reeves, the impartial witness are members of that school of thought in medicine. Claimant's medical witness, Dr. Max Schumann, on the other hand, is of the school of thought that trauma can cause, aggravate or accelerate a condition of leukemia. 

Dr. Jones, while of the opinion that trauma cannot cause or aggravate leukemia, admitted that medical science does not know the exact cause of leukemia and that there were certain histories cited by the American Medical Association, in which trauma was believed to have been the origin of leukemia. The testimony of the impartial witness, Dr. 

Reeves, followed the same line as that of Dr. Jones and for the same reason—that he was of the opinion that in no case can there be a connection between injury and leukemia. 

Acceptance of the dogmatic opinion of the school of thought in medicine of which both Dr. Jones and Dr. Reeves are members would result, of necessity, in our holding that in no case where leukemia is involved can compensation be paid. In our opinion, such is not a fact. Leukemia is a disease of the blood evidenced by a great increase in the number of leukocytes in the blood. Leukopenia is also a disease of the blood which is evidenced by a decrease of leukocytes in the blood. In Lusk v. Monongahela City Water Co., 164 

Pa. Superior Ct. 354, compensation was allowed where it was determined that the leukopenia of which decedent died was aggravated and accelerated by the trauma. Both leu-

1950] 

 kemia and leukopenia are diseases of the blood and the mere fact that leukemia causes a great increase in the numbers of leukocytes while leukopenia causes a great decrease in leukocytes is no valid reason why leukopenia caused or aggravated by trauma should be considered compensable while leukemia caused or aggravated by trauma should not be considered compensable.

In our opinion the negative testimony of Dr. Reeves and 

Dr. Jones cannot be considered as having more weight than the positive testimony of Dr. Schumann and the referee was in error in adopting their opinion, rather than that of Dr. 

Schumann, as the basis for his findings of fact, conclusions of law and order.

We, therefore, strike out the referee's second and fourth findings of fact and, in lieu thereof, substitute the following 

2. That, on August 31, 1945, while decedent was cleaning a mixing bowl, in the course of his employment with defendant, he suffered a cut of the right hand. 

4. That, as a result of the accidental injury suffered by decedent, infection developed which caused leukemia from which decedent died on November 19, 1945.

All other findings of fact are affirmed. 

We strike out the referee's conclusions of law, and in lieu thereof, substitute the following

 1. That article III of The Workmen's Compensation Act applies to the contract of employment between the parties. 

2. Since decedent met his death as a result of the accidental injuries sustained by him in the course of his employment, claimant, dependent widow of decedent, and the minor children of decedent are entitled to recover compensation in accordance with the terms of The Workmen's Compensation Act.

We strike out the referee's order of dismissal and in lieu thereof, substitute the following Award 1. The board, accordingly, orders, adjudges and decrees that defendant, Dawn Donut Co., and/or its insurance car-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

rier, Liberty Mutual Insurance Co., shall pay to claimant, Theresa Loesch, dependent widow of John B. Loesch, on her own behalf and on behalf of the minor children, Rita Frances 

Loesch, Marie Magdalene Loesch and Gerald R. Loesch, 

662% percent of $70 per week, or $20 per week (the maximum under the Act of 1945) from November 19, 1945 until 

April 24, 1946, a period of 22 2/7th weeks, or the sum of $445.72, on which date the said minor child, Rita Frances 

Loesch, shall have become 16 years of age. 

2. Commencing on April 24, 1946, and continuing until 

July 11, 1949, on which date the minor child Marie Magdalene Loesch, shall have become 16 years of age, a period of 166 5/7 weeks, defendant, Dawn Donut Co. and/or its insurance carrier, Liberty Mutual Insurance Co. shall pay to claimant, Theresa Loesch, dependent widow, on her own behalf and on behalf of the minor children, Marie Magdalene Loesch and Gerald R. Loesch, 6212 percent of $70 per week, or $18 per week (the maximum provided for under the provisions of the Act of 1945) in the total sum of $3,- 002.30. 

3. Commencing on July 11, 1949, and continuing thereafter until August 29, 1951, a period of 111 weeks, defendant, Dawn Donut Co. and/or its insurance carrier Liberty

Mutual Insurance Co. shall pay to claimant, Theresa Loesch, dependent widow of John B. Loesch on her own behalf and on behalf of the minor child, Gerald R. Loesch, 53 percent of $70 per week, or the sum of $15 per week (the maximum provided for under the Act of 1945) in the total sum of $1,665. 

4. Thereafter, from August 29, 1951, until July 6, 1952, a period of 44 3/7 weeks on which date the minor child, Gerald R. Loesch, shall become 16 years of age, defendant, Dawn Donut Co. and/or its insurance carrier, Liberty

Mutual Insurance Co. shall pay to claimant, Theresa 

Loesch, on behalf of the minor child, Gerald R. Loesch, 172 percent of $70 per week, or the sum of $4.50 per week, a total sum of $199.82.

The above award shall bear interest on deferred payments in accordance with section 410 of the act. Claimant's appeal is allowed. Reversed.

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD USNER v. CITY OF COATESVILLE Workmen's compensation—Claim petition—Limitations—Petition for further medical treatment considered as claim petition. 

A referee's order dismissing a claim petition because it was not filed within the statutory period will be reversed, where it appears that claimant did file a petition for further medical treatment within a year from the date of his accident and defendant in its answer to claimant's petition mistakenly asserted that compensation was being paid; under the circumstances claimant's petition for further medical treatment will be treated as a claim petition. 

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Graybill, district no. 4, claim petition no. 116,626, accident no. 

5,177,831. Appeal by claimant from order of Referee Graybill, district no. 4, dismissing petition for further medical treatment under compensation agreement no. 5,177,831. 

Reversed. June 7, 1950. 

Penna. Manufacturers Assn. Casualty Ins. Co., insurance carrier. 

Louis P. Herley, for claimant. 

Paul R. Ferguson and H. M. Engell, for defendant.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

These cases are companion cases before us upon an appeal by Leo J. Usner, claimant, from two orders of disallowance by the referee, one on a claim petition, the other on a petition for further medical treatment. 

The record, although brief, is entangled. On February 4, 1948, claimant filed a petition for further medical treatment, alleging that he was injured on October 2, 1947, while fighting a fire during the course of his employment with the 

City of Coatesville, defendant. The latter filed an answer, admitting the injury on the date alleged, stating that claim-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

ant was being paid compensation, but denying that claimant sustained a fracture of the cervical vertebra. Accepting the verity of the undenied portion of the pleadings, we referred the record to a referee for testimony, and stated, in our opinion, that claimant was being paid compensation. 

Subsequently it was discovered by counsel for claimant that no compensation had been paid claimant, no agreement had been entered into and no claim petition filed. Counsel then promptly filed a claim petition on November 17, 1948, but more than one year after the accident. 

Referee Graybill dismissed the claim petition because it was not filed within the statutory period, and dismissed the further medical petition on the ground that it had been brought improperly. No hearing was held on the merits of either petition.

We conclude that a grievous error has been made in failing to hear claimant on the merits of his cases. 

The purpose of the statute of limitations is to prevent the filing of stale claims. This certainly could not be conceived as such. To the contrary, it was very much alive, for claimant not only filed his petition for further medical treatment within the one-year period, but the referee even rendered a recommendation to us within that period.

The tangled situation here results directly from defendant's misstating the facts in its answer to claimant's petition for further medical treatment, as to payment of compensation. It comes with poor grace for this defendant to now plead the statute of limitations under these circumstances. 

We have, in the past, considered petitions for further medical treatment as claim petitions and we so do here. But even had we not done so, we believe defendant's conduct and misrepresentations are such as to toll the statute. 

In any event these cases should be heard on their merits and we vacate all the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and orders in both cases and remand them to the referee for testimony and disposition in accordance with the law and equity.

The referee shall give these cases priority over all other cases pending before him.

1950] 

 COMMON PLEAS OF LUZERNE COUNTY OVERKO v. GLEN ALDEN COAL CO. et al. 

Workmen's compensation-Computation of average weekly wage— Occupational Disease Act of June 21, 1939, sec. 309(d)—Dis ability occurring subsequent to termination of employment. 

In computing the average weekly wage of a claimant for the purpose of determining the extent of his compensation under section 309 (d) of the Occupational Disease Act of June 21, 1939, P. L. 566, as amended by the Act of May 18, 1945, P. L. 661, the correct standard is the one year prior to the claimant's last day of work or exposure rather than the one year prior to the date of total disability. 

Appeal from decision of Workmen's Compensation Board, 

C. P. of Luzerne County, July term, 1949, no. 244. Affirmed. February 2, 1950. 

Thomas L. Kennedy, Jr., for claimant. 

J. H. Oliver, Franklin B. Gelder and Gilbert S. McClintock, for defendants. 

Elwood H. Jones, for Commonwealth. 

LEWIS, J., for court en banc :

This matter comes before the court on an appeal by the 

Glen Alden Coal Company and the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania from the decision of the Workmen's Compensation Board.

Claimant, Stephen Overko, filed a claim for compensation under the Occupational Disease Act of June 21, 1939, P. L. 566, as amended May 18, 1945, P. L. 661, sec. 1, alleging total disability as a result of anthraco-silicosis. 

Claimant worked in various collieries from 1913 to 1938, and for defendant, Glen Alden Coal Company, from 1938 to July 2, 1946, on which date he ceased work. He has not since worked. Claimant alleges that he became totally disabled on March 26, 1947.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

A hearing was held on May 24, 1947, before Joseph P.

Olexy, referee for the second compensation district. In his opinion of July 24, 1947, the referee found as a fact that claimant had become totally disabled on March 26, 1947. 

He also found as a fact that "claimant's wages exceeded the maximum for compensation purposes". Therefore, he awarded claimant maximum total disability compensation payments under the Occupational Disease Act to be paid at the rate of $20 per week, but not to exceed the total of $4,000. 

On September 16, 1947, the employer, Glen Alden Coal

Company, filed a petition with the Workmen's Compensation Board for modification of this award alleging that under the act, claimant's average weekly wage at the time of his disability on March 26, 1947, was not the maximum as the referee had found, but was only $17.01, thus entitling him to compensation of only $11.34 per week.

The petition for modification was referred to Referee Olexy, and hearing on said petition was held on November 

12, 1947. Thereafter, Referee Olexy dismissed defendant's petition and upheld the award of $20 per week, finding claimant's average weekly wage was $33.49.

From this decision defendant appealed to the Workmen's 

Compensation Board. 

The Workmen's Compensation Board dismissed defend ant's appeal and affirmed the referee's award of $20 weekly. 

In so doing, the Workmen's Compensation Board substituted for the referee's finding a finding of fact that claimant's average weekly wages were $44.63. 

Defendants, Glen Alden Coal Company and Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, took this appeal from the action of the Workmen's Compensation Board. 

There is no dispute in the instant case as to claimant's right to compensation under the Occupational Disease Act, supra. No appeal was taken from the referee's finding that claimant was totally disabled. The only question before the court is the propriety of the amount of weekly compensation awarded to claimant. Defendants contend that claimant's average weekly wages were not sufficient to entitle him to an award of $20 weekly.

1950] 

The average weekly wage of claimant must be computed in accordance with the provisions of the Occupational Disease Act. The first paragraph of section 309 (d) of the act 

"If at the time of the disability the wages are fixed by the day, hour, or by the output of the employe, the average weekly wage shall be the wage most favorable to the employe, computed by dividing by thirteen the total wages of said employe earned in the employ of the employer in the first, second, third, or fourth period of thirteen consecutive calendar weeks in the fifty-two weeks immediately preceding the accident, or in case the employe receives wages monthly, or semimonthly, by dividing by thirteen the total wages of said employe earned in the employ of the employer in the first, second, third, or fourth period of three consecutive calendar months in the year immediately preceding the accident." (Italics supplied.)

Defendants contend that claimant's wages should be computed upon his earnings during the period of one year prior to the date of his total disability, to wit, March 26, 1947. Yet, in computing claimant's wages, defendants took the earnings from March 1, 1946, to March 1, 1947, which, in quarterly periods, are as follows: (This includes vaca tion pay $87.50.) 

Total first quarter earnings 

Total second quarter earnings 

Total third quarter earnings 

Total fourth quarter earnings $221.12 119.31 

Defendant, taking the most favorable of these quarters (the first) computes the average weekly wage at $17.01, and contends claimant is entitled only to $11.34. 

The court notes in passing that defendants mistakenly computed the 52-week period as running from March 1, 1946, to March 1, 1947, and feels that since defendants contend that the 52-week period must be that preceding the date of total disability, they should have computed it from March 26, 1946, to March 26, 1947.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

However, this discrepancy does not seriously affect the primary question before the court. 

On the other hand, the Workmen's Compensation Board computed claimant's earnings during the period of one year prior to the last day he worked, to wit, July 2, 1946. 

Claimant's earnings for the year of July 1, 1945, to July 1, 1946, in quarterly periods are as follows: 

Total first quarter earnings 

Total second quarter earnings 

Total third quarter earnings 

Total fourth quarter earnings $512.91 580.17 443.52 215.36 

The board, then taking the second quarter as the most favorable, held that claimant's average weekly wages were $44.63, which entitled him to compensation of $20 weekly.

Thus, the question before this court is which method of computing the average weekly wages of claimant is correct. Is the statute to be interpreted to mean one year prior to the date of total disability, here March 26, 1947, or one year prior to the last day of work (or exposure), here July 2, 1946? 

The record discloses that for the year preceding March 1, 1947, claimant worked only until July 2, 1946. As claimant testified, he was unable to work because of his physical condition caused by his occupation. Thus, for eight months (two thirds of the entire year), claimant was able to earn nothing. Moreover, for the other four months of that year, claimant was only able to work part of the time, and the record of earnings shows that his wages for those four months were not equal to his wages when he was physically well. Actually, the year should precede March 26, 1947.

Defendants would have us compute claimant's average weekly wages during a period when claimant's earning power was severely curtailed.

The legislature surely never intended to discriminate between those becoming totally disabled on the last day of their employment and others who become totally disabled one year after leaving the occupational disease hazard. 

The former would receive compensation based upon the

1950] 

 best quarter in the last year of active employment. The latter would be penalized for trying to work while sick. 

In a hypothetical situation, let us assume that claimant had been paid a flat wage of $50 per week. He quit work on July 2, 1946, and became totally disabled on March 26, 1947. The case would then have come under section 309 (a) of the Occupational Disease Act, which provides: "(a) If at the time of the disability the wages are fixed by the week, the amount so fixed shall be the average weekly wage."

In that instance, if we were to compute compensation payments as defendant contends, the wage of claimant at the date of total disability, March 26, 1947, would be nothing per week.

We believe that the legislature intended this to mean his weekly wage at the time he last worked. In the hypothetical case, we feel the legislature intended his average weekly wage to be $50 per week.

According to the work records, claimant did not work one full quarter in the year prior to March 26, 1947. There is no way to compute the weekly wages for this claimant, unless we adopt the reasoning of the Workmen's Compensation Board.

The intent of the legislature is to use a full quarter, at least one that would furnish a figure that could reasonably and fairly be construed as earnings for 13 weeks. 

The second paragraph of section 309 (d) indicates clearly such intention on the part of the legislature. However, this provision cannot be applied to a claim arising from anthraco-silicosis, asbestosis or silicosis because section 

301 (g), as amended, provides: "(g) The employer liable for the compensation provided by this article shall be the employer in whose employment the employe was last exposed to the hazard of the occupational disease claimed, regardless of the length of time of such last exposure: Provided, that when a claimant alleges that disability or death was due to silicosis, anthraco-silicosis, asbestosis or any other occupational disease. . . the

[36 Dep. Rep. 

only employer liable shall be the last employer in whose employment the employe was last exposed to the hazard of such occupational disease during a period of six months or more after the effective date of this Act; • • 

As further evidence of the legislature's intent, we note in section 309 (e), there was provided a method to determine the average weekly wage in seasonal occupations, to wit: "... unless it be shown that during such year, by reason of exceptional causes, such method of computation does not ascertain fairly the earnings of the employe, in which case the period for calculation shall be extended so far as to give a basis for the fair ascertainment of his average weekly earnings." (Italics supplied.)

Defendants rely in part on the case of McIntyre v. E. J. 

Lavino & Co., 344 Pa. 163. As pointed out by the board, the date of that opinion discloses that it was decided under the Occupational Disease Act of July 2, 1937, P. L. 2714. 

Section 3 of the 1937 Act provides: 

"The date when the disability occurs from occupational disease shall be deemed to be the date of injury or accident."

This provision was not included in the 1939 Act. Thus, we do not feel compelled to so hold in the instant case. 

The legislature evidently realized that this section was impracticable, unreasonable and would work an injustice to any claimant suffering from silicosis, anthraco-silicosis, asbestosis or any other occupational disease which developed to a point of disablement only after five or more years, and therefore, eliminated this provision in the Act of 1939.

After a study of the entire record and of the controlling statute, we conclude the claimant's average weekly earnings must be computed on the basis of his earnings for the year prior to July 2, 1946, the last day he worked (his last exposure to the hazard), and that the Workmen's Compensation Board was correct in affirming the award of $20 per week.

1950] 

The problem here is not one of ascertaining the point of time at which the total disability occurred. Claimant's right to compensation became complete on March 26, 1947, when he became totally disabled. However, employer's liability for compensation payments attached at the time of claimant's last exposure to the hazard of the occupational disease on July 2, 1946. See Gaydosh v. Richmond 

Radiator Co. et al., 164 Pa. Superior Ct. 154.

The language of the statute itself quoted above states that the average weekly wage should be the most favorable to the employe, computed as therein set forth. The difficulty arises in interpreting the words "fifty-two calendar weeks immediately preceding the accident".

Although there was an entirely different question before the court, the Superior Court in 1945 used this language: "The development of silicosis in an employe, in a sense, is the accident. 

See Agostin v. Pittsburgh 

Steel Foundry Corp. et al., 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 322, 323, affirmed in 354 Pa. 543. "9 It is apparent that in a case arising under the Occupational Disease Act, total disability often is not the result of an "accident", as it is commonly understood, the date of which is easily ascertained. Rather, total disability which entitles an employe to compensation under this act is often the culmination of years of exposure to an occupational hazard. Therefore, since the language of the act itself is not clear, this court feels that it would be unjust and opposed to the intent of the legislature to compute claimant's compensation as defendants contend. 

We believe the legislature improvidently uses the word accident in section 309 (d) insofar as it would apply to anthraco-silicosis.

The framers of the statute prepared an act intended to provide relief for those suffering from various kinds of occupational disease. The legislature distinguishes silicosis, anthraco-silicosis, asbestosis or other similar occupational disease which developed to a point of disablement only after an exposure of five or more years from the other occupational diseases covered by the act.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

However, it is apparent that the legislature in section 309 of the act neglected to distinguish the different diseases when they considered the method of determining the average weekly wages to which a claimant is entitled. 

Therefore, the confusion in the present case. Although the act has been amended at least twice, the wording of the statute has never been corrected or clearly stated. 

To aid us in our interpretation, we have recourse to the Statutory Construction Act of 1937, P. L. 1019, which provides: 

"Section 51. Construction of Laws; Legislative Intent 

Controls. The object of all interpretation and construction of laws is to ascertain and effectuate the intention of the Legislature. Every law shall be construed, if possible, to give effect to all its provisions. 

"Section 52. Presumption in Ascertaining Legislative 

Intent.... the courts may be guided by the following presumptions: ... "(1) That the Legislature does not intend a result that is absurd, impossible of execution or unreasonable; 

"Section 58. Rule of Strict and Liberal Construc tion.-.. "All other provisions of a law shall be liberally construed to effect their objects and to promote justice." 

Where a provision in a statute is fairly susceptible to two constructions, it is proper to consider the injustice, unreasonableness and inconvenience that would follow a particular construction. See Toffalori v. Donatelli Granite 

Co. et al., 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 311 at 315.

It is also proper that we adopt a rule of liberal construction as a guide in order to effectuate the remedial purpose of the act. See Toffalori v. Donatelli Granite Co., supra.

This is humane legislation. The statute should be liberally interpreted. It should be interpreted in a way which would best effectuate legislative intent.

As was stated supra, all agree that claimant is entitled to an award. The only question before this court is the method of determining an average weekly wage. Keeping

1950] 

 in mind the object and purpose of the legislation, we are of the opinion that the average weekly wage must be computed by taking the most favorable quarter of the 52-week period preceding July 2, 1946.

The appeal of defendants is dismissed and the order of the Workmen's Compensation Board is affirmed. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD CARROLL v. STATE WORKMEN'S INS. FUND Workmen's compensation-Accidental injury-Stenographer-Infected ear from use of dictaphone.

A referee's award will be reversed where it appears that claimant, a stenographer, sustained an infected ear and disability as the result of using a dictaphone over a period of several months; the circumstances did not constitute evidence of an accidental injury. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Olexy, District No. 2, claim petition no. 114,005, accident no. 5,187,820. 

Reversed. June 21, 1950.

State Workmen's Ins. Fund, insurance carrier. 

Milton J. Kolansky, for claimant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for defendant.

MURPHY, Chairman: 

Claimant, a stenographer, sustained an infected ear and disability for a period of about three weeks as the result of using a dictaphone. She started to use the machine in 

September 1947 and shortly thereafter noticed there was something wrong with her ears at the point where the ear plugs were inserted. The irritation became infected, and in January her face and ears began to swell. She stopped work on January 30, 1948, and was totally disabled until

[36 Dep. Rep. 

February 25, 1948. The disability was apparently the result of the pressure on the inner ear from the use of the ear plugs. The referee allowed compensation for medical expenses, finding as a fact that claimant sustained an accidental injury. This appeal by defendant followed.

We are of the opinion that the claimant failed to establish an accidental injury. The use of the ear phones caused an infection which was not the result of a sudden or undesigned occurrence. It was caused by a constant irritation on the inner ear, by the voluntary insertion into claimant's ears of the dictaphone plugs. There was no evidence of any sudden change in the physical structure or tissue of the body. Claimant was doing her work in the way she intended to do it without any unusual external occurrence.

Accordingly, we vacate the referee's seventh, eighth and ninth findings of fact, the second, third and fourth conclusions of law and the award. In lieu thereof we make the following 

7. We find as a fact that the claimant's disability was caused by the voluntary use of the ear phones and was not accidental in nature. 

8. The claimant was not disabled by reason of any accidental injury. 

9. Due notice of the occurrence was given the defendant on February 25, 1948.

2. Since the claimant did not sustain an accidental injury while in the course of her employment she is not entitled to receive compensation for disability. Disallowance

The claim petition of Ruth A. Carroll is dismissed.

1950] 

 COMMON PLEAS NO. 3 OF PHILADELPHIA COUNTY MARTIN v. FINEGAN, etc., et al. 

Workmen's compensation—Appeal from referee to board-Time for taking Practice Power of board to remand record on own motion -Common-law marriage-Evidence-Finding by board. 

1. A finding by the Workmen's Compensation Board that an appeal taken by claimant from a referee's decision was taken within the time permitted by law will be affirmed, where the referee's decision was received by the parties on May 5th, where claimant's appeal was postmarked May 29th, and received by the board on June 2nd, but where claimant produced testimony that the appeal was actually mailed, properly addressed, and stamped on May 19th; there being competent evidence to support the board's finding that the appeal was taken on May 19th, and was thus taken within the 20-day period permitted by law, the finding of the board will be affirmed. 

2. Where an appeal has been taken from a referee's decision to the Workmen's Compensation Board, the board may on its own motion remand the record for additional testimony to the referee. 

3. A finding by the Workmen's Compensation Board that a lawful marriage was consummated by claimant and a deceased person will be affirmed, where there is evidence that the parties on a certain date entered into a valid marriage by words to the effect that they took each other as husband and wife, that they lived together thereafter and were known as husband and wife, bought a home as husband and wife and filed joint income tax returns. 

In the Court of Common Pleas No. 3 of Philadelphia 

County, December term, 1949, no. 4082. Appeal by claimant from Workmen's Compensation Board. Affirmed. May 8, 1950. 

Herman Blumenthal, for claimant. 

Raymond A. White, Jr., for defendants. 

MACNEILLE, P. J.:

This is an appeal from the Workmen's Compensation 

Board awarding to claimant compensation for the death of her common-law husband and an additional amount on account of funeral expenses incurred.

Claimant's original petition was filed February 3, 1947, and assigned for hearing before the referee on April 2, 1947.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Claimant's petition was dismissed on May 2, 1947, on the ground that claimant failed to prove a valid common-law marriage. The decision was served on the parties and was received May 5, 1947. Subsequently thereto, on June 3, 1947, defendant insurance carrier received notice of an appeal by claimant, the same having been received by the board in Harrisburg, Pa., on June 2, 1947. On November 5, 1947, the order of the referee was set aside and the case was remanded to the referee for further proceedings. On December 11, 1947, claimant's petition was again dismissed insofar as compensation was concerned, but claimant was allowed the sum of $200 to reimburse her on account of funeral expenses. An appeal again was taken and on April 14, 1948, the board reversed the referee and entered an award in favor of claimant. On June 11, 1948, the board modified its prior award correcting the date from which compensation was to be computed. An appeal from the decision of the board was then taken in this court. The issues involved in the appeal were whether or not claimant had proved a commonlaw marriage; whether the board had authority to remand the record to the referee for further testimony, and whether claimant's original appeal from referee's decision was taken within the time allowed by law. This court remitted the record to the Workmen's Compensation Board for the taking of further testimony and for the making of findings relating to the circumstances concerning the action of the Workmen's Compensation Board in allowing the filing of claimant's original appeal after the lapse of the period provided for in the act of assembly. Hearings in accordance with the order of the court were held before the referee and on June 10, 1949, the referee filed his decision disallowing compensation. Claimant filed another appeal to the board and on 

January 11, 1950, the board filed its opinion again reversing the referee and awarded compensation to claimant. An appeal was then taken to this court from the decision of the Workmen's Compensation Board, which is presently before us for consideration.

The questions involved in this appeal as set forth in defendant's exceptions and brief are as follows:

1950] 

I. Where the envelope containing the appeal papers from the referee to the board shows a United States Post Office mailing date of more than 20 days after claimant's receipt of referee's decision, and claimant does not show any legal cause for extending the 20-day period, is it not reversible error for the board not to rule the appeal too late under section 423?

II. Where claimant does not file any petition under section 426, does the board have power on its own motion to refer the matter back to the referee for testimony which would only be to strengthen or change testimony already given?

III. Where the credible and substantial testimony of claimant is simply that parties went to live together, is there a common-law marriage proved?

The first question involves the issue of whether or not the original appeal to the board from the referee's decision was taken within the time permitted by law. It is undisputed that the referee's decision, dated April 29, 1947, was mailed from Harrisburg on May 2, 1947, and received by the parties on May 5, 1947. The 20-day period began to run on May 6, 1947, and ended May 25, 1947. Claimant's appeal was postmarked May 29, 1947, and received by the board at Harrisburg on June 2, 1947. Claimant has produced testimony that the appeal was actually mailed, properly addressed and stamped on May 19, 1947, and thus was taken in the time allowed by law.

Section 406 of The Workmen's Compensation Act, as amended, June 21, 1939, P. L. 520, sec. 1, 77 PS §717, provides as follows: "All notices and copies to which any party shall be entitled under the provisions of this article shall be served by mail, or in such manner as the Board shall direct. For the purpose of this article any notice or copy shall be deemed served on the date when mailed, properly stamped and addressed, and shall be presumed to have reached the party to be served; but any party may show by competent evidence that any notice or copy was not received, or that there was an unusual or unreasonable delay in its transmis-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

sion through the mails. In any such case, proper allowance shall be made for the party's failure within the prescribed time to assert any right given him by this Act." 

Section 423 of The Workmen's Compensation Act, as amended, June 21, 1939, P. L. 520, sec. 1, 77 PS §853, pro vides as follows: 

"Any party in interest may, within twenty days after notice of a referee's award or disallowance of compensation shall have been served upon him, take an appeal to the Board on the ground: (1) that the award or disallowance of compensation is not in conformity with the terms of this Act, or that the referee committed any other error of law; (2) that the findings of fact and award or disallowance of compensation was unwarranted by sufficient, competent evidence, or was procured by fraud, coercion, or other improper conduct of any party in interest. The 

Board may, upon cause shown, extend the time provided in this Article for taking such appeal or for the filing of an answer or other pleading."

In determining whether or not the appeal was properly taken the burden was on claimant to prove that the appeal was mailed properly stamped and addressed within 20 days of May 5, 1947, or prior to May 25, 1947.

At the rehearing before the referee, claimant called as her witness Suzanne B. Reichlin, former secretary to Arthur S. Werblun, deceased, the attorney who represented claimant at the time the appeal was allegedly taken. She testified that the appeal was prepared and a letter sent to claimant to call at the attorney's office. She stated claimant appeared May 19, 1947, and was taken before a notary public in the same building to acknowledge the appeal papers. Subsequent thereto on the same day the appeal papers were placed by her in a stamped envelope properly addressed to the Workmen's Compensation Board and immediately dropped in a United States mail chute in the North American Building, Broad and Sansom Streets, Philadelphia, Pa.

The only testimony produced by defendants on this issue was the testimony of Mr. Andrew J. Gavin, a carrier fore-

1950] 

 man, employed by the United States Post Office. He testified that the envelope in which the appeal was mailed was postmarked "May 29, 1947, 3:30 P. M., Philadelphia, Pennsylvania". He stated that "3:30 P. M." meant that the envelope was received and cancelled at the main office at Thirtieth and Market Streets or at the William Penn Annex at Ninth and Market Streets, Philadelphia, Pa., at that time. He testified that he did not know when the envelope was deposited or exactly what time it was mailed, but collections were made in the North American Building every half hour. 

The Workmen's Compensation Board in its opinion dated 

January 11, 1950, made the following comment: "We believe that although the appeal was received by the 

Workmen's Compensation Board on June 2, 1947, and post marked on May 29, 1947, that it was in fact mailed on May 19, 1947. The envelope was properly addressed, stamped and mailed by the secretary of claimant's attorney on May 19, 1947. We accept her testimony as credible and believe that the transmission of the letter to the board was delayed by matters beyond the control of claimant. We regard the appeal as having been delivered to the board when deposited in the mails on May 19, 1947."

The seventh finding of fact contained in the decision of the Workmen's Compensation Board dated January 11, 

1950, is as follows: "7. We find as a fact that notice of the referee's decision dated April 29, 1947, disallowing compensation was received by claimant on May 5, 1947. The secretary of claimant's attorney, on May 19, 1947, took claimant before a notary public, where claimant signed and acknowledged the appeal forms. The appeal from the referee's decision was placed in an envelope, stamped and addressed to the Workmen's Compensation Board, Harrisburg, Pa. The envelope and its contents were mailed to the board by being deposited in a United States mail chute in the North American Building, Broad and Sansom Streets, Philadelphia, on May 19, 1947, and within 20 days of the receipt of the referee's decision."

[36 Dep. Rep. 

The duty of this court on appeal from the Workmen's Compensation Board is limited to consideration of the record to ascertain whether there is competent evidence to support the findings of the board and if on such findings the law has properly been applied. A court may not review the facts and set aside findings of fact by the Workmen's Compensation Board which are supported by legally competent and substantial evidence. See Johnson v. State 

Workmen's Ins. Fund, 100 Pa. Superior Ct. 12; Bradley v. 

Pioneer Oil Co. et al., 109 Pa. Superior Ct. 585; Workman v. Hazle Brook Coal Co., 134 Pa. Superior Ct. 38; Wilbert v. Commonwealth, Second Injury Reserve Account et al., 

143 Pa. Superior Ct. 37; Howard v. McClane et al., 146 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 212; Worbnoff v. Mesta Machine Co. et al., 286 Pa. 199.

The findings of the compensation board in a workmen's compensation case supported by competent proof, and the inferences to be drawn therefrom, are as conclusive as the verdict of a jury. See Krischunas v. Philadelphia &

Reading Coal & Iron Co., 296 Pa. 216. 

We have considered the testimony taken before the board, but in view of our limited powers of review we do not believe we would be justified in setting aside the finding of the Workmen's Compensation Board concerning the taking of the appeal. There was competent evidence to support the board's finding and we find no legal error in the action of the board in this regard. 

We next have for consideration whether the board had the power on its own motion to refer the case back to the referee for further testimony. This issue concerns whether or not the board properly returned the record to the referee to take further testimony concerning the circumstances under which claimant and deceased became husband and wife. We find no merit in this objection of defendants. As stated supra, the appeal from the referee's finding to the Workmen's Compensation Board was taken within the time allowed by law. An appeal having properly been taken, the authority of the board to remand the record for additional testimony is contained in sections 423 and 425 of The Work-

1950] 

 men's Compensation Act, as amended June 21, 1939, P. L. 

520, sec. 1, 77 PS §854, 856. See also 2 Skinner's Pennsylvania Workmen's Compensation Law, 827, where it is stated: "On appeal to the Board if it appears that the referee's order is based on insufficient or incompetent evidence, orders are frequently made remanding the case to the referee for further hearing and determination, which is the equivalent of an order for a rehearing." 

The third issue raised by the appeal is whether or not claimant proved, by credible and substantial testimony, a common-law marriage with deceased. When this case was first before us on appeal, we considered the evidence concerning the common-law marriage but did not pass specifically on this question, although at the time we had no doubt as to the fact that claimant had proven a commonlaw marriage by competent and legal evidence. 

In order to be entitled to compensation, claimant had the burden of proving a common-law marriage with deceased.

At the original hearing before the referee claimant testified that deceased was her husband. Her relevant testimony follows: "Q. How were you married? "A. We just went to live together. "Q. When was that? "A. September 6, 1937. "Q. And when did you decide that— "A. Well, in September, when we went together. "Q. What did you decide to do? "A. Just to live as man and wife."

At the second hearing claimant, in further describing what took place on September 6, 1937, testified: "A. He said, 'I take you as my wife'. He said, 'I'll take you as my wife and we'll live together as husband and wife', and I said 'I'll take you as my husband and we'll live together as husband and wife-'. "Q. And did you subsequently go together as husband and wife as you have testified?

[36 Dep. Rep. 

"A. Yes. "Q. And then all those things, the filing of the joint income tax and the buying of the property together, and the holding out to the family and all the persons whom you knew as husband and wife did that follow? "A. That's right." 

And further on claimant testified as follows: "A. We went to 1816, because he asked, 'Shall we go to live together from this day?' and I said, 'Yes'."

The evidence further reveals that claimant and deceased did, from September 6, 1937, live together and were known as husband and wife. The parties bought a home as husband and wife and filed joint income tax returns as husband and wife.

Based on the above testimony we are convinced that the board was justified in finding as a fact that a lawful common-law marriage was consummated. 

Claimant testified that deceased said to her, "I take you as my wife" and "I'll take you as my wife". Claimant then testified that she replied: "I'll take you as my husband and we'll live together as husband and wife." Claimant further testified that deceased said to her, "Shall we go to live together from this day?" and she replied, "Yes".

From the language used, and the actions of the parties in commencing to live together as man and wife from that time on we are convinced that the parties meant to enter into a valid marriage at the time the words were uttered. 

The words "I'll" and "We'll" do not necessarily connote the future. Where, as here, the persons were unlettered, they frequently become confused in the use of tenses. 

Taking into account the actual words used, the circumstances and actions of the parties, we believe a commonlaw marriage was consummated on September 6, 1937. See 

Caddy v. Johnston Firemen's Relief Assn., 129 Pa. Superior Ct. 493. Said the court at page 599: "Unlettered persons frequently become confused in the use of tenses and it is difficult to get them to testify to the exact language used in a conversation, in words of the first and second person and using the present tense, rather than

1950] 

 their understanding of its import, in words of the third person and using the past tense; but when it is considered that the parties had started on their way to get married that day, and had been interrupted by an accident, we think the testimony of the plaintiff instead of establishing that no marriage contract was made between them, is entirely consistent with a finding that the parties agreed and consented then and there to become husband and wife and live together in that relation, without a marriage ceremony, and that following that agreement they cohabited together as husband and wife and were generally recognized and reputed to be such, until his death. See Sullivan v. American Bridge Co., 115 Pa. Superior Ct. 536, 176 A. 24; Mc- 

Grath's Est., 319 Pa. 309, 179 A. 599." 

See also McGrath's Estate, 319 Pa. 309; Horton's Estate, 357 Pa. 30; Sullivan v. American Bridge Co., 115 Pa. Superior Ct. 536; Caviston v. Lang et al., 152 Pa. Superior Ct. 51.

Order 

And now, to wit, May 8, 1950, the exceptions of defendants are overruled and their appeal taken in this matter is hereby dismissed.

Accordingly, judgment is hereby entered against James 

E. Finegan, employer, and United States Casualty Company, insurance carrier, defendants, in favor of Veronica E. Martin; and defendants are ordered and directed to pay 

Veronica E. Martin, claimant, the sum of $12 per week from October 29, 1946, for a period of 300 weeks, a total sum of $3,600, with interest on deferred payments in ac cordance with section 410 of The Workmen's Compensation 

Act.

Judgment is further entered in favor of claimant and against defendants in the sum of $200 on account of funeral expenses incurred and as awarded in accordance with the provisions of The Workmen's Compensation Act.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD KYNER v. FOUST 

Workmen's compensation — Injury in course of employment - Truck driver—Permitting stranger to drive without employer's consent.

A referee's award of compensation to claimant will be reversed where it appears that claimant's deceased husband employed by defendant as a clerk and truck driver was required to drive to a distant city for produce to be sold in defendant's store, that on the trip during which he was fatally injured, decedent without defendant's permission or consent to have assistance for driving arranged with a 

16-year-old cousin to accompany him on the trip, and that the cousin was driving the truck at the time the accident occurred; under the circumstances decedent was not in the course of his employment at the time of the accident. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Patterson, district no. 6, claim petition no. 114,244, accident no. 

5,274,364. Reversed. June 16, 1950. 

Coal Operators Casualty Co., insurance carrier. 

Paul M. Crider, for claimant. 

Penrose Hertzler and R. H. Cunningham, for defendant.

KNOLL, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by defendant from an order of the referee awarding compensation payments to the claimant widow of Robert G. Robinson, deceased.

The claim petition, filed on October 13, 1948, alleged that 

Robert G. Robinson died on August 5, 1948, while in the employ of defendant as a truck driver. Defendant filed a responsive answer denying that the deceased died as the result of an accident occurring in the course of his employment.

The facts in the case are not in dispute. Defendant operated a grocery store in Chambersburg, Pa., where he employed decedent as a clerk and truck driver. In the course of his duties, decedent was required to drive weekly to

1950] 

Baltimore, Maryland, for produce to be sold in defendant's store. On August 4, 1948, decedent worked as a clerk from 

8 a.m. until 8 p.m., with time out for lunch and supper. He returned to the store about 9 p.m. for a list of the produce he had to buy in Baltimore and for blank checks to pay for the produce. Subsequently, he went to Waynesboro, Pa., where he made arrangements with his 16-year-old cousin, Allen Stevens, to accompany him to Baltimore.

Decedent left his home at about midnight, picked up Allen Stevens, and they alternated in driving the truck to Baltimore. On the return trip, decedent operated the truck to a point named Koontz Creamery where Stevens took over the driving of the truck and decedent fell asleep. Stevens drove to a point near Emmetsburg when the truck skidded from the road and turned over and decedent suffered fatal injuries. A week before the accident decedent had complained of a strain from lifting and defendant had agreed to send another employe with him to Baltimore to load the produce. On this last trip, decedent had not requested defendant's permission or consent to have assistance for driving or loading the truck, and he had acted contrary to the orders of defendant against picking up riders or passengers.

It is our opinion that the referee erred in his conclusion of law that decedent was in the course of his employment at the time of the accidental injuries and death on August 5, 1948. Under somewhat similar facts, the court in Ginther v. J. P. Graham Transfer Company et al., 348 Pa. 60, disallowed compensation and stated as follows: "A majority of this Court, however, is of opinion that at the time of the accident deceased had removed himself from the course of his employment by reason of his unauthorized delegation of the duty entrusted to him. When Ginther left the wheel and delegated its operation to another he completely abandoned his most important service to the employer and was not in the course of his employment within the meaning of the Workmen's Compensation Act." 

In the opinion of the court, the employe had left the course of his employment, stating that "To say that Ginther

[36 Dep. Rep. 

remained in the course of his employment under the circumstances here presented would deprive the employer of the control of his business and of the person to whom he had entrusted duties concomitant with the furtherance of that business. To permit an employee entrusted with the performance of a particular duty to delegate that duty to another, when he has no authority to so delegate, and no emergency making such an arrangement necessary arises, would tend to destroy the relation of master and servant. 

It would permit the employee to remove from the employer all control over that employee and his activities, to which he is entitled by virtue of, and which is necessary to, the existence of the relation of master and servant.” 

We do not believe that the inference that decedent was tired and sleepy from working a long day constitutes an emergency warranting a finding that decedent had the right to delegate his duties to Allen Stevens. If decedent were tired and sleepy, he had ample opportunity to so advise his employer and to make arrangements agreeable to the employer.

Accordingly, the referee's findings of fact are affirmed, but his second conclusion of law and order are hereby set aside, and, in lieu thereof, we make the following 

Conclusion of Law 

2. That decedent was not in the course of his employment with defendant at the time of the accidental injuries and death on August 5, 1948.

Order 

Claimant's petition no. 114,244 is hereby dismissed. 

Defendant's appeal is sustained.

1950] 

 SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA DEBATTISTE v. LAUDADIO & SON et al. 

Workmen's compensation—Disability—Evidence-Motion picture film as incontrovertible proof of claimant's ability to work—Finding by board.

An order of the court below reversing an award of the Workmen's Compensation Board to claimant for partial disability and remanding the record to the board will be reversed and judgment for claimant entered on the board's award where the order of the court below was based on certain motion pictures taken and exhibited to the court by defendant insurance carrier which pictures taken by its investigator showed claimant doing heavy work on various dates during the period for which the award was entered, and which pictures the court below held furnished incontrovertible proof that claimant suffered no impairment of ability to do the heavy work of a laborer so that oral testimony and medical opinion to the contrary must be disregarded. The motion pictures did not furnish conclusive proof that claimant suffered no disability and the doctrine of incontrovertible physical facts was not applicable.

Appeal from order of C. P. No. 1 of Philadelphia County, 

September term, 1948, no. 1081. Reversed and record remanded, July 20, 1950. Superior Court of Pennsylvania, October term, 1950, no. 116. 

George H. Detweiler, for appellees. 

Herbert H. Hadra, for appellant.

RHODES, P. J.: 

This is a workmen's compensation case in which the 

Workmen's Compensation Board made an award in favor of claimant for partial disability. The principal question presented on this appeal is whether certain motion pictures taken by agents of the insurance carrier and showing claimant performing his ordinary work as a laborer for a cement contractor irrefutably established that claimant was not partially disabled during the period in question.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Claimant was injured on November 8, 1945, in the course of his employment when thrown from his employer's truck in a collision. He filed a claim petition on December 5, 1945, and on May 16, 1946, the referee made an award of compensation of $20 a week for total disability until February 21, 1946, at which time claimant returned to work. In his original order of May 16, 1946, the referee found that "although the claimant has returned to work February 21, 1946, with no loss in earnings, he still has a definite disability as a result of the accident in the case". The referee then entered an order suspending compensation as of February 21, 1946, "until such time as the disability of the claimant . . . reflects itself in a definite loss in earning capacity". Cf. Holtz v. McGraw & Bindley, 161 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 371, 54 A. (2d) 905.

On June 17, 1946, claimant filed a petition alleging that his former wage rate of one dollar an hour was reduced to 

50 cents per hour as of May 1, 1946. After hearings, the referee awarded compensation at the rate of $4.80 per week on the basis of a 15 percent partial disability, although he found claimant's wages had been reduced from $1 to 50 cents per hour, or from $48 to $24 per week. Claimant appealed to the board which, in its opinion of September 15, 1948, modified the referee's order, and awarded claimant compensation for partial disability from May 1, 1946, reflected in a 50 percent loss of earning power, at the rate of $16 per week until such time as the disability should cease or change. Both claimant and defendant insurance carrier appealed to the 

Court of Common Pleas of Philadelphia County. Over claimant's objection, counsel for defendant insurance carrier then exhibited to the court certain motion pictures taken by its investigators. Before the court of common pleas handed down its decision, defendant insurance carrier filed a petition with the board for recall of the record and for a rehearing. The insurance carrier's petition was granted by the board for the purpose of viewing the motion pictures. 

After viewing the pictures, the board held that they were not conclusive on the question of claimant's continuing partial disability, and on September 28, 1949, handed down an

1950] 

 opinion republishing its former findings, conclusions and award.¹ With reference to the 400 feet of film presented by the carrier, showing claimant doing heavy work, and taken on April 30, May 1, 2, 27, and 28, 1947, the board stated: "They [the motion pictures] disclosed claimant's activity for a total period of twenty minutes and took sixteen minutes to show. The five reels were fastened together and do not show claimant's continuous activity for any extended period of time. They show that claimant was able to do some work, but we still believe that claimant is partially disabled and suffers a loss of earning power. The films have not persuaded us that claimant has made a complete recovery from his injuries. The claimant's medical evidence and the testimony of the impartial physician concerning claimant's disability are more convincing to us than the 'excerpts' of claimant's activity as presented by the films." 

From this order of the board, both parties appealed to the court of common pleas. Claimant contended that he should receive an award for total disability; and the carrier claimed that claimant suffered no loss of earning power and that there should be no award of compensation. The court of common pleas held that the motion pictures furnished incontrovertible proof that claimant suffered no disability: "The authenticity of these pictures is not questioned. The claimant was present when they were shown before the Referee and is easily recognizable. They were taken by investigators employed by the insurance carrier and show the claimant at work, for the same employer for whom he worked at the time of the accident, on various masonry and construction 'jobs'. He can be seen lifting and carrying long planks, lifting and carrying 95 pound bags of cement, mixing concrete in a trough with a hoe, and lifting a con- 1 "The defendant employer and/or the insurance carrier are directed to pay the claimant compensation for partial disability at the rate of $16.00 per week beginning May 1st, 1946, and continuing until such time as the disability shall change or cease within the meaning of the Act. Interest shall be payable on the award as of June 17th, 1946."

[36 Dep. Rep. 

tractor's iron wheelbarrow above shoulder height to place it in a truck. The pictures were taken on April 30, May 1 and May 2, 1947, and on May 27 and May 28, 1948 [1947] at various times of day, including the late afternoon working hours." After stating the board's reaction to the pictures, the court below continued: "We do not think that the pictures can be so lightly brushed aside. They furnish absolutely incontrovertible proof that the claimant can lift his injured arm above his head and otherwise use it in ways he claimed he could not, and that he is not 'totally disabled for hard or heavy work' as found by the board. In fact, they support but one conclusion and that is that the claimant suffers no impairment of ability to do the heavy work of a laborer. The oral testimony and medical opinion to the contrary must therefore be disregarded, Brett v. Philadelphia Transportation Co., 154 Pa. Superior Ct. 429, and are not competent evidence to justify findings of fact as re quired by section 422 of The Workmen's Compensation Law as amended." The court then remitted the record to the board "for further proceedings not inconsistent with [its] opinion".

In his appeal to this court, claimant contends that the pictures were not conclusive and that the court had no authority to overrule in effect the board on a factual matter. 

The insurance carrier has filed a motion to quash claimant's appeal to this court on the ground that the order of the court below remanding the record to the board for further proceedings is interlocutory.

Although never offered in evidence formally and always in the possession of agents of the insurance carrier, the pictures were viewed by this court on the appeal. It is admitted that they were taken without the knowledge or consent of claimant. They are amply described by the excerpts quoted from the opinion of the court below. The pictures undoubtedly have some bearing on the extent of claimant's disability after his return to work for the same employer. 

They show that he is apparently able to do work at times as a laborer. Motion pictures, properly authenticated, may well be admissible for evidence for the purpose of establishing

1950] 

 facts (Com. v. Roller, 100 Pa. Superior Ct. 125, 83 A. L. R. 

1315; Boyarsky v. G. A. Zimmerman Corp., 240 App. Div. 

361, 270 N. Y. Supp. 134; Morris v. E. I. DuPont De Nemours & Co., 346 Mo. 126, 139 S. W. (2d) 984, 129 A. L. R. 

352; Annotation, 129 A. L. R. 361-368), and in cases involving personal injuries they have been admitted to show that the plaintiff or claimant has acted in ways inconsistent with his alleged disability: 3 Wigmore on Evidence, 3d Ed., §798 (b), pp. 203-206. It is equally clear that, whatever their evidentiary value, the pictures do not furnish conclusive proof that claimant suffered no disability and consequent loss of earning power. The doctrine of incontrovertible physical facts which appellee urges is not applicable to the situation before us: Streilein v. Vogel, 363 Pa. 379, 69 A. (2d) 97; Cunningham v. Pennsylvania R. Co., 352 Pa. 571, 575, 43 A. (2d) 825; Long v. Pennsylvania Truck Lines, Inc., 335 Pa. 236, 240, 5 A. (2d) 224.

The question of the extent of physical disability and consequent loss of earning power is a complex factual matter dependent on many variables: Winters v. State Workmen's 

Insurance Fund, 136 Pa. Superior Ct. 293, 7 A. (2d) 112;

Michetti v. State Workmen's Insurance Fund, 143 Pa. Supe rior Ct. 458, 17 A. (2d) 712; Svestka v. Union Collieries Co., 149 Pa. Superior Ct. 468, 472, 27 A. (2d) 675; Leaver v. 

Midvale Co., 162 Pa. Superior Ct. 393, 399, 400, 57 A. (2d) 

698. Such question cannot be conclusively resolved in the present case by the modern mechanical device of motion pictures showing claimant performing some work. Cf. Hinkle v. Niblett, 43 D. & C. 441, 443, 444. The pictures leave many questions unanswered. How does claimant's present physical ability and earning power compare with his physical ability and earning power before the accident? The pictures show claimant's activity for very limited periods of time. Was claimant then working faster or slower than before the accident? The pictures cannot accurately measure his speed, energy and efficiency at work; they do not constitute an infallible measure of either disability or earning power. There was ample medical and other testimony to support the board's findings as to the extent of claimant's

[36 Dep. Rep. 

disability and his loss of earning power.2 Two physicians, testifying for claimant, gave their professional opinions that claimant was 50 percent disabled for light work and 100 percent disabled for heavy work. Claimant testified that he could do some work for limited periods, accompanied with pain. His employer testified that claimant could do only light work. Dr. Maxwell F. White, the impartial expert appointed by the board, testified that claimant had a permanent disability of 10 to 15 percent.

Appellee's motion to quash the appeal is without merit. 

The court below erred in holding the motion pictures irrefutably and conclusively established that claimant suffered no disability. By its opinion and order of remission, the court below undertook to direct the board how to find the facts. This action was entirely outside the province of the court and its order of remission under such circumstances was not interlocutory: Fronko v. United States 

Sanitary Mfg. Co., 155 Pa. Superior Ct. 636, 644, 39 A. (2d) 

363; Rozauski v. Glen Alden Coal Co., 165 Pa. Superior Ct. 

460, 465, 69 A. (2d) 192.

The order of the court below is reversed, and the record is remitted to the court below with direction to enter judgment for claimant on the board's award. 

2 "The claimant therefore has had partial disabilities varying from 50% to 15%, which reflected in a loss of earning power, which we believe is equal to his loss of wages. He is therefore entitled to receive 

66%% of $24.00 or $16.00 per week from May 1st, 1946."
