 SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA KEYSTONE MINING COMPANY v. UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION BOARD OF REVIEW 

Unemployment compensation - Leaving employment without good cause - Coal miners · ·Application for pension — Effect of 

pension plan on availability for work. 

Where claimants who were coal miners left their employment allegedly because the work was too heavy for them, and requested lighter work which was not available, and immediately after ceasing work applied for pensions to which they were entitled as members of their union, an order of the Unemployment Compensation Board of Review awarding compensation will be vacated and the record remanded to the board to take additional testimony as to the terms of the pension and its impact upon claimants' availability for suitable work and their good faith in registering for work. 

Appeal from the decision of the Unemployment Compensation Board of Review, no. B-18979, vacated and records remanded, Superior Court of Pennsylvania, February term, 1950, nos. 29, 30. 

Franklin B. Gelder, for appellant. 

T. McKeen Chidsey, Attorney General, and William L. 

Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, for appellee.

RENO, J.: 

Keystone Mining Company, employer, appealed from a decision of the Unemployment Compensation Board of Review awarding compensation to Robert Pendleton (no. 29) 

and Alexander D. Alexander (no. 30). The separate appeals involved identical questions of law; were argued together; and will be decided in one opinion.

The records will be remitted for further hearing, and, accordingly, we state the board's version of the facts without committing this court to its approval. Claimants had been employed in the bituminous coal industry for most of

[36 Dep. Rep. 

their lives, and were in appellant's employ for over 20 years. 

Pendleton is 68 years of age and Alexander is 64. Both left their employment on December 31, 1948, because their work was "too heavy". Pendleton testified that the heavy work had aggravated his hernia; Alexander that the wet and damp conditions prevailing in the mines had injured his health. They had requested lighter work but none was available. The board allowed compensation, holding that the effect of the work upon claimants' health constituted a good cause for leaving their employment, within the meaning of the Unemployment Compensation Law, section 402(b), 43 PS §802. We have held that the physical condition of an employe is an important factor in determining the existence of good cause: Filchock Unemployment Compensation Case, 164 Pa. Superior Ct. 43, 63 A. (2d) 355;

Miller Unemployment Compensation Case, 158 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 570, 45 A. (2d) 908.

The board also decided that claimants were "able to work and available for suitable work" within the meaning of section 401 (d), 43 PS §801, although there were no immediate opportunities for securing lighter work for which they were capable in the vicinity in which they lived. See 

Sturdevant Unemployment Compensation Case, 158 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 548, 45 A. (2) 898. The evidence upon which this finding was rested will not now be reviewed. 

The undisputed testimony is that immediately after ceasing work both claimants applied for pensions to which they were entitled as members of the United Mine Workers of America, and Pendleton also applied for old age assistance under the Social Security Act of Congress.¹ This is the pinch of the case, and yet the board gave it no consideration. 

It found no facts in respect to it, and its decision totally ignored the question. Appellant applied for a rehearing upon this ground, inter alia, but the board refused it out- 1 The record does not disclose the exact date when the applications for pensions were filed. At the hearings, claimants testified that they were then receiving their pensions, but the dates when payments of pensions commenced are not stated. Payments may have been made as of the date of the applications but that fact has not been developed.

1950] 

 of-hand, an administrative practice not to be commended, since the board had previously decided similar cases against other claimants.2 4 The effect of acceptance of industrial pensions upon an employe's claim for unemployment compensation has not hitherto occupied the attention of this court, and our research has not uncovered appellate expressions elsewhere on the point. On August 1, 1949, more than 11,000 plans for industrial pensions were in operation. These plans, varying in detail while following a general pattern, will, more and more, call for administrative and judicial interpretation. It is probably too early to attempt formulation of guiding principles, and only provisional dicta may now be suggested.

It is safe to assert that pension payments are not wages within the meaning of the law, section 4(x), 43 PS §753, and that their receipt will not disqualify an employe who meets the other requirements of the law. Nor are payments made by an employer to a pension fund regarded as wages: 

Unemployment Compensation Law, section 4(x) (2) (A).

The purpose of the unemployment legislation is "the compulsory setting aside of unemployment reserves to be used for the benefit of persons unemployed through no fault of their own": Unemployment Compensation Law, section 3, 43 PS §752. By it the legislature seeks to prevent "the spread of indigency”, but an employe need not be indigent to secure the benefits provided by the law. If he meets the requirements of the law, he is entitled to compensation even though he has other resources and from them receives in- 2 The cases have not been made available to us, but we gather from the briefs that they denied compensation claims where claimants were receiving pensions under the United Mine Workers of America Health and Welfare Fund. 

3 In Hall Unemployment Compensation Case, 160 Pa. Superior Ct. 

65, 49 A. (2d) 872, claimant's pension was only incidentally involved.

The factual issue was whether or not she had been “laid off”, and this court decided only that the evidence supported the board's finding that she had separated from her employment voluntarily. 

4 Pension and Profit Sharing Service. (Prentice-Hall.) Supplement of August 1, 1949.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

come adequate for his needs; e.g., interest on savings accounts, mortgages, United States bonds, or rent for real estate owned by him. The purpose of a pension plan is "to pay additional compensation for services rendered in the past": Kline v. State Employes' Retirement Board, 353 Pa. 

79, 85, 44 A. (2d) 267. However, it is not remuneration within section 4 (u), 43 PS §753, since the pensioner performs no service during the period covered by the pension payments.

There is another aspect to an industrial pension: its tendency to withdraw the recipient from the labor market.

Indeed, at one time and possibly now, withdrawal of older men from the labor market formed an objective in the union's advocacy of pension plans. If the condition of a pension is that the recipient shall permanently retire from all gainful labor he is certainly not available for suitable work, and is disqualified for unemployment compensation. 

If the condition is that he shall retire from a specified industry, to that extent his availability becomes restricted, and it is an open question as to how far a worker in covered employment may circumscribe his availability for work and still remain qualified for compensation. Even if a pensioner is permitted, under the terms of his pension, to work in other industries by waiving pension payments during such service, his potential available is reduced by the amount of his pension. That is, a pensioner who receives $25 a week will not accept referred work which pays only $25 per week, where acceptance of employment entails loss of his pension, temporarily or permanently. He will not, in rerum natura, sacrifice the stipend of $25, which he secures without rendering any service, so that he can earn $25 by labor, be it hard or light work. Will he forego his $25 pension to 

5 "In the depression years of the 1930's, however, unions adopted a contrary view. Unions then favored compulsory retirement of older workers in order to make more jobs available to new members of the labor force and provide some income to a greater number of individuals": Employee Pensions in Collective Bargaining, 59 Yale Law Journal, 678, 696. See also, passim, Collective Bargaining, by Leonard J. 

Smith. (Prentice-Hall.)

1950] 

 accept employment at $35 or $40 a week? If he says he will, if he should register for such work, his good faith becomes relevant. These considerations do not apply to the employe who has income from investments; that income accrues whether he works or not; and he does not sacrifice it by accepting employment.

This brings us to defects of these records. There is oral testimony to the effect that under the United Mine Workers' pension plan a member receives $100 a month upon condition that he permanently withdraw from work in the mines. (This would seem to exclude the possibility of engaging in light work in the mines.) Whether he can, without losing his pension, temporarily or permanently, engage in other occupations has not been developed. The board's counsel relies largely upon statements in a colloquy between the referee, a union representative and the attorneys, but, as we read the record, the conversation was fruitless; it developed more heat than light. Undoubtedly the terms of the pension exist in written or printed form, and, although the board is not bound by the niceties of technical rules of evidence (Unemployment Compensation Law, section 505, 43

PS §825), it should have required production of it, regardless of where the burden of proof lay. Only with knowledge of the exact text of the rules can the board or this court determine the terms of the pension plan and its impact upon claimants' availability for suitable work and their good faith in registering for work. It has been said that some pension plans require a retired employe to maintain his union membership "in good stand- pay- • The agreement between the United Mine Workers and the coal operators has been printed, but the rules under which the Health and Welfare Fund grants pensions have not been found. The agreement provides that the "Trustees shall designate a portion of the ments... [to be] used for providing pensions or annuities for the members of the United Mine Workers of America": 5 Labor Law Re porter, page 53. (Commerce Clearing House.) The rules governing the administration of the fund should be produced.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

ing". This suggests that the constitution and bylaws of the union should be examined to determine whether a member who engages in work outside of his craft may continue his membership. The terms under which a member may retain his membership as a condition for drawing his pension are relevant upon the question of his availability for work. 

Consideration has been given to the position of the employer who, like appellant, has furnished the whole of the pension fund and finds his experience rating raised by wards of pensioners' claims for unemployment compensation. The problem has had the attention of experts in the field, who have suggested either revision of existing legislation or suitable covering provisions in collective bargaining agreements. The writers agree that, in the absence of modified legislation or agreements, the eligibility of pensioners for unemployment compensation must be determined under prescribed statutory qualifications. That presents a question of law which can be decided only upon records which contain adequate evidential data and suitable findings of fact.

Pendleton's status as a recipient of old age assistance is established. He receives $37.22 a month which he loses only during months when his wages for services rendered amount to $15: Act of Congress of August 14, 1935, as amended, 53 Stat. at L. 1367, 42 U. S. C. §403 (d) (1). Our study of the act did not uncover any prohibition against the acceptance of unemployment compensation.

The decisions are vacated, and the records are remanded to the board for further proceedings with directions to take additional testimony.

 SUPREME COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA AMERICAN CASUALTY CO. OF READING v. 

KLIGERMAN et al. 

Workmen's compensation Agreement 

Validity Declaratory judgment.

The common pleas court has no jurisdiction to entertain a petition for a declaratory judgment in a workmen's compensation case to determine the validity of a compensation agreement; all questions concerning the validity of such an agreement must first be determined by the Workmen's Compensation Board or its referee, subject to review on appeal to the court of common pleas, and thereafter to the Superior Court; hence, where judgment was entered in the court below under the Uniform Declaratory Judgments Act, determining the validity of an agreement between an employer, its insurance carrier and the widow of a deceased employe, an appeal taken from the judgment so entered will be quashed and the judgment vacated. 

Appeal from judgment of the Court of Common Pleas 

No. 7 of Philadelphia County, December term, 1947, No. 

1299. Appeal quashed and judgment vacated, June 26, 1950. 

Supreme Court of Pennsylvania, January term, 1950, nos. 131,135. 

George H. Detweiler, for plaintiff. 

S. Regen Ginsburg, for defendant, Kligerman.

ALLEN M. STEARNE, J.: 

These appeals are from a judgment under the Uniform 

Declaratory Judgments Act of June 18, 1923, P. L. 840, sec. 

1 et seq., as amended, 12 PS §831. The purpose of the proceeding was to determine the validity of a compensation agreement between the insurance carrier, the employer and the widow of a deceased employe within the terms of The 

Workmen's Compensation Act of June 2, 1915, P. L. 736, as amended, 77 PS §1 et seq.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Workmen's compensation, in essence, is a part of a social insurance plan which also includes old age and unemployment insurance. Its operation has proven of tremendous benefit both to employe and employer. The purpose of the act is to afford a workman protection against injuries, and to give relief in case of accident by way of accident insurance in place of common law rights. Proceedings for the adjustment of compensation claims when the parties interested agree upon the amount payable, and the enforcement of liability in case of disagreement, are outlined in the act. The methods adopted, the practice and procedure followed are contrary to the ordinary rules of the common law.

For administration purposes, the State is divided into districts, with a referee or referees in each district, a district consisting of certain designated counties. The Workmen's Compensation Board consists of a chairman and three members, one of whom, the Secretary of Labor and Industry, is an ex officio member who does not vote on orders, decisions or awards. The act provides that jurisdiction of all cases arising under the compensation law is vested in these referees and the board. Such jurisdiction cannot be changed or enlarged by contract or consent.

The courts of common pleas are without jurisdiction in compensation cases, except on appeal. The parties are not entitled to a jury trial and jurisdiction cannot be conferred upon the court by stipulation. 

The rights and obligations of the parties are determinable under the act (a) by agreement, and (b) a claim petition, heard initially before a referee or board. The "department" as mentioned in the act is defined as "Department of Labor and Industry of this (Pennsylvania) Commonwealth", while bureau means the Bureau of Workmen's Compensation in such department.

If the parties cannot agree, the employe is required to file, within one year, a claim petition which is forwarded to the bureau at Harrisburg. This petition will then be assigned to the referee of the proper district and notice served upon

1950] 

 the parties. The defendant must then file his answer to the petition with the referee. After a hearing the referee files his report with the department, and the case is reviewed by the board which files its report. An appeal may then be taken to the court of common pleas. A further appeal may thereafter be taken to the Superior Court and to the Supreme Court, if specially allowed. Appeals to the Superior 

Court, in these cases, are regardless of the amount involved. 

Compensation agreements are provided for in the act. 

On or after the seventh day of disability, after an accident occurs, the employer and employe, or the dependents in a fatal case, may execute an agreement for the payment of compensation which is forwarded to the bureau for approval. Approval notices are mailed to the claimant and defendant by the bureau. Such agreements are specifically regulated both as to substance and form by the various statutes, and the agreements must be approved by the department and filed therein.

The applicable statutes and a host of cases supporting the foregoing may be found in the authoritative encyclopedic treatise of William A. Skinner, Esq., titled "The

Workmen's Compensation Law of Pennsylvania" (2 vol umes, fourth edition).

In the case now before us, the widow of the deceased injured workman and the employer executed a compensation agreement which, at first, the director of the Bureau of Workmen's Compensation refused to aprove. An action of mandamus was commenced by the widow in the Court of Common Pleas of Dauphin County seeking to require the director to approve it. Appellant, the insurance carrier, petitioned and was allowed to intervene in the case. Before a decision was rendered, the director approved the agreement. It was filed in the department, where it presently reposes: Kligerman v. Chesnut, Secretary, 56 Dauph. 304.

With the record in this position, the insurance carrier filed a petition under the Declaratory Judgments Act, supra. 

The learned court below entertained the petition. It ruled that the agreement was valid and directed the widow to

[36 Dep. Rep. 

file with the prothonotary a certified copy of the agreement and the board's order approving the same. This appeal followed. 

It is appellant's contention that the compensation agreement, so approved and filed, is void and inoperative under the terms of the act. 

Compensation agreements are specifically provided for in 

The Workmen's Compensation Act. An agreement permit ting a commutation of payments contrary to the provisions of the act, or varying the amount to be paid, or the period during which compensation shall be payable, is wholly null and void: Bair v. Susquehanna Collieries Co., 335 Pa. 266, 6 A. (2d) 799. 

The amending Act of June 21, 1939, P. L. 520, 77 PS §602, provides that agreements relating to compensation for death must be executed within one year of death. But, as pointed out by Judge Richards in the mandamus case, supra, "when one party has deluded the other to the advantage of the first and to the hurt of the second" the claim is not barred by limitation: Horn v. Lehigh Valley Railroad Co., 274 Pa. 42, 117 Atl. 409; Horton v. West Penn Power Company et al., 119 Pa. Superior Ct. 465, 180 Atl. 56; Guy v. 

Stoecklein Baking Company et al., 133 Pa. Superior Ct. 38, 1 A. (2d) 839; Demmel v. Dilworth Company et al., 136 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 37, 7 A. (2d) 50; Meyers v. Lehigh Valley

Transportation Company et al., 138 Pa. Superior Ct. 569, 10 A. (2d) 879; Rowles v. State Workmen's Insurance Fund et al., 141 Pa. Superior Ct. 193, 14 A. (2d) 551; Reichert v. 

Pennsylvania Railroad Co. et al., 156 Pa. Superior Ct. 213, 40 A. (2d) 158.

In specified circumstances, the board, or a referee designated by the board, may, at any time review and modify or set aside an original or supplemental agreement, upon petition filed by either party: Act of June 21, 1939, P. L. 520, 77 PS §771. This reads: 

"The Board, or a referee designated by the Board, may, at any time, review and modify or set aside an original or supplemental agreement, upon petition filed by either party

1950] 

 with the Board or in the course of the proceedings under any petition pending before such Board or referee, if it be proved that such agreement was procured by the fraud, coercion, or other improper conduct of a party, or was founded upon a mistake of law or of fact. . 

The validity of the agreement, or whether it has become ineffective because of the statutory limitation concerning time of filing, or of its proper execution, is a mixed question of fact and law.

A reading of the statute and its many amendments makes it manifest that the legislation relating to workmen's compensation was designed and intended to establish exclusive jurisdiction, practice and procedure in all matters pertaining to such subject matter. It is clear that the jurisdiction of the courts of common pleas and of the appellate courts relates solely to review on appeal. The statutory provisions relate to both cases commenced by petition and to agreements for compensation. The common pleas court below, therefore, possessed no jurisdiction to entertain a petition for a declaratory judgment in a workmen's compensation case, especially relating to an approved agreement for compensation, filed in the Bureau of Workmen's Compensation.

All questions concerning the validity of such an agreement must first be determined by a compensation board or its referee. Such judgment may thereafter be reviewed, on appeal, by the court of common pleas, with an appeal to the Superior Court. A declaratory judgment is an inappropriate remedy in a workmen's compensation case. It is not an optional substitute for established and available remedies, especially where another statutory remedy has been specifically provided: Gerety Estate, 349 Pa. 417 37 A. (2d) 

702; Keefer Estate, 351 Pa. 343, 41 A. (2d) 666; Fahey Estate, 356 Pa. 535, 52 A.(2d) 580; Thompson Estate, 360 

Pa. 566, 63 A. (2d) 55; Philadelphia Manufacturers Mutual 

Fire Insurance Company v. Rose, 364 Pa. 15, 20, and cases therein cited.

The appeal is quashed and the judgment of the court below is vacated.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD TARKAR v. PITTSBURGH COAL CO. 

Workmen's compensation-Occupational disease-Filing of petition 

-Knowledge of employe-Claim petition filed more than two years after beginning of disability-Section 315 of

Workmen's Compensation Act. 

Where claimant first knew that he was totally disabled from anthracosilicosis more than two years after he last worked, his total disability beginning when he left defendant's employ, his claim petition filed within one year after he knew of his disability was not barred by the limitation in section 315 of the Workmen's Compensation Act. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Foust, District No. 9, claim petition no. 116,560, accident no. 5392389. 

Affirmed, July 26, 1950. 

John Wusels, for claimant. 

H. Y. Crossland, for defendant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for Commonwealth.

KNOLL, Commissioner: 

These are appeals by the defendant employer and the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, from an award of compensation in an occupational disease case. 

The undisputed facts are that claimant had worked in defendant's coal mines for a period in excess of ten years immediately prior to November 15, 1946; that on November 

15, 1946, claimant left defendant's employ and was thereafter totally disabled solely from anthracosilicosis; and that claimant first knew that he was disabled from the occupational disease on May 1, 1949, more than two years after he last worked. The claim petition was filed on May 12, 1949. 

The record shows that claimant had been treated by two physicians, employed by defendant, after he had ceased

1950] 

 working and that the doctors advised him he was disabled as the result of bronchial asthma and was not entitled to compensation. In April 1949, claimant consulted a specialist of chest diseases who reported to him, on May 3, 1949, that he was disabled from anthraco-silicosis. 

The defendants contend that the claim petition was barred by the failure of the claimant to file his petition within one year after the beginning of his disability, i.e., within one year from November 15, 1946, Section 315.

The construction of the word "disability" as it appears in section 315 of the act means "total disability" in a claim for anthraco-silicosis: Agostin v. Pittsburgh Steel Foundry 

Corp., 354 Pa. 543, 550, 551. The statute of limitations does not run until the employe is disabled and defintely knows that he is disabled from the occupational disease: Roschak v. Vulcan Iron Works, 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 227. These and many like decisions of our courts have held that the limitations of the statute as to notice of beginning of disability must be liberally construed in order to effectuate the humane purposes of the act. We are not able to find authority construing the effect of the one year limitation of section 315, and none is cited by the defendant, in the instant case where claimant did not know of his total disability until more than two years after its inception. However, in view of the insidious latent nature of the disease and the difficulty of medical diagnosis, we believe that the purpose of the statute will be best served by adopting the reasoning and the ruling of the Superior Court in the Roschak case, supra, and that, therefore, the statute of limitations began to run from the date when claimant first knew that he was disabled by the occupational disease. It follows that the claim petition filed on May 12, 1949, was timely and claimant is entitled to compensation under the act.

The referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of award are affirmed. 

Defendant's appeals are dismissed.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD HUDAK v. STATE WORKMEN'S INS. FUND et al. 

Workmen's compensation—Accidental injury—Skin infection—Public assistance investigator-Contact with children suffering with skin disease.

An award for disability by claimant employed as an investigator to visit homes of applicants for public assistance will be affirmed, where the disability according to medical testimony resulted from a rash which appeared on her neck and spread over the rest of her body, which was the result of contact with children who suffered from a parasitic skin disease, and whom she had visited on a certain date; under the circumstances, since the injury was not a gradual development from long continued exposure, but the result of unexpected contact on a definite date, it constituted an accident within the meaning of The Workmen's Compensation Law. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Phillips, district no. 3, claim petition no. 114,219, accident no. 

5,282,642. Affirmed. July 19, 1950.

State Workmen's Ins. Fund, insurance carrier. 

Milton J. Kolansky, for claimant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for defendant.

MURPHY, Chairman: 

Claimant filed a petition for compensation, alleging that she was accidentally injured on April 26, 1948, in that she contracted a skin infection while making investigations for her employer. She claimed disability from April 26, 1948, to 

July 15, 1948. Defendant filed an answer denying liability.

After due hearing the referee made an award, finding as a fact that claimant's disability was the result of contact with persons who were infected with a skin disease while in the course of her employment.

Claimant testified that she was employed as an investigator and was required to visit the homes of various appli-

1950] 

 cants for public assistance; that on or about April 20, 1948, she visited a family who had children who suffered from a skin disease. The children came in contact with claimant. 

Shortly thereafter, a rash appeared on her neck and then spread over the rest of her body. She was laid up for approximately three months and returned to her work on July 15, 1948. She had consulted Dr. Paul Kubasko, who attended her during her disability.

Dr. Paul Kubasko, called by claimant, testified that he examined claimant on April 26, 1948, and found a skin rash which covered the back of her neck, chest, back, legs and arms. Claimant developed scabies thereafter and also had a superimposed infection from scratching. He prescribed a lotion. The claimant later developed a fever for which he treated her until July 15, 1948. He was of the opinion that claimant's disability was the result of contact with the children who suffered from a parasitic skin disease. 

We have carefully reviewed the record and are of the opinion that the referee properly decided this case. The injury sustained by claimant and her subsequent disability were the result of an unexpected contact with claimant's skin. It was not a gradual development from long continued exposure naturally incident to her work. It was "a mishap, unexpected, undesigned, or untoward event outside the usual course of her work" and constituted an accident within the meaning of the law: McCauley v. Imperial 

Woolen Co., 261 Pa. 312, 328; Lacey v. Washburn and Williams, 309 Pa. 574. In the last cited case, Mr. Justice

Drew, speaking for our Supreme Court, cited various cases of illness caused or manifested by germs, pneumonia, inter alia, which were declared compensable because there was violence in some form, some unusual factor inadvertently or unexpectedly started the chain of circumstances, which, taken together, constituted an accident. The channel of infection in this case was abnormal and what happened was extraordinary, a mishap and accidental. 

The testimony of Dr. Kubasko connected the claimant's disability with the actual contact of the children who were

[36 Dep. Rep. 

suffering from the skin disease. We are of the opinion that the evidence supports the order of the referee. We, therefore, affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and the award. 

Defendant's appeal is dismissed.

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA ANDERSON v. CITY OF GREENSBURG et al. 

Workmen's compensation—Injury-Subsequent death from pneumonia 

--Causal connection-Expert testimony-Sufficiency.

An award of compensation sustained by the court below will be set aside where the sole question was whether the evidence sustained the finding of causal relationship between the injury and the death of claimant's husband, where it appeared that he had suffered a head injury which had caused a stroke resulting in total disability until his death two and one-half years later as the result of lobar pneumonia, the only testimony to establish the causal connection between the accident and death being that of his attending physician to the effect that the accident lowered his resistance in such a way that it predisposed toward hastening his death.

Appeal from Common Pleas Court of Westmoreland 

County, August term, 1949, no. 327. Reversed. July 20, 1950, Superior Court of Pennsylvania, April term, 1950, no. 63. 

Joseph M. Loughran, for claimant. 

James J. Burns, Jr., and Andrew Romito, for defendant. 

ARNOLD, J.:

In this workmen's compensation case, the referee made an award for the death of claimant's husband. This was sustained by the board and the court below, and defendant appealed. The sole question is whether the evidence sustains

1950] 

 the finding of causal relationship between the injury and the death. 

Claimant's husband, 72 years of age, while in the course of his employment, met with an injury to the head. Prior thereto he had hypertension or high blood pressure. The head injury caused a hemorrhage in the brain or, as commonly termed, a "stroke", rendering his left arm useless and his left leg affected. The accident occurred on May 18, 1945, and he received compensation for total disability until his death on March 28, 1948-more than two and one-half years after the accident.

His death was the result of lobar pneumonia, a germ disease. The only testimony to establish the causal connection between the accident and the death was that of his attending physician, whose testimony was that the deceased had suffered "a gradual deterioration" and that there was a connection between his injury and death; that "He went down hill gradually, deteriorated physically . . . I feel positive that there was a definite connection between this man's accident and his death". He further testified: "Q. Did the accident contribute anything to his death? A. I would say it lowered his resistance in such a way that it predisposed toward hastening his death." (Italics supplied.) This physician "couldn't say" how the pneumonia was contracted, although agreeing that it was a germ disease. 

Because of the long period of time between the injury and the death, and there being "no obvious causal relationship between the two, unequivocal medical testimony was necessary to establish causal connection" (Italics supplied): 

Baughman v. Hockensmith Wheel & Mine Car Co. et al., 

161 Pa. Superior Ct. 601, 56 A. (2d) 111. “. . . where death occurs long after the accident and lowered resistance alone is relied upon to maintain the continuity of events, the burden is on the claimant to clearly establish [this] causal connection... by expert testimony": Bannon v. Pittsburgh, 145 Pa. Superior Ct. 201, 204, 21 A. (2d) 500. The difficulty with the claimant's case is the same as was pointed out in McCoy v. Jones & Laughlin Steel Co., 275

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Pa. 422, 425, 119 Atl. 484, that the "whole of . . . [the expert] testimony might be transferred from the case before the court to any other case, involving injury of any kind, followed in time by disease of any kind, and be as material, or as immaterial. the present case 'required a careful and specific application, to its facts, of the expert knowledge of the witnesses, in order to give to that knowledge any value as evidence'; and 'the requirements of the law could not be met by a mere statement of general principles'." These principles are particularly true where, as here, the employe suffered from arteriosclerosis and was past the age of 70 at the time of the original injury. In 

Anderson v. Baxter et al., 285 Pa. 443, 446, 132 A. 358, the 

Supreme Court said: "In the instant case the pneumonia was so remote in time from the . . . [accident, 81 days] that common observation would suggest no connection between them. ." See also Morgan v. Philadelphia & Reading Coal & Iron Co., 273 Pa. 255, 116 Atl. 891. In the present case, the testimony of claimant's expert shows that his conclusion of causal connection between the accident and the death was based solely on the lowered resistance of the decedent. The testimony did not show any direct relationship between the accident and the disease causing the death, but merely a conclusion that the lowered vitality of the decedent "predisposed toward hastening his death". In short, claimant's medical testimony was merely that the decedent, in the three years following his injury, gradually deteriorated, and that at the time the lobar pneumonia was contracted his resistance was so lowered that he could not resist the germ disease. This is not sufficient. It may be noted in passing that the claimant's medical expert also testified: "I would say it [his condition after the accident] actually weakened this man to such extent if it weren't pneumonia something else would have come along..

I think if anything would have come along with an acute fashion it would have taken him away." Judgment reversed, and the award of the referee, approved by the Workmen's Compensation Board, is set aside.

1950] 

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD FANNING, etc., v. APAWANA GOLF CLUB Workmen's compensation-Employe-Golf caddy-Liability of golf club as employer.

An award for the loss of the right eye of claimant's minor son sustained while acting as a caddy on defendant's golf course when struck by a golf ball will be affirmed, where it appears that although there was no express employment of the minor as a caddy by defendant, the use of caddies existed with defendant's knowledge, was for the benefit of defendant as an accommodation to such members as desired this convenience, that caddies used facilities of defendant adjacent to the clubhouse as their headquarters and eating place, and some supervision was exercised in supplying caddies to players who requested or desired such service. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Phillips, District No. 3, claim petition no. 113,557, accident no. 

5,237,243. Affirmed. July 5, 1950. 

Maryland Casualty Company, insurance carrier. 

Milton J. Kolansky and James W. Scanlon, for claimant. 

Louis Wagner, for defendant.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

Martha M. Fanning, mother of John Fanning, a minor, filed the instant claim petition on July 29, 1948. Compensation is claimed for the loss of the son's right eye, sustained while acting as a caddy on the golf course of defendant, Apawana Golf Club, on July 30, 1947. Hearings were held before Referee Phillips on January 27, 1949, and December 8, 1949, and the referee entered an award on December 

29, 1949. This appeal was taken by defendant on January 

14, 1950, and it was heard by the board on April 26, 1950.

The accidental loss of the eye in question is clearly established. The only question involved is whether John Fanning was an employe of defendant at the time of the acci-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

dent. The record discloses that on July 30, 1947, defendant, a nonprofit corporation owned and operated a golf club and a nine hole golf course in the Borough of Dunmore.

The affirmative burden of proof was upon claimant: Con dron v. Phila. & Reading Coal & Iron Co., 78 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 133. Claimant called John F. Neelon, a member of the golf club, who testified that, about six weeks prior to the accident, John and his brother, James, accompanied him and his son to the golf course to watch the witness play golf. The two brothers expressed a desire to work as caddies so that the witness took the matter up with Stanley Wykowski, who had supervision of the course. The witness stated that he visited the course about three times a week to play golf and that he saw the two boys there at those times. The number of caddies present varied from time to time. Nealon testified that on one occasion, there was an argument about caddy priority and that he took the matter up with Wykowski, chairman of the Greens Committee, who "told this big fellow he gave those two boys permission to go up there and caddy". James J. Dempsey, also a member of the defendant club, testified that he was on the golf course when the accident happened. The witness described what happened as follows: "As I was going out to play he come out and asked to caddy. I told him I wasn't playing around, I was just hitting some balls off No. 1 tee. And he went down, he was shagging some balls, taking them back to me and about the middle of the morning another fellow come down to drive through, so we let him go through and Mr. Fanning,

John, got out of the way and went over to the side and he hit his first ball. . . . And as he commenced to hit a second ball, and the ball struck John Fanning."

Dempsey reported the accident to Walter Searfoss, the club steward. Under cross-examination, the witness testified that it was the first time he had used a caddy to "shag" balls for him and that he had intended giving the boy a tip. He stated that he had used John Fanning as a caddy on several occasions and had paid him each time the fee

1950] 

 the caddy fixed for his services. James Fanning, the injured boy's younger brother, testified there were as many as 10 boys assembled to caddy and that on one occasion some larger boys tried to chase the two Fanning boys away but that the matter was straightened out on Nealon's intervention with Wykowski, and the two brothers had no further difficulty. The youngster stated that a boy, 18 or 

19 years of age, was in charge of the caddies, and the caddies went out in rotation as they arrived at the golf course. 

John Fanning, 14 years of age, testified that he worked at the links almost every day for a month prior to the accident. He stated that Nealon told him he could caddy and that he frequently saw Dietrick and Wykowski around the links. He also testified in regard to the altercation with some of the other boys about the brothers caddying. John testified: "Q. Why didn't they want you to caddy? "A. With somebody else there that means they couldn't go out that much. "Q. In other words, if you and your brother didn't work there, the other boys could go out many times oftener, is that it? "A. Yes. "Q. After that, what happened? "A. Mr. Nealon asked Mr. Dietrick and he said we could caddy there. "Q. After this conversation with Mr. Nealon and Mr. 

Dietrick did anybody stop you and your brother from caddying? "A. No. "Q. And you continued to caddy up until the time of your accident? "A. Yes."

The claimant stated also that one older boy was in charge and saw to it that "we went out on our turns". The claimant testified that she took a lunch to her two boys at the golf course eight or nine times.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Elwell P. Dietrick testified in behalf of defendant that he was president of defendant club at the time of the accident, that after the golf club was incorporated, the employment of caddies was discontinued and that, to his knowledge, there was no authorization or supervision of caddies by the club after that time. He stated that the caddy house was moved and converted into a storage house. Dietrick testified that "the practice of the majority of members is to play without a caddy". Under crossexamination, the witness stated that a caddy would be chased off the grounds if he misbehaved, such as stealing balls or clubs. Dietrick testified: "Q. I mean after the players started on a great many occasions they had caddies and went around the course, didn't they, without any objection from you or anybody at the club? "A. That's right. "Q. And during the time these caddies were waiting to go out on their turn they made their headquarters underneath this canopy, didn't they? "A. Yes. "Q. You remember, don't you, a dispute up there between some Throop boys and Dunmore boys in the forepart of the season of 1947? "A. I heard of it, I don't know much about it. "Q. Weren't you there on the day when this dispute was going on? "A. Not that I recall. "Q. You heard the testimony of Jimmy Fanning, didn't you? "A. Yes. "Q. That you were interviewed by Mr. Wykowski and he came back and reported to the boys they'd have to go out on their turn? "A. I don't remember that."

1950] 

Under further cross-examination, the witness admitted that he saw Nealon and Dempsey on the course at different times accompanied by caddies and made no objection. He also admitted that he saw some of the boys eating their lunches in a canopy on the grounds, where the boys assembled. Stanley Wykowski, called by defendant, testified that he was a director of the club and chairman of the Greens Committee, that the club did not hire caddies or a caddy manager and that "we had warned the players taking the wrong caddies with them". Under cross-examination,

Wykowski testified that he saw caddies on the links and, if the boys behaved, they were not bothered. The witness denied that boys ate their lunch under the canopy. John

Fanning, recalled, testified that the boys brought their lunches and ate in the tent or canopy, situated about 100 feet from the club house. The referee described for the record a facial disfigurement which the boy has resulting from the accident.

We are convinced that there was no express employment of John Fanning as a caddy by defendant. Compensation then must necessarily depend upon an implied contract which resulted from the conduct of the parties. From a careful independent review of the record, we are satisfied that there was an implied contract of employment. Not only were the golfing facilities available to members of defendant club, but nonmembers were also eligible to play upon payment of certain greens fees. While the arrangements existing for the use of caddies were rather loose, caddies were available, and we conclude that this service was known to defendant and had its tacit approval. This service inured to the benefit of defendant as an accommodation to such members as desired the convenience of a caddy and may also be considered as a factor in promoting the business of greens fees. The fact that the caddies were paid by the players direct, rather than by defendant is of no moment or consequence. A goodly number of boys for the nine holes course were available and some authoritative direction was exercised in assigning the boys to players

[36 Dep. Rep. 

who requested or desired caddy service. The boys used facilities of defendant, adjacent to its club house, as their headquarters and eating place. We are in accord with the referee that the caddies served with the knowledge and consent of defendant. The business of the club was furthered thereby, and caddy service was an adjunct of the golf course. The question of employment must be resolved in claimant's favor.

We hereby affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and award. 

The appeal of defendant is dismissed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD YAHNER v. CAMBRIA COUNTY COMMISSIONERS Workmen's compensation-Employe-Prisoner at county jail-Injury while hauling hay at county children's home. 

1. An award of compensation to claimant at the minimum rate allowed by the act for disability sustained while he was a prisoner in a county jail after pleading guilty to a criminal charge and awaiting sentence will be affirmed, where, at the time of his injury, claimant was working at the county children's home unloading hay for which work he received 50 cents a day; in such case the rate of compensation to which claimant was entitled was not governed by section 309 of The Workmen's Compensation Act, which provides that the average weekly wage shall include board and lodging received from the employer. 

2. Where claimant, a prisoner in a county jail, was permitted to work if he so desired and was injured while assisting in hauling hay at the county children's home, he was not engaged in agriculture within the meaning of that term as used in The Workmen's Compensation Act excluding from the benefits of the act employes engaged in such employment.

1950] 

Appeal by claimant from award of Referee Schwing, district no. 7, claim petition no. 117,037, accident no. 5,295,964. 

Affirmed. July 19, 1950. 

Penna. Mfrs. Assn. Cas. Ins. Co., insurance carrier. 

Paul D. Larimer, for claimant. 

Robert J. Wharton and H. M. Engell, for defendant.

MURPHY, Chairman: 

This is an appeal by the claimant from an award of the referee. 

Claimant, in his petition filed on June 23, 1949, alleged that, while assisting in hauling hay at the Cambria County Children's Home, in the course of his employment on July 15, 1948, he suffered an accident, as a result of which the first and second fingers of the right hand were severed at about the first joint, the little finger of his right hand was almost severed and badly fractured so that it is now crooked and stiff, the tip of the little finger on his left hand was severed and the second and third fingers of his left hand were badly smashed and fractured. Defendant's answer, filed July 9, 1949, denied claimant's employment. After hearings, the referee found that claimant was an employe of defendant at the time of the accident and, in accordance with such finding, entered an award in claimant's favor for the loss of one-half of the index finger, one-half the loss of the middle finger and one-half the loss of use of claimant's little finger of his right hand, said award covering a 40week period at the rate of $5 per week, the minimum rate of compensation allowed by the act. This appeal followed. 

The record in this case shows that on June 28, 1948, claimant was confined to the Cambria County jail; that, on that day, he appeared in court, waived the finding of a true bill by the grand jury and pleaded guilty to a charge of burglary; that the court deferred sentence and remanded claim-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

ant to the Cambria County jail; that the accident, which is the basis of this claim, occurred on July 15, 1948, at which time claimant still confined to the jail, was working at the Cambria County Children's Home, for which work he received 50 cents a day; that, on August 2, 1948, claimant withdrew his plea of guilty and was released on bond; that a true bill was returned on September 8, 1948, and, on September 23, 1948, claimant was placed on probation for two years and ordered to pay the costs of prosecution. 

Claimant argues that the referee erred in not determining the rate of compensation per week as provided for in section 309 of the act. While claimant does not refer to the specific subsection of section 309 which he believes is applicable, we believe that his reference is to that part of section 309 (e) which reads as follows: "The terms 'average weekly wage' and 'total wages' shall include board and lodging received from the employer, and when so received, the board shall be rated at fifty cents per day, and board together with lodging shall be rated at one dollar per day. . .

If we correctly construe claimant's argument as referring to the above, we find no merit in claimant's contention. The above quoted subsection, in our opinion, was not intended to apply to a situation such as the present one where an individual, involuntarily kept in jail for the commission of a crime, is injured while working. It would certainly be against public policy to permit a prisoner to benefit because of his commission of an unlawful act. To agree with the argument advanced by claimant would have this result. 

Defendant, while not appealing the decision of the referee, argues that claimant was engaged in agriculture at the time of the accident, and, since injuries suffered in such employment are expressly excluded from the act, no award should have been made. We do not agree with this argument. 

The testimony shows that Cambria County permitted prisoners to work, if they so desired, during their incarceration, receiving a salary of 50 cents a day for their labor.

As defendant points out in its brief, some prisoners of

1950] 

Cambria County are employed in the recorder's office, doing clerical work; others are employed in the jail; others in the 

Cambria County Home and others in the Cambria County Children's Home. The testimony of the claimant is that he was working in the jail and was asked if he wished to work, temporarily, at the Cambria County Children's Home. This he agreed to do and received his injuries while unloading hay. While the act expressly excludes from its provisions persons engaged in agriculture, in our opinion claimant was not engaged in agriculture within the meaning of the act. Even if one incidentally performs agricultural work in connection with his other work, he is not engaged in agriculture within the meaning of the act. 

We affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and award. Claimant's appeal is dismissed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD MYERS v. PHILADELPHIA DAILY NEWS Workmen's compensation-Employe—Fatal injury by third person— Right of subrogation-Funeral expenses. 

An employer who is entitled to the right of subrogation under sec tion 319 of The Workmen's Compensation Act against a third person responsible for a fatal injury to one of his employes, against whom suit is instituted by the widow of the deceased employe, is entitled to recover out of a settlement with the third party the sum allowed by the board as funeral expenses in addition to payments of compensation made to the widow. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Sheldrake, district no. 1, claim petition no. 113,958, accident no. 

5,214,781. Reversed. July 26, 1950.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Penna. Mfrs. Assn. Cas. Ins. Co., insurance carrier. 

M. R. Beckman, for claimant. 

Paul H. Ferguson and H. M. Engell, for defendant.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by defendant, Philadelphia Daily News, from an order entered by Referee Sheldrake, February 1, 1950, directing payment of $200 to claimant, Mrs. Margaret 

Myers, to cover the funeral expense of her deceased husband, John Myers. Claimant filed her claim petition September 15, 1948, and the referee heard the evidence December 8, 1949. However, on January 24, 1950, all issues but subrogation of the defendant to the extent of the funeral expense were disposed of by stipulation of counsel framed in the following terms: "It is stipulated by and between counsel for the plaintiff, M. Robert Beckman, and counsel for the defendant, Paul 

Ferguson, that John Myers, an employee of the Daily News, was involved in an accident on April 19, 1948, that as a result of the accident he died on April 19, 1948, the same day, that as a result of suit instituted by the plaintiff against Rosenberg and Company, the third party involved, a settlement was effected in the amount of $6,500. It is further stipulated and agreed that the amount so recovered is sufficient to justify the referee in holding that the defendant is subrogated to the amount of its liability for Workmen's Compensation; that the sole question for the referee to decide is whether the funeral bill allowed by The Workmen's Compensation Act in the sum of $200 is covered within the subrogation amount. If the subrogation amount does include the funeral bill then the defendant shall pay the sum of $200. If the subrogation does include the funeral bill a verdict may be entered for the defendant."

On appeal, defendant has introduced its argument by citing the language of the court in Haley v. Mathews, 104

1950] 

Pa. Superior Ct. 313, pointing out that a third party wrongdoer could not escape liability for medical and hospital expenses and that the employer under the equitable doctrine was entitled to be subrogated to the extent of such charges. 

It is then contended that by analogy funeral expenses are in the same category of damages and warrant the same treatment. In answer to this, claimant has called attention to a determination by the Supreme Court (Paolis v. Tower Hill 

Connellsville Coke Company, 265 Pa. 291) that medical expense was not to be comprehended as coming within the meaning of the term compensation. Defendant's rejoinder is that the authority cited was distinguished in the Haley case, supra. The next step in the dispute is citation by the claimant of a lower court case (Patterson v. General Refractories Company, 42 D. and C. 628), from which some of the language used by the Superior Court in the Haley case, supra, is quoted, but counsel for the claimant did not read far enough when studying the Patterson case. In that decision, the court went on to say "that in a liberal construction of the term compensation it should and does include funeral expenses.. "?

In most of the cases we have read on the issue, it has been argued that the legislature intended to set liability for funeral expenses aside as something separate from compensation by providing in paragraph 7 of section 307 (77 

PS §561) that they should be paid directly to the funeral director whether or not there are dependents. In our opinion, however, such a view overlooks sound common sense and the true intention of the legislature. It is our thought that the legislature inserted the provision as a device to prevent money awarded to cover funeral expenses from falling into irresponsible hands and possibly be diverted from the use for which it was ordered paid. Doctors and hospitals do not have to worry about being paid by the employers who provide their services. The paragraph of the section in dispute, supra, gave the same assurance to undertakers. That is the explanation as we understand it and any argument that the change had the effect of declassify-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

ing funeral expenses from compensation is, in our estimation, specious. In any event, the statement by the court in the Patterson case, supra, is determinative so far as we are concerned, and we will treat the issue in the present appeal accordingly. 

Referee Sheldrake's finding of February 1, 1950, wherein it restates the stipulation filed in this case, is affirmed. 

However, the last sentence of the said finding to the effect that "subrogation covers only the compensation and not the funeral bill", is hereby stricken from the record. 

The conclusions of law and order of Referee Sheldrake dated February 1, 1950, are vacated and set aside and the following substituted therefor:

1. Funeral expenses are comprehended within the meaning of the term "compensation". 

2. Defendant is entitled to subrogation in this case to the extent of funeral expenses in the amount of $200. Order Wherein the petition filed in this case represents anything but the establishment of defendant's subrogation claim, the same is dismissed. 

The appeal is sustained.

1950] 

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD LUCAS v. STATE COLLEGE CONSTRUCTION COMPANY Workmen's compensation—Injury in course of employment—Leaving work to engage in horseplay.

An award in favor of a claimant will be set aside where it appears that the injury from which his disability resulted was the consequence of horseplay, which claimant initiated when he temporarily went to another part of the premises where he was working to get a drink of water and assailed a fellow employe.

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Schwing, district no. 7, claim petition no. 119,460, accident no. 

5542104. Reversed. August 23, 1950. 

Penna. Manufacturers Assn. Casualty Ins. Company, insurance carrier. 

Edward L. Wullard, for claimant. 

John G. Love and H. M. Engell, for defendant.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal from an award in favor of claimant, Wilson W. Lucas, and against defendant, State College Construction Company, rendered by Referee Schwing on May 25, 1950. The claim petition in the case was filed March 1, 1950, and the referee held the only hearing on the facts 

April 18, 1950.

The date of the alleged accidental injury in the course of employment is alleged as August 23, 1949, and there is no doubt that claimant fractured his right leg on that date. 

By reason thereof he was disabled until the following October 20th. The mishap occurred during working hours but the trouble is, as the evidence shows, that the consequence was due to horseplay which he initiated. He was painting stairway risers at the time and according to his testimony went to the kitchen of the house under construction by his employer to get a drink of water. In doing so, he met Frank

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Sills and his story is that someone "goosed" Sills and the latter "took a swing" at him causing him to fall. As he explains it, Sills fell on top of him and sometime after the two became disentangled he noticed the swelling which turned out to be the sympton of a broken leg. Sill's account of the matter is a little different. He has stated that as he came in to the kitchen claimant set his bucket down and went after him, forcing him to back away. He was unable to make an escape so that when Lucas took hold of him, he reacted defensively throwing his assailant a little too hard and causing the difficulty which is at the bottom of this case. 

This witness admitted that he had the reputation for being sensitive in such a manner as to be the mark for such sportiveness on the part of his fellow employes and his account of what happened rings with such genuine truthfulness that we think the referee should have accepted it. It is not often that we take the prerogative of resolving such factual discrepancies away from a referee but our review of the evidence in the present case convinces that we cannot do otherwise. 

Accepting Sills' version of what occurred it remains to apply the law and in this phase of the matter we note that defendant has cited Mason v. Wildman Mfg. Company, 12

W. G. B. 326. This has evidently been taken from the collation of the authorities on the subject beginning with Hale v. Savage Fire Brick Company, 75 Pa. Superior Ct. 454, set forth in Skinners Pennsylvania Workmen's Compensation law at page 200. Reference to that source brings out the attitude this board has always taken in such cases as the present one and the support which has been given in interpretation of the applicable law by the courts. There is no need to list here all of the citations noted in the article. 

Suffice it to say that the law gives no protection to claimant. 

A victim might claim it if injured as a result of horseplay while at work but it is very plain that one cannot leave his work to perpetrate it and expect to be eligible for compensation benefits to cover the sorry outcome if such there is. 

Injuries during play at any time of recess are quite another matter.

Incidentally, claimant was a probation trainee of the local

1950] 

 police bureau. He suffered no loss of earnings from that source during the course of disability. 

The first, second, third, fifth, sixth, seventh and eighth findings of fact, and first conclusion of law of Referee 

Schwing, dated May 25, 1950, are affirmed.

The fourth finding of fact, second conclusion of law and award, including the direction to pay medical expense, of the same date are vacated and set aside and the following substituted therefor:

Finding of Fact 

4. On August 23, 1949, during working hours claimant departed from the duties he owed defendant and engaged in horseplay of which he was the instigator and as a result thereof sustained a fracture in the right leg which disabled him until October 20, 1949.

Conclusion of Law 

2. Since under the law claimant's disability rose from an injury which was due to an act of his own which took him out of the course of his employment, he is not entitled to compensation.

Order Claim petition filed in this case, March 1, 1950, is hereby dismissed. 

The appeal is sustained.

COMMON PLEAS OF CAMBRIA COUNTY 

Toth v. Berwind-White Coal Mining Company et al.

Workmen's compensation—Occupational disease-Anthraco-silicosis— 

Total disability also resulting from unrelated ailment— 

Notice to employer 

1. An award of compensation under the Occupational Disease Act for total disability resulting from anthraco-silicosis will be affirmed where claimant was totally disabled as a result of that disease alone, although he suffered from hypertension, which in itself totally disabled him, there being however, no connection between that ailment and the anthraco-silicosis.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

2. Notice by claimant to his employer was timely within the provisions of section 311 of the Occupational Disease Act, where claimant was totally disabled from anthraco-silicosis on June 18, 1946, was informed of his disability in August but was not told of the cause of it until November 25th, following which he filed a claim petition on December 6th, since the statutory limitations did not begin to run until claimant knew he was disabled by the occupational disease. 

In the Court of Common Pleas of Cambria County, September term, 1948, no. 64. Appeal by defendant from deci sion of the Workmen's Compensation Board. Affirmed. May 

22, 1950. 

George Jerko, and Llewellyn E. Lloyd, for claimant. 

Francis A. Dunn, for defendants.

GRIFFITH, J.: 

It is admitted that claimant is totally disabled by reason of anthraco-silicosis, and the referee awarded compensation under the Occupational Disease Act of 1939, P. L. 566, 77 

PS §§1401 et seq., and the board affirmed the referee. 

Defendants appealed to the court on two grounds: (1)

That although the anthraco-silicosis from which claimant is suffering solely and of itself is sufficient to cause his total disability, yet he is also suffering from hypertension, an unrelated disease, which likewise solely and of itself would be totally disabling, and (2) that claimant failed to give the notice of disability to the employer required by section 311 

of the Occupational Disease Act. 

It is admitted that the referee was warranted in finding that the claimant suffered a sufficient exposure to the anthraco-silicosis hazard. Defendants called no witnesses but relied for its first position on the testimony of claimant's physician, who testified: "This man presents the following clinical findings, either of which is totally disabling: (1) hypertension, (2) advanced pneumoconiosis (silicosis). . "Q. Doctor, you find that the claimant is totally disabled as the result of hypertension, is that right? "A. That's right.

1950] 

"Q. You also find that the claimant is totally disabled from pneumoconiosis of the silicosis type, is that correct? "A. That's right. "Q. Now this silicosis, Doctor, what do you attribute that to? "A. I would say occupational. "Q. You say that is due to his occupation as a coal miner? "A. That's right. "Q. Is that condition (the hypertension) in any wise associated with the second condition (anthraco-silicosis) of which he suffers? "A. No. "Q. Is that condition of itself disabling? "A. Correct. "Q. To what degree? "A. Could be totally disabling. "Q. Is it your opinion that it would be totally disabling even in the absence of the second condition? "A. I would think so. "Q. Now the pneumoconiosis of the silicotic type, is that disabling? "A. I feel it is, yes. "Q. In the absence of the hypertension, is the degree of silicosis sufficient so that in your opinion it would disable him? "A. I believe it would. "Q. So is it correct to say that the claimant is suffering from total disability the result of the combination of then the hypertension and pneumoconiosis of the silicotic type? "A. Correct."

The effect of the physician's testimony is that claimant is totally disabled as a result of anthraco-silicosis alone and that he is likewise totally disabled as a result of hypertension alone, and that neither the compensable or the noncompensable disease is in any way related to, associated with or attributable to the other. 

"Section 301 (e) of The Occupational Disease Act, supra, provides that: ". . . Compensation shall be payable.. for total disability caused solely (as definitely dis-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

tinguished from a contributory or accelerating cause) by anthraco-silicosis . . ." 

As we view it, claimant's total disability is caused solely by anthraco-silicosis and the fact that he had another total disability caused solely by hypertension in no way lessens or detracts from the total disability caused solely by the occupational disease. The word "solely" in the act is specifically stated therein to be used as meaning not "contributory or accelerating." In the light of the physician's testimony that the silicosis is alone, and of itself, sufficient to disable the claimant and that this condition is not in any wise associated with the hypertension, it cannot be said that the silicosis is merely a "contributory or accelerating cause" of his disability. The act does not require the claimant to be free of all disease save the silicosis.

The Pennsylvania Occupational Disease Act must be liberally construed to effectuate its remedial and humanitarian purposes: Nickelay v. Hudson Coal Co., 164 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 550; Roschak et ux. v. Vulcan Iron Works, 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 227. Certainly it should not be construed to deny compensation to a claimant who worked in the mines for 40 years and who is admittedly totally disabled by reason of anthraco-silicosis unless such such construction cannot be avoided. 

In a death case (Treaster v. North American Refractories 

Co., 156 Pa. Superior Ct. 567) compensation was awarded even though physical disorders not connected with the silicosis concurrently existed, and the court said that obviously it was not the purpose of the legislature to extend the benefits of the act only to such employes who were free from physical infirmities save the occupational disease. Here, the physical infirmity of hypertension was not even a "necessary condition" of the disability: Kelley v. Pittsburgh Casualty Co., 256 Pa. 1, 7. Nor was the silicosis "irritated" or "advanced" by the hypertension as it was by the inhalation of tetryl powder in Anderson v. Schroeder Monumental 

Works, 159 Pa. Superior Ct. 620, 623.

In Dobash v. Jeddo-Highland Coal Co., 141 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 62, claimant succeeded in setting aside a final receipt and obtaining compensation for injury to a kidney. The court

1950] 

 said that claimant's disability was due both to an arteriosclerotic form of heart disease and the kidney disease, only the latter being of traumatic origin. Claimant's physician said "the heart disease is very important here and if he had that alone he would be unable to work but he also has a severe kidney disease which of itself alone would prevent him from working also." The facts there were, therefore, quite similar to those in the present case. The court said: "The kidney ailment being one of two concurrent causes, the presence of other contributing causes does not relieve from liability. Compare Restatement, Torts, Section 432, subsection (2); Section 439. 'If two distinct causes are operating at the same time to produce a given result, which might be produced by either, they are concurrent causes. 

They run together, as the word signifies, to the same end:' 

Herr et ux. v. City of Lebanon, 149 Pa. 222, 226, 24 A. 207. "Appellant relies upon Gausman v. Pearson Co., 284 Pa. 

348, 131 A. 247, and kindred cases, such as Mudano v. Phila. 

Rapid Transit Co., 289 Pa. 51, 137 A. 104, for the proposition that where an injury may be the result of one or two or more causes, for one of which defendant is liable, the burden is on the plaintiff to individuate that one as the proximate cause. The principle of these cases does not apply here where there are two concurrent contributing causes, for one of which defendant is liable. As the present Chief Justice said in Gorman v. Charlson et al. (No. 1), 287 Pa. 

410, 135 A. 250, at page 414, 'where two causes combine to produce injuries a person is not relieved from liability because he is responsible for only one of them.'"

It is true that the purpose of The Occupational Disease Act is to compensate only for diseases caused by employment and an employer is not an insurer of his employe's health generally. It is true, however, even in silicosis cases, that where an employe's disability is unquestionably caused by an occupational disease unassisted by any other infirmity, the broad humanitarian purpose of the act would not be served by denying him compensation merely because he has another nonoccupational disease which of itself is likewise totally disabling. The defend-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

ant's appeal can, therefore, not be sustained on the first ground.

Defendant also contends that the notice to the employer required by section 311 of The Pennsylvania Occupational 

Disease Act of June 21, 1939, P. L. 566, 77 PS §1411, was not given within the statutory period of 90 days after the beginning of disability. With this contention we cannot agree. Claimant was incapacitated from June 18, 1946, and was examined by the physician on August 16, 1946, and was told that he was disabled, but the nature of his disability was not revealed to him then as the physician who examined him wished to check his findings with another physician. On August 18, 1946, he told his mine foreman that he was unable to work because of "trouble in his chest."

The examining physician, having in the meantime had his findings checked by another physician, made a report to the office of the United Mine Workers of America, which represented claimant, dated November 11, 1946, and claimant was called to its office on November 25, 1946, and for the first time was advised that his disability was silicosis. At that time he was advised also to report this fact to his mine foreman and did so on or about that date. The claim petition was filed December 6, 1946. 

Since the statutory limitation does not begin to run until "the employee is disabled and definitely known he is disabled by the occupational disease" (Tracey v. M. & S. 

Coal Co., Inc., 165 Pa. Superior Ct. 569, 571; Nickolay v. Hudson Coal Co., supra, and Roschak et ux. v. Vulcan

Iron Works, supra, we believe the notice was given to the employer in ample time.

It is true that in his sixth finding of fact, affirmed by the Workmen's Compensation Board, the referee found that "the first notice (August 18, 1946), to the mine foreman was sufficient notice to the defendant". The sufficiency of this notice may well be doubted because the examining physician testified that he had not as yet revealed the nature of the disability to claimant and claimant merely told the foreman that he had "trouble in his

1950] 

 chest". However, the referee also found in the same finding of fact, likwise affirmed by the board, "that definite notice was given the foreman on November 25, 1946, that claimant was suffering from silicosis". This was the date on which claimant was first advised of the nature of his disability. This finding, we believe, is supported by competent evidence. Moreover, the claim petition was filed 

December 6, 1946, or 11 days thereafter. We are satisfied that the testimony supports the conclusion that the first date upon which claimant definitely knew or should know that he was disabled by silicosis was November 25, 1946, and that notice to the employer was given within the statutory period thereafter.

We, therefore, enter the following Decree

Now, May 22, 1950, after argument and upon due consideration, it is hereby ordered and decreed that the appeal be dismissed, and the findings of fact, conclusions of law and award of compensation be affirmed and approved, and the prothonotary is directed to enter judgment in favor of claimant and against defendants as follows:

Against the defendant company, the Berwind-White Coal Mining Company, and Berwind Exchange, its insurance carrier, for compensation at the rate of $9.816 per week, beginning as of June 26, 1946; said payments to be continued until there has been paid to the claimant the total sum of $3,200, together with interest at the rate of six percent per annum on accrued compensation from the date it became due and payable, and costs; subject, however, to any future termination, modification, suspension, or reinstatement, justified by the death of the claimant, or by a change in the character of his disability, and to liquidation, at any time and from time to time, for the aggregate of installments then due and unpaid. 

Against the defendant, the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, Department of Labor and Industry, Bureau of 

Workmen's Compensation, for compensation at the rate of $2.454 per week, beginning as of June 26, 1946; said payments to be continued until there has been paid to the

[36 Dep. Rep. 

claimant the total sum of $800, together with lawful interest at the rate of six percent per annum on accrued compensation from the date it became due and payable, and costs; subject, however, to any future termination, modification, suspension, or reinstatement, justified by the death of the claimant, or by a change in the character of his disability and to liquidation, at any time and from time to time, for the aggregate of installments then due and unpaid. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD HENCHELL v. LIFSHER, etc. 

Workmen's compensation-Accidental injury-Disability from heat fumes from hot air pipe.

A referee's disallowance of compensation based on his finding that claimant failed to prove an accidental injury will be affirmed, where according to claimant's evidence he suffered a cerebral engorgement in his head due to hot air fumes from a hot air duct around which he was laying brick in defendant's office; assuming the facts to be in accordance with claimant's evidence they did not constitute the happening of an accident.

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Mc- 

Govern, District No. 8, claim petition no. 115,473, accident no. 5,347,482. Affirmed. October 2, 1950. 

New Amsterdam Casualty Co., insurance carrier. 

Harry A. Sherman, for claimant. 

Wm. W. Matson, for defendant.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

This is an appeal by the claimant, Stanley S. Henchell, from an order of disallowance.

Claimant, in his petition, filed on February 3, 1949, alleged that while laying furnace bricks, in the course of his employment with defendant, M. Lifsher & Son, on January

1950] 

31, 1948, "strong fumes affected his head, causing fear of falling from scaffold, suddenly grabbed ceiling, let self down and couldn't continue working". Claimant stated the nature of his injuries to be "stroke, optic atrophy, paralysis, left arm, left leg, pneumonia and complications". Defendant's answer, filed February 14, 1949, admitted the employment but denied all other allegations of claimant's petition. 

After hearings, the referee found that claimant had not proven the happening of an accidental injury in the course of his employment and that his petition had not been filed within one year of the date of his alleged accidental injuries and dismissed his petition. This appeal followed. 

The testimony relative to the occurrence of January 31, 1948, is as follows: Defendant was enlarging its office space and connecting a storeroom in the rear of one office with another office. The alteration required an extension of the hot air duct leading from the furnace in the front offices back into the storeroom. To extend the hot air duct it was passed through a brick wall, separating both rooms, just above the door. After the duct, which was about 14 feet long and four or five inches in diameter, was pushed through the wall over the door, claimant, who was employed as a handyman, was engaged to fill the hole about the pipe with brick. The doing of this work by him took about one hour, and, in doing it, he worked from a bricklayer's scaffold about four feet above the floor. As claimant would stand on the scaffold, he would occasionally pass in front of the hot air which came from the duct. This same hot air from the duct was also fed into the front office where five or six other employes were continuously engaged in the doing of their work. Claimant did the work of bricking between 8 o'clock a.m. and 9 o'clock a.m. and alleges that he became dizzy from the hot air fumes during this time. He finished the job and then did some work in defendant's warehouse, but feeling ill, was driven home by defendant's foreman at noon time.

Claimant's medical witness, Dr. Robert R. Donoghue, testified that he had treated claimant from January 31, 1948, and that, as a result of the history received by him from claimant and as a result of his examination of claimant,

[36 Dep. Rep. 

he was of the opinion that claimant suffered a cerebral engorgement in his head, due to the gas fumes and that this pressure was the cause of his dizziness and his subsequent loss of sight and paralysis.

Defendant's medical witness, Dr. J. G. Lloyd, who had examined claimant on October 13, 1948, testified it was his opinion that claimant suffered from hemapligia brought about by hypertension or an abnormality of some of the vessels.

The primary question raised in this case is whether claimant has proven the happening of an accident. In order to recover compensation, there must be proof of an accident. 

An accident cannot be inferred merely from an injury. 

There must be some evidence of an accident, either direct or circumstantial, in the latter instance clearly and logically indicating it: Adamchick v. Wyoming Valley Collieries Co., 332 Pa. 401.
