In our opinion this case belongs in that class of cases, which is not compensable, where the exposure causing the condition is not only intentional but deliberate and in the regular course of the employe's work and, therefore, there is no accident either in the happening of an untoward event, or in an unexpected and exceptional result, or in a chain of extraordinary events subjecting the employe to an exigent and unanticipated exposure foreign to his ordinary employment: Parks v. Miller Printing Machine Co., 336 Pa. 455.

Even if we assume the facts to be as alleged by claimant, cerebral engorgement resulting from the willful and deliberate inhalation of gas fumes is no more an accident in the "usual, ordinary, popular sense" of the word Lacey v. Washburn & Williams Co., 309 Pa. 574, than contracting typhoid fever by drinking contaminated water: London v. Shautt & Son, 140 Pa. Superior Ct. 106, or contracting Weills'

Disease by eating contaminated food: Simon v. Fine, decided by the Superior Court on April 20, 1950.

We, therefore, affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order. 

Claimant's appeal is dismissed.

1950]  

WORK MEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD LONGLANTZ v. STATE WORKMEN'S INS. FUND et al. 

Workmen's compensation—Anthraco-silicosis-Total disability also resulting from unrelated ailment. 

Where claimant suffered from anthraco-silicosis as the result of his employment by defendant coal company, and also suffered from arteriosclerosis each of which ailments independent of the other totally disabled him, his claim was compensable under the Occupational Disease Act and a referee's order dismissing his petition will be reversed.

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Bufalino, specially presiding in the Fifth District, claim petition no. 113,863, accident no. 5,276,312. Reversed. October 11, 1950.

State Workmen's Ins. Fund, insurance carrier. 

T. L. Kennedy, Jr., for claimant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for defendant and Commonwealth of Pennsylvania.

KNOLL, Chairman: 

This is an appeal by claimant from an order of disallowance by the referee. 

Claimant, in his petition filed on September 7, 1948, alleged that he became totally disabled on August 18, as a result of anthraco-silicosis contracted in the course of his employment. Defendant's answer, filed on September 15, 1948, denied the material averments of claimant's petition. 

After hearings, the referee found that claimant's total disability was not due solely to anthraco-silicosis, but to a combination of peripheral arteriosclerosis, anthraco-silicosis, second stage and pulmonary emphysema, and in accordance with such finding, dismissed claimant's petition. This appeal followed.

The question raised in this case is whether proof of the

[36 Dep. Rep. 

fact that claimant suffers from peripheal arteriosclerosis and pulmonary emphysema, which of themselves are totally disabling, will prevent the entering of an award in favor of claimant, where there is also proof that the occupational disease from which he suffers is also, of itself, totaly disabling. 

At the time of the hearings, claimant's medical witness, Dr. J. B. Duffy, testified that claimant had first come under his care on May 10, 1948 and, after examination and the taking of X-rays of claimant, he was of the opinion that claimant suffered from advanced or third stage anthracosilicosis resulting in total disability. The witness further testified that he found no evidence of arteriosclerosis. 

Defendant's medical witness, Dr. P. B. Mulligan, testified that he did a clinical and X-ray examination of claimant on September 20, 1948, as a result of which he was of the opinion that claimant "is in the early phase of advanced anthraco-silicosis; probable arteriosclerotic heart disease; that there was no causal relationship between the arteriosclerotic heart disease and the anthraco-silicosis and that claimant was not totally disabled but could do light work". 

Defendant's other medical witness, Dr. W. R. Glenney, testified that he had examined claimant on October 13, 1948, and was of the opinion that claimant suffered from arteriosclerosis of the aorta with delitation, paripheal arteriosclerosis involving both extremities with circulatory deficiencies, anemia of the secondary type and anthraco-silicosis, second stage. This witness further testified that, in his opinion, there was no causal relationship between any of the other conditions suffered by claimant and the anthracosilicosis and that claimant was not totally disabled, but could do light work in general.

Subsequent to the taking of the above testimony, Dr. Burgess Gordon was appointed as an impartial and thereafter testified before the referee. The impartial testified that, in his opinion, the anthraco-silicosis of which claimant suffered was totally disabling even though the arteriosclerosis of which claimant suffered could be disabling as an independent process.

While the degree of disability suffered by claimant is, of

1950] 

 course, a question of fact to be determined by an analysis of all the medical testimony presented and a determination of such fact is to be made, in the first instance, by the referee, who should not be reversed except for obvious error, we are of the opinion, after carefully reviewing all of the medical testimony presented in this case, that the referee erred in finding that the total disability of claimant is not due solely to anthraco-silicosis. As we review the testimony, we conclude that claimant suffers from two conditions, anthraco-silicosis and arteriosclerosis, each of which, independently of the other, is totally disabling. While, of course, a combination of both conditions is totally disabling, this fact does not necessitate a finding that either condition alone cannot be totally disabling.

Thus, as we have stated above, the issue in this case narrows down to a determination of whether, where a claimant suffers from two conditions, each of which is totally disabling, one of which is covered by the provisions of the Occupational Disease Act, and the other of which is not, is he properly entitled to an award of compensation under the Occupational Disease Act? In our opinion, this question must be answered in the affirmative. It could not have been the purpose of the legislature in enacting the Occupational Disease Act, to require a claimant to prove that he was entirely free of physical infirmities, except the occupational disease, in order that the occupational disease be considered as the sole cause of disability, since, if it were the legislature's intent, there would be few industrial workers who couuld qualify physically for the benefits created. That such an unreasonable result was not intended may be presumed, particularly as the Occupational Disease Act is to be liberally construed with a view toward effectuating its humanitarian objectives: Treaster v. North American Refractories 

Co., 156 Pa. Superior Ct. 567; Staller v. Staller, 144 Pa.

Superior Ct. 83. If the occupational disease from which a claimant suffers is totally disabling, under a proper definition of that term, the mere concurrent existence of an independent physical weakness is insufficient to tip the scales in the employer's favor.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

In view of the above, we set aside the referee's seventh finding of fact, and, in lieu thereof, substitute the following 

7. The board finds as a fact that claimant is totally disabled as a result of anthraco-silicosis contracted in the course of his employment. 

8. The board further finds as a fact that, aside from the total disability resulting from anthraco-silicosis, claimant is totally disabled as a result of a combination of peripheral arteriosclerosis and pulmonary emphysema.

All other findings of fact are affirmed. 

We strike out the referee's second conclusion of law, in lieu thereof, substitute the following

2. The board concludes, as a matter of law, that since the claimant is totally disabled as a result of anthraco-silicosis contracted in the course of his employment, he is entitled to compensation in accordance with the provisions of the Oc cupational Disease Act. 

All other conclusions of law are affirmed. 

We strike out the referee's order of disallowance and enter the following Award

Compensation is awarded, and defendant, and/or its insurance carrier, is ordered and directed to pay to Charles Longlantz, claimant, compensation at the rate of $20 per week beginning on August 25, 1948, and continuing thereafter until the sum of $3,600 shall have been paid claimant, unless claimant's disability shall have changed in degree prior to such time.

Together with interest, at the rate of six percent per annum, on deferred payments of compensation, in accordance with section 410 of the act. 

Claimant's appeal is sustained. Reversed.

1950] 

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD STEWART v. LOMBARDI & SONS Workmen's compensation—Accidental injury-Weil's disease— 

Infection from exposure to water in sewer construction An award of compensation for disability resulting from Weil's disease will be affirmed, where there was evidence that claimant employed by defendant as a laborer tearing out old sewers skinned the knuckles of his hand while removing timbers bracing a ditch, that water from the sewer came in contact with his hand in the course of his work, that a few days later he became ill and that his illness was subsequently diagnosed as Weil's disease, commonly caused by exposure to water infected with the excretion of rats and other animals. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Alessandroni, District No. 1, claim petition no. 111,559, accident no. 5,148,- 

361. Affirmed. August 9, 1950.

American Mutual Liability Ins. Co., insurance carrier. 

A. Bernard Hirsch, for claimant. 

Frederick L. Fuges, for defendant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for Commonwealth.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

Claimant, Charles Stewart, was employed by Joseph Lombardi & Sons, defendant, as a laborer. In August of 1947, defendant was engaged in tearing out old sewers and putting in new ones at Seventeenth and Master Streets in Philadelphia. Claimant testified that on August 8th or 10th, he skinned the knuckles of his right hand across the fingers, while removing some timbers bracing a ditch. The knuckles bled, and claimant states that he showed his hand to Jim Brody, who washed the hand and promised to apply medication, if the hand pained claimant. Stewart continued to work, but a few days later he felt ill and consulted Dr. Robinson who treated him for about a week, when claimant noticed

[86 Dep. Rep. 

little spots in his eyes. He then went to the Wills Hospital, where he was treated. 

Claimant explained the sewer was wet and he worked right in the drainage. Gloves were not worn, he stated. 

Water from the sewer touched claimant's knuckles where they were skinned, he stated, and he recalls water contacting his eyes when he wiped them.

It appears that claimant never had had trouble with his eyes before. 

Dr. Harold Hanno, a specialist in internal medicine, claimant's witness, attended claimant at the Wills Hospital and made a diagnosis of Weil's disease, an acute systemic infection contracted by exposure to waters affected with the excretion of rats, cats and certain other animals. The commonest mode of infection is through stagnant waters in construction work, sugar cane fields and the like. A microbe organism, called spirochete, enters the body through a small abrasion in the skin or through the intact mucous membrane of the eye, ear, nose or throat. Weil's disease is characterized by fever, joint pains and aches, and in more serious cases by jaundice. Dr. Hanno first examined claimant on 

October 22, 1947, and made the diagnosis of Weil's disease, which was confirmed by the complement fixation and glutenation tests, each taken twice. The incubation period for the disease is 4 to 19 days, he explained.

Claimant's witness, Dr. Albert F. Cleveland, a specialist in ophthalmology, first examined claimant on September 27, 1947, finding claimant deeply jaundiced. Both eyes were red and inflamed, the pupils small and the anterior chamber filled with turbid exudate. The posterior surface of the cornea was clotted with numerous keratitic precipitates. The doctors made a diagnosis of anterior uveitis of both eyes. 

From the history, laboratory tests and physical findings, the doctor made a diagnosis of Weil's disease, which he felt was due to claimant's occupation. The doctor received no history of an accident. He admitted the disease was caused by spirochete which could enter the body through any microscopic opening in the skin.

The referee found that claimant sustained an accidental

1950] 

 injury and he rendered an award, from which decision defendant has taken this appeal.

Weil's disease is rare and not easily diagnosed. Claimant filed a claim under the Occupational Disease Act, but was allowed to amend, at the hearing before the referee, to bring the case under The Workmen's Compensation Act. 

The primary question is whether claimant sustained an accident; if he did, we then have the question whether his disability was caused by the same.

Claimant's testimony of skinning his knuckles, if believed, is sufficient basis for the finding of an accident. The referee found claimant's testimony credible and the record is not such as to lead us to conclude otherwise. Even had the referee found to the contrary, the mere entering of the spirochete causing Weil's disease into claimant's body from an outside source was such an unexpected, undesigned and untoward event outside the usual course of claimant's work as to constitute an "accident" within the meaning of The Workmen's 

Compensation Act. See McCauley v. Imperial Woolen Co., 261 Pa. 312. 66 Injury from a germ infection in order to be compensable must be shown to have been a sudden development from some abrupt violence to the external or internal physical structure of the body . . ." Troxell v. Shirk, 130 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 40, 44. See also Micale v. Light and State Workmen's Insurance Fund, 105 Pa. Superior Ct. 399.

If the bruised knuckles were not the port of entry for the spirochete causing Weil's disease, an award might still be proper, according to the dicta in Troxell v. Shirk, supra, at 

46, where the court stated: 66 . . We think the most that could be said upon this branch of the case is that claimant's eye might be considered a port of entry for the destructive germs and if there appeared upon this record any competent evidence that claimant had, in fact, inadvertently and within one week of October 26, 1935, wiped her eye with a piece of linen containing stains such as described by claimant, then judgment might be sustained. The chief difficulty is that we are unable to find such evidence."

[36 Dep. Rep. 

In the instant case, claimant testified that he worked in the sewer water without gloves, that sewer water got all over him: "Q. What did you mean when you said when you shoveled and lifted the dirt out it dripped? "A. When we start digging-the crane goes down and takes a bite over our head. That's when it would drip. "Q. Would it strike you? "A. Yes, water got all over you."

Page 17: "Q. Do you ever remember any of this water coming in contact with your eyes, either one? "A. Yes, I wipe my eyes out."

Claimant was clearly exposed to elements from which Weil's disease emanates. Although the physicians who testified were unable to determine the actual port of entry, we deem this immaterial. It does not appear that claimant was elsewhere exposed to the spirochete causing this disease. 

From the entire record we can only conclude the referee's award was proper, that the spirochete entered claimant's body during the course of his employment. 

The referee, in line with the testimony of Dr. Cleveland, found claimant's disability ceased December 17, 1948, until which date claimant was unable to work because of his eyes. 

Dr. Hanno stated that claimant could return to work February 1948, but that was aside from claimant's eye condition. 

We believe the referee properly found claimant to be disabled until December 17, 1948.

We conclude the instant case compensable and we affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and award. 

Defendant's appeal is dismissed.

1950] 

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD WIDDIS v. COLLINGDALE MILLWORK CO. 

Workmen's compensation-Death-Suicide-Evidence-Note written by decedent showing state of mind.

A referee's award to a widow will be set aside where claimant admitted that following the amputation of two fingers of his hand as the result of injury in the course of his employment decedent shot himself; in such case the burden was upon claimant to show that decedent killed himself while possessed by an uncontrollable insane impulse or while in a delirium, or frenzy, without rational knowledge of the consequences of his act; notes written by decedent indicating that he had been depressed over having quit a prior job which he had held for many years were properly admitted in evidence to show his state of mind prior to his suicide and to prove that he was rational. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Sheldrake, District No. 1, claim petition no. 112,294, accident no. 

5,127,522. Reversed. September 26, 1950. 

Penna. Mfrs. Assn. Cas. Ins. Co., insurance carrier. 

Maurice S. Levy, for claimant. 

Paul H. Ferguson and H. M. Engell, for defendant.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

This is an unusual death case, the claim petition being filed by Mrs. Alice J. Widdis, widow of Charles N. Widdis, deceased, against Collingdale Millwork Company, defendant. 

The facts are not in serious dispute, the main issues being the admissability of suicide notes and the conclusions to be drawn from the facts.

Decedent, a woodworker, was accidentally injured during his employment November 3, 1947, necessitating amputation of the index and middle fingers of his right hand. 

His widow stated that before the accident, her husband was pleasant, worked regularly and was not depressed.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Following the accident, he came home and cried. Thereafter, he appeared moody and depressed. He ate little and seldom spoke, except about the injured hand. 

On November 9, 1947, Widdis fatally shot himself at his home, after penning four suicide notes.

The suicide notes, admitted into evidence by the referee over objection of claimant's counsel, revealed that decedent had thought life was empty since he quit his job at the Pennsylvania Railroad, where he was employed for 20 years, that he blamed one Adams, who coaxed him to quit the railroad and enter his employ at a woodworking plant. The notes reveal decedent became disillusioned when he found Adams had allegedly misrepresented matters, and after one year of employment, Widdis quit to take the job with defendant.

Defendant's witness, Walter J. Lyster, a police detective, testified that he interviewed claimant on November 9, 1947, that claimant told him her deceased husband had been depressed for two years, since he quit his position with the Pennsylvania Railroad, that he constantly worried and brooded about the future. The witness also was told by claimant that she had hidden the revolver Widdis used, several times, but he always found it and talked of suicide. 

The defendant's testimony concerning the gun was contradicted by claimant. 

Defendant's witness, Dr. Joseph C. Yaskin, a specialist in mental diseases, from a hypothetical question posed to him, felt that Widdis was conscious of the consequences of his act in taking his life, that the accident here played only a small part of his life, the significant factor being the worry over having quit the railroad to work for Adams. 

Claimant's witness, Dr. Ely Marcovitz, a specialist in neurology and psychiatry, in response to a hypothetical question, stated that he believed decedent suffered a severe mental shock when he became aware of the extent of his disability due to the accident, that he had no rational control over his actions, which were not performed on the basis of any voluntary judgment. While the doctor felt that decedent knew the consequences of his act, he did not perform

1950] 

 the act on a rational basis, that he did so only to secure revenge on Adams.

The instant case, although unusual, is not without precedent, at least as to legal principles, in the annals of workmen's compensation cases. The application of those established principles of law, is not without difficulty however.

The Workmen's Compensation Act provides (77 PS 

§431) that "no compensation shall be made when the injury or death be intentionally self-inflicted, but the burden of proof of such fact shall be upon the employer". Here claimant admitted the death was self-inflicted and defendant was, therefore, relieved of that burden. It then rested upon claimant to prove that her husband "killed himself while possessed by an uncontrollable insane impulse, or while in a delirium, or frenzy, without rational knowledge of the physical consequences of his act": Lupfer v. Baldwin Locomotive Works, 269 Pa. 275; Gasperion v. Consolidated Coal 

Co., 293 Pa. 598; Cubit v. Philadelphia, 138 Pa. Superior

Ct. 325. It was also incumbent upon claimant to prove her decedent's death was induced by the accident he sustained here.

Defendant offered the suicide notes in evidence to show decedent's state of mind prior to his suicide, to prove that he was rational and not irrational. As part of the res gestae, we deem the notes admissable for the purpose offered. See Commonwealth v. Marshall, 287 Pa. 512.

The fact that an individual commits suicide is not proof of insanity, otherwise every case of suicide would be one of insanity. It is only where the decedent was possessed by an uncontrollable insane impulse or while in delirium or frenzy, without rational knowledge of the physical consequences of his act, that his dependents may be given compensation for his death.

Widdis was never delirious or in a frenzy. At most, he was moody and depressed, his actions appear premeditated and planned. The suicide notes do not reveal a deranged mind, but rather a rational one, which had lost courage due to adverse fortunes. In our independent judgment, we do

[36 Dep. Rep. 

not believe decedent was incapable of knowing the physical consequences of his act; rather, we think he understood the consequences of his contemplated self-destruction, and voluntarily chose those consequences. For self-destruction, under such circumstances, compensation is not payable. the award of the referee must be vacated and compensation set aside.

We vacate the referee's fourth finding of fact, conclusion of law and award, substituting in lieu thereof the following 

Finding of Fact Fourth: The Workmen's Compensation Board finds as a fact that decedent committed suicide by shooting himself on 

November 9, 1947.

Seventh: The Workmen's Compensation Board finds as a fact that decedent was rational at the time he committed suicide, that he understood the consequences of his act.

 First: The Workmen's Compensation Board concludes as a matter of law that since decedent committed suicide, intentionally and while rational, no compensation is due claimant.

Order The claim petition of Mrs. Alice J. Widdis, claimant, is hereby dismissed.

The balance of the referee's findings of fact, are affirmed. 

Defendant's appeal is sustained.

1950] 

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD HEIM v. MCFADDEN SPRING CO. 

Workmen's compensation-Disability-Loss of hearing in one ear— 

Prior loss of hearing in other ear—Suspension of compensation. 

Where following an injury as a result of which he sustained loss of hearing in his right ear, claimant returned to work and where it further appeared that prior to his accidental injury he had been deaf in his left ear so that following his injury he was totally deaf, any compensation payable to claimant for his injury was provided for by section 306(a) or 306(b) of the Workmen's Compensation

Act and not under section 306 (c), since the latter makes no provision for the payment of compensation for the loss of hearing in one ear; while defendant would not be liable for disability resulting from the fact that claimant was totally deaf, since he sustained some disability from loss of hearing in one ear as the result of his injury although not presently reflected in a loss of earning power, a referee's order suspending compensation payments rather than terminating the same will be affirmed. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Alessandroni, District No. 1, claim petition no. 116,778, accident no. 5,- 

389,215. Affirmed. October 11, 1950. 

Eureka Casualty Co., insurance carrier. 

David L. Ullman, for claimant. 

George M. Brodhead and Eureka Casualty Co., for defendant.

KNOLL, Chairman: 

This is an appeal by defendant from an award of the referee. 

Claimant's petition, filed on June 1, 1949, set out that while placing a spring upon a shelf in the course of his employment on March 12, 1949, a spring fell, striking him on the head, as a result whereof he lost his hearing in his right ear. Defendant's answer admitted the averments of claim-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

ant's petition but averred that his injury was restricted to the loss of hearing in his right ear as a result of which his loss of earning power or disability ceased on June 22, 1949.

After hearing, the referee entered an order awarding claimant compensation for total disability from March 20, 1949, to October 8, 1949, and suspending the payment of compensation from that date until such time as claimant's disability was reflected in loss of earning power. Defendant has taken this appeal, alleging that since claimant has returned to active employment, an order of termination, rather than an order of suspension, should have been entered.

In determining the issue raised in this case, it is important to remember that it is admitted by both parties and the referee has so found as a fact, that prior to the accident, which is the basis of claimant's present petition, claimant had been deaf in his ear and that, as a result of the accident forming the basis of this case, he has lost the hearing in his right ear, so that, at present, he is totally deaf.

It is clear that section 306 (c) does not provide for the loss of hearing in one ear but merely provides for complete loss of hearing in both ears as a result of an injury or injuries sustained in an accident. Thus, this section has no application to the facts of the instant case. It therefore follows, in our opinion, that the compensation payable to claimant must be determined under either section 306 (a) or section 306 (b) of the act.

Defendant argues that it cannot be held responsible if, in the future, claimant suffers a loss of earning power because of the fact that he is now totally deaf and further, that this case is controlled by Lente v. Luci, 275 Pa. 217.

We do not agree that this case is controlled by Lente v. 

Luci, supra. In that case, the question for determination was whether the employer was liable for total disability under section 306 (a) of the act or for the disability specified in section 306 (c) which covers disability resulting from permanent injuries through incapacitation or loss of one or more members. In the instant case, since section 306 (c) makes no provision for the payment of compensation for the loss of hearing in one ear, it necessarily follows that the amount

1950] 

 of compensation payable to claimant for his admitted accidental injury cannot be determined under that section.

We are in accord, however, with the argument of defendant that it cannot properly be held liable for any loss of earnings suffered by claimant, resulting from the fact that he is now totally deaf, where, as a result of the injuries suffered by him in the accident of March 12, 1949, he has suffered the loss of hearing in one ear only. However, having lost the hearing in one of his ears, as a result of the accident of March 12, 1949, it is obvious that claimant does suffer some degree of disability, even though such degree is not presently determinable and is not presently reflected in a loss of earning power. Since this is so, it is always possible that, at some future date, the disability suffered by claimant as a result of his accidental injuries of March 12, 1949, may be reflected in loss of earnings and, if this should occur, claimant would be entitled to compensation, within the provisions of the act, for such loss of earnings as he might be able to prove. The action of the referee, therefore, in suspending the payment of compensation as of October 8, 1949, rather than terminating same on that date, was proper and must be affirmed.

We, therefore, affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order. 

Defendant's appeal is dismissed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD KRAMER v. LEO DRESS COMPANY Workmen's compensation-Employe-Executive in charge of defendant's business. 1 Claimant was not an employe of defendant corporation within the meaning of the Workmen's Compensation Act where it appeared that claimant was foreman, manager, and secretary of the corporation, that his wife owned 48 shares of stock out of 50 outstanding, that

[36 Dep. Rep. 

during his absence from work defendant's plant was shut down because claimant was the only person who could run the business. 

Appeal by claimant from disallowance of Referee Olexy, District No. 2, claim petition no. 111,051, accident no. 5,- 

117,094. Affirmed. November 1, 1950. 

American Guaranty & Liab. Ins. Co., insurance carrier. 

David Yelen, for claimant. 

J. P. Harris, for defendant.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

This case comes before the Workmen's Compensation 

Board upon an appeal by Max Kramer, claimant, from an order by the referee dismissing his claim petition against 

Leo Dress Company, defendant.

Defendant is a corporation, in which claimant's wife owns 

48 shares of stock, out of 50 outstanding. Claimant owns one share and is secretary of the corporation. The company has about 75 employes, all female, with the exception of claimant, and is engaged in the manufacture of dresses. 

Claimant's official title was foreman and manager. 

Claimant testified that his wife worked at the plant about six hours a day and acted as foreman and overseer, that she signed checks and would ". . . look over the work and examine everything". 

Claimant sustained a hernia on November 25, 1946, and was operated upon about December 10, 1946. He returned to work on February 14, 1947. During his absence, the defendant plant was shut down, as there was no one to operate the plant. Claimant admitted that he was the only one who could take care of the operation of the plant; that he ran the business.

The only question we need decide here is whether claimant was an employe, within the meaning of the Workmen's Compensation Act. 

In the case of Troxel v. Troxel, 156 Pa. Superior Ct. 402, the Superior Court, through Justice Hirt, stated, at page 403:

1950] 

"A salaried official evercising control of the business of a corporation by directing its affairs is not a 'servant' and the corporation is not its 'master' in the sense contemplated by the compensation law. Amendment of June 21, 1939, P. L. 520, §1, 77 P. S. 21, 22. Such executive office in general is not entitled to compensation as an employee. Santi v. 

Amer. Coal Exchange, 91 Pa. Superior Ct. 271; Mirenstein v. Colang, Inc., et al., 111 Pa. Superior Ct. 72, 169 A. 404; 

Carville v. Bornot & Co., 288 Pa. 104, 135 A. 652."

We think it clear from claimant's own testimony here, that he was an executive official exercising control of the business and not an employe, within the contemplation of the Workmen's Compensation Act.

We affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order. 

Claimant's appeal is dismissed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD MCINTYRE v. VILLA CAFE Workmen's compensation—Agreement—Petition to set aside for lack of jurisdiction-Limitations. 

A petition by claimant to review and set aside a compensation agreement on the ground that the Workmen's Compensation Board lacked jurisdiction of the claim was properly dismissed, where the petition was not filed until nearly two and one-half years after the agreement was executed and more than one year after the date of the last payment of compensation by defendant; under such circumstances the agreement entered into was prima facie evidence of a compensable accident and defendant was under no duty to offer other evidence that the proceedings were subject to the jurisdiction of the board, further, claimant's petition was barred by the provisions of section 413 of the Act, requiring a petition to review an agreement to be filed within one year from the date of the last payment of compensation.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Appeal by claimant from order of Referee Dillman, District No. 8, dismissing petition to review under compensation agreement no. 4,947,071. Affirmed. November 1, 1950. 

Continental Casualty Co., insurance carrier. 

John E. Evans, Sr., for claimant. 

Sanford M. Chilcote, for defendant.

KNOLL, Chairman: 

This is an appeal by claimant from the order of Referee 

Alvin E. Dillman, of the Eighth Compensation District, dismissing petition to review filed on behalf of claimant. 

On December 31, 1946, compensation agreement no. 4,- 

947,071 was entered into between claimant and defendant, providing for the payment of compensation at the rate of $20 per week for an indefinite number of weeks. The agreement set forth the business of the employer as that of restaurant and the occupation of employe as "bartender", and further set forth: "Claimant being taken home from work in employer's car which was involved in accident with another car; claimant sustained mild cerebral concussion and a low back sprain."

On June 3, 1947, defendant filed its petition for termination of the aforesaid agreement, averring that all disability of claimant, arising out of the accident heretofore referred to, had terminated as of February 1, 1947, and that compensation had been paid to claimant to January 31, 1947, inclusive, in the amount of $240. No answer was filed by claimant to this petition. The matter was duly referred to 

Referee Dillman, district no. 8, before whom testimony was offered on behalf of defendant.

It appears from a study of the record that, prior to the hearing held before the referee, a letter dated February 14, 1947, was forwarded by J. I. Simon, as attorney for claimant, to W. R. Gallagher, secretary of the Workmen's Compensation Board, setting forth, inter alia, that there was no objection to the order of termination for the reason that

1950] 

 claimant was not in the course of his employment at the time of the accident and that proceedings were being instituted in the Court of Common Pleas of Allegheny County by claimant herein.

At the hearing held before Referee Dillman, medical testimony was offered on behalf of defendant in support of its petition. 

The referee, by decision dated December 16, 1947, found as a fact that all disability of claimant, arising out of the accident of November 1, 1946, terminated as of January 30, 1947, the date to which compensation had been paid, and, based upon this and other findings of fact, entered an order of termination as of January 30, 1947. No appeal was taken by claimant from the ruling of the referee. 

On June 8, 1949, claimant filed a petition to review and set aside compensation agreement and proceedings for lack of jurisdiction, setting forth that the accident occurred off the premises of the employer and was, therefore, not within the jurisdiction of the Workmen's Compensation Bureau; that no testimony was offered on behalf of the employer to es tablish jurisdiction of the Workmen's Compensation Board to adjudicate the matter, that he was induced to execute the compensation agreement as a result of representations made by a representative of the insurance carrier: and that proceedings had been instituted in the Court of Common Pleas of Allegheny County against defendant to recover for the injuries sustained by claimant. To this petition a responsive answer was filed by defendant, denying the material allegations of the said petition and setting forth that claimant was being taken home from work in the employer's car, in accordance with the contract of employment between the parties.

At the time that the matter was called for hearing before Referee Dillman, extensive discussion took place before the referee, at which time counsel for both parties submitted their positions in the matter and defendant moved for dismissal of the petition. 

The referee, by decision dated November 22, 1949, dismissed the petition filed on behalf of claimant in accordance

[36 Dep. Rep. 

with the provisions of section 413 of the act. From this ruling, claimant has appealed.

We have made a careful study of the record in this case and are of the opinion that the ruling of the referee should be affirmed.

The record discloses that, as a result of the compensation agreement entered into between the parties, claimant was paid compensation in the sum of $240, which sum is still in the possession of claimant and has not, up to the present time, been tendered to defendant herein; and that, as a result of a petition filed on behalf of defendant, an order of termination was entered by the referee on December 16, 1947, terminating further payments of compensation as of 

January 30, 1947, the date to which compensation had been paid. Due notice of the aforesaid hearing was given to claimant, and he had adequate opportunity to be present at the hearing to raise the question of jurisdiction if he so desired, but claimant did nothing. On June 8, 1949, for the first time, claimant contended that the board was without jurisdiction to entertain the matter.

It is our opinion that claimant's contention that the burden was upon defendant to establish affirmatively that the proceedings were subject to the jurisdiction of the Workmen's Compensation Board, is without merit. The compensation agreement entered into between the parties was prima facie evidence of a compensable accident and the employer was under no duty to go beyond the compensation agreement. See Rehm v. Union Collieries Co., 152 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 461.

Moreover, the record discloses that the petition to review and set aside the compensation agreement was filed more than one year subsequent to the date of the most recent payment of compensation made prior to the filing of the petition. Section 413 of the act provides that, except in cases of eye injury, a petition to review, modify or set aside an original or supplemental agreement founded upon a mistake of law or fact must be filed within the one-year period. 

Claimant has failed to do this and, by reason thereof, is barred by the provisions of section 413 of the act. See

1950] 

Roberts, admx., v. John Wanamaker, Philadelphia, et al., 151 Pa. Superior Ct. 297.

Nor do we believe that there is merit to claimant's contention that there can be no payment of compensation and the board is without jurisdiction for the reason that the accident happened off the premises of the employer. As heretofore set forth, the compensation agreement was prima facie evidence of a compensable accident. Moreover, where the means of transportation is furnished to the employe by the employer as part of the agreement of employment, expressly or impliedly, and for the interest of the employer as well as the employe, the employer is liable for injury sustained by the employe while being transported, or while using the means of transportation, on his way home from work: Bock v. Reading, 210 Pa. Superior Ct. 468. 

Considering the record in its entirety, we are of the opinion that claimant's petition is without merit and is barred by the statute of limitations as set forth in section 413 of the act. By reason thereof, the referee properly dismissed the petition filed on behalf of the claimant. 

We therefore affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of dismissal. 

The appeal of the claimant is dismissed. 

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD MALIQ v. JONES & LAUGHLIN STEEL CORP. 

Workmen's compensation-Partial disability-Method of computing wages after return to work-Loss due to illness and strikes.

Where following an injury for which claimant received compensation for total disability, he returned to work while suffering a certain partial disability, in determining the question whether claimant's average earnings after his return to work, computed on a 13-week basis equaled or exceeded his wages before the accident, claimant was entitled to compensation in those 13-week periods when his earnings

[36 Dep. Rep. 

divided by 13 although reduced by time lost on account of illness or strikes did not equal his average weekly wage prior to his injury.

Appeal by claimant from order of Referee Carmalt, District No. 8, suspending compensation under agreement no. 

4,726,022. Reversed. November 1, 1950. 

Samuel Krimsly, for claimant. 

A. L. McNaugher, for defendant.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

Fati Maliq, claimant, sustained an accidental injury during the course of his employment with Jones & Laughlin Steel Corp., defendant, on September 5, 1945, causing him to fall and sprain his back. The parties entered into an open agreement for payment of compensation for total disability. 

Claimant returned to work on February 4, 1946, but he claims that he still suffers disability in his back. Counsel for claimant and defendant apparently agree that claimant has a 20 percent partial disability, and the referee so found. 

The only question before us is whether claimant's average earnings after the injury, computed on a 13-week basis, equaled or exceeded his wages before the accident. The referee found that claimant sustained no loss of earnings, and he suspended compensation.

The real dispute here is the method of computation of claimant's wages after the accident. It appears that claimant lost considerable time from work due to illness and mine strikes. Defendant contends that for the periods claimant missed work due to illness or strikes, the 13-week period used for computation of wages should be reduced by the period of time lost. To illustrate: If claimant lost five weeks due to strikes, in a 13-week period, his total wages for the period should be divided by 8 instead of 13, to determine his average weekly wage. The arithmetical formula, which defendant desires to use results in a higher average weekly wage for claimant, and a resulting smaller compensation payment, if any.

1950] 

In resolving the question before us, we have the benefit of a determination of the precise question by our Superior 

Court in Scipani v. Pressed Steel Car Co., 150 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 410.

The court, in an opinion by Judge Hirt, stated, at page 415: "... "The claimant's loss of earnings (wages received) was caused by reductions in defendant's working force, weather, other accidents and matters not related to the claimant's physical impairment'. Upon this finding, coupled with the fact that claimant's rate of pay was not less than before his injury, the Board affirmed the conclusion of the referee that claimant suffered no loss and suspended payments on that ground. We think there is an error in this conclusion. The 

1939 Amendment refers to the actual amount of wages received regardless of the rate of pay and the lack of opportunity for employment."

The pronouncement by the appellate court seems decisive entitled to compensation for 20 percent partial disability, in those 13-week periods when his earnings did not equal $48.71 of the question, and accordingly, we find that claimant is per week, his average weekly wage prior to the accident. 

We vacate the referee's second and third findings of fact, conclusions of law and order, substituting in lieu thereof the following

 2. The Workmen's Compensation Board finds as a fact that claimant returned to work February 5, 1946, at which time his disability decreased to 20 percent partial which partial disability continues to the present day. 

3. The Workmen's Compensation Board finds as a fact that claimant's earnings following his return to work February 5, 1946 are as follows: Term 

1st quarter 1946

Total Wage 

$275.27 2nd quarter 1946 399.13 3rd quarter 1946 589.71 

4th quarter 1946 576.80 

1st quarter 1947 738.88

[36 Dep. Rep. 

 Term Total Wage 

2nd quarter 1947 668.41 

3rd quarter 1947 688.11 

4th quarter 1947 803.49 

1st quarter 1948 688.14 

2nd quarter 1948 579.05 

3rd quarter 1948 758.54 

4th quarter 1948 734.44 

1st quarter 1949 

2nd quarter 1949 282.18 628.76 

3rd quarter 1949 

4th quarter 1949 281.60 317.14

 1. The Workmen's Compensation Board concludes as a matter of law that claimant is entitled to compensation for 

20 percent partial disability from February 5, 1946, to the present date, except for those 13-week periods when claimant earned wages equal to his average weekly wage prior to the accident.

The Workmen's Compensation Board hereby orders and directs Jones & Laughlin Steel Corp., defendant, to pay to 

Fati Maliq, claimant, compensation for 20 percent partial disability at a rate of $6.48 per week from March 16, 1946 to June 16, 1946; at the rate of $3.84 from June 16, 1946 to 

September 16, 1946; at a rate of $3.84 per week from September 16, 1946 to December 15, 1946; at a rate of $1.23 per week from March 16, 1948 to June 16, 1948; at the rate of $4.17 per week, from December 16, 1948 to March 16, 1949; at the rate of $6.48 per week, from March 16, 1949 to June 

16, 1949; at a rate of $.41 per week from June 16, 1949 to 

December 16, 1949; at a rate of $6.48 per week, and at a similar rate thereafter, except for those 13-week periods when claimant earns wages equal to his average weekly wage prior to the accident, all within the limitations of the 

Workmen's Compensation Act, as amended.
