 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD PNELLIE v. NORTHWESTERN MINING & EXCHANGE 

CO. et al.

Workmen's compensation-Second injury-Act of June 21, 1947Claim for total disability—Filing prior to expiration of compensation for specific loss

Where claimant, having lost the use of his left arm as a result of a prior injury, sustained the loss of his right hand during his employment by defendant for which he was receiving compensation under section 306 (c) of the Workmen's Compensation Act, a referee's award for total disability against the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania under the Second Injury Reserve Act of June 21, 1947, will be set aside as premature, where payments for the loss of the hand will continue until April 1951, since the question of his total disability must be determined as of that date; in such case the claim petition will be dismissed without prejudice to the right of claimant to file a new petition at a future date.

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Smith, District No. 10, claim petition no. 117,104, accident no. 5,289,- 

275. Reversed. November 1, 1950.

State Workmen's Insurance Fund, insurance carrier. 

J. W. Stephenson, for claimant. 

Smith, Maine & Whitsett, for defendants.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

This case is before the Workmen's Compensation Board upon an appeal by the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania from an award in favor of Antonio Pnellie, claimant. 

The case is novel, the claim being made under the second injury reserve, created and provided for by the legislature by the Act of June 21, 1947, P. L. 873, 77 PS §516. The act provides: "If an employee who has incurred (through accident or otherwise) permanent partial disability, through the loss, or loss of use of, one hand, one arm, one foot, one leg or

[36 Dep. Rep. 

one eye, incurs total disability through a subsequent injury, causing loss, or loss of use of, another hand, arm, foot, leg or eye, he shall be entitled to additional compensation as follows: 

"After the cessation of payments by the employer for the period of weeks prescribed by Clause (c) hereof, for the subsequent injury, additional compensation shall be paid during the continuance of total disability, at the weekly compensation rate applicable for total disability, for the remainder of the 500 weeks period provided in Clause (a) hereof. This additional compensation shall be paid by the 

Commonwealth only upon an award by a compensation referee or the Board. All claims for such additional compensation shall be forever barred unless the employee shall have filed a petition therefor with the Board within one year after the last payment made under Clause (c) hereof out of the General Fund in the State Treasury."

It is uncontradicted here that claimant lost the use of his left in arm in 1912, that he sustained the loss of his right hand on August 24, 1948, during the course of his employment with Northwestern Mining and Exchange Co., that he is being paid compensation for the latter loss under section 306 (c) of the Workmen's Compensation Act, under an agreement for the specific loss, which payments of compensation extend to April 1951. The claimant filed the claim petition here, alleging that he is totally disabled and requesting an award against defendant, Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, in accordance with the above quoted act. The referee awarded claimant compensation against defendant from April 4, 1951.

We believe the claim petition and award to be premature, irrespective of the merits of the case, and we have not considered the merits. For an award, claimant must be totally disabled. Whether he will be totally disabled in April of 

1951, no one knows. Whether he is totally disabled now is of no account. If he is, his disability, may decrease; if he is not, he may be totally disabled in April of 1951. (Italics added.) An adjudication of claimant's disability now is useless, in our opinion.

1950] 

Claimant has one year following the date of last payment of compensation to file a claim petition under this act. While we do not mean to imply that he must wait until April 4, 1951, to file that petition, we believe that it should be filed not too far in advance, so that there may be appropriate testimony of the degree of claimant's disability on or after 

April 4, 1951.

Accordingly, we vacate the referee's sixth finding of fact, first and second conclusions of law and award. We enter the following Order 

The Workmen's Compensation Board hereby dismisses the claim petition of Antonio Pnellie, claimant filed, against defendant, Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, without prejudice to the right of claimant to file a similar petition at a future date, but no later than one year following the date of last payment for the injury he sustained on August 24, 1948. 

Defendant's appeal is sustained.

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA GADD v. BARONE et al. 

Workmen's compensation-Employe or independent contractor—Fore man engaged to do steel work in construction of building. 

An order of the court below reversing an award of Workmen's Compensation entered by the board on the ground that claimant was an independent contractor and not an employe of defendant, will be reversed and judgment entered on the award, where it appears that defendant, the owner of a lot engaged in the process of constructing a building thereon, was approached by claimant with reference to obtaining work for himself as foreman and for his crew of journeymen structural iron workers to lay the steel beams in the building, that the parties verbally agreed that claimant and his men would lay the steel beams, that defendant would pay them an hourly wage, that defendant withheld federal income tax deductions from plaintiff's wages; no contract for the completion of the work nor for any definite

[36 Dep. Rep. 

period of employment having been executed, defendant being free to discharge claimant at will without incurring legal liability for breach of contract, their relationship was that of employer and employe. 

Appeal from judgment of C. C. of Allegheny County, no. 

716 of 1949. Reversed. September 27, 1950. Superior Court of Pennsylvania, April term, 1950, no. 35. 

Karl E. Weise, with him Paul Kern Hirsch and Hirsch & 

Weise, for appellees. 

George J. Schafer, with him McCrady & Nicklas, for appellant. 

Ross, J.:

The only question involved in this workmen's compensation case is whether at the time he was injured, on August 25, 1947, claimant Gadd was an independent contractor or an employe of the defendant Barone. The compensation authorities, after concluding that the relationship between claimant and defendant was that of employe and employer, made an award for total disability. The defendant's appeal to the County Court of Allegheny County was sustained and judgment entered for defendant and his insurance carrier on the basis that at the time of his accident claimant was an independent contractor and not an employe of defendant. 

Claimant's appeal followed.

The basic facts are undisputed. Defendant was the owner of a lot in Pittsburgh, and in August 1947 was in the process of constructing a building thereon. Gadd approached him with reference to obtaining work for himself as foreman and for his crew of journeymen structural iron workers to lay the steel beams in the building. After subsequent negotiations, the parties orally agreed that claimant and his men lay the steel beams and that defendant would pay them an hourly wage. In accordance with the agreement, claimant and his helpers laid the steel beams on the first floor on 

August 4, 1947, and returned on August 25th to do similar work on the second floor. While so engaged, claimant lost his balance while lifting a steel girder and injured his back.

1950] 

To be entitled to compensation, claimant had the burden of proving that at the time of his injury he was an employe of the defendant: Sechrist v. Kurtz Bros., 147 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 214, 24 A. (2d) 128. The vital test in determining whether a workman is a servant of the person who engages him for the work is whether he is subject to the latter's control or right of control not only with regard to the work to be done but also with regard to the manner of performance: 

Cookson v. Knauff, 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 401, 43 A. (2d) 402; 

Thomas v. Bache, 351 Pa. 220, 40 A. (2d) 495; Felten v. 

Mellott, 165 Pa. Superior Ct. 229, 67 A. (2d) 727. In this case, we believe that control "with regard to the manner of performance" is of little or no importance because of the nature of the work, the defendant describing it as follows: "It wasn't a complicated job, just a couple, three pieces of steel, one right after the other. There was no plan made; the beams were there and all he had to do was put the steel on top of each beam; it was a very simple job."

It is not the fact of actual interference or exercise of control by the employer, but the existence of the right or authority to interfere or control, which renders one a servant rather than an independent contractor: Feller v. New Amsterdam Cas. Co., 363 Pa. 483, 70 A. (2d) 299. No contract for the completion of the work or for any definite period of employment having been executed, it is clear that had defendant chosen to do so, he could have discharged claimant and his men at will, incurring no legal liability for breach of contract. His only obligation in such case would have been to pay them for the hours they actually worked on his building. This is one of the indicia of a master-servant relationship. The power of an employer to terminate the employment at any time is incompatible with the full control of the work which is usually enjoyed by an independent contractor, and hence is considered as a strong circumstance tending to show the subserviency of the employe: 

Blum Unemployment Compensation Case, 163 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 271, 60 A. (2d) 568; Feller v. New Amsterdam Cas. Co., supra.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

The method of payment is not a determining factor in establishing the relationship of the parties: Sechrist v. 

Kurtz Bros., supra, 147 Pa. Superior Ct. 214, 24 A. (2d) 

128. However, in Thomas v. Bache, supra, 351 Pa. 220, 40 A. (2d) 495, Mr. Chief Justice Maxey, speaking for the

Supreme Court, said at page 230: "Thomas [the claimant] received from the job no profit, either directly or indirectly, but only a daily wage. While this fact is not conclusive, yet when a workman is employed at a daily wage, the inference that he is a servant and not an independent contractor, will in the vast majority of cases be in accordance with the fact." 

It seems clear from the testimony that the parties intended their relationship to be that of employer-employe, and to create such relationship the assent of both parties is essential: Harris v. Seiavitch, 336 Pa. 294, 9 A. (2d) 375.

Both defendant and claimant testified to the fact that defendant requested a "flat price" for the work but that claimant was unwilling to submit one, with the result that the hourly wage agreement was entered into. Furthermore, it is of record that defendant withheld Federal income tax deductions from claimant's wages, an act consistent only with an employer-employe relationship.

In Gailey v. State Workmen's Insurance Fund, 286 Pa. 

311, 133 Atl. 498, Mr. Chief Justice Moschzisker said, at page 314: "When... the evidence shows one, claiming as employe of a defendant, to be an independent contractor, relief under the Compensation Act must be denied; but neither the compensation authorities nor the courts should be solicitous to put claimants in that position when a reasonable view of the evidence warrants a finding that the injured person was an employe."

Viewing the evidence as a whole, we are convinced that claimant in this case has qualified as an employe within the meaning of The Workmen's Compensation Act. Cf. Cook son v. Knauff, supra, 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 401, 43 A. (2d) 

402, and Thomas v. Bache, supra, 351 Pa. 220, 40 A. (2d) 495. 

The judgment is reversed, and the record is remitted for the entry of judgment on the award.

1950] 

 SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA GEARHART v. SUMMIT FAST FREIGHT, INC. 

Workmen's compensation-Employe or independent contractor—Owner driver of truck-Contract with common carrier to transport steel An order of the court below affirming an order of the Workmen's Compensation Board dismissing claimant's petition for compensation on account of the death of her husband on the ground that at the time he suffered his fatal injury he was an independent contractor and not an employe of defendant will be affirmed, where it appeared that decedent and defendant had entered into a written contract whereby decedent was to transport steel with his truck from a certain place in Pennsylvania to various points outside of the state designated by defendant, a common carrier engaged in the business of hauling freight, and the contract further provided that decedent could take any route available under the defendant's ICC permit, that decedent collect freight charges from consignees, retaining a portion thereof as his compensation, that he pay for all gas, oil and truck repairs, and provide Workmen's Compensation coverage for his employes and pay social security taxes, it further appearing that decedent was not bound to transport for defendant exclusively but in fact did hauling for other freight lines as well. 

Appeal from judgment of C. C. of Allegheny County, no. 

A-623 of 1948. Affirmed. September 27, 1950. Superior 

Court of Pennsylvania, April term, 1949, no. 52. 

Evans, Ivory & Evans, for appellant. 

Dalzell, McFall, Pringle & Bredin, for appellee. 

Ross, J.:

In this workmen's compensation case, the only question before us is whether, at the time he suffered a fatal injury, claimant's husband, Norman D. Gearhart, was an independent contractor or was an employe of defendant. The referee dismissed the claim petition on the ground that claimant's deceased husband was an independent contractor. 

The referee was affirmed by the board, exceptions thereto dismissed by the court below, and judgment entered for defendant. Thereupon, claimant took this appeal.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Gearhart was killed on December 4, 1946, when his truck, in which he was transporting steel for defendant from Beaver Falls, Pa., to Chicago, Ill., was involved in a collision with another vehicle near Bryan, Ohio. Defendant is engaged as a common carrier in the business of hauling freight from Pennsylvania to various points in other States. At the time of his fatal injury, Gearhart was transporting the steel pursuant to a written contract entered into on September 24, 1946, between him and defendant, and the relationship of the parties arises from this contract. 

To be entitled to compensation, claimant had the burden of proving that at the time of his injury her husband was an employe of the defendant: Sechrist v. Kurtz Bros., 147 

Pa. Superior Ct. 214, 24 A. (2d) 128. As stated by President Judge Rhodes in Sones v. Thompson Furniture Co., 163 Pa. Superior Ct. 392, 394, 62 A. (2d) 116: "The relation of employer and employee, as contemplated by the 

Workmen's Compensation Act . . . arises only where there is a contract of hiring, express or implied .. and the burden of proving that deceased was an employee upon claimant." We agree with the conclusion of the compensation authorities and the court below that claimant in this case failed to meet the burden imposed upon her. was

By the terms of the contract, Gearhart was to transport freight for defendant common carrier in his privately owned truck, choosing his way from routes approved in interstate commerce permits issued to defendant. He collected freight charges from consignees, retaining 75 percent as his compensation and remitting the remaining 25 percent to defendant. All gas, oil, truck repairs and license plate and registration fees were paid by him, and he was responsible for possible traffic fines. The hiring of additional help was a matter of personal discretion with him, the contract providing that he might have his truck operated "by himself or by competent employees", and he agreed to pay wages, and charges incident to coverage of workmen's compensation, unemployment compensation and other social security benefits for assistants hired, and such other charges as were involved in his work.

1950] 

Where a contract is let for work to be done by another in which the contractee reserves no control over the means of its accomplishment, but merely as to result, the employment is an independent one establishing the relation of a contractee and contractor and not that of master and servant. The distinguishing criterion is the right to control the means of accomplishing the result: Simonton v. Morton, 275 Pa. 562, 119 Atl. 732; Healey v. Carey, Baxter & Kennedy, Inc., 144 Pa. Superior Ct. 500, 19 A. (2d) 852; 

Sechrist v. Kurtz Bros., supra, 147 Pa. Superior Ct. 214, 24 A. (2d) 128. Applying this well established test to the undisputed facts in this case, it is clear that Gearhart was an independent contractor. While to reach his destination he was required to traverse a route for which defendant was certificated, Gearhart had a choice of several such routes and the choice was his to make. Aside from this limitation, the only instructions he received from defendant were where to pick up the consignment of freight and where to deliver itclearly control only of the result and not of the means of accomplishment. 

Appellant relies heavily on the case of Myers v. Maurer & Myers, 144 Pa. Superior Ct. 385, 19 A. (2d) 579, where it was held that a master-servant relationship existed and compensation was allowed. However, that case is readily distinguishable on its facts from the instant case. In the 

Myers case, the instructions received by the drivers of the firm-owned trucks, as well as by the owner-drivers, were exactly the same, whereas here defendant's regular employes, who were paid regular wages (as against the 75 percent freight retention of the private truck owners) were instructed as to routes to be followed. They could charge gas, oil, truck repairs and needed equipment to the company's account; private truck owners, including Gearhart, paid their own. In the Myers case, claimant's husband hauled exclusively for defendant; here Gearhart hauled for others in addition to the defendant. In that case, a helper hired by defendant was riding on the truck with deceased at the time of the injury; here Gearhart hired and paid his helpers.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

Furthermore in this case, the written contract executed by defendant and the deceased provided in part as follows: "3. The relationship herein created is that of independent contractor and not that of employer and employee. Second 

Party is a contractor only and is not the employee of the First Party." While we are not bound by the nomenclature the parties applied to their relationship (Capozzoli v. Stone & Webster Engineering Corp., 352 Pa. 183, 42 A. (2d) 524), yet the other terms of the contract and the admitted facts establish that the parties correctly designated their relationship. 

Judgment affirmed.

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA LANEY v. UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION BOARD OF REVIEW 

Unemployment compensation-Discharge for wilful misconduct- 

Sleeping while on duty.

An order of the Unemployment Compensation Board of Review denying compensation to claimant, a mechanic, employed in a garage of a cab company, on the ground that his discharge was occasioned by his own misconduct will be affirmed, where there was evidence that claimant was discharged for sleeping during working hours in violation of a company regulation of which claimant had knowledge, and about which he had received prior warning.

Appeal by claimant from decision of Unemployment 

Compensation Board of Review. Affirmed, November 14, 1950. Superior Court of Pennsylvania, October term, 1950, no. 183. 

Nicasio J. Zagone, for appellant.

Wiliam L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, with him Charles J. Margiotti, Attorney General, and Roland 

M. Morgan, associate counsel, for appellee.

1950] 

 RHODES, P. J.: 

This is an appeal by claimant from the decision of the Unemployment Compensation Board of Review affirming the decision of the referee disallowing compensation benefits. The bureau had issued a decision allowing the claims filed on the ground that claimant met all the requirements of the Unemployment Compensation Law. The denial of benefits by the referee and the board was under section 402 (e) which was added to the Unemployment Compensation Law of December 5, 1936, P. L. (1937) 2897, by the amendatory Act of May 29, 1945, P. L. 1145, sec. 9, 43

PS §802(e) which provides: "An employee shall be ineligible for compensation for any week. . . . (e) In which his unemployment is due to his discharge or temporary suspension from work for willful misconduct connected with his work; . . ."

Appellant was last employed by the Yellow Cab Company, Philadelphia, Pennsylvania. He had been employed for several years as a mechanic in the Delaware County garage of the employer company. On November 28, 1949, appellant was discharged for sleeping during working hours in violation of a company regulation. A week previous to appellant's discharge he was found asleep while on duty and warned about his conduct. On November 26, 1949, appellant was found asleep in one of the company's cabs during his working hours. Having been previously warned that a repetition of such an act would lead to his dismissal, he was discharged on November 28th. Appellant was aware of the company's rule which prohibited maintenance employes from sitting in the cabs of the company at any time, and he admitted the incidents which led to his discharge. These facts were incorporated in the findings of the board, which were supported by substantial and legally competent evidence and justify the board's conclusion of wilful misconduct. In addition thereto it was found by the referee and the board that during a period between February 10, 1949, and May, 1949, appellant reported late for work on 22 occasions, and that he had been warned by his supervisor that continued tardiness would result in his discharge. This action of appellant

[36 Dep. Rep. 

was not the basis of his disqualification under section 402 (e), but was a relevant fact which could be considered in determining that appellant's behavior was tantamount to a deliberate disregard of duties and obligations owed by him to his employer.

Appellant's contention is that his conduct was not such as to make him ineligible for benefits under section 402 (e). 

Aside from the admitted tardiness on 22 occasions, appellant's violation of the company rule, of which he was aware, constituted wilful misconduct. Appellant testified: "Q. John, you heard this testimony of your employer did you not. A. That is right, he caught me asleep... Q.... 

Were you sound asleep? A. Yes, sir. Q. You know there is a rule you are not supposed to sit in cabs at any time? 

A. I heard it, yes sir." Appellant's attempted justification of his conduct was not accepted by the board. The result may be unfortunate for him but the employer was warranted in the action taken. 

Sleeping in a company cab while on duty was a deliberate violation of the company regulation, and consequently constituted wilful misconduct in connection with his employment. Appellant had been warned in this respect, and he admitted knowledge of the company rule. 

Appellant's complaint that the board did not give sufficient consideration to his testimony is without merit. There was no dispute about his entire course of conduct, and his excuses that his acts were due to domestic affairs did not constitute justification. As we have often said in cases of this type, the credibility of the witnesses, the weight of their testimony and the reasonable inferences to be drawn therefrom are for the board: Stillman Unemployment Compensation Case, 161 Pa. Superior Ct. 569, 575, 56 A. (2d) 380.

Where the board's findings are supported by substantial and competent evidence, it is not within our province, even if we should desire to find otherwise, to substitute our judgment for the board's in such matters: Devlin Unemployment 

Compensation Case, 165 Pa. Superior Ct. 153, 156, 67 A. (2d) 639. 

Decision is affirmed.

1950] 

 SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA SLEDZIANOWSKI v. UNEMPLOYMENT BOARD OF REVIEW 

Unemployment compensation-Leaving work without good cause- Offer of employment in work injurious to claimant's health. 

An order of the Unemployment Compensation Board of Review disallowing compensation to claimant on the ground that he voluntarily left his employment without good cause will be reversed where it appears that claimant employed as a stock clerk was offered a job of spray painting because of a recession in his employer's business and no work available in the stockkeeping department, but refused the proffered employment as paint sprayer because of his apprehension that it would be detrimental to his health, that he so advised the foreman and that at the hearing before the referee he offered in evidence a doctor's certificate to the effect that he was allergic to paint and became violently ill after his contact with it; under the circumstances claimant was justified in refusing as unsuitable work which on the basis of his past experience he had reason to believe he could not perform without serious injury to himself.

Appeal from decision of the Unemployment Compensation Board of Review, decision no. B-20891, Reversed. November 14, 1950, Superior Court of Pennsylvania, October term, 1950, no. 189. 

J. P. Gorham, for appellant. 

Charles J. Margiotti, Attorney General, William L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, and Richard H. 

Wagner, for appellee. 

HIRT, J.: 

Claimant applied for benefits under the Unemployment

Compensation Law. The referee in reversing the bureau denied the claim on the ground that claimant voluntarily left his work without good cause under section 402(b), as amended by the Act of May 23, 1949, P. L. 1738, 43 PS §802.

The board, on appeal, affirmed the referee's order of disallowance.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

We are unable to find any serious dispute as to the facts. 

Claimant had been employed by Yale and Towne Manufacturing Company as a stockroom clerk or storekeeper for seven and one-half years. As storekeeper, his assignment involved the keeping of records of materials and his duties were largely supervisory and clerical although they involved some manual work. He previously had been employed by Sears and Roebuck as a stock clerk for 15 years. On 

April 18, 1949, he was given the lower classification of checker in the stockroom with a reduction in wages from $1.32 to $1.16 per hour. The demotion was due entirely to a falling off in the employer's business which made it necessary to reduce the number of workmen in various departments. Claimant's services were always satisfactory. Because of a further recession in the employer's business, no work was available to claimant in that classification after 

June 17, 1949, and his employment in the stockkeeping department terminated on that date. Claimant's foreman, representing the employer, then offered him a job of spray painting rough castings and stores, at a further reduction in wages $1.09 per hour. Claimant refused the proferred employment as a paint sprayer because of his apprehension that it would be detrimental to his health. He gave that as the controlling reason, although he also demurred to the change in his work classification.

The appeal from the decision of the bureau was heard by the referee on October 4, 1949. At the hearing, the employer was represented by counsel but claimant was not. 

He, nevertheless, brought with him to the hearing a certificate signed by his physician to this effect: "This is to certify that Mieceslaw [the claimant] has been treated by me on dates given below, complaining at that time of headache, dizzy spells and nausea. In taking the medical history it has been established that the forementioned attacks follow the use of paints around the house. Treated on May 8, 1947, August 18, 1947, June 14, 1948, May 23, 1949, May 24, 1949.

This patient has been told to refrain from coming in contact with any kind of paints, as he seems to be definitely allergic to them and becomes violently ill after each con-

1950] 

 tact." This statement was admitted in evidence by the referee without objection from the employer's counsel. And, since the doctor's certificate was admitted without objection, it is competent evidence, undisputed in this record, of the statement of facts contained in it. The same principles have application here as in proceedings under the Workmen's Compensation Law. A rule of evidence not invoked must be regarded as waived. "Inadmissible evidence, including hearsay evidence, admitted without objection, is not a nullity or void of probative force, but is to be given its natural probative effect as if it was in law admissible": 

Poluski v. Glen Alden Coal Co., 286 Pa. 473, 133 Atl. 819.

It is conceded that the certificate of claimant's physician "was accepted" by the board. The testimony of a medical witness even though uncontradicted is not binding on the board: Stufflet v. Fraternal Order of Eagles, 164 Pa. Su perior Ct. 473, 65 A.(2d) 443. But there is nothing either in the decision of the referee or of the board to indicate that it was not accepted as proof of plaintiff's allergy to paint. 

It is not of controlling importance that the board, in denying benefits, found: "The claimant was offered this job as a temporary expedient only, since as soon as the emergency had passed he would have been returned to his former position." Whether the work offered claimant was temporary or not is beside the point, although in fact the record refutes every inference that the transfer of claimant was intended as a temporary expedient. The hearing was held before the board on January 10, 1950, and there is no suggestion that his former employment would have been open to him then. Nor is there any evidence that other employes laid off under similar circumstances had ever been recalled. 

Of course, the burden rested on claimant to establish good cause under section 402 (b) in refusing the paint spraying job: Wolfson v. Unemployment Compensation Board, 167

Pa. Superior Ct. 588. In our view, however, the board erred as a matter of law in imposing a more stringent burden of proof on this claimant than was contemplated by the Act. In that connection the board said: ". . . the claimant did not inform the employer of the fact that he had

[36 Dep. Rep. 

suffered from a paint allergy, nor did he seek to ascertain whether or not the paint used in the plant was of the type which he now alleges would have been deleterious to his health. The claimant thus failed in his obligation to place all the facts before his employer, supporting them by medical corroboration, and under the circumstances, we feel that he demonstrated a lack of diligence and a lack of interest in preserving his employment relationship with the company". (Italics added.) Claimant's uncontradicted testimony as to what he said to his foreman and the latter's reaction to it, when he was offered the paint spraying job, was as follows: "I said that job would be detrimental to my health. The foreman showed no interest in my condition.

I told him it would be deterimental . . . I never done anything like that. He said I guess I will have to lay you off”.

Claimant offered to take other employment but was informed that none was available and claimant was laid off by his foreman on June 17, 1949, following the above discussion.

The foreman was not called as a witness. Notwithstanding the attitude of the foreman in not inviting a discussion as to the effect of paint on claimant's health, the board would deny benefits to claimant regardless of an actual disabling allergy because he did not support his statement to his foreman by medical corroboration. 

If, in fact, the paint used by claimant's employer for spraying was not deleterious to one allergic to house paint and claimant had been so informed, he then would have been duty bound to demonstrate his allergy to the spray paint before refusing the proffered job. But under the circumstances claimant was justified in refusing as unsuitable, work which on the basis of his past experience, he had reasonable apprehension to believe he could not perform without serious injury to himself. Cf. Suska Unemployment 

Compensation Case, 166 Pa. Superior Ct. 293, 70 A. (2d) 397.

The order is reversed and the proceeding is remitted to the board for the entry of an appropriate award.

1950] 

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD COOPER v. ECLIPSE PATTERN CO. 

Workmen's compensation—Specific injury—Loss of finger—Prior in juries to other fingers-Compensation for loss of use of hand.

A referee's order terminating compensation to claimant after payment for the loss of a little finger will be affirmed, where prior to the accident resulting in the loss of the finger, claimant had lost the first, second and third fingers and contended that his disability as the result of the subsequent loss of his little finger amounted to the loss of use of a hand; claimant could recover for the loss of the little finger only in the absence of evidence that the loss of use of the hand was due to the consequent effects of the amputation of that finger and not to prior injuries to the other fingers.

Appeal by claimant from order of Referee McGovern, District No. 8, agreement no. 5,379,379. Affirmed. October 

2, 1950. 

American Mutual Liability Ins. Co. insurance carrier. 

Sidney Rosenblum, for claimant. 

D. S. Newman, for defendant.

KNOLL, Chairman: 

This is an appeal by the claimant from an order of the referee terminating compensation under an agreement. 

On February 14, 1949, the parties entered into a compensation agreement, providing for the payment of compensation at the rate of $20 a week, commencing on February 8, 1949, and describing the accident of January 31, 1949, and the resulting injuries as follows: "Claimant was running board over joiner and hand slipped and little finger came in contact with knife blade. 

Amputation of little finger on left hand."

A supplemental agreement entered into on March 14, 1949, set out the injury as above and provided for the payment of compensation at the rate of $20 a week from Feb-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

ruary 8, 1949, for a period of 15 weeks. Thereafter, on 

October 14, 1949, claimant filed its petition for termination alleging that claimant had been paid compensation in full for the loss of the left little finger. Claimant's answer, filed on October 20, 1949, alleged that "loss of little finger has now changed my disability to loss of hand a/c previous injury." After hearings, the referee ordered the termination of the compensation agreement at the end of the 15 week period, namely, May 24, 1949. This appeal followed.

Much as we may regret the result, we are of the opinion that we are bound by the decision of the Superior Court in the case of Doves v. American Reduction Co., 146 Pa. Superior Ct. 474, and, therefore, must affirm the action of the referee.

In that case, the evidence established that before claimant, in the accident in question, cut the little finger of his left hand, resulting in its amputation, he had three injured and deformed fingers, the injuries to which had affected the hand as a whole, leaving only the thumb and the little finger intact. It was held on appeal that the loss of the use of the hand was not due to the accident to the little finger, but to the prior injuries to the hand and that claimant was not entitled to compensation for the loss of the use of his hand but only for the loss of the little finger.

In the instant case, also, the evidence is to the effect that, previous to the accident resulting in the amputation of the little finger of claimant's left hand, which is the basis of the present action, claimant had lost the first, second and third fingers at the joint between the proximal and middle phalanx, at the second joint.

The causal connection between the particular accident and the disability claimed must be established by the evi dence: Miller v. Textor Transfer Co., 112 Pa. Superior Ct. 

528. 

As was stated in Dover v. American Reduction Co., supra: "A man who has little more than the stump of a hand, outside of his thumb and little finger, as a result of prior accidents, cannot ask an insurance carrier to pay for the loss of the hand because he accidentally loses the little finger re-

1950] 

 maining on the stump. If the accident is limited to the amputation of the finger, his compensation is limited to the loss of the finger. If an employee, who has previously lost one eye, accidentally loses his other eye, he is paid compensation for the loss of an eye-not for the loss of two eyes, even though he is then totally blind. Lente v. Luci, 275 Pa. 217.”

Thus, in the absence of proof establishing that the loss of the use of the hand is due to the accident to the little finger and the consequent effects of the amputation of that finger and not to prior accidents and injuries to other fingers, the defendant is liable for only the loss of the little finger. The proof of the required facts, as set out above, is not present in this case. The order of the referee must, therefore, be affirmed.

We add the following findings and conclusions to the referee's discussion and order. 

1. That on January 31, 1949, claimant was engaged in the employ of defendant at an average weekly wage of $82. 

2. That neither claimant nor defendant had rejected arti cle III of the Pennsylvania Workmen's Compensation Act. 

3. The board finds as a fact that on January 31, 1949, while claimant was running a board over a joiner, the course of his employment, with defendant, his hand slipped and the little finger of his left hand came in contact with a knife blade, resulting in the amputation of the little finger of his left hand. 

4. That, 45 years previous, claimant had lost the first, second and third fingers of the left hand at the joint between the proximal and middle phalanx at the second joint. 

5. That claimant did not suffer the loss of his left hand as a result of the accident of January 31, 1949.

1. The board concludes as a matter of law that both claimant and defendant were bound by the provisions of the Penn sylvania Workmen's Compensation Act.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

2. The board further concludes that claimant did not suffer the loss of his left hand, as a result of the accidental injuries suffered by him on January 31, 1949, within the meaning of the Workmen's Compensation Act and, there fore, that he is not entitled to compensation for the loss of his left hand. 

3. Further, the board concludes that claimant is entitled to compensation for the loss of the little finger of his left hand and that defendant is entitled to have compensation payments under the existing agreement terminated as of May 24, 1949, the end of the 15 week period provided under the Act for the loss of the little finger."

The order of the referee is affirmed. 

Claimant's appeal is dismissed.

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA WOLFSON v. UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION BOARD OF REVIEW 

Unemployment compensation-Leaving work without good cause—Refusal to work six days a week-Housewife. 

An order of the Unemployment Compensation Board of Review denying a claim for compensation on the ground that claimant left her employment voluntarily and without good cause within the meaning of the act will be affirmed, where it appears that claimant who was employed as a saleswoman five days a week for a period in excess of a year was informed that her regular schedule would be changed to a six-day week, which assignment she refused to accept advancing as the sole reason therefor that she needed one day a week to do her housework. 

Appeal from decision of Unemployment Compensation 

Board of Review, January 27, 1950, Decision no. B-19966. 

Affirmed. November 13, 1950. Superior Court of Pennsylvania, October term, 1950, no. 127.

1950] 

Beatrice Wolfson, p.p., appellant. 

Charles J. Margiotti, Attorney General, William L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, Roland M. Morgan, associate counsel, and R. C. Grasberger, for appellee.

HIRT, J.: 

The board refused the appellant's claim for unemployment benefits on the ground that leaving her employment was voluntary on her part and without good cause, within the meaning of section 402 (b) of the Unemployment Compensation Law as amended by the Act of June 30, 1947, P. L. 1186, 43 PS §802. 

These facts are undisputed: Claimant had been employed by Luz Luball, Inc., as a saleswoman for almost 15 months. 

She originally applied for work on a part time basis, and she accepted employment on her understanding that she was to work but three days a week. But immediately after she was hired, she acceded to her employer's request that she work five days each week, with Thursday as her day off. There is no evidence of an agreement that her status then was that of a part time employe. On occasion thereafter, her employer requested her to work on Thursdays also and for about two months within the period of her employment she worked on a full weekly schedule of six days. 

Early in June 1949, claimant's employer informed her that her regular schedule thereafter was going to be changed to a six-day week. Claimant refused to accept the changed schedule as a regular assignment, for the reason alleged that she would be unable to perform her regular household duties. She quit rather than work an additional day every week. She is married and her husband is employed. She has three children, 11, 14 and 17 years of age. But it was not necessary that she care for them since the eldest was able and competent, and had been looking after the two younger children. Findings to the above effect, in material respects, have not been questioned by appellant; no exceptions were taken to the findings and since they are based on sufficient competent testimony they are binding upon us,

[36 Dep. Rep. 

under section 510 of the Act of December 5, 1937, P. L. 

2897, 43 PS §830; Kaylock Unemploy. Compensation Case, 165 Pa. Superior Ct. 376, 67 A. (2d) 801.

The burden was on claimant to establish good cause for leaving her work, since she left voluntarily: Wescoe Un employment Comp. Case, 166 Pa. Superior Ct. 355, 71 

A. (2d) 837. And, under the circumstances, in determining whether she quit her job voluntarily without good cause, it becomes necessary, under the provisions of section 402(b), to determine whether the board was right in its conclusion that the offered employment for a full six-day week was suitable work within the purview of section 4 (t) of the act, as amended May 23, 1949, P. L. 1738, 43 PS §753.

We have recognized that "good cause" under the Unemployment Compensation Law need not necessarily arise out of the employment, to the exclusion of all extraneous factors, including reasons personal to the claimant: Teicher Unem ployment Comp. Case, 154 Pa. Superior Ct. 250, 35 A. (2d) 

739. But as was made clear by Judge Reno in Sturderant 

Unemployment Comp. Case, 158 Pa. Superior Ct. 548, 45 

A. (2d) 898, personal reasons when asserted as "good cause" under the statute must be substantial reasons arising from the circumstances which fairly "compel the decision to leave employment"; and "always the element of good faith" must be considered in determining whether the excuse for leaving is adequate.

The present claimant does not contend that it was necessary for her to leave her work to take care of her children. 

And the sole reason advanced by her for refusing the proffered employment on a full time basis was that she needed one day a week to do her housework. Many married women are away from home for 44 hours each week in outside employment and still are able to do their housework. And, if this claimant found that impossible, she might have engaged another for one day in the week to do the housework which she had previously performed on her day off. A married woman may leave her work voluntarily and with good cause because of domestic obligations which are necessitous and compelling: Mattey Unemployment Comp. Case, 164

1950] 

Pa. Superior Ct. 36, 63 A. (2d) 429. But clearly good cause under section 402(b) for voluntarily leaving work, does not include the reason advanced by the present claimant based solely on matters of personal convenience to her. 

Compulsion in a fair degree and not convenience is the test. 

The proffered work of the same kind which claimant had been performing was suitable employment. When she quit because unwilling to accept full time employment, she left her work without good cause within the intent of section 

402 (b) of the Act. 

Order affirmed.

WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD CLARKE v. KOTROSKI Workmen's compensation—Employe—Injury in course of regular busi ness of defendant-Carpenter hired to build house. 

A referee's award of compensation will be affirmed, where it appears that claimant, a carpenter, had been hired by defendant, a truck driver and bread salesman to build a home for defendant, and was injured in the course of his employment; in such case claimant's employment was neither casual nor was it outside the regular course of defendant's business within the meaning of the Workmen's Compensation Act. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Carmalt, specially presiding in Second District, claim petition no. 

118,462, accident no. 5,478,532. Affirmed. November 30, 1950. 

Milton J. Kolansky, for claimant. 

A. J. Zawoiski, for defendant.

JACOBY, Commissioner: 

The facts in this case are not in great dispute. 

Charles Clarke, claimant, aged 66, a carpenter, was hired by Joseph Kotroski, defendant, to help build a home for the

[36 Dep. Rep. 

latter. Claimant's wage was $9 a day. On September 15, 1949, when claimant had been on the job five weeks, Clarke stepped on a loose joist and fell from the attic into the cellar, sustaining lacerations of the head and bruises of the body. 

Dr. Robert Koons, a general practitioner, last examined claimant on October 10, 1949, at which time he felt that claimant had a 50 percent disability, due to difficulty in using his right arm. The referee found that claimant was totally disabled to December 22, 1949, when all disability ceased. From that decision defendant has taken this appeal on the ground that claimant's employment was casual and not in the regular course of the business of defendant. 

It is well settled at law that unless claimant's employment was both casual and not in the regular course of defendant's business, he is an employe within the meaning of the Workmen's Compensation Act.

The word "regular" modifies "course" not business and the question is whether an employment is in the regular course of defendant's business, not whether the employment is in the course of defendant's regular business. See Snavely v. 

Redemptorist Fathers, 151 Pa. Superior Ct. 625. That defendant here was a truck driver and bread salesman is immaterial and of no import. He was engaged in the business of building a home when claimant was injured. 

Nor do we think the employment was casual. Building a house is a project, and claimant had been employed for five weeks when injured.

Claimant testified that he was totally disabled for three months and the referee so found. Although claimant contends that he is still disabled, he has had no medical treatment since October 10, 1949.

We affirm the referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and award. 

The defendant's appeal is dismissed.

1950]  

SUPERIOR COURT OF PENNSYLVANIA SABATELLI v. UNEMPLOYMENT COMPENSATION BOARD OF REVIEW et al. 

Unemployment compensation-Discharge for wilful misconduct-Bus operator-Failure to register fares-Absence of intent to defraud-Finding by board.

An order of the Unemployment Compensation Board of Review denying compensation to claimant on the ground that his discharge from his employment was due to his wilful misconduct connected with his work will be affirmed, where there was evidence to support the board's finding that claimant, employed as a bus operator, by a transportation company disregarded the company's rules on several occasions as to collecting and registering fares; in such case the fact. that the board concluded there was no intention to defraud the company did not make his conduct any the less wilful, there being ample evidence to sustain the board's finding that claimant's failure to register fares was not merely inadvertence.

Appeal from Unemployment Compensation Board of Re view, no. B-44-99-G-3306, decision no. B-18691. Affirmed. 

November 22, 1950, Superior Court of Pennsylvania, October term, 1950 no. 11. 

A. B. Gerber, with him Sydney B. Kolovsky, Garber &

Galfant, F. Harry Galfand, for appellant. 

William L. Hammond, Special Deputy Attorney General, with him Charles J. Margiotti, Attorney General, and 

Richard H. Wagner, for appellees. 

Francis Ballard, with him Hamilton C. Connor, Jr., Frederic L. Ballard, Ballard, Spahr, Andrews & Ingersoll, for employer, intervenor.

GUNTHER, J.: 

Claimant, in this unemployment compensation proceeding, appeals from the disallowance of benefits. He was dis-

[36 Dep. Rep. 

charged by his employer on November 23, 1948; the bureau denied benefits on the ground that claimant's discharge was due to ". . . willful misconduct connected with his work" under section 402 (e). The referee reversed the bureau's decision and allowed benefits, and the employer appealed to the board. The board reversed the decision of the referee, reinstated the bureau's decision and denied benefits. 

Claimant was employed as a bus driver by the Philadelphia Transportation Company, employer, from January 13, 1944, to November 23, 1948. At the time of hiring, claimant was given several weeks instruction in the operation of the bus, including the proper collection and registering of fares. 

The evidence indicates that for a period of about three months after his employment in 1944, he was warned about his failure to properly register fares. From April 14, 1944, when claimant was last warned about neglect of fares, until September 14, 1948, claimant satisfactorily performed his services as a bus driver. Between September 14, 1948, and 

November 20, 1948, claimant was checked independently by eight inspectors, all of whom reported improper fare registrations on 24 different occasions, and as a result he was discharged on November 23, 1948. Claimant sought to explain his fare neglects on November 20, 1948, in that (1) he was under considerable nervous strain because his motherin-law was suddenly taken seriously ill by a heart attack, and (2) a football game created an extraordinary rush of bus patrons. No explanations were attempted for fare neglects on the other dates. The board found as facts that "The company discharged the claimant on November 23, 1948, for the several failures to collect and register the fares according to the rules of the company. These rules were known by the claimant. The claimant on these days turned in money in excess of the number of registered fares but not enough to cover the reported neglect". The following finding by the 

1 Unemployment Compensation Law, sec. 402 (e), 43 PS §802, provides "An employe shall be ineligible for compensation for any week (e) In which his unemployment is due to his discharge. from work for willful misconduct connected with his work." ...

1950] 

 board gives rise to the present appeal. The board found: "We appreciate that under such circumstances the claimant would be under some strain and if the neglects had occurred only on that day, we would be inclined to the view that there was no willful misconduct. However, the record discloses that during the last two or three months of his employment there were numerous failures to register fares. The company does not contend and the record does not indicate that the claimant was trying to defraud the company. The claimant's course of conduct, however, indicates such a careless disregard of the company's rules and its welfare that we must conclude that his action constitutes willful misconduct within the meaning of Section 402 (e) of the law." 

Claimant concedes that neglect of fares in these circumstances is "misconduct", but contends that in light of the board's finding that he was "not trying to defraud the company" such misconduct was not "willful" and that, therefore, the order of the board cannot be sustained. 2 

Claimant argues that the word "willful" means an intentional act; that since the board concluded there was no "intention" to defraud the company, an integral requirement of section 402 (e) is lacking. Briefly summarized, claimant contends that "willful" is synomous with "actual intent"; that since the board found claimant was not "trying to defraud the company", he had no actual intention sufficient to convert his misconduct into "willful misconduct"; that the board's finding of "careless disregard" indicated merely a lack of care or negligence. Claimant's argument, however, is only a half truth. Negligence consists of inattention or inadvertance. Wilfulness exists where the injury to the employer, though realized, is so "recklessly disregardful" that, even though there be no actual intent, there is at least a willingness to inflict harm, a conscious 

2 An examination of the unemployment compensation laws of other jurisdictions indicates that Pennsylvania and three other states are the only jurisdictions using the phraseology "willful misconduct" barring compensation in these circumstances.

[36 Dep. Rep. 

indifference to the perpetration of the wrong. In such case a constructive intention is imputable to him. Cf. Kasanovich v. George, 348 Pa. 199, 203, 34 A. (2d) 523; 38 Am. Jur. 

692, §48; 55 Yale Law Journal, 162 (1945); A. L. I. Restate ment of the Law of Torts, $282, comment (d). There is ample evidence to sustain the finding of the board that the improper registration of fares was not merely inadvertance. 

Claimant was not a novice but a veteran operator. He was familiar with the company's rules, was experienced in the operation of a bus and the registration of fares, and for a long period of time demonstrated his ability to perform his work properly. Viewed in these circumstances, the board properly concluded that claimant's misconduct was "willful" within the meaning of section 402 (e). 

Decision affirmed. 

3 There is no authority in this jurisdiction on the precise question raised: Guede Unemployment Compensation Case, 162 Pa. Superior Ct. 

479, 58 A. (2d) 197; Howard Unemployment Compensation Case, 160 

Pa. Superior Ct. 636, 53 A. (2d) 819; and Devlin Unemployment Compensation Case, 165 Pa. Superior Ct. 153, 67 A. (2d) 639, are of little assistance.

COMMON PLEAS COURT NO. 6 OF PHILADELPHIA COUNTY MOTLEY v. C. F. BRAUN CONSTRUCTION COMPANY 

1 

Workmen's compensation-Dependency-Widow living apart from husband.

An order of the Workmen's Compensation Board denying compen sation to claimant, widow, on the ground that she was not dependent upon decedent for support will be reversed, where, although decedent was not living with claimant at the time of his death, during the last four years of his life she received about eight percent of her income from him, and where her only other means of support amounting to less than $30 per week, included board from her daughter from which she had to pay for her daughter's food; the only conclusion to be

1950] 

 drawn from such facts was that at the time of decedent's death, claimant was receiving from him a substantial portion of her support and was dependent upon him within the meaning of the act. 

In the Court of Common Pleas No. 6 of Philadelphia 

County, December term, 1948, no. 3851.

Appeal by claimant from Workmen's Compensation Board. Reversed, December 

4, 1950. 

John M. Smith, Jr., for claimant. 

Vincent P. Sumerfield, Jr., for defendant.

FLOOD, J.: 

Claimant seeks to reverse an order of the Workmen's Compensation Board denying her claim in connection with the death of her husband. He was killed as a result of an accident in the course of his employment but compensation was denied her by the board on the ground that she was not actually dependent upon him or receiving substantial support from him at the time of his death and that she was not living with him at that time.

The evidence shows that the deceased left claimant, married another woman bigamously, and had a child by the bigamous wife several years before his death. Although he visited the claimant a number of times later, it does not appear that she was informed of the bigamous marriage until a few weeks before his death. 

1. The evidence supports the board's finding that decedent was not living with claimant at the time of his death. Therefore, under the amendment of June 21, 1939, P. L. 520, sec. 1, 77 PS §562, in order to recover, claimant must establish both (1) that claimant was actually dependent upon the deceased at the time of his death, and (2) that she was receiving from him a substantial portion of her support. 

The relevant facts, as stated by the board are as follows: 

In 1945, decedent left the common home and did not return again, except for six visits in 1945, three in 1946 and

[36 Dep. Rep. 

about six in 1947. He never remained overnight and only slept a few hours with claimant on these visits. During the period of separation, decedent made small contributions to claimant totaling $319, and claimant was obliged to seek employment as a cashier at a weekly salary of $16. She also received $5 per week as board from a daughter and a monthly allotment of $37 from a son in the service. Nevertheless, she was constantly in debt. Since the time of the separation, no attempt was made by her to secure a court order for support against the decedent. 

2. The referee found as a fact that claimant was not dependent upon the decedent for support. The board affirmed the finding. However, this is not such a finding as binds us. It is merely a conclusion or an inference from the facts above recited. Upon such a matter we are entitled, and therefore required, to exercise our independent judgment and to reverse the board's conclusion if in our opinion it is an improper one in the light of the basic facts found by it: Haas v. Brotherhood of Transportation Workers, C. P. No. 6 of Phila. County, September term, 1940, no. 

3836, affirmed in 158 Pa. Superior Ct. 291, 301 (1945); Cf. 

Sheaffer v. Penn Dairies, Inc., 161 Pa. Superior Ct. 583, 586 (1948).

It seems quite clear to us that claimant was dependent upon the deceased. Excluding his contributions, she had an income of less than $30 a week, which included board from her daughter and from which, therefore, she had to pay for her daughter's food. It is not hard to understand how in the postwar years she was constantly in debt under these circumstances. She was, therefore, dependent upon anything she could get from her husband. She apparently did get sums from him, even though irregularly, averaging approximately $9 per month. "To sustain an award claimant was not obliged to establish total dependency. . . Although claimant was not living with deceased, if she was being supported by him in whole or in part at the time of his accidental death, she was a dependent within the meaning

1950] 

 of the statute": Sandy v. Hazle Brook Coal Co., 138 Pa. 

Superior Ct. 581, 583 (1940). 

3. To obtain an award, claimant is also required to show that she was at the time of decedent's death "receiving from him a substantial portion of her support". As to whether the facts here found met this requirement, the authorities give no clear answer. The fact that he was not giving her a fixed weekly or monthly sum does not bar her: Petrucci v. Payne Coal Co., 157 Pa. Superior Ct. 122 (1945). The case last cited indicates that, if the husband, neither by his words or conduct, repudiates his obligation to support the claimant, the requirement of the act is satisfied. By the same token, if the facts found indicate that she was constantly attempting to make him fulfill his obligation of support and had some little success, she should recover, even though for some time prior to his death she had been receiving nothing from him: Binkley v. Stone & Webster 

Engineering Co., 156 Pa. Superior Ct. 381 (1944). Under the circumstances here shown, the fact that she did not go to the extent of taking him to court does not bar her. Cf. 

Berman v. George J. Blair Co., 137 Pa. Superior Ct. 193, 196 (1939).

The figures given by the board show that the wife during the last four years of her husband's life received about eight percent of her income from him. It seems to us that this is sufficient to meet the requirement of the act that she was receiving a substantial portion of her support from him, even though she received it in irregular payments. 

The Superior Court has said on more than one occasion that the purpose of this amendment was to bar fraudulent claims: Petrucci v. Payne Coal Co., supra, at page 124; 

Binkley v. Stone & Webster Engineering Corp., supra. at page 384. We have a claim here by decedent's lawful wife, who apparently did not know until a feew weeks before his death that he had been living bigamously with another woman. She was unquestionably dependent upon the small amount he gave her to eke out her living. From these facts,

[36 Dep. Rep. 

the board should have found that she was receiving a substantial portion of her support from decedent. 

In our opinion, from the basic facts found by the board, the only proper conclusion is that claimant was at the time of decedent's death dependent upon him and receiving from him a substantial part of her support. Since this question is one of law, we need not send the matter back to the board: Rovere v. Interstate Cemetery Co., 164 Pa. Superior 

Ct. 233, 235 (1949). We now reverse the board's conclusion and enter judgment for claimant. 

And now, December 4, 1950, claimant's exceptions numbered 3, 4, 5, 6 and 7 to the order of the Workmen's Compensation Board (affirming the referee's disallowance of compensation and dismissing the appeal) are sustained.

Judgment is entered for claimant, Mrs. Martha C. Motley, against defendant C. F. Braun Construction Company, and against insurance carrier defendant Lumberman's Mutual 

Casualty Co. in the sum of $4,500 being the compensation payable for 300 weeks from November 9, 1947, at the rate of $15 per week. The amounts of compensation actually due at the time the first payment is made shall bear interest at the rate of six per centum per annum, from the day the claim was presented to the board.

1950] 

 COMMON PLEAS COURT NO. 1 OF PHILADELPHIA COUNTY MYERS v. PHILADELPHIA DAILY NEWS et al. 

Workmen's compensation-Section 319 of Workmen's Compensation Act-Subrogation rights of employer— 

Funeral expenses.

Under section 319 of the Workmen's Compensation Act providing that the employer shall be subrogated to the rights of his employe or the latter's personal representative against a third party legally responsible for the injuries to the employe, to the extent of the compensation payable by the employer, the right of subrogation includes funeral expenses for which the employer is liable under section 307 of the act.

In the Court of Common Pleas No. 1 of Philadelphia 

County, June term, 1950, no. 4977. Appeal from Workmen's

Compensation Board. Affirmed. October 5, 1950. 

M. R. Beckman, for plaintiff. 

P. H. Ferguson, for defendants.

HAGAN, J.: 

This is an appeal from a decision of the Pennsylvania 

Workmen's Compensation Board holding that the defend ant employer was subrogated to burial expenses against claimant's recovery from a third party.

Claimant filed a petition against defendant for compensation by reason of the death of her husband resulting from an automobile accident which occurred in the course of his employment. Defendant disclaimed liability. An action was instituted by claimant against a third party, which action was settled for the sum of $6,500. At the trial of the compensation case, the only issue upon stipulation was whether the defendant employer's right of subrogation in respect to claimant's recovery against the third party extended to burial expenses in the sum of $200 specified in section 

307 (7) of the Workmen's Compensation Act. The referee

[36 Dep. Rep. 

decided against the employer, but the board reversed and found that the employer was entitled to be so subrogated. 

The sole question presented to this court is whether the word "compensation" as used in section 319 of the Work men's Compensation Act, 77 PS §671, includes the liability of the employer for funeral expenses imposed by section 

307 of the act: 77 PS §561. Section 319 provides: 

"Where the compensable injury is caused in whole or in part by the act or omission of a third party, the employer shall be subrogated to the right of the employe, his personal representative, his estate or his dependents, against such third party for the balance of any sum recovered in litigation, or paid in compromise settlement, after subtraction of reasonable attorney's fees and other proper disbursements, but only to the extent of the compensation payable under this article by the employer. Any recovery against such third person in excess of the compensation theretofore paid by the employer shall be paid forthwith to the employe or to the dependents, and shall be treated as an advance payment by the employer on account of any future installments of compensation."

The relevant portion of section 307 of the act is as follows: "In case of death, compensation shall be computed on the following basis and distributed to the following persons: "7. Whether or not there be dependents as aforesaid, the reasonable expense of burial, not exceeding two hundred dollars, which shall be paid by the employer or insurer directly to the undertaker (without deduction of any amounts theretofore paid for compensation or for medical expenses).

The term "compensation" has been given a narrow interpretation as including only wage payments, and also the broader interpretation of including in addition thereto, medical, surgical and hospital expenses.

The Supreme Court in Paolis v. Tower Hill Connellsville 

Coke Co., 265 Pa. 291, held that the word "compensation"

1950] 

 as used in section 315 of the act, which provides that any payment of "compensation" within a year tolls the statute of limitations, does not include medical, surgical and hospital expenses. However, that decision, having to do with limitation of action, does not determine the meaning of the word "compensation" for all purposes or as used in other sections of the act.

In Haley, to use, v. Matthews, 104 Superior Ct. 313, the question presented to the court by stipulation was "whether an employer, who claims the right of subrogation against the one who caused the injury to the employee, may recover the sums expended for medical and hospital bills." The court held that "compensation" as used in section 319 included medical, surgical and hospital bills because "the Legislature evidently intended that where a person is responsible for an injury to the employee the employer, who has been subrogated to the employe's right, is substituted, not to a portion of, but to all, his rights, until he is reimbursed for whatever sums he was required to pay the employe under the compensation act. There appears to be no sound reason why an employer should be obligated to pay medical and hospital expenses, where he is in no way responsible for the injury, and the wrong-doer escape the liability therefor."

Claimant contends that the Haley decision does not control the instant case. He argues that the action there was between the employer as use plaintiff and the third party, and that the act does not affect the liability of the third party. When the action is between the employer and the employe, as in the instant case, plaintiff contends that the act governing the liabilities has to be liberally construed; and, as between the two, the employer has to bear the cost of medical or burial expenses. This argument seems to ignore the fact that the court in the Haley case, passing upon the meaning of section 319, decided that it provided that the employer was subrogated to medical and hospital expenses. 

It seems inconceivable that the legislature intended that if

[36 Dep. Rep. 

the employer first paid the claim, the employer could sue the third party and recover his medical expenses, but if the employer disputed the claim and the employe proceeded to recover against the third party, that the employer would not be subrogated to such expense. 

There remains only the question, as claimant contends, whether the legislature by providing in section 307 (7) for the payment of funeral expenses directly to the undertaker "without deduction of any amount theretofore paid for compensation or for medical expenses" thus intended to distinguish between funeral expenses and compensation. It is true that the act does make some distinction, but it is made only as to the manner and requirement of payment. The board seems to be on firm ground in holding "that the Legislature inserted the provision as a device to prevent money awarded to cover funeral expenses from falling into irresponsible hands and possibly be diverted from the use for which it was ordered paid." This in no way affects the question of whether it is to be considered "compensation" under section 319. The equitable principles applicable to medical expenses apply equally as well to funeral expenses and is similarly to be considered as included within the meaning of "compensation" in section 319.

The appeal of claimant is dismissed, and the order of the 

Workmen's Compensation Board is affirmed.

1950] 

 WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION BOARD TUCKER v. STATE WORKMEN'S INS. FUND Workmen's compensation—Jurisdiction—Residence of both parties in another state-Performance of work within Pennsylvania.

The Workmen's Compensation Board has jurisdiction of a claim for compensation where claimant, a resident of New York State, sustained an accidental injury in the course of his employment for defendants who were also residents of New York State, where the contract of hiring was made in New York, but the work was to be performed in Pennsylvania, and defendants had secured compensation insurance coverage by the State Workmen's Insurance Fund. 

Appeal by defendant from award of Referee Smith, District No. 8, claim petition no. 118,652, accident no. 5,488,983. 

Affirmed. November 15, 1950.

State Workmen's Ins. Fund, insurance carrier. 

C. M. Bliss, for claimant. 

Ralph H. Behney, for defendant.

KNOLL, Chairman: 

This is an appeal by the State Workmen's Insurance Fund, defendant's insurance carrier, from the referee's award of compensation.

The facts are not in dispute. Claimant, a resident of 

Bolivar, New York, suffered accidental injuries to his left hand on October 6, 1949, while in the course of his employment for the defendant. The accident occurred in Mt. Jewett, Pa. The employer was the G. & M. Abandoning Company formed by C. W. Greene and R. C. Monroe, both individuals being residents of New York State. The contract of hiring was made in New York State and claimant was informed that he would be covered by the compensation laws of Pennsylvania. The G. & M. Abandoning Company was insured

[36 Dep. Rep. 

by the State Workmen's Insurance Fund by a policy effective as of May 5, 1949.

Defendant contends that, since the parties were not residents of this Commonwealth and the contract of hiring was made in New York State, the compensation authorities of Pennsylvania are without jurisdiction in this claim. The only authority cited by defendant is Miller v. McAler Mfg. 

Co., 16 W. C. B. 143, and we copy from the brief as follows: "An employee of an employer, resident of another state, is not entitled to compensation under the Pennsylvania Act, although injured in this state. The Board has no jurisdiction of such a claim."

Our review of the Miller case discloses that it was decided on the theory that our compensation authorities had no jurisdiction because the claimant had elected to enter into a compensation agreement under the statute of the State of Michigan and had signed a final receipt and had not filed exceptions to the final receipt. We do not find anywhere in the opinion the sweeping statement of law quoted by defendant.

In the instant case, the parties had elected to accept the jurisdiction of the Workmen's Compensation Act of Penn sylvania. The work was to be done in Pennsylvania, the employer had secured compensation insurance coverage in Pennsylvania and the accident occurred in the Commonwealth.

Article I, sec. 101 of the act provides that it applies to all accidents occurring within this Commonwealth, irrespective of the place where the contract of hiring was made, renewed or extended. 

Accordingly, since the parties had elected to come under our statute and since the accident occurred in Pennsylvania, it is our opinion that we have jurisdiction in this case. 

The referee's findings of fact, conclusions of law and order of award are affirmed. 

Defendant's appeal is dismissed.
